## Página PDF 6

Preface 
We are delighted to introduce the proceedings of the 20th European Alliance for Innova-
tion (EAI) International Conference on Collaborative Computing: Networking, Appli-
cations and Worksharing (CollaborateCom 2024). This conference brought together 
researchers, developers and practitioners around the world who are interested in fully 
realizing the promises of electronic collaboration from the aspects of networking, tech-
nology and systems, user interfaces and interaction paradigms, and interoperation with 
application-speciﬁc components and tools. 
This year’s conference accepted 62 submissions. Each submission was reviewed by 
an average of 3 reviewers. The conference sessions were: Session 1 – Edge comput-
ing & Task scheduling-1; Session 2 – Edge computing & Task scheduling-2; Session 
3 – Deep Learning and applications-1; Session 4 – Deep Learning and applications-2; 
Session 5 – Blockchain applications; Session 6 – Security and Privacy Protection; Ses-
sion 7 – Representation learning & Collaborative working-1; Session 8 – Representation 
learning & Collaborative working-2; Session 9 – Graph neural networks & Recommen-
dation systems-1; Session 10 – Graph neural networks & Recommendation systems-2; 
Session 11 – Federated learning and applications-1; Session 12 – Federated learning 
and applications-2. Apart from high-quality technical paper presentations, the techni-
cal program also featured two keynote speeches that were delivered by Kaizhu Huang 
from Duke Kunshan University and Xiaofeng Tao from Beijing University of Posts and 
Telecommunications. 
Coordination with the steering chair, Xinheng Wang, and steering members Song 
Guo, Bo Li, Xiaofei Liao, Honghao Gao, and Ning Gu were essential for the success 
of the conference. We sincerely appreciate their constant support and guidance. It was 
also a great pleasure to work with such an excellent organizing committee team for 
their hard work in organizing and supporting the conference. In particular, the Technical 
Program Committee, led by our General Chair Honghao Gao and General Co-Chairs 
Xinheng Wang, Shuiguang Deng, and Iqbal Muddesar, Program Chair Kuang Li, and 
TPC Co-Chairs Liang Chen and Tasos Dagiuklas and who have completed the peer-
review process of technical papers and made a high-quality technical program. We are 
also grateful to the Conference Manager, Natasha Onofrei, for her support and to all the 
authors who submitted their papers to the CollaborateCom 2024 conference. 
We strongly believe that CollaborateCom provides a good forum for all researchers, 
developers, and practitioners to discuss all science and technology aspects that are rele-
vant to collaborative computing. We also expect that future CollaborateCom conferences 
will be as successful and stimulating as indicated by the contributions presented in this 
volume. 
Honghao Gao 
Xinheng Wang

## Página PDF 13

Time-Aware Recommendations with 
Motif-Enhanced Graph Learning 
Guiyue Xu1,2, Guiling Wang1,2(B), Jiayu Zhang1,2, Hongying Qian1,2, 
and Jian Yu3 
1 Beijing Key Laboratory on Integration and Analysis of Large-scale Stream Data, 
Beijing, China 
wangguiling@ncut.edu.cn 
2 School of Information Science and Technology, North China University of 
Technology, Beijing, China 
3 Department of Computer Science, Auckland University of Technology, 
Auckland, New Zealand 
jian.yu@aut.ac.nz 
Abstract. The
time-aware
recommendation
model
captures
the 
dynamic evolution of user interests and item popularity by analyz-
ing users’ historical behaviors, resulting in more accurate recommenda-
tions. Recent research has increasingly shifted from traditional sequen-
tial models to Graph Neural Networks to more eﬀectively encode com-
plex dynamic collaborative information. Despite these advances, many 
approaches often overlook important higher-order relations at the motif 
level and the spatial dynamics within graphs. In this work, we propose 
a novel time-aware recommendation method named MoDynRec (Motif-
Enhanced Dynamic Graph Learning for Recommendations), which eﬀec-
tively models higher-order features and their spatiotemporal evolution by 
incorporating motifs into dynamic graph representation learning. The 
approach employs a two-fold strategy: (1) a motif-preserving structural 
encoder, which aggregates higher-order information from diverse motifs 
to retain critical structural patterns, and (2) an attention-based tem-
poral encoder, which integrates multi-head self-attention mechanisms to 
capture sequential evolution patterns in the temporal dimension. This is 
further enhanced by dynamic graph convolution networks, which explore 
spatial correlations among nodes, improving adaptability to evolving 
relationships over time. Extensive experiments on two publicly available 
datasets demonstrate the eﬀectiveness of the proposed model. 
Keywords: Dynamic recommendations · Network motif · Dynamic 
graph neural networks. 
1
Introduction 
In the era of digital collaboration, the rapid growth of online services makes it 
diﬃcult for users to ﬁnd services that align with their collaborative requirements.

## Página PDF 14

4
G. Xu et al.
Recommendation systems emerge as an eﬀective strategy for alleviating these 
challenges and enhancing collective productivity by providing users with accu-
rate recommendations. Unlike conventional recommendation models, which often 
employ techniques like collaborative ﬁltering, treating users’ historical behaviors 
as time-independent interactions, time-aware recommendation models dynami-
cally capture shifts in user behavior, leveraging temporal patterns of both users 
and items to deliver more personalized recommendations. 
Previous dynamic recommendation models have employed various techniques 
such as the Markov chain model [ 19], Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) [ 9,18], 
and Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) [ 24] to model the complex tran-
sition relationships among items within user interaction sequences. Recently, 
inspired by the formidable capabilities of Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) in 
learning graph-structured data, some works [ 11,34] have proposed leveraging 
GNNs to extract dynamic collaborative signals from the user-item interaction 
graph. Despite achieving remarkable performance, accurately modeling the char-
acteristics of users and items remains challenging in time-aware recommendation 
tasks, which is mainly manifested in two aspects: 
(1) In essence, GNNs-based models primarily focus on direct connections 
between nodes, often neglecting the valuable insights embedded in higher-
order structural patterns. For instance, users who interact with the same 
items are more likely to share similar interests. This critical sub-graph 
structure, or motif [ 1,16], captures implicit relations between users, thereby 
signiﬁcantly enhancing recommendation performance [ 27,33,35]. Despite 
some recognition that these complex relationships evolve over time [ 15], 
few research has explored how dynamic, higher-order structural informa-
tion impacts user and item behaviors, which remains a important gap in 
understanding their inﬂuence on recommendation outcomes. 
(2) Traditional approaches to modeling the dynamics of users and items often 
focus exclusively on the temporal dimension, tracking how their states evolve 
over time [ 10,30]. However, this overlooks the fact that as time progresses, 
the context these nodes also change, aﬀecting the relationships between 
them. As a result, the dependencies between nodes continuously shift in 
response to factors such as current states and past interactions. It is essen-
tial to capture these evolving relationships to more accurately model the 
dynamic interactions between users and items. 
To tackle these challenges, we present MoDynRec (Motif-Enhanced Dynamic 
Graph Learning for Recommendations), a time-aware recommendation model 
that leverages Dynamic Graph Neural Networks (DGNNs) to capture spatiotem-
poral evolution patterns while incorporating motifs to explore higher-order topo-
logical structures. In particular, we construct a discrete-time dynamic graph 
based on interactions between users and items, and propose a motif-preserving 
structural encoder. By aggregating motif-speciﬁc information, our approach facil-
itates the learning of higher-order latent features for users and items at each time 
step. Subsequently, we employ an attention-based temporal encoder to model the

[Vista original de la página 14](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-14.png)

![Página PDF 14, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-14.png)


<!-- PDF page 15 -->

## Página PDF 15

Time-Aware Recommendations
5
dynamic features of users and items along both the temporal and spatial dimen-
sions. It consists of multi-head self-attention mechanisms to capture temporal 
evolution patterns and dynamic graph convolution networks to adaptively adjust 
the strength of spatial relationships between nodes, thereby generating expres-
sive dynamic representations of nodes. After optimizing the interaction features 
between users and items using Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP), we can obtain 
the ﬁnal recommendation results. To validate the eﬀectiveness of our proposed 
model in dynamic recommendation tasks, we conduct extensive experiments on 
two publicly available datasets. The results demonstrate that our model sur-
passes state-of-the-art baselines across multiple evaluation metrics. 
The key contributions of this paper are summarized as follows: 
– We propose an innovative dynamic graph encoder that integrates motifs for 
encoding the evolution of higher-order structural information in dynamic rec-
ommendation scenarios. To the best of our knowledge, this is the ﬁrst incor-
poration of motifs in dynamic graph-enhanced recommendation models. 
– We emphasize the critical role of modeling the dynamics of users and items 
along both the temporal and spatial dimensions through our development of 
an attention-based temporal encoder, which eﬀectively captures the dynamic 
relationships between users and items. 
– We conduct extensive experiments on two representative datasets, and the 
results demonstrate the signiﬁcant superiority of our proposed MoDynRec. 
The remainder of this paper is organized as follows: Section 2 introduces the 
related work. Section 3 deﬁnes the time-aware recommendation problem and 
associated terminologies. Section 4 provides a detailed description of MoDynRec. 
Section 5 presents experimental results and analysis, and Section 6 concludes the 
paper. 
2
Related Work 
Dynamic Graph Neural Networks. DGNNs [ 23] are neural network archi-
tectures designed to encode dynamic graphs with topological structures that 
evolve over time. Recently, the study of DGNNs has garnered signiﬁcant atten-
tion. DySAT [ 21], for instance, utilized both structural and temporal attention 
mechanisms to learn dynamic features of nodes over an ordered sequence of graph 
snapshots. GCRN-M2 [ 22] enhanced the convLSTM framework by substituting 
traditional convolution operations with graph convolution, while EvolveGCN [ 17] 
integrated RNNs with Graph Convolutional Network (GCN), enabling a more 
comprehensive modeling of evolving graph data. TGAT [ 31] proposed attention-
based temporal aggregators to handle more ﬁne-grained interactions. DyRep 
[ 25] employed deep temporal point process models to characterize two distinct 
temporal evolution processes. 
While existing methods have made signiﬁcant strides in dynamic graph repre-
sentation learning, they overlook the evolving inﬂuence strengths among neigh-
boring nodes. To eﬀectively address this when modeling temporal dynamics,

[Vista original de la página 15](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-15.png)

## Página PDF 16

6
G. Xu et al.
we integrate dynamic graph convolution networks with attention mechanisms 
into the temporal encoder (Section 4.2), enabling dynamic modeling of spatial 
inﬂuences and improving the learning of dynamic features. 
Dynamic Recommendations. In real-world scenarios, achieving more accu-
rate recommendations necessitates modeling the dynamic characteristics of both 
users and items. Early approaches involve utilizing collaborative ﬁltering or 
Markov chains to capture dynamic preferences for users [ 19]. Due to the limita-
tions of Markov chains in handling non-linear dependencies, researchers explore 
RNNs [ 9] and CNNs [ 24], which excel in sequence modeling. However, these 
models often encounter performance degradation when dealing with long inter-
action sequences. Inspired by the remarkable performance of Transformer [ 26] 
in natural language processing tasks, many works [ 13,14] have applied them to 
recommendation tasks, thereby improving recommendation performance. Trans-
former eﬀectively captures global dependencies through self-attention mecha-
nisms, whereas GNNs have demonstrated a comparative advantage in model-
ing collaborative relationships between diﬀerent sequences and local relation-
ships among individual elements [ 20]. For example, DGSR [ 34], DRL-SRe [ 2], 
and PTGCN [ 11] designed dynamic graph recommendation modules to extract 
dynamic features of nodes from the user-item interaction graph. HyperRec 
[ 29] explored high-order relationships between nodes by constructing sequential 
hypergraphs. 
However, these approaches typically either model only the lower-order fea-
tures of individual nodes or edges, or they neglect the dynamics related to items. 
Inspired by the eﬀectiveness of motifs in capturing crucial structural patterns 
and higher-order relations within networks, we propose a motif-preserving struc-
tural encoder that encodes higher-order connections between users and items in 
the dynamic interaction graph through motifs (Section 4.1). Simultaneously, our 
temporal encoder (Section 4.2) eﬀectively captures their temporal dynamics, 
generating dynamic features for users and items. 
Network Motif. Network motifs, which are recurring and signiﬁcant inter-
connection patterns within complex networks, occur at much higher frequencies 
in real networks compared to random networks [ 1,16]. Currently, existing stud-
ies have recognized the importance of motifs in capturing crucial higher-order 
structural patterns. For instance, Milo et al. [ 16] demonstrated that motifs such 
as feed-forward loops play a pivotal role in information processing compared to 
basic pairwise relationships within neurons and gene regulation networks. MTSN 
[ 15] and NeurTWs [ 12] introduced a novel method to model dynamic graph 
motifs. MGSR [ 28] proposed a motif adjacency matrix generation algorithm, uti-
lizing motif-based Graph Attention Network (GAT) to aggregate higher-order 
information. HMGSR [ 35] enhanced MGSR by introducing a hierarchical motif-
based attention mechanism. MoSeR [ 4] extracted various motifs that represent 
sequential transition relationships to improve sequential recommendation.

[Vista original de la página 16](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-16.png)

## Página PDF 17

Time-Aware Recommendations
7
Our method distinguishes itself from previous studies when leveraging motifs 
to explore higher-order relations. It considers the inﬂuence of motifs at diﬀerent 
time steps and employs two distinct attention mechanisms within the motif-
preserving structural encoder (Section 4.1) to aggregate information at various 
levels, thereby generating higher-order representations for the current time step. 
3
Deﬁnitions and Problem Statement 
To more eﬀectively model the dynamic behavior of users and items while cap-
turing richer structural and temporal information, we assign each interaction 
to a speciﬁc time step based on its occurrence, thereby constructing a dynamic 
interaction graph that reﬂects evolving relationships. 
Deﬁnition 1. (Dynamic User-Item Interaction Graph). A dynamic user-item 
interaction graph, denoted as G= \left { {G}^{1},{G}^{2},...,{G}^{ n}\right }G =
G1, G2, ..., Gn , is formed by a series of 
interaction graphs from diﬀerent time steps. {G}^{s} = \left ( {U}^{s},{V}^{s},{E}^{s}\right )Gs = (U s, V s, Es) is a directed 
bipartite graph, where U^s \subseteq U
s ⊆U and V^s \subseteq V
s ⊆V jointly constitute its node set, and 
E^sEs represents its edge set. If there is an interaction between user uu and item {v}v
during the ss-th time step, it implies the existence of an edge e \in {E}^{s}e ∈Es originating 
from uu and terminating at vv. Here, UU and VV respectively denote the sets of users 
and items, and nn represents the number of time steps. 
Because directly modeling higher-order information within interaction graphs 
is challenging, we utilize motifs to capture these intricate relationships. As elabo-
rated in Section 2, motifs are essential for understanding higher-order proximity. 
Below, we introduce two key concepts related to motifs. 
Deﬁnition 2. (Motif Adjacency Matrix). Given a user-item interaction graph 
G^sGs at a speciﬁc time step and a certain motif M^kM k, if two nodes n_ini and n_jnj
in G^sGs co-occur in mm instances of M^kM k, the entry at position (i, j)(i, j) in the motif 
adjacency matrix \smash{\boldsymbol{A}^{M^{k}}_s}AM k
s
is assigned the value mm. Here, motif instances of M^kM k
refer to subgraphs of G^sGs that are isomorphic to M^kM k. 
Deﬁnition 3. (Motif-based Neighborhood). In the motif adjacency matrix \smash{\boldsymbol{A}^{M^{k}}_s}AM k
s
, 
if the element at position (i,j)(i, j) is non-zero, nodes n_ini and n_jnj are considered recip-
rocal neighbors based on motif M^kM k. The set of all motif M^kM k-based neighbors of 
node n_ini is denoted by \smash{\mathcal{N}_{s,i}^{M^{k}}}N M k
s,i . 
To clarify, this study considers only motif-deﬁned neighbors to explore higher-
order information, rather than including all possible neighbors. 
Diﬀerent motifs inherently possess unique graph properties. For instance, 
while three-node motifs play a critical role in social networks by capturing local 
community structures, their importance in interaction graphs often diminishes 
due to the distinct relational characteristics inherent in these contexts. Figure 
1 (c) illustrates the seven motifs utilized in this study, which encompass all 
motif types with up to four nodes in directed bipartite graphs. Here, circles 
represent users, squares represent items, and arrows pointing from users to items

indicate the interaction relationships between them. These motifs have been 
proven eﬀective in capturing critical structural information within interaction 
graphs [ 28,35]. Notably, motifs with more than four nodes would signiﬁcantly 
increase computational complexity while oﬀering limited performance beneﬁts. 
Therefore, we have restricted our selection to motifs containing a maximum of 
four nodes. 
Then, we formally deﬁne the problem of time-aware recommendations: 
Problem Statement. For a target user uu, given a dynamic user-item interac-
tion graph G= \left { {G}^{1},{G}^{2},...,{G}^{ n}\right }G =
G1, G2, ..., Gn , our objective is to recommend KK items to user 
uu that are most likely to be of interest at the (n+1)(n + 1)-th time step. 
4
Methodology 
In this section, we provide a detailed analysis of our proposed MoDynRec, includ-
ing its main modules and optimization processes. Figure 1 (a) depicts the overall 
architecture of our model, which comprises three key components. In particular, 
the motif-preserving structural encoder characterizes the higher-order features 
of nodes at each time step, while the attention-based temporal encoder is utilized 
to model the dynamics of nodes across both temporal and spatial dimensions. 
Each of these encoders comprises a stack of identical layers. Furthermore, we 
utilize a MLP to learn interaction features between users and items, yielding the 
recommendation results.

4.1
Motif-Preserving Structural Encoder Layer 
In this layer, we focus on encoding higher-order information from diﬀerent motifs 
at each time step. Considering that various motifs and their neighborhoods oﬀer 
unique insights and inﬂuences on the target node, we employ distinct attention 
mechanisms at both the node and motif levels to eﬀectively capture these unique 
contributions and selectively aggregate information from motif-based neighbors 
and structures. 
Node-Level Attention. The diverse positional structures and relational char-
acteristics of higher-order neighbors relative to the target node contribute varied 
impacts on the target node. To eﬀectively quantify these eﬀects, we utilize self-
attention mechanisms to evaluate their respective signiﬁcance. 
In particular, for a user-item interaction graph {G}^{s}Gs at a speciﬁc time step, we 
compute attention weights for all neighboring nodes relative to the target node 
uu within motif M^kM k, as illustrated in (1). The calculated attention weight \smash{{\alpha}^{s,M^{k}}_{uv}}αs,M k
uv
represents the importance of node vv’s features to node uu.

embedding dimension. (2) Linear transformation: To reduce dimensionality, a 
trainable weight matrix \smash{{\boldsymbol{W}^{M^{k}}}\in {\mathbb{R}}^{{F}^{\prime}\times F}}W M k ∈RF ×F is applied to project the node features 
into a lower-dimensional space, where F^{\prime}F represents the transformed dimension. 
(3) Attention score calculation: The attention mechanism is then parameter-
ized by a learnable weight vector \smash{{\boldsymbol{a}}^{{M}^{k}} \in {\mathbb{R}}^{2F^{'}}}aM k ∈R2F . This step employs a LeakyReLU 
activation function to reﬁne the relationships between nodes. The transposition 
operation {\cdot}^{T}·T and concatenation \parallel
are used, and the exponential function \text{exp}\left ( \cdot \right )exp (·)
is introduced to scale the attention scores. (4) Normalization: After comput-
ing the raw attention scores, they are normalized to produce the ﬁnal attention 
weights, ensuring the learned relationships are balanced and meaningful. 
By leveraging the learned weights, we adaptively aggregate information from 
neighboring nodes to generate more discriminative node embeddings:

varied structural information for the same node, and given that these variations

model this scenario. To capture the nuanced relationship between motifs and the 
ﬁnal node representation, we employ an attention mechanism with a two-layer 
feed-forward neural network (FNN). This approach assigns adaptive weights to 
the motifs’ structural features (Equation (3)) and fuses them to generate more

on the overall model. The detailed calculation is as follows:

4.2
Attention-Based Temporal Encoder Layer 
To eﬀectively capture the temporal evolution of higher-order relations between 
nodes and model the inﬂuence of historical behaviors shaped by temporal 
updates at each time step, we incorporate two distinct temporal modules in this 
layer. As shown in Fig. 1 (b), our approach employs multi-head self-attention 
to capture dynamic evolution along the temporal dimension and dynamic graph 
convolution networks to model spatially relevant dynamics. In order to ensure 
stable training, we further introduce residual connections, layer normalization, 
and dropout regularization after each component. 
Multi-head Self-attention. The multi-head self-attention mechanism exhibits 
notable capability in capturing complex dependencies within a sequence, making

Notably, when applying multi-head attention to all nodes, we account for 
the fact that not every node is active at each time step. For time steps where 
a node lacks interaction, we carry forward its representation from the previous 
time step. This method ensures that the model eﬀectively captures the temporal 
dynamics of nodes, even in the absence of interactions.

cal associations from node interactions and dynamically reﬁne the current state 
representation, we integrate dynamic graph convolution networks enhanced by 
self-attention mechanisms. This approach oﬀers a more adaptive and context-
sensitive modeling of node behaviors, accounting for temporal changes and reﬁn-
ing the node’s evolving state. The process for updating the current state at the 
ss-th time step, inﬂuenced by prior interactions, is formally deﬁned as shown in 
(7).

4.3
Recommendation 
In this work, to leverage the full temporal context, we use the representations 
from the last time step for the recommendation process, as they encapsulate 
all preceding temporal information. Speciﬁcally, for the target user uu and a 
particular item vv, we concatenate their representations and employ a two-layer 
MLP to learn their interaction features. The resulting interaction probability, 
which represents the likelihood of user uu interacting with item vv in the next 
time step, is formulated as follows: 
\bALT{} \hat{y}\left (u,v \right )= \sigma \left ( \text{MLP}\left ( {\boldsymbol{S}}''^{n}_{u}\parallel {\boldsymbol{S}}''^{n}_{v};{\Theta }^{MLP}\right )\right )\label{mlprecommendation}\eALT{} ˆy (u, v) = σ
MLP S

S
n
v ; ΘMLP
(11) 
where \sigma \left ( \cdot \right )σ (·) represents a sigmoid function, restricting values to the range of (0, 1)(0, 1). 
{\Theta }^{MLP}ΘMLP encompasses all parameters involved in the MLP, including two weight 
matrices and biases. The recommendation results are generated by ranking all 
items in descending order based on their interaction probabilities with user uu.

4.4
Optimization 
The objective of our optimization process is to maximize the interaction prob-
ability between the target user uu and its positive item vv by optimizing the 
model parameters. To achieve this, we introduce Binary Cross-Entropy Loss 
(BCE Loss) as the main recommendation loss, deﬁned as follows: 
\bALT{} {\mathcal{L}}_{c} = - \frac{1}{N}\displaystyle\sum_{}\left ( y\left ( u,v\right )\log{\hat{y}\left ( u,v\right )}+ \left ( 1- y\left ( u,v\right )\right )\log{\left ( 1-\hat{y}\left ( u,v\right )\right )}\right )\label{recommendationloss}\eALT{} Lc = −1
N
(y (u, v) log ˆy (u, v) + (1 −y (u, v)) log (1 −ˆy (u, v)))
(12) 
where NN represents the number of training instances, y\left ( u,v\right )y (u, v) is the actual inter-
action probability between user uu and item vv, and \hat{y}\left ( u,v\right )ˆy (u, v) is the corresponding 
predicted probability. 
While the recommendation loss can enhance the model’s ability to model user 
interaction behaviors, it may not fully capture the intrinsic structure information 
among nodes. Recognizing that nodes within the same community and those 
with similar structures tend to exhibit similar features at the same time step [ 3], 
we employ a simple contrastive learning method inspired by [ 6]. This method 
encourages neighboring nodes that appear in the same ﬁxed-length random walk 
sequence to have similar representations while distinguishing more distant nodes, 
thus preserving richer contextual information within the graph. Following [ 21], 
we utilize BCE Loss to achieve this, formally deﬁned as follows: 
\bALT{} {\mathcal{L}}_{p}= \displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{n}\displaystyle\sum_{i \in {U}^{s}\cup{V}^{s}}\left ( \displaystyle\sum_{{i}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{walk(i)}^{s}}- \log{\sigma \left ( <{\boldsymbol{S}}''^{s}_{i},{\boldsymbol{S}}''^{s}_{{i}^{\prime}}> \right )} \right. \\ \left. \small - \displaystyle\sum_{{i}''\in{\mathcal{N}}_{neg(i)}^{s}}\log{\left ( 1-\sigma \left (< {\boldsymbol{S}}''^{s}_{i},{\boldsymbol{S}}''^{s}_{{i}''}> \right )\right )}\right )\label{auxilossfunction}\eALT{} Lp =

where <\cdot>< · > denotes the inner product operation, {{\mathcal{N}}_{walk(i)}^{s}}N s
walk(i) is a set of nodes 
co-occurring with node ii in a ﬁxed-length random walk sequence in {G^s}Gs, and 
{\mathcal{N}}_{neg(i)}^{s}N s
neg(i) is a set of nodes obtained through a negative sampling strategy. 
To balance user interaction modeling with the preservation of intrinsic struc-
tural information, we implement a joint optimization strategy, combining the 
two losses through a weighted summation, as depicted in (14), 
\bALT{} \mathcal{L}= {\mathcal{L}}_{c}+ \gamma {\mathcal{L}}_{p}\label{combineloss}\eALT{} L = Lc + γLp
(14) 
where \gammaγ is a hyper-parameter utilized to regulate the impact of diﬀerent losses.

5
Experiments 
5.1
Datasets 
To evaluate the eﬀectiveness of the proposed model, extensive experiments are 
conducted on two representative and publicly available datasets, Yelp 1 and 
MovieLens [ 7], which include detailed interaction timestamps. 
Yelp is a large-scale dataset comprising user ratings for businesses. We system-
atically collect all interactions over the period of January to December 2018 for 
our experiments. 
MovieLens is a dataset that includes movie ratings and user details. Speciﬁ-
cally, our experiments utilize the ML-1M version, which consists of one million 
movie ratings spanning from April 2000 to February 2003. 
For all datasets, in accordance with [ 2,13], we retain positive interactions 
with ratings greater than 3 and ﬁlter out users and items with fewer than 5 
interactions. Given the varying time spans and sparsity patterns in Yelp and 
MovieLens, we partition the interaction timelines of each dataset independently 
to derive the corresponding time steps. Table 1 summarizes the fundamental 
statistical for both datasets. 
In our experiments, the proposed model is trained and evaluated indepen-
dently for each input set G= \left { {G}^{1},{G}^{2},...,{G}^{ s}\right }G =
G1, G2, ..., Gs , where s\in\left { 1,2,...,n- 1\right }s ∈{1, 2, ..., n −1}. We 
randomly sample 60%% of interactions at the (s+1)(s + 1)-th time step for training, 
20%% for testing, and use the remaining 20%% for validation to ﬁne-tune hyperpa-
rameters. The evaluation results from the nn-th time step are then utilized for 
comparative analysis with other models. 
5.2
Evaluation Metrics 
We choose three widely used metrics in recommendation tasks [ 11,18,32], Hit 
Ratio@KK (HR@KK), Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain@KK (NDCG@KK), 
and Precision@KK(P@KK), to evaluate the performance of our model and base-
lines. Here, we set KK to 5, 10, and 20. 
5.3
Baselines 
We select several representative recommendation models, including those that 
employ various approaches to model temporal dynamics, utilize motifs to capture 
higher-order relations, and use dynamic graph motifs to extract evolving higher-
order structural information, and compare them against our MoDynRec. 
– GRU4Rec+ [ 9] proposes an enhanced Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU) for 
session-based recommendation tasks.
1 https://www.yelp.com/dataset.

Time-Aware Recommendations
15
– PTGCN [ 11] introduces a novel aggregator that captures sequential inter-
action patterns and high-order relationships between nodes. 
– DySAT [ 21] employs both structural and temporal attention mechanisms to 
learn dynamic representations of nodes. 
– LightGCN [ 8] is a streamlined version of GCN, omitting the feature trans-
formations and non-linear activations present in traditional GCN. 
– HMGSR [ 35] presents a hierarchical attention mechanism to learn higher-
order features on motif adjacency matrices. 
– MTSN [ 15] combines a motif-preserving encoder and temporal shift opera-
tions within a single layer to model higher-order pattern evolution. 
– NeurTWs [ 12] proposes a novel sampling strategy for extracting represen-
tative dynamic graph motifs, along with a corresponding encoding strategy 
for these motifs.

5.4
Parameter Settings 
We conduct our experiments on an Ubuntu server equipped with an Intel(R) 
Xeon(R) Silver 4210 CPU, an NVIDIA A100 SXM4 GPU (40GB), and 251GB of 
memory. The model is implemented in PyTorch 1.13, compiled within a Python 
3.9 environment. 
To achieve optimal performance, we ﬁne-tune all hyperparameters and report 
the key settings. The experiment employs the AdamW optimizer with mini-
batch gradient descent for training, setting the learning rate to 0.005 and the

batch size to 512. For both the motif-preserving structural encoder layer and the 
attention-based temporal encoder layer, we set the number of layers to 1, the 
number of attention heads (K^{\prime}K and {K}''K ) to 16, the output dimensions (DD and 
D^{\prime}D ) to 128, and the dropout rate to 0.5. The weight of auxiliary loss (\gammaγ) is set 
to 0.1. Additionally, in (13), the length and count of random walks are speciﬁed 
as 20 and 10, respectively. 
For all baselines, we utilize the source code provided by the authors and 
perform hyperparameter tuning. Then, we compare these optimized results with 
those obtained from our proposed model. 
5.5
Performance Comparison 
Table 2 summarizes the experimental results of all models across the two 
datasets. The optimal performance for each evaluation metric is highlighted in 
bold, while sub-optimal results are indicated with an underscore. 
In all experiments, we observe that the RNNs-based model GRU4Rec+ con-
sistently demonstrates inferior performance. This can be attributed to the inher-
ent limitations of RNNs, which focus solely on transition patterns within individ-
ual interaction sequences, neglecting the relationships between these sequences. 
The notable models like PTGCN and DySAT leverage DGNNs to explicitly 
encode dynamic collaborative information and high-order relationships between 
entities, leading to signiﬁcantly improved performance. In contrast, LightGCN 
and HMGSR, which rely on GNNs to learn static user and item representations, 
struggle with dynamic recommendation tasks due to their lack of temporal mod-
eling. While HMGSR shows better results by incorporating motifs, it still falls 
behind sequential models, particularly in dynamic settings. 
MTSN and NeurTWs enhance dynamic graph representation learning by 
encoding dynamic graph motifs, which allows them to capture the evolving 
higher-order structural information in dynamic graphs. However, MTSN over-
looks the roles of diﬀerent higher-order patterns, which may introduce addi-
tional noise when capturing temporal transition relations, thus limiting its per-
formance. NeurTWs introduces a novel approach to extract and encode repre-
sentative dynamic graph motifs, enabling it to learn dynamic representations 
more eﬀectively and achieve superior results. It is noteworthy that our pro-
posed MoDynRec not only models the higher-order features of users and items 
in dynamic scenarios but also captures this evolving patterns from a spatiotem-
poral perspective. The achieved optimal performance across all datasets serves 
as a robust demonstration of the eﬃcacy of our model. 
Furthermore, we observe substantial variations in the performance of these 
models across diﬀerent datasets, with signiﬁcantly better results on MovieLens 
than on Yelp. Through a thorough analysis of the experimental data, we attribute 
this diﬀerence primarily to the higher data density in MovieLens, where the 
average number of user interactions is considerably greater, enabling nodes to 
aggregate more information. Moreover, the MovieLens dataset exhibits stronger 
temporal characteristics compared to Yelp, providing an additional explanation 
for the eﬀectiveness of the results.

5.6
Ablation Study 
To evaluate the contribution of crucial components in MoDynRec to the over-
all performance, we conduct ablation experiments on Yelp and MovieLens. As 
shown in Table 3, we evaluate diﬀerent model variants: (1) “w/o motif” cap-
tures only lower-order relationships among nodes without incorporating motifs. 
(2) “w/o DGCN” removes the spatial self-attention from the dynamic graph con-
volution networks. (3) “w/o motif and DGCN” omits both motifs and spatial 
self-attention simultaneously. (4) “w/ FNN” replaces the dynamic graph con-
volution networks with a two-layer FNN for updating node representations. (5) 
“w/ RNNs” employs RNNs, speciﬁcally GRU in this experiment, instead of the 
attention-based temporal encoder to capture temporal information. 
From the results presented in Table 3, we observe that the “w/o motif” 
variant experiences a slight performance drop compared to the full model. This 
can be attributed to its inability to capture the higher-order patterns encoded 
in motifs, which limits its ability to learn more sophisticated representations. 
Similarly, the “w/o DGCN” variant shows a signiﬁcant decline in performance, 
highlighting the importance of modeling the varying impact of spatial relations 
between nodes in MoDynRec. These observations are further supported by the 
“w/o motif and DGCN” variant, where the combined omission of both compo-
nents compounds the performance drop. Moreover, the performance diﬀerences 
between the “w/ FNN” and “w/o DGCN” variants illustrate the beneﬁts of 
incorporating GCN after multi-head self-attention, enabling the model to cap-
ture richer information compared to using FNN. It becomes evident that “w/ 
RNNs” consistently performs the least among all variants. This is primarily due 
to the inherent limitations of RNNs, such as diﬃculties in capturing complex 
transitional relationships within sequences and their susceptibility to the vanish-
ing gradient problem. In contrast, our model and other variants beneﬁt from the 
self-attention mechanism, which better captures temporal dynamics. Notably, 
our model consistently outperforms others, highlighting the crucial role played 
by these components. 
5.7
Sensitivity of Parameters 
In this section, we investigate the impact of four primary hyperparameters on 
model performance. Fig. 2 illustrates the results of HR@20 and NDCG@20 on 
Yelp under various conﬁgurations.

We ﬁrst experiment with varying the number of attention heads, stacked 
layers, and hidden dimensions in both the motif-preserving structural encoder 
and the attention-based temporal encoder. As shown in Fig. 2 (a), increasing 
the number of attention heads leads to a modest improvement in model perfor-
mance, suggesting that the model beneﬁts from capturing more comprehensive 
structural information across multiple subspaces. Similarly, Fig. 2 (c) demon-
strates that larger hidden dimensions contribute to better performance by pre-
serving more diverse features. However, as illustrated in Fig. 2 (b), while per-
formance initially remains stable with an increase in the number of layers, it 
sharply declines when the layers increase from 2 to 4, indicating potential issues 
such as over-smoothing. Notably, our model performs better with a single layer 
compared to models that stack multiple layers to capture high-order relation-
ships. Furthermore, in Fig. 2 (d), we explore the impact of varying the weights 
of the auxiliary loss and observe that appropriately emphasizing our proposed 
auxiliary task enhances model performance.

5.8
Time Cost 
In this section, we compare the runtime cost of our model against several strong 
baselines. Table 4 presents the runtimes, measured in seconds per epoch, for each 
model under consistent experimental settings. 
In all experiments, HMGSR consistently achieves the fastest runtimes, even 
on larger datasets, with execution times remaining within a few seconds. How-
ever, this eﬃciency comes at the cost of performance, as its simple, time-
independent structure results in signiﬁcantly poorer outcomes compared to 
dynamic recommendation models. In contrast, DySAT eﬀectively learns from 
discrete-time graphs by utilizing structural and temporal self-attention mecha-
nisms that enable parallel processing across various subspaces and time steps, 
leading to enhanced runtime eﬃciency. PTGCN and NeurTWs, while achieving 
strong performance, require extensive computations and frequent updates when 
working with continuous-time graphs, making them considerably less eﬃcient, 
especially with large-scale datasets, where runtimes increase substantially. Our 
model, though incorporating motifs that slightly increase its computational cost 
compared to DySAT, consistently outperforms both PTGCN and NeurTWs in 
recommendation tasks. It strikes a superior balance between accuracy and run-
time eﬃciency, allowing it to achieve high performance without a signiﬁcant 
sacriﬁce in computational speed. 
5.9
Impact of Motifs 
In this section, to understand the role of motifs in MoDynRec, we investigate 
two key aspects: the density of higher-order relations captured by motifs and the 
attention weights assigned to these motifs. This analysis is illustrated using the 
Yelp dataset and presented in Fig. 3. 
In Fig. 3 (a), we assess the density of higher-order relationships in the graph 
by calculating the ratio of higher-order neighbors captured by motifs to their the-
oretical maximum. The height of each bar reﬂects the density level. Due to motifs 
can capture complex structural patterns and retain more information beyond

[Vista original de la página 29](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-29.png)

## Página PDF 30

20
G. Xu et al.
direct relationships, we observe that the higher-order relationships captured by 
motifs {M}^2M 2 - {M}^7M 7 generally exhibit greater density compared to direct relation-
ships (retained by {M}^1M 1). Meanwhile, homophily theory suggests that nodes with 
common interactions tend to form stronger connections and exhibit more similar 
characteristics. This implies that motifs not only capture diverse higher-order 
relationships but also that these higher-order connections may hold more signif-
icance than isolated direct interactions. 
Moreover, we visualize the attention weights assigned to motifs by MoDynRec 
in the experiments, as shown in Fig. 3 (b). The intensity of color represents the 
magnitude of attention scores. Throughout the majority of time steps, {M}^5M 5, {M}^6M 6
and {M}^7M 7 consistently exhibit signiﬁcant attention weights. This aligns with our 
hypothesis that users interacting with the same items tend to develop similar 
preferences over time, and likewise, items interacted with by the same users often 
share similar attributes. It’s noteworthy that while some motifs are subgraphs of 
others, more intricate motifs can capture unique structural information, thereby 
providing complementary insights to those oﬀered by simpler motifs. 
Fig. 3. Density of higher-order relations and attention weights for motifs at diﬀerent 
time steps. 
6
Conclusion 
This paper proposes MoDynRec, a time-aware recommendation model built on 
DGNNs. By incorporating motifs, MoDynRec enhances the structural encoder to 
capture complex higher-order dependencies, while its temporal encoder innova-
tively integrates multi-head self-attention and dynamic graph convolution net-
works to jointly model the evolving patterns of these structures across both 
temporal and spatial dimensions. Based on this approach, our model is capa-
ble of generating more expressive representations. Experimental results on two

datasets demonstrate the signiﬁcant superiority of MoDynRec over other repre-
sentative baselines. In our future work, we will explore diverse node attributes 
and interaction relationships within the dynamic user-item interaction graph to 
enrich node representations.

## Página PDF 34

Spatial-Temporal Graph Attention 
Networks Based on Novel Adjacency 
Matrix for Weather Forecasting 
Ang Guo1 
, Yanghe Liu2 
, Shiyu Shao1 
, Shuze Jia1 
, Xiaowei Shi2 
, 
and Zhenni Feng1(B) 
1 School of Computer Science and Technology, Donghua University, Shanghai, China 
fzn@dhu.edu.cn 
2 Hubei Key Laboratory of Intelligent Yangtze and Hydroelectric Science, China 
Yangtze Power Co., Ltd., Yichang, Hubei, China 
Abstract. Weather forecasting is closely related to our daily life and has 
become one of the important application domains of machine learning. 
However, existing methods often suﬀer from certain problems. First, by 
only considering spatial proximity, the graph structure may limit the abil-
ity of model to fully capture spatial dependencies. Second, most methods 
typically model temporal and spatial correlations separately, which sub-
stantially limit the representational capacity of the models. Third, most 
methods lack the simultaneous capture of long-term and short-term tem-
poral dependencies, which constrains the model’s capability of capturing 
the dynamic temporal dependency. In this work, we propose a model 
based on spatial-temporal fusion graphs and self-attention mechanisms, 
which we refer to as STGATAM. Speciﬁcally, STGATAM ﬁrst con-
structs a novel static spatial adjacency matrix, then constructs a spatial-
temporal fusion graph through the method of time step concatenation, 
and also constructs the corresponding spatial-temporal fusion graph 
adjacency matrix. We then utilize a self-attention mechanism to capture 
temporal dependencies, and use a graph attention network to respec-
tively capture spatial and spatial-temporal dependencies, inputting the 
fused features into a decoder for prediction. We conduct multiple experi-
ments on a real-world dataset, and the experimental results demonstrate 
that the method we propose signiﬁcantly outperforms existing methods. 
Keywords: Spatial-temporal data mining · Deep learning · Time 
series prediction · Adjacency matrix · Attention mechanism 
1
Introduction 
Lives and well-beings depend on reliable weather predictions every day [ 1]. Tradi-
tional weather prediction models are based on solving physical equations numer-
ically, an approach known as numerical weather prediction(NWP). Generative 
c ICST Institute for Computer Sciences, Social Informatics and Telecommunications Engineering 2026 
Published by Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026. All Rights Reserved 
H. Gao and X. Wang (Eds.): CollaborateCom 2024, LNICST 626, pp. 24–41, 2026. 
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-93257-1_2

[Vista original de la página 34](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-34.png)

![Página PDF 34, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-34.png)


<!-- PDF page 35 -->

## Página PDF 35

STGATAM
25
artiﬁcial intelligence weather models work diﬀerently as they do not rely on 
an understanding of physics to make predictions. Instead, they learn statisti-
cal patterns from historical observational data to probabilistically forecast likely 
weather outcomes [ 7]. This data-driven approach shows considerable promise 
for weather forecasting. Unlike physics-based numerical models, generative arti-
ﬁcial intelligence models can capture complex nonlinear relationships in massive 
datasets without full comprehension of the underlying atmospheric dynamics. 
Recently, deep learning-based methods have been widely applied to weather 
forecasting. However, there are still several issues that have been overlooked in 
these existing methods. 
First, most existing methods only consider the local spatial correlation, while 
the monitoring stations in the weather network have both local and non-local 
correlations. Two monitoring stations that are far apart may still have a close 
correlation, and monitoring stations that are relatively close may exhibit com-
pletely diﬀerent features. If the network is modeled using only the given adja-
cency matrix, this characteristic will be overlooked. 
Secondly, most existing methods model time and space separately, captur-
ing temporal dependencies and spatial dependencies independently, and thus 
indirectly capturing spatial-temporal correlations, neglecting the complex rela-
tionship between the temporal and spatial dimensions. 
RNN-based models are commonly used to capture temporal dependencies, 
but they struggle to adapt quickly to sudden changes in features and exhibit 
delayed response characteristics. Besides, RNN-based methods suﬀer from issues 
such as gradient vanishing and gradient exploding when capturing long-term 
sequences [ 36]. 
GNN-based models have been widely used to capture spatial dependencies. 
However, standard GNNs have been proven to suﬀer from over-smoothing prob-
lems, where all node representations converge to the same value as the number 
of layers increases. These shortcomings severely limit the depth of GNNs and 
make it diﬃcult to obtain deeper and richer spatial features [ 8]. 
Furthermore, weather changes exhibit periodic patterns over long time peri-
ods, yet have signiﬁcant uncertainties in the short term. As such, capturing 
both long-term periodicity and short-term temporal dependencies is necessary 
to improve the predictive capabilities of weather models. However, most existing 
methods lack the capacity to capture long-term temporal dependencies. 
Based on the above analysis, weather forecasting is an extremely challenging 
task. To address the aforementioned problem, we propose our spatial-temporal 
graph attention networks based on novel adjacency matrix, namely STGATAM, 
for weather forecasting, which can eﬀectively capture the spatial-temporal cor-
relations between diﬀerent stations. The key contributions of this work can be 
summarized as follows: 
– We propose a novel method for constructing a static spatial adjacency matrix 
based on geographical distance and historical data relevance, which can more 
comprehensively reﬂect the spatial relevance between monitoring stations.

## Página PDF 36

26
A. Guo et al.
– We construct a spatial-temporal fusion graph to fully exploit the spatial-
temporal dependence in time series data, which can further explore the 
dynamic change patterns in the time dimension on the basis of retaining 
spatial relevance, thereby improving the predictive performance of the model. 
– We introduce graph attention network to address the issue of over-smoothing, 
which eﬀectively capture the short-term spatial-temporal dependence based 
on the spatial-temporal fused graph and the spatial dependence based on the 
static spatial adjacency matrix. 
– We conducted experiments on two real-world datasets. The experimental 
results showed that compared with the most advanced models, our model 
achieved signiﬁcant improvement. 
2
Related Work 
2.1
Weather Forecasting 
The analysis of atmospheric data, such as temperature, wind speed, and humid-
ity, is deﬁned as weather forecasting [ 9]. Weather forecasting has always been an 
important task due to the signiﬁcant temporal and spatial variations in weather 
conditions [ 3]. Traditional weather forecasting relies on Numerical Weather Pre-
diction (NWP), which employs complex equations to describe the atmospheric 
environment. NWP requires extensive data and computational resources, and its 
ability to predict rare events is limited [ 4]. Moreover, NWP has shown subop-
timal performance in predicting short-term weather conditions [ 32]. Nowadays, 
weather forecasting has evolved from a labor-intensive task to one heavily reliant 
on computational methods [ 14]. 
In recent years, data-driven models based on artiﬁcial intelligence have been 
widely applied in weather forecasting [ 26]. In the analysis of temperature time 
series, Artiﬁcial Neural Networks (ANN) and Support Vector Machines (SVM) 
are the most commonly used strategies [ 5, 6,23]. 
With the advancement of deep learning techniques, there has been signiﬁcant 
improvement in extracting features from time series data [ 25]. Recurrent Neural 
Networks (RNN) and Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN), along with their 
variants, have started to be applied in weather forecasting [ 15,17,35]. Shi et al. 
extended the fully connected Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) and combined it 
with CNN to propose a Convolutional LSTM model capable of capturing spatial-
temporal correlations for the problem of short-term precipitation forecasting 
[ 27]. Geng et al. utilized a convolutional LSTM and introduced a dual spatial-
temporal encoder to extract features based on both simulation and observation, 
enabling the prediction of lightning occurrences [ 11]. Wang et al. introduced 
a truncated Gaussian Density Network based on Convolutional Transformers, 
which achieved accurate wind speed forecasting and reliable probabilistic wind 
speed forecasting [ 34]. 
Despite the extensive research and notable achievements in weather forecast-
ing, there is still room for improvement in terms of accuracy.

[Vista original de la página 36](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-36.png)

<!-- PDF page 37 -->

## Página PDF 37

STGATAM
27
2.2
Attention Mechanism 
In recent years, attention mechanisms have been widely employed in various 
ﬁelds such as natural language processing, image captioning, and speech recog-
nition [ 13]. The Transformer model, which incorporates attention mechanisms, 
introduced the concept of multi-head attention [ 30]. Geng et al. propose a spatial-
temporal Gated Attention Transformer model based Graph Neural Network, 
which enhances the predictive capacity for long-term scenarios and the abil-
ity to handle unexpected traﬃc incidents, leading to more accurate traﬃc ﬂow 
forecasting [ 12]. 
The Graph Attention Network (GAT) employs attention mechanisms to 
assign diﬀerent weights to neighboring nodes, without requiring any expensive 
matrix operations or relying on a prior knowledge of the graph structure [ 31]. 
Zheng et al. proposed a graph multi-attention network (GMAN) from the per-
spective of spatial-temporal factors to predict traﬃc conditions at diﬀerent time 
intervals on a road network graph [ 38]. DMGAN leverages dynamic graph mod-
eling and multi-hop operation to capture time-varying relations across road sec-
tions and long-range spatial dependency. Additionally, a fusion-attention module 
is utilized to capture both long- and short-term temporal dependencies jointly 
[ 20]. 
2.3
Spatial-Temporal Dependency Capture 
Accurately capturing spatial-temporal dependencies can eﬀectively enhance the 
service quality of real-world applications such as weather forecasting and trans-
portation networks [ 28]. Traﬃc prediction is a typical application that relies 
on capturing spatial-temporal dependencies. A recent trend is to utilize GNN 
models for spatial-temporal forecasting [ 22]. To capture these dependencies 
more eﬀectively, Li et al. proposed the DCRNN model, which models traﬃc 
ﬂow as a diﬀusion process on a directed graph, using graph convolutional net-
works and recurrent neural networks (RNNs) to capture spatial and temporal 
dependencies, respectively [ 21]. Wang et al. proposed a novel spatial-temporal 
graph neural network with a positional attention mechanism, which combines 
recurrent neural networks and Transformer layers to capture local and global 
temporal dependencies [ 33]. Lan et al. introduced a data-driven strategy for 
dynamic spatial-temporal perception, which replaces the commonly used pre-
deﬁned static graph in traditional graph convolutional methods. They capture 
spatial-temporal dependencies using an improved graph neural network and 
gated convolutions [ 18]. Pu et al. designed a dual-graph spatial module that 
simultaneously captures relationships in static and dynamic graphs, and they 
employ gated convolutions to capture temporal dependencies [ 24]. 
Inspired by the aforementioned research, taking into account the graph struc-
ture of the monitoring stations in the weather network and the spatial-temporal 
patterns of weather feature data, we decided to employ a model based on spatial-
temporal fusion graphs and self-attention mechanisms for modeling weather fea-
tures. Through this approach, the model we proposed considers both long-term

and short-term temporal and spatial dependencies and achieves modeling of 
spatial-temporal correlations, resulting in excellent model performance. 
3
Problem Formulation 
In this study, we represent the spatial network of weather monitoring stations 
using a graph G = \left( V, E, A \right)G = (V, E, A), where VV is the set of monitoring stations, \lvert V\rvert = N|V | = N, 
NN is the number of monitoring stations, EE is the set of edges between stations, 
and A\in R^{N\times N} A ∈RN×N is the adjacency matrix of the graph GG. Each monitoring station 
has the same sampling frequency in the graph GG. Deﬁne X_t \in R^{N\times D}Xt ∈RN×D to be 
the observations of the N stations at time step tt, where DD is the number of 
features, and in our model, D is assigned the value of 1. Furthermore, we deﬁne 
X = X_1, X_2, X_3 \ldots , X_T \in R^{T \times N\times D}X = X1, X2, X3 . . . , XT ∈RT ×N×D to be the observations of all monitoring 
stations in the past TT time steps. Weather forecasting aims to predict future 
weather conditions through a series of historical observations detected at weather 
monitoring stations. In this paper, we predict the stations’ data for the future PP
time steps by using the observation data of the past TT time steps on the basis of 
GG. The problem is deﬁned as shown in Eq. (1), where \ThetaΘ is the model parameter. 
\bALT{}\label{eq1} X_{t+1}{,X}_{t+2},\ldots{,X}_{t+P} = F\left({X,G,\Theta}\right)\eALT{} Xt+1, Xt+2, . . . , Xt+P = F (X, G, Θ)
(1) 
4
Methodology 
In this section, we will provide a detailed exposition of the STGATAM model 
proposed in this study, with the overall architecture of the model depicted in 
Fig. 1. The model comprises four key components, namely, the construction of 
novel static spatial adjacency matrix, which will be expounded upon in Sect. 4.1. 
Subsequently, in Sect. 4.2, we will elucidate the approach employed in capturing 
temporal correlations within the model. Moving forward, Sect. 4.3 will introduce 
the construction of spatial-temporal fusion graphs. Finally, Sect. 4.4 will delve 
into the capture of spatial-temporal correlations. 
4.1
Construction of Static Spatial Adjacency Matrix 
The conventional approach to constructing static adjacency matrices for spa-
tial relationships typically relies on Euclidean distance. However, spatial cor-
relations often exhibit both local and non-local characteristics. For instance, 
two nodes that are far apart may not have a direct connection, yet they can 
still exhibit strong spatial correlations. Therefore, the conventional method that 
solely employs Euclidean distance to construct static adjacency matrices tends 
to overlook such correlations. In this paper, we propose a novel approach that 
combines Euclidean distance with the Maximum Information Coeﬃcient (MIC) 
to construct a more comprehensive static spatial adjacency matrix.

The Maximum Information Coeﬃcient is a method used to detect the cor-

follows:

In the proposed method of this paper, we utilize the complete historical 
data from the past two months for each monitoring station. MIC is sensitive to 
anomalous data. Therefore, we correct outliers or missing values by using the 
average of their preceding and succeeding observations. We calculate the MIC 
values between each pair of monitoring stations and select the top k values for 
each station. Subsequently, we set the corresponding adjacency matrix value A_{ij}Aij
to 1, as shown in Eq. (5).

By employing MIC to capture non-linear correlations, this approach eﬀec-
tively addresses the local limitations of Euclidean distance in capturing spatial 
relationships. It enables the capture of non-local spatial correlations, resulting 
in a more accurate construction of the static spatial adjacency matrix. Conse-
quently, the constructed matrix can more reliably reﬂect the spatial correlations 
between nodes. 
4.2
Temporal Self-attention Layer 
Time plays a signiﬁcant role in weather conditions, as the weather at a partic-
ular location is typically not independent but closely related to its past over 
a certain period, and this relationship often exhibits non-linear characteristics. 
Nevertheless, past weather conditions still can be considered as an important 
reference factor for current weather. Furthermore, temporal correlations are not 
limited to the past and present only. By analyzing past weather patterns and 
trends, future weather conditions can be predicted. 
While Transformer was initially developed for natural language processing, it 
has gradually been applied to handle time series data as research has progressed. 
The unique attention mechanism of Transformer allows for dynamic focus on 
diﬀerent positions within a sequence and captures long-term dependencies. In our 
STGATAM model, we employ a self-attention layer with multi-head attention 
mechanism to capture temporal dependencies. 
In processing time series data, position embedding plays a crucial role as 
it provides information about the relative distances and order between time 
elements. This enables the model to accurately model and predict time series 
data. In this paper, we divide time into three elements: month, day, and hour. 
We represent each of these elements using both their sine and cosine components 
as position embeddings. 
Let’s take the element ‘hour’ as an example. By using this type of position 
embedding, we ensure that 0 o’clock is closest in the embedding space to both 
23 o’clock and 1 o’clock. Similarly, 3 o’clock is closest to 2 o’clock and 4 o’clock 
in the embedding space. Through the joint representation of sine and cosine 
components, the model can better capture the periodicity and sequentiality of 
time. 
The self-attention mechanism is a core component of Transformer. The query, 
key, and value vectors are represented as shown in Eq. (6), where W_{qi}Wqi, W_{ki}Wki, 
and W_{vi}Wvi are learnable parameters. The calculation of attention scores is given 
by Eq. (7). 
\bALT{}\label{eq6} Q_{i} = XW_{qi},~~~{K}_{i} = XW_{ki},~~~{V}_{i} = XW_{vi}\eALT{} Qi = XWqi,
Ki = XWki,
Vi = XWvi
(6) 
\bALT{}\label{eq7} \alpha_{i} = softmax\left( \frac{Q_{i}K_{i}^{T}}{\sqrt{\sigma}} \right)V_{i}\eALT{} αi = softmax

Fig. 2. The temporal self-attention layer 
In addition, it is common to utilize multi-head attention mechanisms to cap-
ture diﬀerent aspects of correlations and features [ 10]. Similarly, we also employ 
a multi-head attention mechanism to capture temporal correlations, as shown in 
Eq. (8). 
\bALT{}\label{eq8} MHA\left({Q,K,V} \right) = Concate\left({h_{1},h_{2},{\ldots,h}_{n}} \right)W^{O},\eALT{} MHA (Q, K, V ) = Concate (h1, h2, . . . , hn) W O,
(8) 
where W^{O}W O represents learnable parameters. 
Each attention head operates on diﬀerent learned projections of the query 
(Q), key (K), and value (V) vectors, allowing the model to attend to diﬀerent 
aspects of temporal information. The ﬁnal output of the multi-head attention 
mechanism captures diverse temporal correlations and features. 
The structure of our temporal self-attention layer is illustrated in Fig. 2, com-
prising feed-forward layer and Add & Norm layers. Add represents the residual 
connection, used to prevent network degradation. Norm represents Layer Nor-
malization, which normalizes the activation values of each layer. 
4.3
Construction of Spatial-Temporal Fusion Graph 
In most previous studies, it is common to capture temporal and spatial corre-
lations separately and then integrate them. However, this approach is indirect 
and may not fully capture the spatial-temporal correlations of nodes.

[Vista original de la página 41](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-41.png)

![Página PDF 41, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-41.png)


<!-- PDF page 42 -->

## Página PDF 42

32
A. Guo et al.
Inspired by STFGNN [ 19], we have decided to adopt a diﬀerent approach. 
We concatenate the data of all nodes for kk time steps, forming a spatial-
temporal fusion graph of size k\times Nk × N. Due to the complete connectivity of 
the spatial-temporal fusion graph, by constructing an appropriate adjacency 
matrix  \in R^{kN \times kN}∈RkN×kN, we can directly capture the spatial-temporal correlations of 
nodes, instead of using separate modules to capture temporal and spatial corre-
lations. This method enables us to explore potential spatial-temporal patterns 
and dependencies in the data more comprehensively. 
Fig. 3. The adjacency matrix of fusion graph 
As shown in Fig. 3, the adjacency matrix of the spatial-temporal graph we 
constructed consists of three components: self-connection matrix based on the 
nodes’ temporal information, the static spatial adjacency matrix mentioned in 
Sect. 4.1, and time series similarity relationship matrix constructed based on 
Dynamic Time Warping (DTW). 
The self-connection matrix, which is based on the temporal associations of 
nodes, reﬂects the connections between the same node at diﬀerent time steps. 
Taking node i as an example, in the self-connection matrix, node ii at time 
step tt is connected to node i at time steps t-1t −1 and t+1t + 1. Such self-connection 
relationships help us understand the temporal characteristics of individual nodes 
and enable modeling of nodes in terms of time series.

[Vista original de la página 42](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-42.png)

![Página PDF 42, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-42.png)


<!-- PDF page 43 -->

## Página PDF 43

STGATAM
33
The static spatial adjacency matrix mentioned in Sect. 4.1 describes the spa-
tial relationships between nodes. This adjacency matrix is constructed based 
on the physical distances between nodes as well as the Maximal Information 
Coeﬃcient (MIC), which allows for a more accurate representation of spatial 
adjacency relationships. 
Finally, utilizing the DTW algorithm, we measure the similarity of time series 
among nodes based on a length of kk. By doing so, we construct a temporal 
relationship matrix among nodes. This type of relationship matrix enables us to 
explore local temporal correlations between nodes more eﬀectively. 
4.4
Spatial-Temporal Correlation Capture 
In our spatial-temporal fusion graph, we adopt a sliding window approach to 
select kk time steps for concatenation to form the spatial-temporal fusion graph. 
We then combine the static spatial adjacency matrix, self connection matrix, 
and temporal relationship matrix to dynamically construct an adjacency matrix 
\in R^{kN \times kN}∈RkN×kN. Next, to eﬀectively capture the spatial-temporal correlations in the 
spatial-temporal fusion graph, we decide to use a Graph Attention Network 
(GAT). 
GAT introduces attention mechanisms in graph neural networks, allowing it 
to adaptively capture the importance and relevance between nodes. This enables 
GAT to eﬀectively capture complex correlations and features in graph-structured 
data. In this paper, the Graph Attention Network is used to capture features 
from the spatial-temporal fusion graph, enabling GAT to simultaneously consider 
both temporal and spatial relationships and better understand the dependencies 
in spatial-temporal data. 
To compute the attention coeﬃcients, GAT calculates the similarity coeﬃ-
cients between each node and all their neighboring nodes, as shown in Eq. (9), 
where W is learnable parameters, aa is shared attentional mechanism, hh is the fea-
ture extracted by the temporal self-attention layer in Sect. 4.2 and e_{ij}eij represents 
the importance of node j’s features to node i. After obtaining the similarity coef-
ﬁcients, they are normalized to obtain the attention coeﬃcients, as shown in Eq. 
(10), the LeakyReLU activation function is applied to introduce non-linearity. 
\bALT{}\label{eq9} e_{ij} = a\left( {Concate\left( {Wh_{i},~Wh_{j}} \right)} \right),\eALT{} eij = a (Concate (Whi, Whj)) ,
(9) 
\bALT{}\label{eq10} \alpha_{ij} = \frac{exp\left( LeakyReLU\left( e_{ij} \right) \right)}{\sum_{k \in N_{i}}{exp\left( LeakyReLU\left( e_{ik} \right) \right)}},\eALT{} αij =
exp (LeakyReLU (eij))
k∈Ni exp (LeakyReLU (eik)),
(10) 
where \alpha_{ij}αij represents the normalized attention coeﬃcients obtained by applying 
the softmax function to the unnormalized attention coeﬃcients. The sum in the 
denominator is taken over all neighboring nodes jj of node ii (denoted as {N}_iNi). 
Based on the computed attention coeﬃcients, we can weight the features of 
other nodes and perform a weighted sum to obtain the aggregated feature h_i^{'}hi of 
node i which captures the neighborhood information. As shown as follows: 
\bALT{}\label{eq11} h_{i}^{'} = \sigma\left( {\sum_{j \in N_{i}}{\alpha_{ij}Wh_{j}}} \right),\eALT{} hi = σ

where \sigmaσ denotes an activation function. The sum is taken over all neighboring 
nodes jj belonging to the set \mathcal{N}iNi, and each node’s feature h_jhj is weighted by its cor-
responding attention coeﬃcient \alpha_{ij}αij. The weighted features are then transformed 
by a parameter matrix W before applying the activation function \sigmaσ. 
By aggregating the features from the neighboring nodes based on the atten-
tion coeﬃcients, the GAT model can eﬀectively capture the relevant information 
and emphasize important features for each node in the graph. 
To enhance the representation capacity of the model and capture richer node 
representations, this paper also utilizes the multi-head attention mechanism, as 
shown in Eq. (12), where K represents the number of attention heads. 
\bALT{}\label{eq12} h_{i}^{'}(K) = \sigma\left( \frac{1}{K}{\sum\limits_{1}^{K}{\sum_{j \in N_{i}}{\alpha_{ij}^{(k)}W^{(k)}h_{j}}}} \right),\eALT{} hi(K) = σ

where h_i^{\prime}(K)hi(K) represents the aggregated feature of the K attention heads for

are computed independently for each 
attention head. 
By employing multiple attention heads, the model can capture diﬀerent 
aspects of information and learn diverse representations for each node. The out-
puts of all the attention heads are then averaged to obtain the ﬁnal representation 
of each node, providing a more comprehensive and expressive representation of 
the graph data. 
The predicted value of the ﬁnal weather features depends on two factors: 
the long-term temporal correlations captured through static graph and self-
attention, and the short-term spatial-temporal correlations captured through 
spatial-temporal fusion graphs. These two features are then concatenated to 
form the ﬁnal representation of weather features. The prediction is performed 
using a decoder layer composed of fully connected layers, resulting in the pre-
dicted value. 
5
Experiments 
5.1
Datasets 
We validated the performance of our STGATAM model on the real world weather 
network dataset. This dataset is sourced from Kaggle [ 2] and includes weather 
data from 30 regions in the United States and Canada. The data consists of 
various parameters such as temperature, humidity, and atmospheric pressure, 
with a sampling frequency of 1 h. This implies that there are 24 data samples 
per day for each region. To standardize the input data, we applied Z-score nor-
malization. We utilize 48 h of historical data to predict the next 24 h of data, 
which implies using the past 48 consecutive time steps to forecast the future 24 
consecutive time steps. In our experiments, we utilized humidity and tempera-
ture as two features within our dataset to validate the eﬀectiveness of our model.

5.2
Baseline Methods 
– FC-LSTM [ 16,29]: Fully-Connected LSTM is a variant of LSTM that utilizes 
fully-connected layers and ﬁnds extensive application in time series prediction 
tasks. By employing a structure of fully-connected hidden units, FC-LSTM 
enhances the memory and expressive capacity of the LSTM model. 
– Transformer [ 30]: The Transformer, owing to its remarkable sequence model-
ing capability, can also be applied to time series forecasting tasks. By lever-
aging self-attention mechanisms and positional encoding, the Transformer 
eﬃciently captures long-range dependencies in time series, thereby demon-
strating excellent performance in time series prediction. 
– STGCN [ 37]: Spatial-Temporal Graph Convolutional Networks is designed 
for traﬃc prediction. It utilizes graph convolutional networks and time-gated 
convolution to capture spatial and temporal dependencies in traﬃc data, 
respectively, to enable prediction of future traﬃc patterns. 
– STSGCN [ 28]: Spatial-Temporal Synchronous Graph Convolutional Networks 
is a model that eﬀectively captures both spatial and temporal dependencies 
in graph-structured data by performing graph convolutions and aggregating 
information across multiple time steps simultaneously. 
– DSTAGNN [ 18]: Dynamic Spatial-Temporal Aware Graph Neural Network 
is a model that employs a data-driven dynamic spatial-temporal percep-
tion graph, which replaces the traditional predeﬁned static graph commonly 
used in conventional graph convolutions. Additionally, the model proposes an 
enhanced multi-head attention mechanism, combined with multi-scale gated 
convolutional layers, to capture both temporal and spatial dependencies.

5.3
Performance Comparison 
Table 1 presents a comparison among diﬀerent models in predicting temperature 
and humidity for the next 24 h within a 48-hour forecast horizon. The exper-
imental results indicate that our STGATAM model consistently outperforms 
the baseline model in terms of MAE, MAPE, and RMSE, demonstrating its 
overall superior performance on the temperature and humidity datasets. Com-
pared to the second-best performing model, DSTAGNN, the RMSE metric shows 
improvements of 11.5% and 4.21% on the two datasets, respectively. 
Furthermore, the experimental results indicate that baseline models such as 
FC-LSTM and Transformer, which solely focus on temporal dependencies and 
do not leverage the spatial dependencies of a spatial-temporal network, per-
form worse than baseline models that incorporate spatial dependencies, such 
as STGCN and STSGCN. These spatially aware models are able to capture 
the spatial-temporal relationships within the data, leading to signiﬁcantly bet-
ter experimental performance than models that only capture temporal depen-
dencies. This indirectly demonstrates the importance of spatial dependencies 
between monitoring stations in time series forecasting. 
Although STSGCN constructs spatial-temporal synchronized graphs to cap-
ture spatial-temporal dependencies simultaneously, it only considers captur-
ing local spatial-temporal dependencies while neglecting long-term temporal 
dependencies. In comparison, our STGATAM model not only takes short-term 
spatial-temporal dependencies into account but also considers long-term tem-
poral dependencies. Furthermore, our model exhibits improved construction of 
both static spacial adjacency matrix and fusion graph adjacency matrix. 
Therefore, the experimental results demonstrate the eﬀectiveness and robust-
ness of our STGATAM model in capturing spatial-temporal dependencies. 
5.4
Ablation Experiments 
In order to better demonstrate the eﬀectiveness of each component of our 
STGATAM model, we compared it with the following variants and conducted a 
series of experiments. The settings for each variant are as follows: 
– Case1: This variant does not utilize the MIC to construct the static spatial 
adjacency matrix. Instead, it solely relies on Euclidean distance to build the 
adjacency matrix.

– Case2: This variant does not employ Transformer-based self-attention mech-
anism to capture long-term temporal dependencies(LTD). 
– Case3: This variant does not employ the DTW algorithm to construct the 
spatial-temporal fusion graph. Speciﬁcally, the spatial-temporal fusion graph 
is solely constructed based on the static spatial adjacency matrix and the 
temporal self-connection matrix. 
– Case4: This variant does not utilize spatial-temporal fusion graphs(STFG) to 
simultaneously capture spatial-temporal dependencies. 
– Case5: This variant does not employ graph attention networks to capture 
spatial dependencies between individual nodes. 
– Case6: Our STGATAM model incorporates all the aforementioned modules. 
The experimental results, as presented in Table 2, demonstrate that all mod-
ules contribute positively to the improvement of our model’s performance. 
When comparing diﬀerent cases, particularly the comparison between Case2 
and Case5, it can be observed that despite Case2 not accounting for long-term 
temporal dependencies, it still outperforms Case5 which does not consider spatial 
dependencies on temperature dataset. This implies that in temperature fore-
casting tasks, the importance of spatial dependencies for accurate predictions 
surpasses that of long-term temporal dependencies. 
Furthermore, in the comparison between Case3 and Case4, it is evident 
that Case3 slightly outperforms Case4 across all metrics, exhibiting lower MAE, 
MAPE, and RMSE values. This indicates that the utilization of spatial-temporal 
fusion graphs does indeed assist the model in capturing spatial-temporal depen-
dencies to a certain extent, thereby enhancing predictive performance. 
Based on the above results, the following conclusions can be drawn: When 
considering the construction of the spatial adjacency matrix using MIC, the 
Transformer-based self-attention mechanism, the construction of the spatial-
temporal fusion graph using the DTW algorithm, and the utilization of graph 
attention networks, our STGATAM model exhibits the best performance in 
terms of prediction accuracy, with the lowest MAE, MAPE, and RMSE val-
ues. This indicates the eﬀectiveness of incorporating these modules in capturing 
spatial-temporal dependencies and improving prediction performance. In con-
trast, models that solely rely on Euclidean distance, neglect long-term temporal

5.5
Case Study 
In order to better demonstrate the eﬀectiveness of our proposed method, we 
conducted a case study on the temperature dataset. As shown in Fig. 6, the blue 
line represents the model input, which is the historical data of the past 48 h at 
the site. The green line represents the model’s predicted values, while the red 
line represents the actual ground truth values. From the graph, it is evident 
that our STGATAM model successfully predicted the temperature variations, 
and the green line closely aligns with the red line, indicating the high accuracy 
and predictive capability of our model. Furthermore, as the prediction horizon 
increases, it results in a gradual decline in the model’s prediction accuracy.

6
Conclusion 
In this paper, we propose a novel deep learning framework, STGATAM, for 
weather forecasting. Our STGATAM model constructs a novel static spatial 
adjacency matrix, builds a spatial-temporal fusion graph and its corresponding 
adjacency matrix, and eﬀectively captures the temporal and spatial dependen-
cies using self-attention mechanism to achieve sequence prediction. Compared 
to baseline methods, our STGATAM achieves state-of-the-art predictive perfor-
mance on two public datasets. Additionally, we conduct ablation studies and 
case studies to demonstrate the eﬀectiveness of our model.
