## Página PDF 52

Repository-Level Code Generation 
Method Enhanced by Context-Dependent 
Graph Retrieval 
Hanxiao Zhang1 
, Anqi Li1 
, Zhirui Kuai1 
, Zhao Wei2, Li Kuang1(B), 
and Yingjie Xia3 
1 School of Computer Science and Engineering, Central South University, 
Changsha, China 
{224711045,8209210323,kuaizhirui,kuangli}@csu.edu.cn 
2 Tencent, Shenzhen, China 
zachwei@tencent.com 
3 Hangzhou Dianzi University, Hangzhou, China 
xiayingiie@zju.edu.cn 
Abstract. Large language models (LLMs) excel in NL2CODE (natural 
language to code) tasks, but they are prone to generating hallucinatory 
or redundant code in repository contexts due to a lack of global per-
spective. To mitigate the problem, preliminary works have attempted to 
extract task-relevant context from repositories to aid code generation. 
Nonetheless, these methods do not delve deeply enough into processing 
repository context information since they overlook the hierarchical and 
dependency relationships among contexts. Therefore, we propose a code 
generation method enhanced by context-dependent graph retrieval. First, 
we present a method for constructing a Repository Context Dependency 
Graph (RCDG) designed toencapsulate multiple types of code entities 
within a repository, along with their interdependencies. Subsequently, we 
propose a code generation framework enhanced by context dependency 
graph retrieval. Semantically related context collections are obtained 
through heterogeneous graph retrieval and Breadth-First Search(BFS). 
These collected contexts undergo a further reﬁnement process through 
the use of LLMs, aiming to elevate the relevance of the retrieved con-
texts. Finally, we automatically construct prompts for the contexts and 
requirements to eﬀectively utilize the retrieved relevant contexts, guiding 
the model to generate high-quality code. Experimental results indicate 
that our method outperforms current state-of the-art methods across 
multiple metrics. 
Keywords: Code Generation · Prompt Engineering · Code Repository 
Knowledge Mining 
1
Introduction 
Automatically converting natural language descriptions into executable source 
code (NL2CODE) has always been one of the critical research hotspots in the

## Página PDF 53

Repository-Level Code Generation Method
43
ﬁeld of intelligent software engineering [ 1– 6]. The onset of the large pre-trained 
language models (LLMs) era has signiﬁcantly propelled advancements in this 
domain. Modern LLMs, equipped with billions of parameters and trained on 
vast datasets comprising both text and code, have demonstrated remarkable 
capabilities in addressing the NL2CODE challenge [ 7– 12], even under few-shot 
or zero-shot conditions [ 13]. Notable code LLMs like Codex [ 8], AlphaCode [ 10], 
and CodeLlama [ 9] have not only showcased superior performance across various 
benchmarks, such as HumanEval [ 8] and MBPP [ 14], but also underscored the 
potential of LLMs in achieving parity with human programmers in complex cod-
ing tasks, thereby marking a monumental shift in how programming challenges 
are approached and resolved. 
Despite the commendable reasoning capabilities demonstrated by LLMs in 
the NL2CODE task [ 15– 17], their performance signiﬁcantly decreases in the 
complex scenarios of generating interdependent functions at the repository level 
[ 18,19]. This limitation largely stems from the models’ inability to consider the 
repository’s context holistically during code generation. Typically restricted to 
the code present in the current ﬁle, LLMs struggle to leverage information dis-
persed across various modules, impairing their eﬀectiveness in scenarios demand-
ing a comprehensive understanding of the repository [ 20]. Much eﬀorts are pro-
posed to mitigate the problem, such as expanding the model’s input window 
[ 21,22] to encompass the entire repository context, but they still confront inher-
ent limitations. Research indicates LLMs exhibit a ‘U-shaped’ performance curve 
when processing extensive texts [ 23], suggesting a decreased ability to assimilate 
and utilize all contextual information eﬀectively. Moreover, directly inputting 
these contexts without any processing may lead to the introduction of excessive 
irrelevant information, which could adversely impact the reasoning abilities of 
LLMs. 
Recent advancements have seen an exploration into enhancing LLMs’ capac-
ity to generate repository context-dependent functions. The primary objective 
of these studies has been to reﬁne LLMs’ grasp of repository context through 
strategic incorporation or extraction of repository-speciﬁc knowledge [ 20,24– 31]. 
Various methods have been introduced, including prompt engineering, agent-
based strategies, model ﬁne-tuning, and hybrid approaches. Among these, model 
ﬁne-tuning speciﬁcally aims to elevate the model’s repository context compre-
hension by creating a specialized ﬁne-tuning dataset that integrates repository 
context into the training phase, utilizing KV(key-value) caches to store this 
knowledge [ 30]. Proxy-based strategies employ external resources, such as code 
autocompletion tools, web search engines, and coding testing tools, creating a 
synergistic approach that strategically combines multiple resources [ 26,27]. The 
prompt engineering approaches treat LLMs as a black box, preprocessing reposi-
tory context before inference and utilizing information retrieval techniques (e.g., 
vector similarity calculations) to gather diﬀerent sources and types of context as 
part of the input to the model. 
However, these methods fall short by not adequately addressing the complex-
ity of repository context. Speciﬁcally, they lack in-depth exploration beyond mere

[Vista original de la página 53](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-53.png)

![Página PDF 53, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-53.png)


<!-- PDF page 54 -->

## Página PDF 54

44
H. Zhang et al.
traversal of repository source ﬁles for capturing ﬁxed-length code snippets with a 
sliding window mechanism [ 31], or in extracting comprehensive function informa-
tion from repositories [ 29]. This approach disregards the intricate, hierarchical, 
and dependency relationships that exist among code entities within repositories. 
Such an oversight results in a superﬁcial representation of the repository context, 
which may inadvertently prioritize the context of semantically similar modules 
over functionally dependent ones. Furthermore, the practice of constructing a 
“function base” [ 29] for the entirety of the repository can mistakenly assign 
equal importance to all modules. This not only risks conﬂating the signiﬁcance 
of very diﬀerent modules but also introduces the potential for irrelevant infor-
mation to be included, exacerbated by the model’s limited capacity to process 
input. This shallow handling of complex repository structures can signiﬁcantly 
hamper the model’s ability to accurately understand and represent the nuanced 
relationships and dependencies within code, leading to less accurate outcomes. 
To address this limitation, we propose a code generation framework enhanced 
by repository context dependency graph retrieval. First, we develop a method for 
constructing a Repository Context Dependency Graph (RCDG). This method 
is capable of identifying and extracting various types of code entities and their 
interdependencies within a repository, thereby encapsulating the repository’s 
context information comprehensively. Second, to augment the relevance of the 
extracted context to the speciﬁc coding task at hand, we employ a two-step 
retrieval process based on the constructed RCDG. Initially, we utilize graph 
search to identify semantically related functional units within the repository. 
Subsequently, LLMs evaluate the relevance of these units, ﬁltering out entities 
that are irrelevant to task requirements. Last, to facilitate eﬀective code gen-
eration that mirrors the human development process and mitigates hallucina-
tion problems at the repository level, we automatically generate prompts from 
the reﬁned context and requirements. Leveraging the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) 
method [ 32], we steer the LLMs to either reuse or refer to pertinent context, 
thereby enhancing the quality and applicability of the generated code. 
To evaluate the eﬀectiveness of our approach, we have expanded the existing 
repository-level assessment benchmark, CoderEval [ 18], by mining information 
on the calls to custom modules within the repository by non-independent func-
tions, serving as a reference for reusability assessment. The experimental results 
demonstrate that our method outperforms existing methods across multiple eval-
uation metrics. The contributions of this paper are summarized as follows: 
1. We investigate the code generation approach within repository-level develop-
ment settings, which is crucial for understanding and enhancing the perfor-
mance of LLMs in real-world scenarios. 
2. Our method for constructing a repository context graph captures both the 
global context and dependency data of repositories, facilitating the extraction 
of diverse yet pertinent contexts for repository-level code generation. 
3. We introduce a new code generation framework powered by context depen-
dency graph retrieval, which enhances the precision and relevance of context 
retrieval. This framework navigates the complexities of repository-level func-

Repository-Level Code Generation Method

tion generation, yielding code that integrates eﬀortlessly into existing repos-
itories. 
4. We conducted experiments on public benchmarks for practical project code 
generation and validated our code generation method’s superiority over exist-
ing methods across multiple metrics. 
The data of our method is available at link 1. The rest of the paper is orga-
nized as follows. Section 2 introduces the related work on repository-level code 
generation. Our research method is presented in Sect. 3. Section 4 introduces 
the research questions and experiment settings. Section 5 details the ﬁndings of 
our experiments and oﬀers a comprehensive analysis in relation to the research 
questions posed. The conclusion of this paper is given in Sect. 6. 
2
Related Work 
2.1
Repository-Level Code Generation 
Repository-level code generation [ 26– 28,30] explores and exploits the capabilities 
of LLMs in the context of complex real-world project development. Shrivastava 
et al. [ 28] introduced a repository-level prompt generator that classiﬁes and com-
bines repository context based on heuristic rules, optimizing LLM performance 
on single-line code completions without adjusting model parameters. They also 
proposed the RepoFusion framework [ 30] to integrate retrieved repository con-
text in model training. Some studies have also explored agent-based methods, 
integrating external tools (such as search engines, code interpreters, etc.) into 
the LLM generation process and interacting with LLMs through design combi-
nation strategies during code generation. For example, Zhang et al. [ 27] used 
web search engines and identiﬁer navigators to retrieve relevant reference code 
and context and use code testing tools to generate feedback to improve the 
quality of LLM-generated code. Wang et al. [ 26], on the other hand, used IDE’s 
code auto-completion tool in the model ﬁne-tuning and inference stages, built 
a data-enhanced repository internal dataset for model ﬁne-tuning, and gradu-
ally predicted function tokens using ﬁne-tuned LLMs and suggestions from the 
auto-completion tool. 
2.2
Retrieval-Augmented Code Generation 
Retrieval-Augmented-Generation (RAG) combines information retrieval and 
text generation techniques to assist LLMs in generating more accurate tex-
tual content by retrieving relevant information from external knowledge sources. 
Applying retrieval ideas to scenarios involving associated repository contexts has 
become one of the most popular research frameworks. Zhang et al. [ 31] explored 
code contexts across multiple ﬁles, using a sliding window method to obtain 
ﬁxed-size code snippets to create a candidate context database and retrieving
1 https://github.com/Rinkalin/RCDG.

## Página PDF 56

46
H. Zhang et al.
relevant snippets for line-level completion and API invocation. Zhou et al. [ 33] 
introduced DocPrompting, training a retriever to fetch documents based on user 
natural language queries, which are then combined with human intent into a long 
prompt for code generation. Parvez et al. introduced RECODER [ 34], which 
used summary information as a query during retrieval phase to search for rele-
vant code snippets in CodeSearchNet, providing more reference information dur-
ing generation. Liao et al. [ 29] pre-constructed a function library for the entire 
project and retrieved relevant function snippets through vector similarity during 
generation. They incorporated descriptive information from these snippets into 
prompts to enhance the LLM’s awareness of repository knowledge. 
2.3
Code Generation Evaluation 
As the focus of code generation shifts towards more complex, repository-level 
challenges, several studies have focused on establishing new evaluation bench-
marks to assess model capabilities in these contexts, particularly concerning code 
generation benchmarks that involve non-independent functions derived from real 
projects. For instance, Tang et al. [ 24] developed a benchmark speciﬁcally for 
bioinformatics code generation, leveraging contexts from high-quality bioinfor-
matics repositories. Yu et al. [ 18] introduced CoderEval, which evaluates models 
on practical tasks with 460 function generation tasks from Python and Java 
repositories, considering project context dependencies and including annotated 
requirements and a testing environment for correctness. Li et al. [ 19] proposed 
EvoCodeBench, which gathered 2690 samples from 119 real projects across 10 
domains, ensuring consistency with real projects in program distribution, depen-
dencies, and context length. Meanwhile, Ding et al. [ 35] introduced CrossCodeE-
val, a diverse code completion benchmark based on four popular programming 
languages (Python, Java, TypeScript, and C#), aiming to evaluate the abil-
ity of code autocompletion models in understanding cross-ﬁle contexts. These 
advancements underline the growing complexity of code generation tasks and 
the corresponding need for benchmarks that accurately reﬂect real-world coding 
environments and dependencies. 
3
Approach 
3.1
Overview 
The code generation framework enhanced by context dependency graph retrieval 
is shown in Fig. 1. This method takes the function signature and docstring 
information as inputs and produces a comprehensive function implementation. 
The framework comprises three main components: construction of the reposi-
tory context dependency graph, retrieval of code semantics-related context, and 
context-enhanced code generation. (1) Construction of Repository Con-
text Dependency Graph: This step involves scanning through various source 
ﬁles within the repository to identify two primary code entities—classes and 
functions—alongside their dependent modules, creating summary descriptions

Fig. 1. Framework for Code Generation Enhanced by Context-Dependent Graph 
Retrieval 
for these entities. A context dependency graph is then formed using these entities 
and the relationships between them. (2) Retrieval of Key Contexts Perti-
nent to Code Semantics: This component employs a two-tier retrieval process 
to pinpoint the most pertinent context. Initially, it locates functional units within 
the repository that have programmatic semantic dependencies, as outlined in the 
context dependency graph. Subsequently, a judge prompt template is utilized, 
engaging an LLM to sift through these semantically linked functional units, serv-
ing as supplemental information for generating code at the repository level. (3) 
Context-enhanced Code Generation: To eﬃciently leverage the key context 
sourced from various origins, this phase adopts a CoT method [ 32] and devises a 
generation prompt template. This template is capable of integrating both local 
and overarching contextual information based on the sub-steps derived from the 
decomposition, directing the model to craft code that ﬁts seamlessly into the 
current ﬁle. 
3.2
Construction of Repository Context Dependency Graph 
A repository is a rich ecosystem comprising various components such as mul-
tiple source ﬁles, conﬁguration ﬁles, API documentation, and version history, 
each oﬀering insights into the repository’s underlying knowledge framework. To 
eﬃciently encapsulate and access this repository context, we prioritize three pri-
mary types of code entities: source ﬁles, classes, and methods. Given the diver-
sity and complex interdependencies of these code context types and sources, 
we have developed a methodology to excavate and catalog these code entities’ 
meta-information (including source code, signatures, types, and locations). Addi-
tionally, we explore these entities’ contextual dependencies, such as inheritance

[Vista original de la página 57](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-57.png)

![Página PDF 57, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-57.png)


<!-- PDF page 58 -->

## Página PDF 58

48
H. Zhang et al.
or references to classes, calls to custom APIs or variables, etc. We craft semantic 
descriptions for these entities based on this comprehensive collation of meta-
information and dependency contexts. The culmination of this process is the 
creation of a context dependency graph. This graph, starting from the ﬁle level, 
integrates the meta-information and summary information into each entity node. 
This structured representation illuminates the dependencies within the reposi-
tory and serves as a valuable tool for more detailed context retrieval. 
Extraction of Code Entity Information 
Metadata: The Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) plays a crucial role in the extraction 
and processing of source code. During the phase of entity information extraction, 
we leverage the ast module in Python to gather various details such as names, 
types, and source code of two fundamental code entities: classes and functions. 
Additionally, to facilitate the mining of dependency information and to circum-
vent the issue of identical names for functions or classes within the repository, we 
distinguish each entity by its unique location information throughout the repos-
itory. For instance, the location of a method deﬁned within a ﬁle is denoted by 
a comprehensive identiﬁer: file path.file name.class name.function name. 
This systematic approach ensures precise identiﬁcation and aids in the thorough 
analysis of code entities. 
Semantic Information: Semantic information refers to the characterization of an 
entity through summary descriptions. Representing an entity’s functionality with 
a summary description rather than its source code fragments not only compresses 
the context length but also facilitates the assessment of whether a deﬁned mod-
ule can be reused for the requirements of code to be generated. Unlike standalone 
code summarization methods, entities within a repository often have complex 
dependencies among them. For instance, generating a summary for a method 
cannot solely consider the method’s body itself. This is because the functionality 
of a method is not just reﬂected in its internal code but is also closely related to 
the context of its callers and callees. Consequently, the absence of context might 
lead to an inaccurate semantic representation of the method. Moreover, sum-
maries often directly reference information from other methods (such as names), 
and overlooking the method’s context may challenge the model’s ability to gener-
ate such information. Therefore, when generating summary descriptions for code 
entities in a repository, it is crucial to provide the summary generation model 
with not only the entity itself but also the related dependency information. To 
obtain an entity’s dependency context, we utilize the ast module to capture the 
main body and objects of the entity’s call nodes and match them with all entity 
identiﬁers within the repository, ﬁltering out calls to built-in functions and third-
party library APIs. Finally, based on the entity’s source code and dependency 
context, a summary is generated as the entity’s semantic information.

[Vista original de la página 58](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-58.png)

![Página PDF 58, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-58.png)


<!-- PDF page 59 -->

## Página PDF 59

Repository-Level Code Generation Method
49
Construction of Repository Context Dependency Graph 
Contrary to existing methodologies that construct a function information repos-
itory encompassing the entire repository, yet overlook potential dependencies 
among entities therein, our approach entails the assembly of a context depen-
dency graph for each ﬁle within the repository. This strategy aﬀords a compre-
hensive overview of the dependencies between repository modules and imparts 
granular insights into the code entities housed within each module. Traditional 
static code analysis tools, such as Pyan 2, suﬀer from limited accuracy and fail 
to satisfy our requirements for ﬁne-grained construction. Moreover, tools like 
PyCallGraph [ 36] necessitate correct project compilation, presenting an addi-
tional constraint. To address these limitations, we have devised a customized 
dependency identiﬁcation logic to forge a dependency graph tailored to the repos-
itory’s context. Illustrated in Fig. 1, our context dependency graph encompasses 
three varieties of entity nodes: ﬁles, classes, and non-class functions. Each entity 
node is annotated with ﬁve types of attribute information: type, name, location, 
source code deﬁnition, and summary. The graph delineates three distinct types of 
inter-node relationships: (1) The import relationship, observable between ﬁles, 
as well as between ﬁles and classes, and between ﬁles and functions; (2) The 
invocation relationship, which occurs between functions, and between functions 
and classes; (3) The containment relationship, present between ﬁles and class 
nodes, and between ﬁles and function nodes. 
3.3
Retrieval of Key Contexts Pertinent to Code Semantics 
Considering the presence of contexts within a program that, while not seman-
tically similar, exhibit semantic dependencies (e.g., imports, inheritance), our 
approach begins by identifying subgraph indicative of semantic dependencies 
within the context dependency graph of the repository. Subsequently, we learn 
the representations of graph’s nodes. Through computing the similarity of node 
embeddings, we are able to identify a collection of nodes that are semantically 
similar to the task. Furthermore, to mitigate the detrimental eﬀects of irrele-
vant information present in the entities encompassed by the subgraph on the 
model’s performance, we employ a retrieval technique predicated on instruc-
tional prompts. By crafting a retrieval prompt tailored for relevance evaluation, 
we conduct a secondary retrieval of these entities, thereby obtaining crucial entity 
contexts. 
Graph Search 
Structure-dependent Subgraph Search: 
For a repository context dependency 
graph G = (V; E; X)G = (V ; E; X), VV is the set of nodes, EE is the set of edges, and XX is the 
attribute information of nodes. To obtain the subgraphs related to the current 
task semantics, and considering the varying importance of information from dif-
ferent sources for the task generation, we divide the search scope into in-ﬁle and
2 https://github.com/davidfraser/pyan.

[Vista original de la página 59](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-59.png)

![Página PDF 59, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-59.png)


<!-- PDF page 60 -->

## Página PDF 60

50
H. Zhang et al.
Fig. 2. Judge Prompt Template 
cross-ﬁle based on the current task’s location. We deﬁne the connected graph, 
consisting of the current ﬁle node and all nodes that have a containment relation-
ship with it, as the in-ﬁle subgraph. For the cross-ﬁle related subgraph, starting 
from the current ﬁle node, we employ a Breadth-First Search (BFS) algorithm 
to conduct the subgraph search. To avoid introducing too much irrelevant infor-
mation, we set a search depth DD to control the size of the structure-dependent 
subgraph. 
Expansion of Semantically Similar Nodes: In addition to nodes that are con-
textually dependent on the current ﬁle node, research indicates that reposi-
tories with semantically similar contexts (e.g., similar names) serve as signiﬁ-
cant sources of information [ 23]. To acquire nodes semantically similar to the 
task at hand, we initially employ an encoder to transform the attributes of a 
node into node representation vectors. Subsequently, we adopt the Heterogeneous 
Graph Transformation (HGT), proposed by Hu et al. [ 37], for representational 
learning of nodes. This method utilizes a Transformer-based attention mecha-
nism that considers the types of nodes and edges when calculating attention 
weights. Speciﬁcally, HGT computes separate attention values for each node-
edge-node pattern (e.g., user-views-item). This type-aware attention mechanism 
allows HGT to capture complex relationships between nodes in heterogeneous 
graphs. After learning, we can get the updated node representation. Then, for 
any two nodes (v_ivi) and (v_jvj), their embedding similarity (S(v_ivi, v_jvj)) can be cal-
culated through measures such as cosine similarity:  S(v_i, v_j) = \frac{h_{v_i} \cdot h_{v_j}}{|h_{v_i}||h_{v_j}|}S(vi, vj) =
hvi·hvj
|hvi||hvj | By cal-
culating the embedding similarity, a set of nodes with task-semantic similarity 
can be obtained, serving as a supplement to the cross-ﬁle relevant context. 
Relevant Entity Judge 
We design a prompt to judge the relevance between the dependent entities and

[Vista original de la página 60](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-60.png)

![Página PDF 60, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-60.png)


<!-- PDF page 61 -->

## Página PDF 61

Repository-Level Code Generation Method
51
the requirements of the current two sources, and realize the reﬁned retrieval 
of key contexts. Given the task requirements and the functional summary of 
the corresponding entity, LLM determines whether the entity can be reused 
by the task requirements or provide a reference. The prompt mainly includes 
(1) role-playing instructions, (2) task description instructions, and (3) input 
and output instructions. Figure 2 is an example of a prompt template. The 
judge prompt serves as the input of the relevance discriminant function. For the 
context node set from the two sources, we discriminate the entities one by one. 
The input is the description information of the entity and the corresponding 
code generation requirements (i.e., function signature and function docstring 
information). The description information of the entity includes the type of entity 
(class or function), the name of the entity, and the summary of the entity. During 
the judgment, the relevance discriminant function is used to determine whether 
it is useful for requirements of the to be generated code. If it is useful, the source 
code of the entity is added to the candidate entity set. 
3.4
Context-Enhanced Code Generation 
Leveraging the key contexts retrieved from two sources, we design an automatic 
prompt generation method to construct context prompts that integrate various 
pieces of information for user requirements. This method integrates user require-
ments with key contexts to guide the model in generating high-quality responses. 
At this stage, we referred to the prompt templates by Liao et al. [ 29], which pri-
marily comprise four components: task instruction description, rules, input, and 
output speciﬁcations. 
In the task instruction description, we adopt a role-playing prompt strategy, 
assigning the system’s role to that of a professional programmer working on 
projects using Python. The task is explicitly deﬁned as generating a function 
that meets requirements based on the given requirements and code snippets 
from in-ﬁle/cross-ﬁle sources within the repository. 
In the rules deﬁnition section, we deﬁne three distinct rule constraints: (1) 
Use of CoT [ 32] method to guide the model in breaking down complex require-
ments into steps, analyzing whether the given context can be directly reused 
for each step; (2) Constraints on the reuse of the given context dictate that the 
current step: if able to reuse the provided context reference, should be directly 
invoked to avoid the redundancy of re-implementation; (3) Constraints on the 
syntax accuracy of the generated code; (4) Constraints on the code format: avoid-
ing natural language explanations and the use of ‘pass’ statements to skip the 
implementation of functionalities. 
Input and output respectively state the description of the given input infor-
mation and the speciﬁcation that the output should conform to. Figure 3 is an 
example prompt template for code generation tasks using contextual informa-
tion.

[Vista original de la página 61](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-61.png)

4
Experiment 
This section primarily introduces the research questions, foundational models 
utilized in the experimental phase, datasets, evaluation metrics, and comparison 
methods. 
4.1
Research Questions (RQs) 
– RQ1: How does the overall performance of our repository context-enhanced 
code generation method compare with existing methods? 
– RQ2: How do diﬀerent types of retrieved context aﬀect the code generated 
by LLMs? 
– RQ3: How eﬀective is our context-dependent graph retrieval method in 
retrieving highly relevant context? 
4.2
Foundational Model 
In the graph search module, the HGT [ 37] graph representation method is 
employed for learning node embeddings. We select GPT-3.5-Turbo as the foun-
dational LLM for judging relevant entities and generating code. Beyond its cost 
advantages and ease of interface use, we chose GPT-3.5-Turbo because it demon-
strates superior performance across a variety of software engineering tasks. Gen-
erally, in frameworks powered by LLM for code generation, the more superior 
the base model, the better capability of the base model enhances the overall 
performance of the framework. During the context construction, retrieval, and

[Vista original de la página 62](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-62.png)

![Página PDF 62, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-62.png)


<!-- PDF page 63 -->

## Página PDF 63

Repository-Level Code Generation Method
53
code generation phases, the same model is used to generate entity summaries, 
determine entity relevance, and produce functions related to the context. When 
conﬁguring the chat model, parameters are set to temperaturetemperature = 1, nn = 10, 
with all other parameters at their default settings. 
4.3
Datasets 
We choose the CoderEval4Python dataset from CoderEval [ 18] for our exper-
iment due to its inclusion of test cases and an evaluation environment. This 
setup enables eﬀective assessment of function accuracy and the correctness of 
repo-level code generated by LLMs. The dataset comprises 230 Python function 
generation tasks spread across 46 repositories. In the dataset, approximately 30% 
of the tasks are standalone tasks, while 70% are non-standalone code generation 
tasks that rely on repository context. 
4.4
Evaluation Metrics 
Building on prior studies, our experimental results are assessed from the follow-
ing three dimensions: 
(1) pass@kk [ 8]: This is an execution-based evaluation metric which reﬂects the 
functional accuracy of the generated code. We found that comparable studies 
did not employ this metric for assessment. However, this metric is critical 
for evaluating the capability of LLMs to generate repo-level code, leading 
us to select it as one of our evaluation criteria. It denotes the probability of 
passing test cases from kk randomly selected answers produced by the model. 
The formula to calculate pass@kk is presented as follows: 
\bALT{} \text{pass@}k := \mathbb{E}_\text{Problems} \left[1 - \frac{\binom{n-c}{k}}{\binom{n}{k}}\right]\eALT{} pass@k := EProblems
1 −
n−c
k
n
k
(1) 
In this context, nn denotes the total number of candidate answers generated 
by the model, cc represents the number of those nn answers that pass the unit 
tests, and \frac{\binom{n-c}{k}}{\binom{n}{k}}(
n−c
k )
(
n
k)
signiﬁes the probability of selecting kk answers out of the 
nn generated, in which all kk answers fail. For our exhaustive performance 
assessment, we set nn = 10, evaluating pass@kk across kk = 1, 5, 10 scenarios. 
(2) CodeBLEU [ 38]: This evaluation metric is based on the matching of reference 
code and is calculated by the weighted sum of the match degrees between 
the generated code and the reference code in terms of AST structures, data 
ﬂow information, and control ﬂow information. This represents the semantic 
similarity between the generated code and the reference code. 
(3) recall@kk: This metric assesses how eﬀectively the generated code reuses cus-
tom APIs within the repository. We mine the call information of the call 
node in the reference code, match with the custom module in the repository, 
and use the successfully matched module as the reference for reusability

[Vista original de la página 63](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-63.png)

![Página PDF 63, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-63.png)

## Página PDF 64

54
H. Zhang et al.
evaluation. The formula for calculation is as illustrated: given a reference 
example reuses APIs a(), b(), and c(), if the generated code calls APIs b() 
and d(), then the recall rate is calculated as 1/3. Here, kk denotes the sample 
size for each evaluation, with kk = 1 used in the experiment. 
4.5
Baseline 
Since the agent-based method and the model ﬁne-tuning method have great 
diﬀerences in speciﬁc tasks and evaluation indicators, and these works are not 
open source, we chose A3 CodeGen [ 29], which is also a prompt engineering-
based method, as a comparison for our method. 
A3 CodeGen [ 29]: This method divides repository knowledge into three types: 
local, global, and third-party library. These contexts are preprocessed before 
LLMs reasoning. For local context, the author extracts the relevant informa-
tion of functions and global variables in the current ﬁle and class instances; 
for global context, this method builds function base for each repository, calcu-
lates the vector similarity between the requirements of the code to be generated 
and the functions in the function base, and obtains the relevant global context; 
the third-party library provides the names of these libraries. Based on the open 
source code of Liao et al. [ 29], we build function base for the repositories involved 
in CoderEval4Python, employing their techniques to extract and retrieve knowl-
edge from the three repository types, integrating this information with the input 
to facilitate code generation. 
– A1 CodeGen: a method for local context enhancement, 
– A2 CodeGen: a method for local and global retrieve context enhancement, 
– A3 CodeGen: a method for local, global, and third-party library knowledge 
enhancement. 
In the original paper’s setting, the model generates 1 answer for each task. To 
be consistent with our experimental setting, we set nn=10, shot-type=zero-shot, 
and kk = 5 when generating the code. When presenting the results, we choose 
the best group of results that provide diﬀerent types of context information for 
comparison. 
Variants of Our Method: We replace the retrieved context snippets with 
signature and summary information and reset the instruction description part 
of the input prompt as one of the baselines for comparison. 
5
Results and Analysis 
5.1
RQ1: How Does the Overall Performance of Our Repository 
Context-Enhanced Code Generation Method Compare 
with Existing Methods? 
Table 1 presents the comparison results between our method and the baseline 
across three metrics: pass@kk, CodeBLEU, and recall@1. As evidenced by the

table, our method outperforms the competing methods. This indicates that the 
generated code demonstrates signiﬁcant improvements in functional, semantic, 
and reuse accuracy for custom APIs in the repository. Upon analysis, we contend 
that the inferior performance of the comparison method stems from its focus on 
the reuse of the custom function module of the repository while neglecting the 
overall functional accuracy of the task. Moreover, the introduction of the retrieval 
context within the repository by the A3 CodeGen method leads to a decline in 
the quality of the generated code. A comparison of the experimental results 
between lines 1 and 2 reveals a slight decrease in the metrics of pass@kk, Code-
BLEU, and recall@1. We argue that retrieving across the entire function base 
neglects the dependencies among contexts, leading to the introduction of irrel-
evant information whose noise exceeds the positive boost provided by relevant 
contexts. 
The Utilization of Context Information Plays a Crucial Role. Both our 
approach and the comparative method aim to select and aggregate context at the 
input phase. However, experimental results from A3 CodeGen and our method’s 
variants reveal that the original context, i.e., the source code snippet, is better 
understood and exploited by the model than the descriptive information of the 
context, such as the short name of the context and the summary information. 
This observation is underscored by replacing the original context with summary 
information of related retrieved entities, leading to a notable degradation in three 
metrics under the ours variants setting, particularly with recall@1 plummeting 
by 0.47. This suggests that the model struggles to fully comprehend and utilize 
context in this summarized format. 
Finally, the design of prompts has a great impact on stimulating 
the model’s reasoning ability in complex scenarios. In comparing the 
prompt templates of A3 CodeGen, it was found that even with identical task 
inputs, variations in the expressions of task instructions and the structure of 
prompt templates led to large diﬀerences in the quality of code generated by 
the model. Additionally, compared with complex prompt templates, concise and 
clear instructions can sometimes achieve better results than complex prompts.

Table 2 demonstrates the overall performance of the model-generated code across 
diﬀerent types of critical contexts. The import information is deﬁned as the 
import statements at the head of the current ﬁle. The in-ﬁle information is 
characterized as the key in-ﬁle context retrieved using the speciﬁed retrieval 
method in our framework; a cross-ﬁle context is represented as a globally rele-
vant context that possesses semantic associations. It becomes evident that the 
inclusion of more varied types of contextual information enhances the model’s 
comprehension and application of repository knowledge, signiﬁcantly improving 
the generated code in terms of functional accuracy and the reuse of existing 
information. We noticed that recall@1 dropped from 0.61 to 0.6 after providing 
cross-ﬁle context. We speculate that this may be due to the inﬂuence of the 
irrelevant global context. 
Figure 4 presents the average pass@10 values of the models for both stan-
dalone and non-standalone tasks given diﬀerent types of contexts. Standalone 
indicates that the task is either self contained or slib runnable, non-standalone 
indicates that the dependency of the task is {ﬁle runnable, class runnable, 
plib runnable, project runnable}. It is observed that the enhancement in pass@kk
resulting from the provision of context information is not particularly pro-
nounced for standalone tasks. For non-standalone tasks, after the application 
of context retrieved by in-ﬁle, the pass@10 value more than doubled, demon-
strating that in-ﬁle context signiﬁcantly enhances the model’s understanding 
of requirements and repository knowledge. The inclusion of retrieved cross-ﬁle 
context further enhances the performance slightly. This demonstrates that our 
approach oﬀers a repository-level perspective for the task.

5.3
RQ3: How Eﬀective is Our Retrieval Method Compared 
with Existing Retrieval Methods? 
We conducted experiments to evaluate the eﬀectiveness of the retrieval meth-
ods, and compared them on four metrics: context length (ctx length), recall@1, 
pass@1, and CodeBLEU. The comparison retrieval methods are as follows: 
(1) non-judge: Retain all contexts with dependencies after graph search, without 
making a second judgment on the entity nodes in the graph search results. 
(2) vector-based retrieval: Construct a function base for all functions in the 
repository, calculate the cosine similarity between related entity embed-
dings and requirement description embeddings, both in-ﬁle and cross-ﬁle, 
and select the top kk = 5 entities as retrieval results. 
(3) random retrieval: Randomly select kk = 5 entities from both the current ﬁle 
and the function base. 
Examination of columns 5 and 6 of Table 3 indicates that a second judgment 
is necessary. Although context semantically related to the task can be derived 
from the context dependency graph, these contexts are likely to contain irrelevant 
information. Consequently, a secondary assessment is conducted after the graph 
search to eliminate the eﬀects of any irrelevant context. The experimental results 
demonstrate that our retrieval module achieves shorter context lengths, higher

random retrieval undoubtedly performs the worst. This outcome underscores the 
negative impact of irrelevant context on the generation task. Although retrieval 
based on vector similarity results in shorter contexts, its overall performance 
falls short of our method due to its neglect of context dependency and failure to 
secure relevant yet distinct contexts. 
6
Threats to Validity 
This study’s ﬁndings are primarily validated within the context of the Python 
programming language. The limitation arises due to the substantial time and 
eﬀort required to construct context graphs and perform graph retrieval across 
multiple repositories. Consequently, our methodology has not been evaluated on 
other widely-used programming languages, such as Java or C. Future research 
will aim to develop methods for constructing context graphs for code repositories 
in various programming languages, which will be instrumental for repository-
level code generation. 
Furthermore, our method demonstrates better performance in context-
dependent code generation tasks. During our experiments, we found that for 
context-independent tasks, despite constraining the model with speciﬁc require-
ments in the prompt, the generated code is still inﬂuenced by contextual infor-
mation, leading to inaccuracies. Therefore, our method is more suitable for gen-
eration tasks that rely on repository context. 
7
Conclusion 
We introduce a framework that leverages a repository context dependency graph 
for enhanced code generation, aimed at alleviating the illusion problem of repo-
level function generation, and enhancing module reuse capabilities. Speciﬁcally,

[Vista original de la página 68](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-68.png)

![Página PDF 68, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-68.png)


<!-- PDF page 69 -->

## Página PDF 69

Repository-Level Code Generation Method
59
the framework is composed of three key components. (1) The repository con-
text dependency graph construction module, which captures the global context 
and dependency information of the repository, and eﬀectively compresses and 
integrates the context; (2) Key context retrieval module: utilizing the context 
dependency graph, this module combines graph retrieval and LLM judgement to 
enhance the accuracy and relevance of context retrieval; (3) Context-enhanced 
code generation module: The module integrates the retrieved context into the 
prompt, employing the chain of thought decomposition method to augment the 
model’s ability to reuse or refer to the relevant context based on the decom-
posed program steps. The experimental results demonstrate that the code gen-
eration method introduced in this paper can signiﬁcantly enhance the relevance 
of retrieval and minimize the impact of irrelevant context, owing to the utiliza-
tion of advanced methods and retrieval techniques across various metrics.

## Página PDF 72

DGSR: Dual-Graph Sequential 
Recommendation with Gated 
and Heterogeneous GNNs 
Bangqi Li, Qing Sun(B), Ji Wu, Haiyan Yang, Yuanxin Ouyang, 
and Wenge Rong 
School of Computer Science and Engineering, Beihang University, Beijing, China 
sunqing@buaa.edu.cn 
Abstract. Sequential recommendation represents a well-explored yet 
challenging domain within research. Despite signiﬁcant advancements in 
GNN-based methods for modeling intricate patterns in user-item inter-
action sequences, these methods face diﬃculties in capturing nuanced 
semantics in sequences with sparse dependencies and noise, and often 
struggle with short sequences. Additionally, distinguishing higher-order 
semantic distinctions among diverse user interests is still challenging, 
and existing GNN-based methods can be computationally intensive. 
To address these challenges, we propose a Dual-Graph approach for 
Sequential Recommendation, DGSR. We construct individual interac-
tion graphs for each sequence, and a heterogeneous global interaction 
graph that incorporates user identity as an attribute of user edges. 
Last-item augmented GGNN is employed within individual interaction 
graphs to mitigate the impact of sparse dependencies and noise, thereby 
extracting the most recent interests for each user more eﬀectively. More-
over, within the global graph, we propose a parameter-eﬃcient hetero-
geneous GNN to extract high-order interest distinctions among diverse 
users while maintaining low computational complexity. Finally, we uti-
lize vanilla transform mechanism to integrate intra- and inter-user inter-
ests from both types of graphs. Experiments on four publicly available 
datasets demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art perfor-
mance, surpassing all baseline methods. 
Keywords: Recommendation Systems · Sequential Recommendation · 
Graph Neural Network 
1
Introduction 
With the rapid proliferation of the internet, Recommendation Systems (RSs) 
have become pervasive, implemented across various sectors including e-
commerce, media, and education, and have been a focal point of academic inquiry 
for numerous years [ 1]. The primary objective of recommendation systems is to 
c ICST Institute for Computer Sciences, Social Informatics and Telecommunications Engineering 2026 
Published by Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026. All Rights Reserved 
H. Gao and X. Wang (Eds.): CollaborateCom 2024, LNICST 626, pp. 62–82, 2026. 
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-93257-1_4

![Página PDF 72, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-72.png)


<!-- PDF page 73 -->

## Página PDF 73

DGSR: Dual-Graph Sequential Recommendation with GNNs
63
predict users’ future interactions and provide recommendations based on user 
proﬁles, item attributes, and the users’ historical interactions with items. How-
ever, such comprehensive information is not always accessible in many scenarios. 
As a result, Sequential Recommendation (SR) emerges as a signiﬁcant area of 
research. Sequential Recommendation treats the historical records of user-item 
interactions as data sequences, striving to understand and model the sequential 
patterns of user behavior and the dynamic shifts in user preferences throughout 
their interactions. They aim to ﬁnd the next item that users are most likely 
to interact with [ 2]. Recent literature has extensively explored sequential rec-
ommendation, yielding substantial advancements. Methods based on Recurrent 
Neural Networks (RNNs) and Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) have been 
a predominant approach to sequential recommendation in recent years. [ 3– 5]. 
The remarkable success of Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) across diverse tasks 
has resulted in considerable exploration and implementation of Sequential Rec-
ommendation approaches employing GNNs. These approaches typically estab-
lish sequence graphs of user-item interactions, with each item in the interaction 
represented as a node, thereby creating a directed graph for every interaction 
sequence. GNNs (mainly GGNN-based structures [ 6]) are employed on a con-
structed directed graph to comprehend the intricate connections between sug-
gested items and their corresponding sequential contexts. GNNs are conducive 
to capturing complex relations within graph data, leading to better outcomes 
and becoming increasingly prevalent in recent research [ 7, 8]. 
Nevertheless, several challenges are inherent in these contemporary studies. 
These challenges primarily arise from the distinct characteristics of sequence 
data, such as sequence sparsity, sequence noise, and the generally short length 
of sequences. The ﬁrst challenge is the diﬃculty in managing uncor-
related or noisy interactions within sequences. Recurrent Neural Net-
works (RNNs) and Gated Graph Neural Networks (GGNNs) typically rely on 
Gated Recurrent Units (GRUs), which presuppose that adjacent items within 
a sequence are interdependent [ 9]. However, in practical applications, interac-
tion data frequently exhibit noise, and consecutive items may often lack depen-
dencies. The second challenge lies in extracting high-order semantic 
distinctions of items for diverse users from complex heterogeneous 
information, compounded by the generally short length of sequences. 
Existing studies have demonstrated that in most scenarios, user sequences are 
typically short, containing insuﬃcient knowledge to adequately capture users’ 
dynamic preferences [ 6, 10]. Several research eﬀorts have attempted to construct 
global graphs incorporating user nodes to elucidate higher-order associations 
between user interests and behaviors [ 11]. However, this approach falls short in 
eﬀectively distinguishing the semantic relationships among diﬀerent users, their 
interactions, and associated items. This limitation poses signiﬁcant challenges 
for graph neural networks, impeding their convergence during training and hin-
dering their capacity to capture more intricate associations related to user inter-
ests. The third challenge lies in the high computational complexity of 
most GNN-based models, which signiﬁcantly limits their applicabil-
ity in real-world recommendation scenarios. Although GNN-based models 
have demonstrated potential in sequential recommendation, they also result in

[Vista original de la página 73](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-73.png)

![Página PDF 73, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-73.png)


<!-- PDF page 74 -->

## Página PDF 74

64
B. Li et al.
heightened network complexity and reduced recommendation eﬃciency, thereby 
failing to satisfy the response requirements in practical recommendation 
scenarios. 
To address the aforementioned challenges, we propose a Dual-Graph Sequen-
tial Recommendation (DGSR) approach, incorporating gated and heterogeneous 
Graph Neural Networks (GNNs). Speciﬁcally addressing the ﬁrst challenge, we 
construct individual interaction graphs for each user and employ a last-item aug-
mented Gated Graph Neural Network (GGNN) to extract latent user interests. 
This method yields item representations that accurately reﬂect the current user’s 
preferences. Previous research has established that the ﬁnal item in an interac-
tion sequence most eﬀectively reﬂects the user’s current interest and is likely 
to have the highest correlation with the subsequent interaction [ 12]. Therefore, 
unlike the vanilla GGNN used in most work, we explicitly incorporate the last 
item in the interaction when the GGNN aggregates neighborhood information. 
This approach enhances the semantic representation of the user’s latest interest 
within the item embeddings. By augmenting the GGNN with the last item, we 
mitigate the inﬂuence of unrelated or noisy interactions in the sequence, thereby 
more eﬀectively capturing the user’s current latent interest. To address the sec-
ond and third challenges, we construct a heterogeneous global interaction graph 
and incorporate users’ identity information as an attribute of the interaction. 
This approach forms a heterogeneous graph that captures high-order distinctions 
of items for users without the need to introduce additional user nodes, mitigat-
ing the issue of insuﬃcient representation of users’ interests due to typically 
short user interaction sequences. Furthermore, we propose a parameter-eﬃcient 
heterogeneous Graph Neural Network (GNN) to learn the heterogeneous global 
interaction graph. Our proposed approach eﬀectively captures high-order user-
user similarities and latent item-item correlations, while also enhancing training 
convergence, model inference, and overall computational eﬃciency. Our main 
contributions are as follows: 
– We address the challenge of noise and uncorrelation in user interaction 
sequences by constructing individual interaction graphs and introducing a 
last-item augmented GGNN, emphasizing users’ most recent interests. It 
eﬀectively captures rapid shifts in user preferences. Empirical results indi-
cate that the enhanced GGNN surpasses the original model in performance. 
– We address the challenge of limited dynamic preference knowledge from short 
user interaction sequences by constructing a global interaction graph that 
incorporates user IDs as attributes, forming a heterogeneous graph. Further-
more, to address the challenge of high computational complexity of most 
GNN-based models, this graph is learned using a parameter-eﬃcient hetero-
geneous GNN, which not only eﬀectively captures complex graph information 
and high-order item interest distinctions but also signiﬁcantly reduces the 
computational complexity typically associated with GNN-based models. 
– We rigorously evaluate our approach using four publicly available datasets 
and compare it against 10 baseline methods. Extensive experimental results 
demonstrate that DGSR consistently outperforms other state-of-the-art

methods, showcasing its eﬀectiveness and superiority in capturing dynamic 
user preferences and interests. 
2
Related Work 
2.1
Sequential Recommendation 
Recommendation systems are essential for helping users identify their preferences 
from extensive items. The ﬁeld of sequential recommendation (SR) has garnered 
signiﬁcant academic attention in recent years. This approach focuses on predict-
ing users’ future interactions by analyzing their historical interaction sequences. 
Early work typically applies Markov chain-based approaches to model potential 
patterns of user-item interactions in a sequence. Some work computes transition 
probabilities directly, and others calculate the embeddings of the Markov chain 
and then compute transition probabilities based on the vector distances between 
interactions [ 9, 13]. With the rapid development of deep learning, RNN-based 
sequential recommendation have been developed based on Markov chains. These 
approaches typically employ GRU units or LSTMs to model user interaction 
behavior [ 14, 15]. CNN-based methods are also common in sequential recommen-
dation. User interaction sequences are embedded in a matrix. They use convolu-
tional ﬁlters to learn sequential patterns as local features of the “image” [ 5, 16]. 
Recently, with the rise of Transformers in various ﬁelds, much work uses self-
attention mechanism to model user interaction sequences in sequential recom-
mendation. Such methods generally use the encoder of Transformers, including 
self-attention and FFN, etc., to model interaction information, form embedding 
vectors in Euclidean space, and compute recommendation scores by inner prod-
uct [ 17– 19]. Nonetheless, these methodologies frequently encounter diﬃculties in 
eﬀectively modeling noisy or sparsely dependent sequential data. Furthermore, 
they often fail to consider the potential variations in how the same item may 
inﬂuence the interests of diﬀerent users. 
2.2
GNN-Based Recommendation 
In recent years, Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have garnered extensive 
research attention and have been applied across numerous ﬁelds due to their eﬃ-
cacy in extracting rich semantic information from structured data and modeling 
higher-order associations [ 20, 21]. This surge in interest has naturally extended 
to the domain of sequential recommendation systems utilizing GNNs. Within the 
context of recommendation systems, the majority of GNN-based methodologies 
transform data into graph structures and employ message-passing mechanisms 
to extract insights from these graph structures. Central to these methodologies is 
the design of message aggregation functions and representation update functions, 
which are pivotal in optimizing the performance of GNNs for recommendation 
tasks [ 22]. Several studies have incorporated attention mechanisms into GNNs, 
resulting in Graph Attention Networks (GATs) based recommendation models.

[Vista original de la página 75](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-75.png)

## Página PDF 76

66
B. Li et al.
These models utilize attention mechanisms to jointly learn the inﬂuence seman-
tics of diﬀerent items on the current user interaction interests [ 23, 24]. Given their 
strength in modeling beyond-pairwise relations, hypergraph-based GNNs have 
been widely adopted in recommendation systems to capture higher-order rela-
tionships between items and cross-user information [ 6, 25]. However, it is crucial 
to acknowledge that items may possess distinct semantic meanings for diﬀer-
ent users within interactions, and eﬀectively capturing these nuances is vital for 
accurate recommendations. Existing methods frequently overlook the diﬀeren-
tiation between higher-order associations and the semantic disparities of items 
across users. Additionally, these approaches often suﬀer from high computational 
complexity, posing challenges to their practical deployment. 
3
Preliminaries 
3.1

tains the corresponding interaction sequences for each user, where each sequence

Based on the above deﬁnition, the problem of sequential recommendation 
can be formulated as follows: 
Input: User’s historical interaction sequence. 
Output: A model that learns from the user’s interaction history and recom-
mends the next item that the user is most likely to be interested in. 
3.2
Individual Interaction Graph \mathcal{G}_sGs
Like most GNN-based sequential recommendation methods [ 12, 26], we construct 
individual interaction graphs \mathcal{G}_s = {\mathcal{V}_s, \mathcal{E}_s\, \mathcal{A}_s}Gs = {Vs, Es As} for each user to capture the 
potential interest reﬂected by each interaction sequence. We construct directed 
graph for each sequence by representing each item in the sequence as a node 
and introducing directed edges between consecutively clicked items. We denote

67
\mathcal{V}_s = {v_1, v_2, \dots, v_{|\mathcal{V}_s|}}Vs = {v1, v2, . . . , v|Vs|} as the set of nodes, corresponding to the set of all 
items for that user interaction. \mathcal{A}_sAs denotes the adjacency matrix of this graph. 
\mathcal{E}_s \subseteq \mathcal{V}_s \times \mathcal{V}_sEs ⊆Vs × Vs is the set of edges, corresponding to the transitions of user inter-
actions. If there is an edge (v_i, v_j) \in \mathcal{E}_s(vi, vj) ∈Es from node v_ivi to node v_jvj, it denotes that 
the interaction sequence history of this user has a record of the next interaction 
item v_jvj after interacted with v_ivi. 
3.3
Heterogeneous Global Interaction Graph \mathcal{G}_gGg
The individual sequence graphs constructed in the preceding section, while con-
ceptually straightforward, encounter substantial limitations in practical applica-
tions. Existing studies have highlighted that user sequences in many real-world 
scenarios tend to be relatively short. For example, the average sequence length 
in the preprocessed Yoochoose1/4^11 dataset is merely 5.71 [ 6, 10]. Consequently, 
graphs derived from such brief sequences exhibit a sparse structure, characterized 
by a limited number of nodes and edges. This sparsity often results in nodes with 
only a single neighbor, thereby failing to encapsulate the complexity necessary 
to accurately represent the dynamic nature of users’ preferences. 
The paucity of information inherent in these sparse graphs signiﬁcantly ham-
pers the eﬀectiveness of GNNs. GNNs are predicated on the ability to learn rich 
node representations by aggregating information from neighboring nodes. How-
ever, in the context of sparse graphs with few interconnected nodes, the capacity 
of GNNs to discern intricate patterns and dependencies is markedly diminished. 
This limitation impedes the GNNs’ potential to fully exploit the data for learning 
and generalizing user preferences. 
Thus, inspired by [ 11], in addition to interaction graphs for individual users, 
we construct a heterogeneous global interaction graph \mathcal{G}_g = {\mathcal{V}_g,\mathcal{E}_g,\mathcal{A}_g}Gg = {Vg, Eg, Ag} to 
learn global item transition patterns and semantic associations between user 
interests, shown in Fig. 1. Similar to the individual interaction graphs, \mathcal{V}_g = {v_1, v_2, \dots, v_{|\mathcal{V}_g|}}Vg =
{v1, v2, . . . , v|Vg|} denotes the set of items that all users have interacted with. 
Unlike previous work mentioned above, our approach incorporates user identity 
as an attribute of each interaction edge. \mathcal{E}_g \subseteq \mathcal{V}_g \times \mathcal{V}_g \times \mathcal{U}Eg ⊆Vg × Vg × U denotes the set of 
edges corresponding to transitions between adjacent interactions of all users. If 
there is an edge e_k = (v_i, v_j) \in \mathcal{E}_gek = (vi, vj) ∈Eg from node v_ivi to node v_jvj, it denotes that 
the interaction sequence of u_kuk has a record where the next interaction after 
interacting with item v_ivi is v_jvj. Incorporating user identities into the global graph 
results in the formation of a heterogeneous graph, which inherently facilitates the 
construction of associations among diverse user-item interactions. This advanced 
structure eﬀectively addresses the common issue of insuﬃcient preference knowl-
edge due to typically short user sequences in practical scenarios. By enabling a 
comprehensive representation of interactions, the heterogeneous graph enhances 
the capability to discern higher-order associations and allows for a more nuanced 
diﬀerentiation of item semantics across varying user interests. 
\mathcal{A}_gAg denotes the adjacency matrix of this graph. Previous research has dis-
cussed that traditional sequence graph construction methods lose some of the

mation by calculating edge weights based on the position of the corresponding

items that come later in the sequence reﬂect the user’s latest interest, and the 
last few items together have greater impact on the next interaction. Under this

weights of the last few items are maximum and close together. Given an interac-

4
Methodology 
In this section, we present our proposed DGSR framework, as shown in Fig. 2. 
It consists of 3 main parts: latent interest capture based on last-item augmented 
GGNN, high-order interest distinction mining based on parameter-eﬃcient het-
erogeneous GNN, and intra-inter interest fusion based on transform mechanism. 
4.1
Last-Item Augmented GGNN 
In this section, we delineate the methodology employed to discern latent user 
interests within each sequential interaction graph \mathcal{G}_sGs as elaborated in Sect. 3.2, 
utilizing a last-item augmented Gated Graph Neural Network (GGNN). By 
constructing individual interaction graphs, we encapsulate the transitions and 
interrelations inherent within the current sequence. The GGNN leverages Gated 
Recurrent Units (GRUs) to iteratively reﬁne node representations within the

[Vista original de la página 78](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-78.png)

graph. These reﬁned representations encapsulate the latent interests of users as 
they engage with various items. 
Vanilla GGNNs frequently encounter diﬃculties in capturing the intricate 
interests of users from sequences characterized by sparse dependencies or noisy 
interaction data. In the context of user interactions, it is logical to posit that 
items appearing later in the sequence are more indicative of the user’s most 
recent interests, which should consequently hold greater signiﬁcance in the rec-
ommendation process. This intuitive idea has also been proposed by [ 12]. In this 
context, our objective is to mitigate issues related to sparse dependencies and 
noisy interaction data by placing an emphasis on the last item in the sequence. 
We propose an augmentation to the message-passing mechanism of the GGNN, 
wherein the node corresponding to the last item in the sequence is designated 
as a virtual star node v^*v∗. This enables us to integrate information from the 
virtual star node into the computation of representations for each node within 
the GGNN, resulting in a unidirectional ﬂow of information. This modiﬁcation 
enhances the semantic representation of the ﬁnal item, thereby mitigating the 
eﬀects of sparse dependencies and extraneous noise on the modeling of user inter-
ests. For a node v_i \in \mathcal{G}_svi ∈Gs, our proposed last-item augmented GGNN updates its 
representation \textbf{h}_ihi as follows:

4.2
Parameter-Eﬃcient Heterogeneous GNN 
This section introduces our proposed heterogeneous GNN, designed to uncover 
high-order semantic distinctions of items for diﬀerent users from the hetero-
geneous global interaction graph constructed in Sect. 3.3. Traditional heteroge-
neous graph GNNs can be described as follows: 
\bALT{} \textbf{m}_{\mathcal{N}_i}^{(l)} = \sum_{j \in \mathcal{N}_i} \alpha_{ij}^{(l)} \phi^{(l)}\bigg(\textbf{h}_j^{(l)}, \textbf{r}_{ij}^{(l)}\bigg) , \textbf{h}_{i}^{(l+1)} = \sigma\bigg(\textbf{m}_{\mathcal{N}_i}^{(l)} + \bm{W}^{(l+1)}\textbf{h}_i^{(l)}\bigg)\eALT{} m(l)

It is evident that, due to the signiﬁcantly larger scale of the heterogeneous 
global interaction graph compared to individual interaction graphs, the learning 
process typically necessitates the estimation of a substantially greater number of

Projection Operation as a substitute for the parameter matrices commonly used

4.3
Intra-inter Interest Fusion with Transform 
In the preceding sections, we have elucidated the extraction of intra-sequence 
user latent interests from the sequential graph, which encapsulates the temporal 
dynamics of potential user interests. Furthermore, we have derived high-order 
inter-user interest semantic distinctions from the heterogeneous global interac-
tion graph. This global graph encapsulates a broader and more intricate context 
of interactions, thereby providing a comprehensive framework for understanding 
the nuanced and multifaceted nature of user preferences. 
For an item node v_ivi, the representation vector obtained from the sequential 
graph for this node is denoted as \textbf{h}_{i}^{s}hs
i, while the representation vector sourced 
from the heterogeneous global interaction graph is denoted as \textbf{h}_{i}^{g}hg
i . For a partic-
ular user u_kuk, the representation vector acquired from the heterogeneous global 
interaction graph is denoted as \mathbf{r}_krk. To compute the recommendation score \hat{\mathcal{S}_{k}^i} ˆSi
k of 
item v_ivi for user u_kuk as the next interaction, we employ a transform mechanism 
that integrates both the intra-user representations \textbf{h}_{i}^{s}hs
i and inter-user interest 
representations \textbf{h}_{i}^{g}hg
i . Since the two embedding vectors of a item are learnt from 
diﬀerent graphs, \textbf{h}_{i}^{s}hs
i and \textbf{h}_{i}^{g}hg
i are projected by \bm{W}_1W1 into the same vector space. 
Thus, the recommendation score is calculated as follows: 
\bALT{} \hat{\mathcal{S}_{k}^i} = \sigma\bigg(\bm{W}_1 \cdot [\textbf{h}_{i}^{s} || \textbf{h}_{i}^{g}] + \bm{W}_{2} \cdot \textbf{r}_{k}\bigg)\eALT{} ˆSi
k = σ W1 · [hs
i||hg
i ] + W2 · rk
(9) 
where \bm{W}_1, \bm{W}_2W1, W2 are learnable parameter matrices. We use them to transform 
embedding vectors into scores. 
4.4
Model Optimization 
We predict the next item v_{k, i+1}^+v+
k,i+1 that user u_kuk is likely to interact with, given their 
historical interaction sequence s_ksk up to the ii-th interaction. To achieve this, we 
compute recommendation scores for potential candidate items for the (i+1)(i + 1)-th 
interaction, denoted as \hat{\mathcal{S}_{k,i+1}}
ˆ
Sk,i+1. We deﬁne a loss function inspired by the Bayesian 
Personalized Ranking (BPR) Loss. Speciﬁcally, for user u_kuk, we randomly select 
a non-interacted item v_{k, i+1}^-v−
k,i+1 to contrast with the actually interacted item v_{k, i+1}^+v+
k,i+1. 
The loss function is designed to maximize the margin between the recommen-
dation score of the interacted item and the score of the non-interacted item. 
Formally, our BPR-based loss is given by: 
\bALT{} \mathcal{L} = \Bigg[\sum_{(v_{k, i+1}^+, v_{k, i+1}^-) \in \mathcal{O}}-\ln\sigma(\hat{\mathcal{S}_{k,i+1}^{+}} - \hat{\mathcal{S}_{k,i+1}^{-}})\Bigg] + ||\lambda_{reg}||^2\eALT{} L =

5
Experiments 
In this section, we introduce our experimental setup and report the empirical 
ﬁndings of our study. Our experimentation primarily aims to investigate the 
following Research Questions (RQs): 
– RQ1: Can our proposed DGSR achieve better performance in the task of 
sequential recommendation, surpassing the best baseline methods? 
– RQ2: What is the contribution of our Last-item Augmented GGNN in com-
parison to the vanilla GGNN in enhancing the results? 
– RQ3: How signiﬁcant is the contribution of our global interaction graph learn-
ing with Parameter-eﬃcient Heterogeneous GNN to the overall results? 
– RQ4: In terms of time eﬃciency, what is the advantage of our proposed 
DGSR over other GNN-based sequence recommendation baseline methods? 
– RQ5: How does diﬀerent weighting hyper-parameter $\tau$τ, which modulates the 
impact of transition positions on edge weight disparities in the global graph, 
inﬂuence the overall performance? 
For RQ1 and RQ4, we compare our proposed method against state-of-the-
art baseline methods across four publicly available datasets. For RQ2 and RQ3, 
we conduct ablation studies to dissect and quantify the contributions of the 
respective components to the experimental outcomes. 
5.1
Datasets 
We performed experiments on four publicly available datasets: Movielens-1M, 
Yelp2022, DIGINETICA and Amazon-Books 2018 [ 29]. These datasets are com-
monly used for sequential recommendation, which makes the comparisons per-
formed on them comprehensive and meaningful. The statistics for these datasets 
are shown in Table 1. It should be noted that the processed version by RecBole 
[ 30, 31] is used for all of our experimental datasets, which is publicly available. 
In alignment with established methodologies, we undertake a pre-processing 
step to ensure the datasets’ suitability for sequential recommendation tasks. 
Speciﬁcally, interactions with ratings below 3 are excised from the Movielens-
1M and Amazon-Books 2018 datasets. Furthermore, we prune inactive users 
and unpopular items across all datasets; this involves removing users and items 
with fewer than 10 interactions. Dataset sparsity, represented in a tabular form, 
is deﬁned as the proportion of unrated elements within the user-item matrix 
relative to the total matrix space. Formally, given UU users, II items, and RR inter-
actions, the sparsity of the dataset is computed as 1 - \frac{R}{UI}1 −R
UI .

5.2
Performance Comparison (RQ1 & RQ4) 
Baselines. We undertake a comprehensive comparative experiment to evaluate 
the performance of DGSR across various datasets, juxtaposing it against ten 
state-of-the-art baseline methodologies. These baselines are categorized into two 
distinct groups: GNN-based sequential recommendation methods and non-GNN 
sequential recommendation methods. 
GNN-based sequential recommendation baselines are: 
– SR-GNN [ 10]: It constructs a graph for each sequence, leveraging interaction 
relations to learn embeddings by GNN and achieves promising results. 
– GCE-GNN [ 11]: It constructs individual graphs and global graph, using 
attention mechanism to eﬀectively extract information in the graphs. 
– GC-SAN [ 12]: It employs multi-layer self-attention to solve the problem 
of capturing long-term interest, and combines the long-term and short-term 
interest with the gating mechanism, represented by the last item. 
– SGNN-HN [ 24]: It introduces a virtual star node, connecting all items, and 
each node can get information from the star node about other items, even if 
they are not directly connected, achieving excellent performance. 
– LESSR [ 27]: It constructs two graphs for a sequence, one that distinguishes 
the order of neighbors, and one that allows a short-cut path from one element 
to all others, solving the problem of information loss in previous approaches. 
– NISER [ 23]: It adds positional embeddings to obtain position-aware item 
representations, balancing consecutive time and ﬂexible transition patterns. 
Non-GNN sequential recommendation baselines are: 
– CORE [ 17]: It uniﬁes the representation space of encoding and decoding by 
using representation-consistent encoders that embed linear combinations of 
input element embeddings as sequences, achieving promising results. 
– LightSANs [ 32]: It maps users’ historical behavioral sequences to potential 
interests, which alleviates over-parameterization by “limiting” the length of a 
user’s historical behavioral sequences in linear time and space. 
– SINE [ 33]: It develops an interest aggregation module to predict the user’s 
current intention, explicitly and eﬀectively models multiple interests. 
– FEARec [ 34]: It presents a frequency-enhanced hybrid attention network 
that captures periodic features and fuses time-level and frequency-level atten-
tion in a joint model, achieving promising performance.
