Experiment Settings. This experiment is conducted on a workstation with 
one NVIDIA A40 GPU and the Intel Xeon Platinum 8358P CPU. In DGSR, we 
set the learning rate to 0.003 and \tauτ to 0.8 for the MovieLens-1M dataset. For 
the Yelp 2022 dataset, we set the learning rate to 0.01 and \tauτ to 1.5. For the 
DIGINETICIA dataset, we set the learning rate to 0.005 and \tauτ to 0.8. For the 
Amazon-Books 2018 dataset, we set the learning rate to 0.01 and \tauτ to 1.5. The 
coeﬃcients in L2 regularization term \lambda_{reg}λreg are all set to 0.0001. 
To ensure fairness in comparison, the batch sizes are all set to 4096, and 
all embedding dimensions of the vectors are set to 64. For all baselines and 
each dataset, we conduct grid searches for parameter selections. In addition, we 
adopt the leave-one-out strategy in the experiment and use the three metrics: Hit 
Ratio (Hits@K), Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR@K), and Normalized Discounted 
Cumulative Gain (NDCG@K) which are common in sequential recommendation. 
Overall Performance Comparison. The experiment results for overall per-
formance are reported in Table 2 and Table 3, where the best results for the met-
rics are bolded. The results that demonstrated the second-best performance 
are underlined. Table 2 presents the comparative performance results of DGSR 
against GNN-based baseline methods, whereas Table 3 delineates the perfor-
mance comparison of DGSR with non-GNN baseline methods. FEARec cannot 
run on the DIGINETICA dataset due to insuﬃcient system memory. Train T and 
Val T in Table 2 denotes the average training time and inference time for each 
epoch with the parameter settings above, respectively. Given that our method-
ology principally targets the reduction of high time complexity associated with 
GNN-based methods, we conﬁned our comparative analysis to training and infer-
ence time metrics against GNN-based baselines. 
By observing the results, we can come to the following conclusions: 
– DGSR achieves state-of-the-art overall performance under all met-
rics. The experimental results indicate that DGSR consistently outperforms 
all baseline models across every dataset and most evaluation metrics. Par-
ticularly on the Yelp2022 dataset, DGSR demonstrates the most pronounced 
performance enhancements. Speciﬁcally, DGSR shows improvements over the 
best baseline for each metric as follows: Hits@10/50 by 10.81% and 8.61%, 
MRR@10/50 by 14.81% and 18.18%, and NDCG@10/50 by 15.79% and 
16.42%. The superior performance of DGSR can be attributed to two pri-
mary factors: Firstly, DGSR mitigates the adverse eﬀects of sparse or noisy 
data by accurately capturing users’ most recent latent interests as manifested 
in intra-sequence interactions. Secondly, DGSR adeptly captures higher-order 
interest distinctions among diﬀerent items at the inter-sequence level, thereby 
uncovering interests that are more closely aligned with individual user pref-
erences. 
– GNN-based methods outperform non-GNN methods mostly. The 
empirical results indicate that GNN-based methodologies (e.g., GCE-GNN, 
LESSR, NISER) consistently surpass non-GNN approaches (e.g., CORE, 
SINE) across all datasets. Notably, although LightSANs exhibited the most

5.3
Ablation Studies (RQ2 & RQ3) 
In this section, we conduct ablation studies to investigate RQ2 and RQ3. We 
perform ablation studies on the Movielens-1M dataset in the same workstation 
environment as in Sect. 5.2, adopting the same strategy. We compare the per-
formance of DGSR, DGSR with the last-item augmentation strategy removed 
(DGSR w/o augment), and DGSR with the individual interaction graph-based 
interest extraction removed (DGSR w/o \mathcal{G}_sGs), as shown in Table 4. 
Table 4. Ablation Studies Results on Movielens-1M.

The results provide insightful evidence regarding the eﬀectiveness of our 
proposed last-item augmented GGNN. We can observe that this augmenta-
tion has an obvious impact on the performance of the model. When this aug-
mentation is removed, a decrease in performance metrics is observed, par-
ticularly for Hits@10(-−2.83%), MRR@10(-−4.82%), and NDCG@10(-−5.13%), 
in contrast to the relatively milder decrease observed for Hits@50(-−0.94%), 
MRR@50(-−0.27%), and NDCG@50(-−1.03%). This diﬀerential impact under-
scores the importance of the last item in improving model accuracy at a nar-
rower scale (top 10 recommendations), which is often more relevant in practical 
applications where the immediate top recommendations are of greater interest 
to users. 
Furthermore, compared to many other baseline methods, our method main-
tains excellent performance even without the individual interaction graph-based 
user potential interest extraction module. For example, comparing with SR-
GNN, DGSR w/o \mathcal{G}_sGs improves 3.12%, 3.52% on Hits@10/50, 8.24%, 10.01% 
on MRR@10/50, and 3.68%, 4.96% on NDCG@10/50. This demonstrates the 
eﬀectiveness of our proposed heterogeneous GNN. It can be attributed that 
the parameter-eﬃcient GNN accurately models higher-order interest associa-
tions among users and diﬀerentiates the high-order semantic dissimilarities of 
items for each user. However, it is important to note that removing this mod-
ule signiﬁcantly decreases the experimental results. The largest decrease is seen 
in NDCG@10, which is reduced by 20.04%. This result further emphasizes the 
importance of the individual interaction graph-based module in our approach.

5.4
Weighting Parameter Sensitivity (RQ5) 
In this section, we examine the eﬀects of the weighting parameter \tauτ, which 
modulates the impact of transition positions on edge weight disparities in the 
global graph. Throughout this investigation, all other hyperparameters are held 
the settings described in Sect. 5.2. We compared the eﬀect of diﬀerent \tauτ values on 
NDCG@10 indicator values on each of the four datasets. The results are shown 
in Fig. 3. 
Fig. 3. Analysis of Diﬀerent Weighting Parameter \tauτ on NDCG@10. 
The experimental data demonstrates that as the parameter $\tau$τ increases, 
NDCG@10 initially improves and then subsequently decreases. Initially, increas-
ing $\tau$τ allows the model to better discriminate between more and less signiﬁ-
cant transitions. By emphasizing transitions that occur at the extremities of 
the sequence (either very early or very late), the model can capture underly-
ing temporal dynamics that are predictive of user behavior, thereby enhancing 
performance. However, as $\tau$τ continues to increase, the weighting function may 
become excessively sensitive to the position of the transitions. This heightened 
sensitivity can lead to an overemphasis on the chronological order, potentially 
at the expense of the intrinsic importance of the transitions themselves. Conse-
quently, this could result in diminishing returns, as critical transitions that occur 
near the midpoint of the sequence may be undervalued.

## Página PDF 89

DGSR: Dual-Graph Sequential Recommendation with GNNs
79
6
Conclusion 
In this paper, we present a novel sequential recommendation framework named 
DGSR. DGSR constructs individual interaction graphs and employs a last-item 
augmented variant of the Gated Graph Neural Network (GGNN) to address the 
challenges posed by sparsely dependent or noisy sequence data, thereby eﬀec-
tively capturing the latent interests of users. Additionally, DGSR constructs a 
heterogeneous global interaction graph, which mitigates the issue of typically 
short user interaction sequences in real-world scenarios by integrating user iden-
tities as edge attributes. We propose a parameter-eﬃcient heterogeneous GNN 
to discern high-order semantic distinctions of items for diﬀerent users, while also 
optimizing time complexity. Furthermore, DGSR utilizes a transformation mech-
anism to integrate intra-user and inter-user interests, thereby deriving item rec-
ommendation scores. We benchmarked DGSR against 10 state-of-the-art base-
line methods across four publicly available datasets, consistently achieving supe-
rior performance. Comprehensive ablation studies were conducted to elucidate 
the contribution of each component to the overall performance.

## Página PDF 93

Disentanglement-Enhanced User 
Representation via Domain-Level Clusters 
for Cross-Domain Recommendation 
Wenhao Xiong1 
, Lei Sang1 
, Wei Li1(B) 
, Shun Lian1,2 
, 
and Yiwen Zhang1 
1 School of Computer Science and Technology, Anhui University, Hefei 230601, 
Anhui, China 
xwh@stu.ahu.edu.cn, {sanglei,zhangyiwen}@ahu.edu.cn 
2 State Key Laboratory of Cognitive Intelligence, Hefei 230088, Anhui, China 
lianshun@ustc.edu 
Abstract. Cross-domain recommendation (CDR) leverages source 
domain information to enhance target domain recommendations. 
Recently, disentanglement techniques are utilized to separate domain-
shared information from domain-speciﬁc information in CDR, reduc-
ing negative migration. However, previous disentanglement works only 
consider individual interaction preference and ignore collective prefer-
ence. It fails to capture comprehensive user preferences. To address this, 
we propose Disentanglement-enhanced user representation via Domain-
level Clusters for Cross-Domain Recommendation (DDC-CDR), a 
novel model integrates individual and collective preference distributions, 
using domain-level cluster preference distributions for collective prefer-
ence distributions. To achieve our goal, we design two modules: (1) A 
domain classiﬁer disentangles user representations into domain-shared 
and domain-speciﬁc components by information theory. (2) An enhancer 
promotes the adoption of domain-shared user representations across 
domains and utilizes hierarchical approximation to capture and align the 
domain-level cluster preferences of the source and target domains, ensur-
ing consistency of domain-level cluster shared information during the 
cross-domain transfers. Based on them, we generate enhanced user rep-
resentations containing comprehensive user preferences. Extensive exper-
imental results on four real-world datasets demonstrate the signiﬁcant 
superiority of DDC-CDR over state-of-the-art baselines. 
Keywords: Cross-Domain Recommendation · Disentangled 
Representation Learning · Comprehensive User Preference · 
Domain-level Cluster Preference 
1
Introduction 
Recommender systems (RS) have found successful applications in numerous real-
world platforms like Amazon [ 2]. Predominantly, current RS models [ 10,11,27],

Fig. 1. (a): A simple example of user preferences in movie domain and music domain.

following a collaborative ﬁltering (CF) paradigm [ 11], learn user and item repre-
sentations to make recommendations. However, the eﬀectiveness of these models 
is hindered by the data sparsity of user-item interactions [ 10]. 
To address the issue of data sparsity, Cross-Domain Recommendation (CDR) 
[ 20] becomes a promising way. It utilizes enriched information from source 
domain to enhance the recommendation performance of the target domain [ 41]. 
Furthermore, dual-target CDR is proposed, aiming to simultaneously improve 
the recommendation performance of two domains by sharing cross-domain com-
mon knowledge [ 32,43]. For example, CoNet [ 12] utilizes a cross-connected 
network to facilitate knowledge transfer between two domains. BiTGCF [ 19] 
uses LightGCN [ 10] as an encoder to aggregate interaction information from 
each domain and introduces a feature transfer layer to enhance the two base 
graph encoders. DisenCDR [ 2] disentangles information into domain-shared and 
domain-speciﬁc information to prevent negative migration, which utilizew varia-
tional techniques and information bottlenecks. CDRIB [ 3] derives unbiased repre-
sentations by utilizing the information bottleneck principle, which forces coding 
domain-shared information representations. DIDA-CDR [ 44], disentangles more 
comprehensive user preferences, domain-shared, domain-speciﬁc and domain-
independent information, further reduces negative migration information.

DDC-CDR
85
In Fig. 1(a), ${`}$‘Category’ and ${`}$‘Theme’ are used in both the ﬁlm and music 
domains called domain-shared information. But ${`}$‘Melody’ is useful only in music 
domain and irrelevant ﬁlm domain called domain-speciﬁc information. As shown 
in Fig. 1(b), previous disentanglement models [ 2, 3,44] primarily disentangle 
domain information through individual interactions. The domain-shared infor-
mation only contains ${`}$‘Category’ and ${`}$‘Theme’ by individual interactions. How-
ever, users encompass both individual preference and collective preference distri-
butions [ 5,21]. Previous disentanglement methods focus on individual preference 
distributions and overlook collective preference distributions, failing to capture 
comprehensive user preferences. We incorporate domain-level cluster preference 
distributions as collective preference distributions. In Fig. 1(c), when User-A is 
clustered with similar users, all users in the cluster share a preference for low-
priced items except User-A. Based on this, User-A adopts this domain-level 
cluster shared preference because they are similar in the cluster. The prefer-
ence for low-priced items serves as cross-domain shared information that can 
be transferred to both the Film domain and Music domain to enhance recom-
mendation performance. This emphasizes the signiﬁcance of domain-level cluster 
preferences, like the domain-level cluster shared preference for low-priced items, 
which cannot be captured by individual preference distributions. 
To disentangle enhanced user representations containing individual inter-
action preference and domain-level cluster preference distributions, we pro-
pose Disentanglement-enhanced user representation via Domain-level Clusters 
for Cross-Domain Recommendation, which introduces a variational clustering 
graph encoder to captures structurally homogeneous user information extracts 
more user preferences, and generates domain-level clusters, along with a domain 
classiﬁer and an enhancer. The domain classiﬁer is designed to disentangle 
domain information into domain-shared and domain-speciﬁc information by 
information theory [ 2]. The enhancer promotes the adoption of domain-shared 
user representations across domains to capture information comprehensively. It 
then utilizes hierarchical approximation [ 5] to capture and align the domain-
level cluster preferences of the source and target domains, ensuring consistency 
of domain-level clusters shared information during cross-domain transfers. Based 
on DDC-CDR, we disentangle comprehensive preference user representations. 
Our contributions are summarized as follows: 
– We introduce the domain-level cluster preference distributions to disen-
tangle comprehensive user representations, extending prior disentanglement 
approaches that relied solely on individual interaction preference distributions. 
– We propose a novel model, DDC-CDR, which independently disentangles 
domain-shared and domain-speciﬁc information. By enriching domain-shared 
information through aligning the domain-level cluster preference distributions 
of the two domains, DDC-CDR captures more cross-domain invariant infor-
mation and comprehensive user preferences. 
– We conduct experiments on four real-world datasets to evaluate the model’s 
performance. Extensive results demonstrate DDC-CDR achieves consistent 
improvements compared to state-of-the-art baselines.

86
W. Xiong et al.
2
Related Work 
In this section, we ﬁrst provide a basic introduction to the two main types 
of cross-domain recommendation in Sect. 2.1. Following that, in Sect. 2.2, we 
introduce disentangled representation learning. 
2.1
Cross Domain Recommendation 
Single-Target CDR. Single-target CDR is currently a predominant recommen-
dation scenario, enhances the recommendation performance of a sparse target 
domain by leveraging abundant source domain information. It primarily divided 
into three sub-scenarios, i.e., (1) content-based transfer [ 38], (2) embedding-
based transfer [ 6], (3) rating pattern-based transfer [ 43]. Content-based trans-
fer involves constructing relationships based on content, selecting common fea-
tures among similar users/items, and transferring/sharing other features across 
cross-domain similar users/items [ 15,28,33]. Embedding-based transfer entails 
generating accurate user and item embeddings or rating patterns, followed by 
transferring the embedding rating patterns of users/items [ 6,13]. Rating pattern-
based approach learns precise mapping relationships between two domains and 
transfers the knowledge acquired from the source domain to the target domain 
based on the learned mapping relationships [ 31]. 
Dual-Target CDR. Dual-target CDR simultaneously enhances the perfor-
mance of two domains by sharing common knowledge. It can be categorized into 
two types, i.e., (1) traditional methods [ 39,41] and (2) disentanglement-based 
approaches [ 2, 3,44]. For traditional methods, CoNet [ 12] introduces a transfer 
learning method, implementing cross-domain recommendation through a neural 
network with cross-mapping connections as the base model for dual knowledge 
transfer. BiTGCF [ 19] uses common users as a bridge for feature propagation and 
knowledge migration, thus improving the recommendation eﬀect of both domains 
simultaneously. GA-DTCDR [ 42] enhances the accuracy of cross-domain recom-
mendation for both domains simultaneously by constructing a heterogeneous 
graph based on rating and content information, and leveraging an element-
wise attention mechanism combined with shared user embeddings across the 
two domains. However, they focus on designing powerful encoders to learn infor-
mation from both domains and ignore the negative transfer phenomenon [ 2]. For 
disentanglement-based approaches, it is usually the separation of domain-speciﬁc 
information that is not useful for cross-domain transfers that reduces negative 
migration. It is divided into two main series. Its one generative model, for exam-
ple, utilizes VAE [ 16] as an encoder and uses some constraints to disentangle the 
ideal representation [ 2, 5,44]. The other utilizes the traditional Graph Convolu-
tional Networks (GCN) [ 27] as an encoder and then disentangles it using some 
technique such as self-supervised learning [ 17,34]or adversarial learning [ 40].

## Página PDF 97

DDC-CDR
87
2.2
Disentangled Representation Learning 
Disentangled representation learning aims to unveil the latent factors underly-
ing user behavioral data [ 3, 5, 7,21,44]. DisenHAN [ 29] applies disentangled rep-
resentations to heterogeneous graphs for explicit modeling of user preferences 
for speciﬁc items. MacridVAE [ 21] explicitly models the separation of macro 
and micro factors, learning disentangled representations based on user behavior. 
Macro disentanglement identiﬁes high-level concepts associated with user inten-
tions, while micro disentanglement, strengthened by an information-theoretic 
approach, reﬂects independent micro factors in each dimension. In DisenCDR [ 2], 
disentangled representation learning is pioneered in cross-domain recommenda-
tion, disentangling domain information into domain-speciﬁc and domain-shared 
information. In DIDA-CDR [ 44], disentangles more comprehensive user prefer-
ences, domain-shared, domain-speciﬁc and further domain-independent informa-
tion, further reduces negative migration information. Further, DR-MTCDR [ 8] 
initially introduces a novel approach to multi-target cross-domain recommenda-
tion from the perspective of disentangled representation learning. It proposes a 
model that incorporates a module to transfer cross-domain shared information, 
disentangling user domain-shared and domain-speciﬁc information, and eﬀec-
tively transferring unbiased domain-shared information across all domains. 
3
The Proposed Model 
In this section, We ﬁrst introduce problem deﬁnition and next introduce our 
proposed model DDC-CDR, as illustrated in Fig. 2.

3.3
User Disentanglement 
In this section, we design a domain classiﬁer and an enhancer. Domain classiﬁer 
disentangles domain-shared and domain-speciﬁc information. Enhancer further 
capture domain-level cluster preference and align them ensuring consistency in 
cross-domain transfer to enhance domain-shared information. 
Domain Classiﬁer. However, domain-shared and domain-speciﬁc information 
are entangled in a way that will cause negative migration [ 44]. In order to ide-
ally disentangle domain-shared and domain-speciﬁc information. Based on two 
mutual information in DisenCDR [ 2], we design the following constraint: 
\bALT{} \begin{aligned} \max & (I(Z^S_u;A,B)-I(Z^S_u;A)-I(Z^S_u;B))\\ \text{s.t.} & \min(I(Z^S_u;Z^A_u;A)+I(Z^S_u;Z^B_u;B)) ,\label{eq9} \end{aligned}\eALT{}

Enhancer. In domain classiﬁer, VCGE fails to adequately capture cross-domain 
shared information, as it only clusters users and items without aligning dual 
domain-level cluster preferences. To address these issues, we introduce two 
enhancers: a domain-shared representation enhancer and a domain-level clus-
ter preference enhancer. Additionally, we introduce two auxiliary variables, $\widebar{Z}^A_u$Z

The Domain-level cluster preference enhancer captures and aligns the 
domain-level cluster preferences of two domains to ensure consistency during 
across domain transfers. Inspired by [ 5], we conduct a two-layer hierarchical 
operation on $\widebar{Z}^A_u$Z
A
u and $\widebar{Z}^B_u$Z
B
u . Initially, these matrices, containing individual pref-
erences and cluster information, are completed by VCGE (ﬁrst layer). Subse-

3.4
Aggregator 
Building on Sect. 3.3, we acquire domain-shared, domain-speciﬁc, and domain-
level cluster shared representations. Employing an element-wise sum approach, 
we combine them to form comprehensive user representations [ 24,30]. The fusion 
operation is outlined as follows: 
\bALT{} Z^A_{com} = Z^A_u + Z^S_u + \widebar{Z}^A_{u,2} ,\eALT{} ZA

com using the same method.

Prediction

com and $Z^B_{com}$ZB
com for 
the two domains, we can derive corresponding item representations $Z^A_v$ZA
v and $Z^B_v$ZB

and item representations, yielding: 
\bALT{} \begin{aligned} \hat{y}^A &= Sig(MLP(Z^A_{com})\odot MLP(Z^A_v)) ,\\ \hat{y}^B &= Sig(MLP(Z^B_{com})\odot MLP(Z^B_v)) , \end{aligned}\eALT{}

where $Sig(\cdot)$Sig(·) represents the sigmoid function. Furthermore, the reconstruct loss 
is deﬁned as follows: 
\bALT{} \mathcal{L}_{cons} = \sum_{y^A \in R^A} \ell(\hat{y}^A , y^A) + \sum_{y^B \in R^B} \ell(\hat{y}^B , y^B),\label{construct}\eALT{} Lcons =
yA∈RA

yB∈RB

where $ \ell(\cdot) $ (·) is the binary cross-entropy loss function. $y^A$yA and $y^B$yB represent the 
observed user-item interactions in domains $A$A and $B$B, respectively, while $\hat{y}^A$ˆyA and 
$\hat{y}^B$ˆyB are the corresponding predicted interactions. To optimize DDC-CDR, we 
employ a multi-task learning mechanism. Speciﬁcally, the ﬁnal loss function is 
formulated as follows: 
\bALT{} \mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_{c} + \mathcal{L}_{dis}+\mathcal{L}_{aug}+\mathcal{L}_{cons}\eALT{} L = Lc + Ldis + Laug + Lcons
(14) 
where $\mathcal{L}_{c}$Lc sums the clustering loss of users and items for the two domains, $\mathcal{L}_{dis}$Ldis
is the domain classiﬁer loss, $\mathcal{L}_{aug}$Laug aggregates $\mathcal{L}_{aug1}$Laug1 and $\mathcal{L}_{aug2}$Laug2, representing the 
enhancer loss, and $\mathcal{L}_{cons}$Lcons signiﬁes the reconstruction loss. 
4
Experiments and Analysis 
In the subsequent experiments, our primary focus is on investigating the follow-
ing questions: 
– RQ1: Does our approach exhibit signiﬁcant improvements compared to other 
state-of-the-art methods? 
– RQ2: How does each module inﬂuence the performance of the model? 
– RQ3: What parameters aﬀect the performance of the model? 
4.1
Datasets 
DDC-CDR utilizes datasets from four real-world benchmark datasets from Ama-
zon [ 9]: Elec, Phone, Sport, and Cloth, without introducing additional cold start 
items. These datasets are merged into four CDR scenarios: Elec & Phone, Sport 
& Cloth, Sport & Phone, and Elec & Cloth. Refer to Table 1 for speciﬁc data 
and details:

4.2
Experiment Setting 
Evaluation Metrics. Following the methodology of DisenCDR [ 2], we employ 
the leave-one-out method to demonstrate performance. We calculate 1000 
records, where DDC-CDR predicts 1000 candidate scores (comprising 999 neg-
ative items and 1 ground truth item) based on learned representations. Subse-
quently, we assess performance using two widely-used metrics, HR (Hit Ratio), 
and NDCG (Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain [ 14]), focusing on the top-
10 ranking results. 
Comparison Methods. To thoroughly validate the eﬀectiveness of DUR-CDR, 
we employ it to compare single-domain recommendation and cross-domain rec-
ommendation methods. 
Single-Domain Recommendation Methods: 
– BPRMF [ 23] is a well-known technique that learns representations using 
pairwise ranking loss. 
– NeuMF [ 11] stacks several MLP layers to learn embedding representations. 
– NGCF [ 27] employs multiple layers of GCN to capture representations by 
incorporating high-order neighbor information. 
– LightGCN [ 10] removes the non-linear activation functions and other com-
plex operations found in NGCF, retaining only the core part of graph convo-
lution, which is the simple neighbor aggregation. 
Cross-Domain Recommendation Methods: 
– CDFM [ 20] transfers information from the source domain to guide the train-
ing of the target domain for enhanced performance. 
– CoNet [ 12] introduces a novel transfer learning method, implementing cross-
domain recommendation through a neural network with cross-mapping con-
nections as the base model for dual knowledge transfer.

– DDTCDR [ 18] focuses on learning user interest migration and utilizing 
implicit orthogonal mapping across domains to maintain the similarity of 
user preferences across domains. 
– PPGN [ 37] explicitly models cross-domain interactions and retains structural 
information to capture multi-hop user preference propagation. 
– BiTGCF [ 19] performs LightGCN in both domains, utilizing feature trans-
formation to merge user representations and obtain the ﬁnal user embedding. 
– DisenCDR [ 2] introduces disentanglement techniques into CDR, disentan-
gling domain information into domain-shared and domain-speciﬁc informa-
tion, thereby reducing negative transfer information. 
– UniCDR [ 1] aims to provide a uniﬁed framework to model diﬀerent CDR 
scenarios by transferring domain-shared information. 
Parameter Settings. For the methods mentioned in Sect. 4.2, we utilize the 
baseline provided by DisneCDR. For UniCDR, we conduct experiments on sce-
nario 1 using the oﬃcially provided code. In DDC-CDR, the GCN structure is 
‘$F \rightarrow F$F →F’ and the MLP structure is ‘$F \rightarrow 2F \rightarrow F$F →2F →F’. Here, $F$F represents the 
initializing embedding dimension, ﬁxed at 128. We employ GCN layers in the 
range {1, 2, 3, 4}, set the number of clusters $K$K to 85, utilize the Adam opti-
mizer with a ﬁxed learning rate of 0.001, maintain a dropout rate of 0.3, and 
use a batch size of 1024. The temperature coeﬃcient $\tau$τ is set to 0.1, the L 2 
regularization coeﬃcient is ﬁxed at 0.0005, and $\beta$β of Beta($\beta ,\beta $β, β) is set to 1. We 
employ multi-head attention with 2 heads. Additionally, $\lambda$λ is chosen from 0.1 to 
0.9, and the slope of LeakyReLU is ﬁxed at 0.1. DDC-CDR is trained for 500 
epochs, and we evaluate the model prediction scores every 10 epochs.

4.3
Performance Comparison (RQ1) 
Table 2 presents a performance comparison of various approaches according to 
HR@10 and NDCG@10 for four CDR scenarios. Based on the experimental 
results, we draw the following conclusions: (1) When compared to BPRMF and 
NeuMF, NGCF and LightGCN show superior performance enhancements, high-
lighting the eﬀectiveness of capturing high-order neighbor information for rep-
resentation. Meanwhile, compared to NGCF, LightGCN signiﬁcantly enhances 
model eﬃciency and lightweightness while maintaining competitive recommen-
dation performance by simplifying the model structure and training process. 
(2) Cross-domain knowledge transfer techniques (e.g., CoNet outperforming 
NeuMF, BiTGCF surpassing LightGCN) have demonstrated superior perfor-
mance compared to single-domain models. This underscores the advantage of 
devising tailored strategies for cross-domain information exchange over using 
a uniﬁed model for amalgamated multi-domain data. (3) The superior per-
formance of CoNet over BPRMF and NeuMF, achieved through knowledge 
transfer between two base networks, demonstrates the promising potential of 
cross-domain knowledge transfer strategies. (4) The improved performance of 
DDTCDR over CoNet suggests that transferring user similarity information 
across domains could be a more eﬀective cross-domain transfer strategy com-
pared to directly transferring user-item interaction features. (5) Compared to 
DDTCDR, which focuses mainly on user similarity propagation, PPGN and 
BiTGCF achieve superior cross-domain recommendation performance by inno-
vatively leveraging model structures and learning strategies such as graph neu-
ral networks, contrastive learning, and attention aggregation to better capture 
and integrate cross-domain user-item interaction and relational information. (6) 
Compared to traditional CDR methods, DisenCDR disentangles domain infor-
mation into domain-speciﬁc and domain-shared information. It selectively trans-
fers domain-shared information that beneﬁts both domains during cross-domain 
transfer, thereby reducing negative transfer eﬀects. UniCDR demonstrates the 
eﬀectiveness of the mask mechanism, contrastive loss, and user attention aggre-
gator in the disentangling process. (7) Compared to state-of-the-art baselines,

[Vista original de la página 105](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-105.png)

Fig. 3. Impact of enhancer. 
DDC-CDR excels across all metrics, except for the NDCG@10 score in the 
Sport&Cloth scenario, where it slightly lags behind UniCDR. This underscores 
the signiﬁcance of domain-level cluster preferences in the disentanglement pro-
cess. 
4.4
Ablation Study (RQ2) 
To validate the eﬀectiveness of each module in DDC-CDR, we create three vari-
ants shown in Table 3. Initially, we integrate Variational Graph Encoder (VGE) 
[ 2] with our domain classiﬁer to create CDR CLA to valid the domain classiﬁer. 
Then, we replace VGE in CDR CLA with VCGE, resulting in CDR VCGE to 
valid VCGE. Finally, we incorporate our enhancer into CDR VCGE, forming 
CDR ENH to valid the enhancer, also known as DDC-CDR. Furthermore, to 
assess the generalization capability of VCGE and the enhancer, we apply them 
to DisenCDR for experimentation. 
Impact of Domain Classiﬁer. When comparing BiTGCN with CDR CLA, as 
depicted in Table 3, it becomes apparent that the performance of CDR CLA sur-
passes that of BiTGCN. The domain classiﬁer eﬀectively disentangles transmit-
ted information into domain-shared and domain-speciﬁc components, proving 
instrumental in mitigating negative transfer eﬀects. Furthermore, this under-
scores the signiﬁcance of domain-shared information in facilitating cross-domain 
transfer. It illustrates that the strategy of transferring domain-shared informa-
tion is powerful and validates the eﬀectiveness of our classiﬁer. 
Impact of VCGE. When comparing CDR VCGE with CDR CLA, the per-
formance of CDR VCGE is superior to CDR CLA as presented in Table 3. It 
highlights the eﬀectiveness of VCGE in DDC-CDR. Additionally, the compari-
son between VCGE and VGE in DisenCDR, as shown in Table 4, illustrates the 
strong generalization ability of VCGE. We posit that VCGE not only aggregates 
two-hop homogeneous user information when generating user representations 
but also integrates semantic structural neighbor information through clustering. 
This broadens the intent of users and captures comprehensive user preferences, 
aﬃrming the importance of incorporating domain-level cluster preferences into 
disentanglement eﬀorts.

Impact of Enhancer. Table 3 indicates that the performance of CDR ENH is 
superior to CDR VCGE, demonstrating the eﬀectiveness of the enhancer in our 
model. Additionally, as depicted in Fig. 3, we further validate the generaliza-
tion ability of the enhancer by comparing the Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO) 
[ 16] with the ELBO plus the enhancer in DisenCDR. From our observations, 
we draw the following conclusions: (1) Learning domain-shared user represen-
tations is crucial in cross-domain recommendation. Disentangling cross-domain 
information into domain-shared and domain-speciﬁc components reduces nega-
tive transfer. (2) Enhancer captures common domain-level cluster preferences, 
facilitating a comprehensive understanding of user preferences and enabling the 
model to learn enhanced domain-shared user representations, thus capturing 
more cross-domain invariant preferences. This reaﬃrms the importance of incor-
porating domain-level cluster preferences into disentanglement eﬀorts. 
4.5
Parameter Sensitivity (RQ3) 
In this section, we investigate two hyperparameters: the number of layers $\ell$ of the 
VCGE and the disentangling ability parameter $\lambda$λ. The ﬁrst two ﬁgures in Figs. 4 
and 5 present results for the former domains (e.g., Elec of Elec&Phone), while 
the latter two depict the latter domain. Regarding the disentangling capacity fac-
tor $\lambda$λ in Fig. 5, scenarios with larger data scales perform better, possibly due to 
containing more information that aids in disentangling shared information, such 
as Elec&Phone. The varying proportions of data from the two domains impact 
disentangling ability, possibly due to capturing more domain-level cluster pref-
erences from both domains, such as 0.7. Figure 4 demonstrates our experiments 
with layer conﬁgurations $\ell$ ranging from 1 to 4. Single-layer models consistently 
performed the worst, highlighting the importance of higher-order information 
aggregation. Interestingly, 2 or 3 layers yielded superior results compared to 4 
layers, suggesting an optimal conﬁguration. Training the VCGE with 4 layers 
may lead to excessive smoothing. Moreover, the experiment results are stable, 
indicating that our model is robust to two parameters.

5
Conclusion 
We have introduced a novel generative model called DDC-CDR. DDC-CDR dif-
fers from previous disentanglement approaches based on individual interactions, 
further incorporates domain-level cluster preferences to capture comprehensive 
user preferences. Speciﬁcally, DDC-CDR introduces three modules: VCGE, a 
classiﬁer, and an enhancer. DDC-CDR encodes information using VCGE, then 
employs the classiﬁer to disentangle domain-speciﬁc and domain-shared infor-
mation. Finally, the enhancer captures and aligns domain-level clustered shared 
preferences. We validated the eﬀectiveness of DDC-CDR on four real datasets 
and chieves consistent improvements compared to state-of-the-art baselines. In 
the future, we extend our model to multi-domain recommendations. 
Acknowledgement. Our work is supported by the National Natural Science Foun-
dation of China (No. 62272001 and No. 62206002), Hefei Key Common Technology 
Project (GJ2022GX15), and the Opening Foundation of State Key Laboratory of Cog-
nitive Intelligence, iFLYTEK (COGOS-2023HE01).

## Página PDF 112

Adaptive Web API Recommendation 
via Matching Service Clusters and Mashup 
Requirement 
Yue Zhu, Guobing Zouenvelope symbol, Song Yang, Shengxiang Hu, Pengtao Li, 
and Chunhua Zeng 
School of Computer Engineering and Science, Shanghai University, Shanghai, China 
{gbzou,yangsong,shengxianghu,22721507,kriya}@shu.edu.cn 
Abstract. With the rapid proliferation of Web services and Web APIs, recom-
mendation systems can effectively address the issue of information overload and 
alleviate the burden of meaningless ﬁltering. Existing approaches can help ﬁlter-
ing appropriate Web services for mashup creation, however, they often fall short 
of developers’ different and personalized needs by recommending only a ﬁxed 
number of APIs and lack precision in aligning mashup requirements across all 
categories. To solve the above issue, this paper introduces a novel Web service 
recommendation framework called AWAR for mashup creation, which focuses on 
the matching strategy between mashup requirements and Web APIs, and enhances 
recommendation effectiveness by integrating natural language processing, opti-
mization algorithms, and deep learning. Extensive experiments conducted on 
large-scale real datasets demonstrate that the proposed approach receives supe-
rior recommendation results on multiple evaluation metrics compared to advanced 
competing baselines. 
Keywords: Web Service cdotService recommendation cdotMashup Creation cdotDeep 
Neural Network cdot0–1 planning 
1 
Introduction 
With the rapid development of service-oriented architecture, the Internet has witnessed a 
surge in the proliferation of Web services and Web APIs [1]. In addition, major Internet 
companies like Yahoo, Google, and Amazon have made their data accessible for integra-
tion with other data sources, resulting in the emergence of new tools enabling developers 
to easily aggregate content from multiple sources to create a new service displayed in 
a single graphical interface, namely mashup service [2]. Therefore, mashup services 
have experienced rapid growth on the network by recommending a set of functionally 
correlated Web API services. 
However, the exponential increase in the number of Web services and the homoge-
nization of these services have presented developers with a challenge that in face of the 
vast sea of service information, they often struggle to quickly locate the services required
© ICST Institute for Computer Sciences, Social Informatics and Telecommunications Engineering 2026 
Published by Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026. All Rights Reserved 
H. Gao and X. Wang (Eds.): CollaborateCom 2024, LNICST 626, pp. 102–118, 2026. 
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-93257-1_6

[Vista original de la página 112](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-112.png)

![Página PDF 112, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-112.png)


<!-- PDF page 113 -->

## Página PDF 113

Adaptive Web API Recommendation via Matching Service Clusters
103
to fulﬁll their development needs, resulting in information overload [3]. Addressing how 
to expedite developers’ discovery of necessary services for mashup creation is a pivotal 
problem to be tackled. 
The recommendation system can effectively address this issue and alleviate the bur-
den of meaningless screening. Many researchers are committed to investigating how to 
recommend Web services to mashup developers to help them effectively building mashup 
services. Web service recommendations for mashup creation are classiﬁed based on the 
recommendation strategy and the information used. Service recommendations based on 
the quality of service often suggest potential high-quality services to developers once 
they have determined a set of required services. Collaborative ﬁltering-based service 
recommendations typically suggest services to users based on service composition and 
historical service invocation records. Social relationship-based service recommenda-
tions often utilize external information, such as developers’ social relationships, to rec-
ommend appropriate services. The function-based service recommendation algorithm 
aims to recommend services to mashup developers that can best meet their functional 
requirements, signiﬁcantly reducing the burden of service discovery on mashup develop-
ers. These service recommendation approaches mentioned above for mashup developers 
can be applied in real-world Web service sharing platforms. 
While existing approaches can help ﬁlter appropriate Web services for mashup cre-
ation, they still fail to fully meet the needs of mashup developers. The primary reason 
for this limitation is that existing approaches typically recommend only a ﬁxed number 
of Web APIs. The size of the recommended API list is often artiﬁcially set, making it 
impossible to dynamically adjust the number of recommended Web APIs based on the 
required functions for creating the mashup service. Another issue arises from the inade-
quacy of the existing function-based service recommendation approaches in accurately 
matching the needs of mashups across all categories. The recommended Web APIs may 
contain multiple APIs with similar functions, yet these functions may not fully cover 
the requirements for creating a mashup service. 
Aiming at the aforementioned research challenges, this paper proposes a novel ser-
vice recommendation approach for mashup creation, called Adaptive Web API Rec-
ommendation (AWAR). In the process of recommending services based on developers’ 
mashup needs, it usually involves matching mashup requirements with candidate ser-
vices. We concentrate on reﬁning the matching strategy between mashup requirements 
and services, leading to enhance the effectiveness of service recommendation through the 
integration of natural language processing, optimization algorithms, and deep learning. 
The main contributions of this paper are summarized as follows: 
• We propose a novel Web API recommendation framework, which applies func-
tional division of mashup requirements and optimization algorithms to adaptively 
recommend an indeﬁnite number of Web API services for creating a mashup service. 
• The recommended results are further optimized using a Multi-layer Perceptron com-
bined with user-service historical service invocation records and other information 
from the mashup services, making the functions of the recommended services better 
cover the functions required for mashup creation. 
• Large-scale experiments are conducted on real datasets, and the results demonstrate 
that the proposed approach receives superior Web API recommendation results.

Sect. 2. Section 3 elaborates on our approach for extracting API business execution 
processes. Section 4 presents extensive experiments and analyzes their performance. 
Section 5 reviews the related work. Finally, Sect. 6 concludes the paper.

is needed to be recommended to the user to meet the requirement. The recommended 
API set is ideal when it includes all the relevant APIs without redundancy and is of an

The ﬂow chart of mashup service recommendation is depicted in Fig. 1. Initially, 
mashup developers, referred to as users, put forward a demand for mashup development. 
Subsequently, the recommendation system employs certain recommendation strategies 
to ﬁlter several APIs from the service repository that could potentially be invoked to 
create the mashup, based on the user demand. These APIs are then recommended to the 
users [7].

## Página PDF 115

Adaptive Web API Recommendation via Matching Service Clusters
105
Fig. 1. The Workﬂow of Web Service Recommendation for Mashup Creation 
3 
Approach 
The purpose is to recommend services based on the functional requirements of mashup 
developers and the API invocation records of mashups. Following popular service rec-
ommendation advancements in recent years, the process ﬁrst typically involves service 
clustering and feature extraction. Subsequently, the similarity between demand features 
and different cluster features is calculated, sorted, and K clusters with the highest similar-
ity are selected. Finally, a certain number of Web services are ﬁltered from each selected 
cluster to obtain the list of recommended services. In these traditional approaches, the 
number of clusters selected is often set manually and the corresponding number of APIs 
cannot be dynamically recommended according to the number of functions required by 
the mashup service to be created. K is usually a value determined through numerous 
experiments to improve experimental results. 
However, in practical applications, the service repository undergoes constant 
updates, necessitating dynamic adjustments to K adapting to real demands of personal-
ized mashup requirements. Therefore, setting K as a ﬁxed value potentially lowers the 
performance of Web API recommendation. Furthermore, there may be multiple APIs 
with similar functions because they are ﬁltered from the same cluster, leading to inad-
equate coverage of the functions required to create a mashup service. For instance, a 
function that occupies a small space in the mashup description is likely to be overlooked 
when selecting the Top-K similar clusters. 
3.1 
Overall Framework of AWAR 
To address the two disadvantages, a novel approach of Web API recommendation is pro-
posed, focusing on improving the selection and matching of functional clusters with user 
requirements. It is called Adaptive Web API Recommendation (AWAR) for mashup cre-
ation, aiming to improve the recommendation accuracy. The workﬂow of WAR involves 
dividing a mashup requirement into multiple subtasks based on existing functional clus-
ter features. It enables the automatic prediction of the number of relevant functional 
clusters and enhances the coverage of recommended APIs to better meet the mashup 
requirements. The overall framework of AWAR is illustrated in Fig. 2. 
• In the component of mashup requirement feature extraction, the mashup requirement 
feature is derived from the demands proposed by the mashup developer through 
feature extraction. 
• In the component of Web service cluster feature extraction, the Web services within 
a service repository are partitioned to a set of service clusters where each cluster

[Vista original de la página 115](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-115.png)

## Página PDF 116

106
Y. Zhu et al.
has Web services with similar functions, and then the features of these clusters are 
extracted to further match the extracted mashup feature. 
• In the component of requirement division, m clusters are selected based on the condi-
tion that the similarity is the highest between the aggregated features of the clusters and 
the requirement features. It is equivalent to approximately dividing the requirements 
into corresponding number of subtasks. 
• In the component of Web service recommendation, a speciﬁc number of services are 
chosen from each of these desired clusters. 
Fig. 2. The Overall Framework of Adaptive Web API Recommendation for Mashup Creation. 
3.2 
Matching Service Clusters and Mashup Requirement by 0–1 Planning Model 
When the requirement is divided into m subtasks, it can be modeled as a 0–1 planning 
problem. From the existing n service clusters, m clusters are selected to be screened out 
so that the aggregated feature vector of these m clusters has the highest similarity with 
the mashup demand feature vector. As a result, the objective function is:

simulating the principles of biological evolution, and demonstrates robust global opti-

upper T is the termination condition dictating when the evolution cease. When implementing 
SGA, parameters such as upper M , T, crossover probability p Subscript c and mutation probability p Subscript m
should be initially set for generating a suboptimal to an originally adaptive Web API 
recommendation problem for mashup creation. 
3.3 
Recommending Adaptive Web APIs by Multi-layer Perceptron 
The Multi-layer Perceptron (MLP) is also known as the Artiﬁcial Neural Network (ANN) 
that is a basic type of deep neural network [9, 10]. MLP is capable of detecting complex, 
non-linear interactions among features and evaluating their different levels of importance 
[11]. In our adaptive Web API recommendation framework, the MLP model in step 2 
is used for capturing the interactions among the invocation records and target mashup 
service and it is depicted in Fig. 3. The output in step 1 signiﬁes the APIs predicted 
for the creation of a mashup service. Relatively, invocation history comprises the record 
of the APIs utilized in crafting existing mashup services, thus the invocation history 
of similar mashup services as the target one serves as a practical reference for mashup 
creation. In the step of MLP, the result from step 1 and the invocation history of the 
Top-N similar mashups Star t S e t upper R 1 comma ellipsis comma upper R Subscript upper N Baseline EndSetare combined to forecast the APIs for mashup

The values of weights and bias are randomly initialized at ﬁrst. After forward propa-
gation, the error between the predicted results and the ground truth values is calculated. 
Then the error of each neurons in each layer is calculated backward during back propa-
gation. As previously mentioned, for each layer of the MLP, the output is y equals upper F left parenthesis w comma b right parenthesis. 
Given the errors of each cell, gradient descent algorithm is applied for updating weights 
and bias in order to minimize errors: 
w = w − η ∂C 
∂w

∂b 
(8) 
where etarepresents the learning rate which may inﬂuence the convergence efﬁciency and 
performance. 
Additionally, the selection of an optimizer is also an important part of deep learning 
model [10]. The mini-batch adaptive moment estimation (Adam) optimization algorithm 
is used to optimize the parameters of our adaptive Web API recommendation model. 
The Adam has the superiority of high computing effectiveness, minimal memory spec-
iﬁcations, and robust interpretability, etc. Furthermore, Adam effectively accepts the 
estimation of the ﬁrst and the second moments of the gradient to calculate the step 
size and efﬁciently integrates the strengths of the previously given two optimization 
algorithms RMSProp and AdaGrad. The function of training accuracy is: 
Accuracy =

4 
Experiments 
4.1 
Experimental Datasets and Setup 
To evaluate the performance of AWAR, a series of experiments are conducted on an 
equipment conﬁgured with an Intel(R) Xeon(R) Gold 6130 CPU@2 and a 192GB 
RAM in the environment of python3.7. A Web information crawler has been devel-
oped and deployed to obtain Web services and related data up to July 1st, 2018 from 
ProgrammableWeb. The dataset crawled comprises 3,989 mashup services and 16,243 
Web services across 403 categories. From these, 7,666 Web services from the top 20 
categories and 2,724 mashup services containing only these Web services are selected 
for the experiments. The distribution of the selected services is presented in Table 1. 
Following data preprocessing, APIs are clustered into several groups. Moreover, the 
language model BERT, introduced by Google in 2018, is implemented for word embed-
ding in the experiments. BERT, based on the transformer architecture, is a pre-trained 
model known for its strong performance in language representation and feature extrac-
tion. In consideration of the relatively limited word count in most service descriptions 
and the computational cost of model training, we implement the BERT-base model (with 
parameters L = 12, H = 768, A = 12) for word embedding of both Web and mashup 
service descriptions. 
4.2 
Evaluation Metrics 
The recommendation performance of AWAR is evaluated by 4 metrics: Precision, Recall, 
F1-score and Hit Ratio. TP is the number of services recommended which meet the 
requirement; FP is the number of services recommended which do not ﬁt the requirement; 
FN is the number of services not recommended but required; HitNumber is the number 
of services recommended are required; GT is the number of Web services the mashup 
service actually required for creation. They are calculated as below. 
Precision =
TP 
TP + FP 
(10) 
Recall =
TP 
TP + FN 
(11) 
F1 = 2 × Precision × Recall 
Precision + Recall 
(12) 
HR = HitNumber 
GT 
(13) 
The values of these 4 evaluation metrics are in the range of [0,1] and have a direct 
ratio with the recommendation performance.

4.3 
Competing Methods 
To evaluate the performance of AWAR, it is compared with 5 representative approaches, 
which are listed as follows. 
• SingleBPR[12]: BPR is effectively employed in Web API recommendation by pre-
dicting user preferences. It learns user behavior through pairwise comparisons of API 
interactions. This enables the model to recommend APIs that match user interests 
closely, ensuring high preference scores and improving recommendation accuracy. 
• LSTM[13]: LSTM is commonly used to track sequential features when recommend-
ing Web APIs. By capturing long-term dependencies, LSTM delivers precise and rel-
evant Web API recommendations that adapt to changing user preferences, improving 
the personalization and efﬁciency of web service discovery. 
• SPR[14]: The SPR model applies the author-topic model to learn how mashup descrip-
tions relate to APIs. By analyzing the topical structure of these descriptions, SPR 
produces Web API recommendations that are more relevant and accord with user’s 
requirement, enhancing the precision of results. 
• Pop[15]: The Pop model recommends services based on their popularity to users. 
In mashup creation, this popularity is quantiﬁed by the number of category-aware 
invocations. APIs with higher usage within these categories are prioritized, ensuring 
that the most frequently used APIs are recommended, thereby enhancing the relevance 
and reliability of the recommendations. 
• MTFM[16]: MTFM model integrates multi-model fusion and multi-task learning to 
enhance prediction accuracy. The inclusion of a semantic component to represent 
service requirements improves the API recommendation results. 
4.4 
Experiment Results and Analyses 
The performance of AWAR is validated by comparing it with ﬁve competing baselines. 
Since AWAR can adaptively adjust the recommended number of Web APIs instead of a 
predeﬁned one, we initially compare its performance with the best performance of each 
competing approach, regardless of the recommended number K. 
Experimental results of Web API recommendation among competing approaches 
are provided in Table 2.

Speciﬁcally, the recommendation performance among competing baselines with dif-
ferent numbers of K are shown in Figs. 5, 6, 7, 8 and 9. SingleBPR and LSTM share 
similar results compared to AWAR. When K = 10 and K = 5, SingleBPR and LSTM 
are observed to have the best performance because their F1 score reaches the maximum 
values. For SingleBPR, LSTM and SPR, they achieve comparable accuracy of Web API 
recommendation. The advantage of SingleBPR lies in direct ranking optimization, while 
LSTM lies in the superior encoding structure of bidirectional LSTM with an attention 
mechanism. The advantage of SPR is that the hidden topic layer can overcome the dis-
advantages of the bag-of-words model and semantically connect the requirements with 
the APIs. 
Additionally, the results of SPR, Pop, and MTFM are similar to each other compared 
to AWAR. Their recall and hit ratio outperform AWAR, but precision and F1 score are 
lower. Compared with all the competing baselines, MTFM has the advantages of both 
CF-based and content-based approaches. AWAR outperforms them signiﬁcantly on all 
the accuracy indicators. 
Since other 3 comparison methods are not self-adaptive, their recall and hit ratio 
may outperform AWAR when K is set to a large number because the more candidate ser-
vices there are, the more correct services will be recommended. However, most mashup 
services generally apply a small number of Web services, as our proposed AWAR adap-
tively recommends a small number of Web APIs. In addition, Ks of the comparison 
methods are artiﬁcially set after a large number of experiments for optimal results, but 
in real application scenarios, setting K in advance is not feasible because the services 
in the repository are not ﬁxed. A conclusion can be drawn that from the perspective 
of self-adaptation, AWAR can make recommendations more stably by better meeting 
users’ requirements and is more effective in practical use.

5 
Related Work 
In recent years, with the continuous development of Web services, API recommenda-
tion for mashup creation have gradually become a research hotspot. Many researchers 
are committed to studying how to recommend Web services to mashup developers, 
aiding them in quickly building their desired mashup services. Currently, service 
recommendations for mashup creation can be divided into four categories: quality-
based service recommendations, collaborative ﬁltering-based service recommenda-
tions, social relationship-based service recommendations, and function-based service 
recommendations. 
Quality-based service recommendation often involves recommending potentially 
high-quality services to developers after they have identiﬁed a set of required services 
[17]. Reference [18] utilized temporal information to reﬁne similarity measurements in 
neighborhood-based CF (Collaborative Filtering). Location information was also inte-
grated to cluster services and users, enabling personalized recommendations based on 
clustering results [19]. Paper [20] proposed a hidden Markov model-based approach to 
assist users in locating services with optimal response times for their requests. Addi-
tionally, Luo et al. [21] introduced the BNLFTs model regarding the issue of QoS data 
ﬂuctuating over time. Biased non-negative latent factorization of tensors were utilized 
for predicting Quality of Service (QoS) in a temporal context. Wang et al. [22] intro-
duced a novel method extending the original Graphplan form and incorporating branch 
structures into composite solutions to address uncertainty in the service composition 
process. 
Collaborative ﬁltering-based service recommendation typically recommends ser-
vices to users based on service composition and call records. Jiang et al. [23] introduced 
an effective personalized collaborative ﬁltering method for Web service recommenda-
tion. When calculating user similarity, this method considers the personalized impact of 
services rather than solely relying on the Pearson correlation coefﬁcient (PCC). Liang 
et al. [24] proposed a recommendation algorithm for secure collaborative ﬁltering ser-
vices. The algorithm integrated deep neural networks and content similarity modules 
to alleviate the problem of data sparsity in Mashup Web service matrix using tradi-
tional collaborative ﬁltering algorithms. In [25], the authors focused on the problem of 
data sparsity and cold start and proposed a service recommendation model. It combines 
knowledge graph for uncovering potential relationships and collaborative ﬁltering for 
API recommendations.

[Vista original de la página 125](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-125.png)

![Página PDF 125, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-125.png)


<!-- PDF page 126 -->

## Página PDF 126

116
Y. Zhu et al.
Social relationship-based service recommendation often uses external information, 
such as the social connections among developers, to recommend services. In [26], the 
authors studied service usage patterns in an evolving service repository called myEx-
periment, proposing a recommendation algorithm based on service correlations within 
the network. Wei et al. [27] proposed the Social-powered Graph Hierarchical Attention 
Network (SGHAN) using a service-level attentional encoder to identify signiﬁcant ser-
vices in friends’ mashups and a friend-level graph attention network to prioritize and 
propagate the social inﬂuences of friends. Additionally, [28] outlined a service recom-
mendation approach grounded in link prediction within a dynamic service co-occurrence 
network. Recent work has applied association mining techniques over service networks 
to discern positive and negative collaboration patterns among services. 
Function-based service recommendation aims to recommend services that best ful-
ﬁll the functional requirements of mashup developers, thereby alleviating the burden of 
service discovery. Wu et al. [29] introduced a probabilistic model for suggesting services 
to mashup developers, applying a topic model to represent mashup requirements, APIs, 
and their relationships. Xia et al. [30] proposed a distributed recommendation method 
comprising two steps: service clustering and distributed service recommendation, which 
help narrow the search scope of mashup developers’ service discovery. Its notable fea-
ture is the recommendation of multiple sorting lists to users, with each list representing 
a category of service sets, rather than recommending all services to developers at once. 
Gao et al. [3] presented a service recommendation framework to suggest sets of services 
to developers, where each service set can be viewed as a potential mashup rather than 
a single service. The framework consists of two stages: service clustering and service 
set sorting. Gu et.al. [31] build a semantic service package repository where the ser-
vice packages are denoted with compositional semantics by mining mashup and then 
recommend Web APIs based on the repository. 
6 
Conclusion and Future Work 
In this paper, we are dedicated to adaptively recommending Web API into service recom-
mendation tailored for mashup creation. The proposed SASSBR focuses on the matching 
process between mashup requirements and candidate API services, improving recom-
mendation effectiveness through the integration of natural language processing, opti-
mization algorithms, and deep learning, where 0–1 planning and multi-layer perceptron 
are applied for accurately performing adaptive Web API recommendation. We con-
duct a series with competing baselines to assess the performance of AWAR, including 
comparative analyses of methods and the impact of parameters within our proposed 
recommendation approach. 
In the future work, we aim to further explore the latest techniques to advance Web API 
recommendations by reﬁning the alignment between the descriptions of Web services 
and mashup requirements. We also focus on improving the way of calculating similarity 
between mashup services and aggregated services by utilizing neural network. Moreover, 
we intend to further optimize the feature extraction method for aggregated service and 
API services.

## Página PDF 129

Multi-channel Heterogeneous Graph 
Transformer Based Unsupervised 
Anomaly Detection Model for IoT Time 
Series 
Qinghui Xi1, Xi Li1, Peng Chen1(B), Juan Chen1, Jianhua Ren2, 
Weijian Song1, and Hongxia He1 
1 School of Computer and Software Engineering, XiHua University, Chengdu, China 
chenpeng@mail.xhu.edu.cn 
2 West China Second University hospital, Sichuan University, Chengdu, China 
Abstract. Detecting anomalies in multivariate time series (MTS) holds 
signiﬁcance for monitoring the behavior of intelligent industrial and 
Internet of Things (IoT) systems. Nevertheless, due to the absence of 
anomaly labels in current IoT system data and the high-dimensional 
complexity of the data, constructing an anomaly detection model that 
accurately identiﬁes anomalies while maintaining robustness poses a 
considerable challenge. The paper introduces an unsupervised anomaly 
detection model, the Multi-channel Heterogeneous Graph Transformer 
model for IoT Time Series Anomaly Detection (MC-HGT). Our model 
implements complex non-linear temporal feature extraction using TCN 
and SENet. The integration of parallel GNN and GAT within a hetero-
geneous graph neural network, connected with the Transformer network, 
eﬃciently discerns local and global temporal feature dependencies. It 
ampliﬁes the model’s ability to extract temporal features by employ-
ing multi-channel feature fusion in conjunction with the Transformer. 
VAR is employed to capture additional linear features, facilitating the 
multi-channel fusion of linear and non-linear features to enhance model 
robustness. We evaluate the detection performance of MC-HGT against 
ﬁve baseline methods on three public datasets. The experimental ﬁnd-
ings reveal that the MC-HGT algorithm achieves a mean F1 Score of 
0.905, surpassing similar algorithms by 6.93%, and a mean AUC of 0.943, 
exhibiting a 2.35% improvement over comparable algorithms. 
Keywords: Multivariate Time Series · Internet of Things · Anomaly 
Detection · Deep Learning · Graph Neural Network 
Q. Xi and X. Li—The two authors contribute equally to this work. 
This research is partially supported by the National Natural Science Foundation 
of China under Grant No. 62376043 and Science and Technology Program of 
Sichuan Province under Grant No. 2024NSFTD0008, No. 2023JDRC0087, and No. 
2024ZHCG0016. 
c ICST Institute for Computer Sciences, Social Informatics and Telecommunications Engineering 2026 
Published by Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026. All Rights Reserved 
H. Gao and X. Wang (Eds.): CollaborateCom 2024, LNICST 626, pp. 119–134, 2026. 
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-93257-1_7

Q. Xi et al.
1
Introduction 
The rapid expansion of the Internet and the maturation of IoT technology have 
ushered in an era of information proliferation. Serving as a nexus between the 
physical and digital realms, IoT has profoundly transformed our daily lives, work 
environments, and industries [ 17]. It constitutes a network of diverse devices, 
including smartphones, computers, smart wearables, and an array of sensors, 
interconnected through various technological means to the internet. IoT has sig-
niﬁcantly facilitated numerous applications and services across various domains, 
from smart homes and cities to industrial automation and healthcare. However, 
the tremendous volume of data generated by IoT is accompanied by a potential 
surge in abnormal behaviors, posing a signiﬁcant threat to the security and eﬃ-
ciency of IoT systems. To safeguard the reliability and authenticity of IoT data, 
the implementation of anomaly detection becomes imperative. 
Fig. 1. The ﬁgure shows common exception type, with exception segments in the red 
boxes. (Color ﬁgure online) 
Abnormal behaviors may include equipment failures, network attacks, data 
tampering, abnormal operations, etc. In a traditional computing environment, 
anomaly detection may be comparatively easy to implement, but in the IoT 
environment, anomaly detection is more challenging due to the heterogeneous, 
real-time, and high-dimensional nature of the data. Figure 1 is a list of com-
mon types of exceptions. Over the past few years, deep-learning-based anomaly 
detection has achieved remarkable results. For example, Informer [ 29] is a new 
model based on Transformer [ 23] that can provide probabilistic predictions, giv-
ing scores of possible future values. STGCN [ 8] addresses the problem of time

[Vista original de la página 130](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-130.png)

![Página PDF 130, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-130.png)


<!-- PDF page 131 -->

## Página PDF 131

Multi-channel Heterogeneous Graph Transformer
121
series detection in the traﬃc domain and improves detection accuracy through 
multi-head self-attention. In addition, there are a number of anomaly detection 
models in cloud-based environments that have demonstrated excellent perfor-
mance, including [ 5, 6,16,20,21,25,27]. However, they do not meet the require-
ments of complex and constantly changing IoT systems, although their high 
detection accuracy is evident in certain situations. Consequently, an anomaly 
detection technique that incorporates unsupervised multivariate time series is 
urgently needed to ensure accurate anomaly detection and robustness of the 
model. 
From this, it can be concluded that the current model faces the following 
challenges in anomaly detection: 
– When facing complex IoT data, relying solely on single or limited feature 
extraction may result in ineﬀective improvement of model detection accuracy. 
– Current anomaly detection models focus more on the extraction of nonlinear 
features, thus neglecting the importance of linear features. 
In response to the challenges outlined above, we present the MC-HGT model. 
This model leverages a parallel GNN and GAT within a heterogeneous graph 
neural network, complemented by a transformer. The objective is to achieve 
both local and global feature representations of time series anomalies. MC-HGT 
enhances the extraction of temporal feature dependencies through multi-channel 
feature fusion. The utilization of TCN [ 13] enables eﬀective convolutional oper-
ations on time series, facilitating the extraction of key feature information. We 
introduced SENet(Squeeze-and-Excitation Network) [ 11], incorporating a chan-
nel attention mechanism to model the signiﬁcance of channels. This adaptive 
focus allows the model to selectively attend to diﬀerent channel information, 
reinforcing its comprehension of essential features, mitigating the impact of irrel-
evant features, and bolstering overall model robustness. Furthermore, VAR is 
employed to preserve the linear relationships within the time series and provide 
forecasts for anomalous values. 
In summary, our key contributions to this work are: 
– To address the challenge of low accuracy in anomaly detection for complex 
time-series data, we leverage the TCN combined with the SENet network for 
the extraction of features from intricate non-linear timing data. 
– To better capture the feature information in the time series data, we use a 
heterogeneous graphical neural network in conjunction with GNN and GAT 
to connect the transformer, which enables our model to better identify the 
local and global feature representation and the extraction of the temporal 
feature is enhanced by multi-channel feature fusion. 
– To improve the learning of the model for linear features, we use VAR for 
learning linear feature information and multi-channel feature fusion with non-
linear feature and linear feature information to improve the robustness of the 
model. 
– Experiments were carried out on publicly available datasets to assess the 
detection capabilities of MC-HGT. Results show that this particular model

The structure of this paper is outlined as follows: Sect. 2 provides a review 
of classical and notable research in multivariate time series anomaly detection. 
Section 3 introduces the MC-HGT model, presenting a comprehensive descrip-
tion of each module. Section 4 details the experimental setup, results, and anal-
ysis. Finally, conclusions are drawn in Sect. 5. 
2
Related Work 
Detecting anomalies in time series data has become increasingly crucial with 
the continued growth of the Internet of Things. Various conventional meth-
ods [ 10,15,19] can be applied for this purpose. Among them, PCA [ 3] is utilized 
for anomaly detection through dimensionality reduction of the data. KNN [ 9] 
relies on distance computation, while LOF [ 2] is designed for anomaly detection 
based on outlier factors. However, most of these methods exhibit limitations in 
capturing non-linearity and fail to account for temporal dependencies in time 
series data. As a result, their performance in multivariate time series anomaly 
detection remains suboptimal. Therefore, we primarily focus on discussing deep 
learning-based methods. 
Deep-learning-based models for anomaly detection, including those refer-
enced in literature [ 4,14,24,30], have displayed promising performance in recent 
studies. Among these methods, the Deep Autocoded Gaussian Mixture Model 
(DAGMM) [ 30] eﬀectively integrates the processes of data dimensionality reduc-
tion and density estimation for end-to-end training. This approach prevents the 
model from getting trapped in local optimal solutions caused by density esti-
mation after data dimensionality reduction, which is a common problem in tra-
ditional methods. However, DAGMM may not extract temporal information 
eﬃciently in time series. In contrast, models like LSTM-NDT [ 12] and MTAD-
GAT [ 28] exhibit high accuracy in detecting abnormalities, but they overlook 
linear feature extraction. 
TCN is a deep learning model used for processing time-series data. It lever-
ages one-dimensional convolutional neural networks (CNNs) to slide along the 
temporal dimension to capture features at diﬀerent time scales. It utilizes dilated 
convolutions to expand the receptive ﬁeld of the convolutional kernel, thereby 
capturing longer temporal dependencies. Such as [ 18] utilizes TCN combined 
with autoencoder and temporal attention mechanism for time series anomaly 
detection. Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) iteratively aggregate information 
from nodes and their neighbors to capture local patterns and relationships 
between nodes in time series data. In time series anomaly detection, GNNs help 
the model understand local anomaly patterns, such as abnormal ﬂuctuations 
at certain time points. Graph Attention Networks (GATs) introduce attention 
mechanisms, allowing diﬀerent weights to be assigned to each time point when 
aggregating neighbor information. In time series anomaly detection, GATs help

[Vista original de la página 132](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-132.png)

![Página PDF 132, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-132.png)


<!-- PDF page 133 -->

## Página PDF 133

Multi-channel Heterogeneous Graph Transformer
123
the model focus on time points that have signiﬁcant impact on anomaly detec-
tion. Due to SENet’s ability to adaptively focus on information from diﬀerent 
channels by modeling important channels, the SENet model can improve accu-
racy and robustness. 
Among these approaches, Transformer has demonstrated remarkable eﬀec-
tiveness in anomaly detection, leading to the development of several recent 
models that leverage attention networks [ 1, 7,22,26]. These models oﬀer clear 
advantages in terms of training speed and the extraction of long-dependent fea-
tures. For instance, 
TranAD is an anomaly detection model employing a deep transformer net-
work and an attention-based sequential encoder to identify anomalies in broader 
temporal trends. It incorporates adaptive and adversarial training, enabling swift 
inference. Graph Deviation Network (GDN) approaches utilize Graph Attention 
Network (GAT) to learn relationships between data patterns, constructing a 
graph that maps structured information from the network to anomaly scores. 
This approach is eﬀective in identifying anomalous nodes. However, these mod-
els primarily emphasize lengthy temporal dependencies and non-linear feature 
extraction, often overlooking regional and linear characteristics inherent in the 
original data. This oversight can impact not only detection accuracy but also 
the model’s overall resilience. 
To solve the above problems, we propose the MC-HGT model with high 
anomaly detection accuracy and strong robustness. The diﬃculty of extracting 
complex non-linear temporal features is well solved by using TCN in conjunc-
tion with SENet. The local and global feature representations of the time series 
are achieved by concatenating the GNN and GAT heterogeneous graph neural 
networks in tandem with the transformer, and multi-channel feature fusion is 
performed on the extracted local and global features by the transformer, which 
greatly enhances the extraction of temporal feature in the model. We use VAR 
for multi-channel feature fusion to enhance the model’s ability to capture linear 
features and improve the model’s robustness. 
3
Methodology 
3.1
Model Architecture 
Overall Architecture: The overall architecture of MC-HGT is illustrated in Fig. 2. 
Initially, we employ TCN and SENet for extracting complex non-linear depen-
dencies within time-series features. To capture both local and global feature rep-
resentations of the time series data, we incorporate parallel GNN and GAT het-
erogeneous graph neural networks, followed by Transformer, facilitating multi-
channel feature fusion. To preserve the linear correlation of the data, we specif-
ically address linear features using a VAR model and perform multi-channel 
feature fusion of both linear and non-linear features. The non-linear feature pre-
dictions generated by the prediction model are integrated with the linear pre-
dictions provided by the VAR via the \varphiϕ weights to arrive at the ﬁnal predicted 
value. The individual modules are delineated as follows:

Fig. 2. The MC-HGT structure. TCNs use a ﬁxed number of convolutional layers to 
ensure a gradual expansion of the receptive ﬁeld, allowing the network to better capture 
long-term dependencies. We have introduced a single coding layer in the Transformer 
model for operational eﬃciency and reduction of computational complexity. 
3.2
Modules of MC-HGT 
TCN and SENet Based Feature Representation. TCN can eﬀectively 
capture long dependencies through the structure of inﬂated convolution, and 
SENet can adaptively focus on the most important channels during the training 
process through the channel attention mechanism, which enhances the model’s 
learning of important features, mitigates the inﬂuence of irrelevant features on 
the model, and improves the model’s robustness. As shown in Fig. 2(1), we eﬀec-
tively capture the temporal features of complex non-linear data by using TCN 
and SENet in tandem. 
In Fig. 3, TCN uses a one-dimensional fully convolutional network framework 
and zero padding to make the sequence length of each layer constant, and the 
input and output lengths are the same, which prevents the leakage of future 
information. The sense ﬁeld of the convolution is also increased by inﬂating the 
convolution in order to adapt to long-time sequence tasks. Excluding the padding 
operation of TCN, the basic single-layer computational ﬂow is as follows:
