Fig. 3. After the data enters the initial feature extraction of TCN, the important 
feature weights are enhanced through SENet. 
TCN module uses a convolution kernel size of kk = 3 and an expansion coeﬃcient 
of d=1, 2, 4\cdotsd = 1, 2, 4 · · · . 
The weight contribution in anomaly detection is often diﬀerent in diﬀerent 
feature channels, and the weight contribution of diﬀerent features is adjusted 
using SENet, as shown in Fig. 2(1.2). The speciﬁc formula of SENet is as follows: 
\bALT{} W=\sigma\left(Conv1D\left(\delta\left(Conv1D\left(AvgPool\left({X}^{\prime}\right)\right)\right)\right)\right)\eALT{} W = σ (Conv1D (δ (Conv1D (AvgPool (X )))))
(3) 
\bALT{} X_{scale}=W \otimes {X}^{\prime}\eALT{} Xscale = W ⊗X
(4) 
where\deltaδ is theReLUReLU function, and\sigmaσ compresses the attention weights of diﬀerent 
channels to [0,1] to achieve channel weight contribution adjustment. \otimes⊗is the 
element-by-element broadcast point multiplication. 
GNN, GAT and Transformer Based Multi-channel Fusion. Due to the 
intricate relationships among nodes in a multivariate time series, the graph struc-
ture of GNNs excels at capturing interdependencies. However, GNNs suﬀer from 
ﬁxed weights for information transfer, leading to information decay in handling 
distant relationships. GAT addresses this limitation by introducing an atten-
tion mechanism, enabling varying attention weights for each node’s neighbors. 
When data node similarity is high and doesn’t require extensive attention dif-
ferentiation, GNNs provide satisfactory performance, thereby reducing compu-
tational complexity. Simultaneous utilization of GNN and GAT heterogeneous 
networks allows for the simultaneous capture of local and global feature depen-
dencies. This approach enhances dependencies extraction and improves model 
generalization. The Transformer’s self-attention mechanism facilitates parallel 
computation, eﬃciently capturing long dependencies in input sequences. By fus-
ing multi-channel features of local and global information, Transformer enhances 
the model’s feature dependencies extraction on time series data. This is achieved 
through multi-channel feature fusion of local and global feature information, as 
depicted in Fig. 2(2).

VAR Based Linear Extraction. As all modules in the model involve non-
linear processing, there is a tendency to overlook the linear relationships present 
in the original data. To ensure the preservation of linear relationships in the 
original data, we introduced the Vector AutoRegressive (VAR) model. The VAR 
model strengthens the model’s ability to handle linear relationships. Through 
multi-channel feature fusion of linear feature information and non-linear infor-
mation, as shown in Fig. 2(3), the model signiﬁcantly enhances its capability 
to extract feature dependencies in time series data. This improvement not only 
contributes to the model’s robustness but also enhances its ability to capture 
the intricacies of temporal data dynamics.

3.3
Anomaly Scoring 
The proposed MC-HGT model obtains the ﬁnal prediction value of the model by 
integrating the non-linear feature information with the prediction value obtained 
by using the prediction model based on the fully connected layer and the predic-
tion value provided by the VAR model through the weights and calculates the 
loss function based on this prediction value. The loss function can be expressed 
as Root Mean Square Error (RMSERMSE): 
\bALT{} Loss=\sqrt{}{\sum}_{i=1}^L {(x_{n+1,i}-\hat{x}_{n+1,i})}^2\eALT{} Loss = √
L

4
Experiment 
4.1
Datasets 
We use three publicly available datasets to validate the eﬀectiveness of our model, 
namely SWaT, SMAP, and MSL. SWaT is collected from a real water treatment 
plant, and the dataset consists of sensors (water level, ﬂow rate, etc.) and actu-
ators (valves and pumps). SMAP and MSL are data from the Mars rover col-
lected by NASA. Where SMAP is soil samples and telemetry information from 
the rover, MSL is data from the actuators and sensors of the rover itself. The 
dataset details, including names, entity counts, dimensionality, training and test 
set sizes, and anomaly rates for each test subset, are summarized in Table 1. In 
Fig. 5, we present data distribution plots for the three datasets. Notably, the 
distinct distributions of each dataset facilitate the assessment of the model’s 
robustness. In order to make our model more robust, we normalised the data 
and added white noise with a signal-to-noise ratio of 50 dB.

4.2
Experiment Setup 
Baselines. We conduct a comparative analysis of the proposed model against 
ﬁve unsupervised anomaly detection models. LSTM-NDT is a deep learning 
network based on the LSTM model, introducing a nonparametric and unsu-
pervised anomaly thresholding approach. OmniAnomaly focuses on acquiring 
a robust representation of multivariate time series through techniques such as 
random variable correlation and planar normalized streaming. This representa-
tion is then utilized for data reconstruction and anomaly identiﬁcation based on 
reconstruction probabilities. MAD-GAN employs Generative Adversarial Net-
works (GANs) and utilizes Long Short-Term Memory Regression Neural Net-
works (LSTM-RNN) to capture temporal correlations in time series distributions 
within the GAN framework. USAD adopts a two-stage adversarial training app-
roach, enhancing its capability in isolating anomalies and achieving accelerated

training times. DTAAD, based on the Transformer and dual Temporal Convolu-
tional Networks (TCNs), elevates prediction accuracy and augments correlation 
variance through the implementation of scaling techniques and feedback mech-
anisms. 
Evaluation Metric. We evaluated the detection performance of all models 
using precision (P), recall (R), Area Under the Curve (AUC), and F1-Score. 
Experimental Environment. We utilized Python 3.8 and PyTorch 1.11.0 to 
execute our approach and all its variations. All experiments were conducted 
using an NVIDIA 3060. We set the time window to 10. Where the convolutional 
kernel size of SENet and TCN is 3, and the padded convolutional kernel is set to 
1. In the Transformer module’s multi-head attention mechanism, we maintain 
consistency with the dataset size by regulating the number of attention heads. 
For the VAR model, we employ a lag order of 1. In this paper, the POT method 
is utilized to automatically determine the anomaly threshold. 
We use the AdamW stochastic gradient descent optimizer to train our model, 
with an initial learning rate of 0.01 and a step scheduler with a step size of 5. 
Our approach involves dividing each time series into a training set of 80% and 
a test set of 20%. We predict and label only the next timestamp in each time

4.3
Experimental Results 
Table 2 presents precision, recall, AUC, and F1 scores for the MC-HGT app-
roach and the baseline method on the three datasets. Our model outperforms 
the majority of baseline models across all datasets. When compared to the 
average F1 and average AUC of the latest baseline models, MC-HGT shows 
a 6.93% improvement in the F1 score and a 2.35% improvement in the AUC 
score. Notably, our model outperforms all others in terms of the F1 score. 
As shown in Fig. 6, we can see the ﬁnal results of MC-HGT and 7 baseline 
models on 3 data sets in the experiment, and our model has the highest F1 score 
on all 3 data sets, which proves that MC-HGT has excellent performance and 
robustness. 
The LSTM-NDT model demonstrates success on the MSL dataset but per-
forms inadequately on the other datasets due to the NDT thresholding method’s 
limited sensitivity to diverse scenarios. In contrast, MC-HGT, along with other 
models, establishes more accurate thresholds using the POT procedure. MAD-
GAN, as a GAN model built on the LSTM, utilizes a discriminator and pre-
diction loss to compute anomaly scores. However, its poor performance may 
be attributed to the lack of consideration for interdependencies or contextual 
information on features. The USAD model introduces an attentional mecha-
nism, signiﬁcantly improving feature extraction for extended temporal depen-
dencies, as evidenced by its superior performance on the SWaT dataset. DTAAD, 
trained with TCN and Transformer and utilizing adversarial training techniques, 
achieves second-level performance across all three datasets. Our model surpasses 
the DTAAD model on the SMAP and SWaT datasets, showcasing superior per-
formance in extracting long-time dependencies and greater overall model robust-
ness. 
4.4
Ablation Experiments 
We performed ablation analysis and showed the validity of the model, we will 
analyse the modules and the experimental results are shown in Table 3. 
TCN and SENet Based Feature Representation. In the absence of the 
TCN module, MC-HGT (w/o TCN) demonstrates an average reduction in F1 
and AUC of 0.163% and 1.4%, respectively, compared to our complete method. 
Similarly, MC-HGT (w/o SENet) exhibits a reduction of 0.17% in average F1 
and 1.5% in AUC. These ﬁndings underscore the signiﬁcant role played by the 
TCN and SENet network components in extracting complex non-linear feature 
information for the model.

Fig. 6. F1-score of MC-HGT and all baseline models 
GNN, GAT and Transformer Based Multi-channel Fusion. Experimen-
tally, we observed a decrease in the mean F1 score and AUC of 0.49% and 
3.33%, respectively, in MC-HGT (w/o GNN) compared to our complete model. 
For MC-HGT (w/o GAT), the mean F1 score and AUC decreased by 0.17% and 
1.53%, respectively. Remarkably, in MC-HGT (w/o Tran), the mean F1 score 
decreased by 7.07%, and the AUC decreased by 19%. These ﬁndings underscore 
the eﬀectiveness of our Transformer network, which utilizes GNN and GAT het-
erogeneous networks in tandem. This approach proves instrumental in identifying 
both local and global feature information in time series data, thereby enhancing 
the model’s capability to extract feature dependencies through multi-channel 
feature fusion. 
VAR Based Linear Extraction. In MC-HGT (without VAR), we observed a 
marginal decrease in the average F1 score by 0.14%, and the AUC decreased by 
2.08%. This highlights our model’s superior ability to extract non-linear features 
from the data after multi-channel feature fusion, while also eﬀectively capturing 
the linear features present in the original data. This dual capability contributes 
to the overall robustness of the model. 
4.5
Parametric Sensitivity 
We investigated the role of sensitivity thresholds, and the experimental results 
are shown in Table 4, demonstrating the eﬀect on F1 scores and AUC scores. 
The {\varphi}ϕ emphasises the predictor of nonlinearity of the anomaly scores, and the 
larger the {\varphi}ϕ and the smaller the {1 - \varphi}1 −ϕ, the more the model tends to learn the 
nonlinear features. We can learn from the table that the best results are obtained 
when {\varphi}ϕ is 0.7.

5
Conclusion 
In this paper, we propose a multivariate time series based anomaly detection 
method, MC-HGT, which uses TCNs in tandem with SENet networks to extract 
complex non-linear time series features and improve the robustness of the model. 
The Transformer network is then connected in series by parallel GNNs and 
GATs, enabling the model to capture both local and global feature dependen-
cies of the time series data, and the Transformer is used for multi-channel feature 
fusion to enhance the model’s extraction of time series feature information. How-
ever, the features captured by the above methods are non-linear. To improve the 
robustness of the model, we connect the VAR model in parallel to extract linear 
feature information. And multi-channel feature fusion of linear feature informa-
tion and non-linear feature information to improve the overall performance and 
robustness of the model. 
In future work, we will prioritize optimizing the parallel graph neural network 
and graph attention network in the model to improve training eﬃciency and 
reduce model training time. Furthermore, we will investigate incorporating real-
time prediction interpretability into the model to enhance its usability.

## Página PDF 145

CBR-FIF: A Novel Dynamic Graph Node 
Embedding Computation Framework 
Mingjian Ni1, Gongju Wang2 
, Yinghao Song2 
, Yang Li2 
, Long Yan2 
, 
Dazhong Li2, Yanfei Wang3, Shikun Zhang1, and Yulun Song2(B) 
1 School of Software and Microelectronics, Peking University, Beijing, China 
2 Data Intelligence Division, Technology R&D Department, China Unicom Digital 
Technology Co., Ltd., Beijing 100166, China 
songyl100@chinaunicom.cn 
3 Unicom (Beijing) Industrial Internet Co., Ltd., Beijing, China 
Abstract. In this paper, we propose a new and scalable framework 
that more fully exploits the temporal contextual information of dynamic 
graph. Dynamic graph node embedding is a technique that embeds nodes 
in a time-varying graph. When performing representation learning on 
dynamic graph nodes, it is necessary to preserve not only the topological 
structure of the graph but also the temporal information. Most existing 
methods focus more on mining the topological structure of graph and 
do not fully utilize the temporal information. A convolutional aggrega-
tion module that considers temporal context is incorporated, enabling 
the computation of a node’s embedding to simultaneously consider the 
information of its neighbors in the current snapshot and the neighbor-
ing snapshots. Additionally, the model integrates BiLSTM to aggregate 
temporal data from both directions. Finally, for each graph snapshot, we 
introduce a topological reconstruction loss function to generate better 
embedding for the nodes. We conducted experiments on two tasks and 
the results of our approach outperform the baseline models. 
Keywords: dynamic graph · node embedding 
1
Introduction 
Graphs are eﬀective models for representing entities and their relationships, 
widely used in areas such as social networks [ 1], transportation networks [ 2], 
pipeline networks [ 3] and power grids [ 4]. Unlike conventional tabular data, graph 
data describes complex interactions between nodes through adjacency matrix. 
Therefore, graph data is usually considered high-dimensional. 
With the rapid advancement of deep learning technology, graph node embed-
ding techniques have emerged, aiming to capture the structural information and 
node attributes within graphs using deep learning models, and transforming 
high-dimensional graph data into low-dimensional feature representations. These 
low-dimensional graph node embeddings not only retain the core information of

## Página PDF 146

M. Ni et al.
the original graph data but also signiﬁcantly reduce computational and stor-
age overhead, enhancing the eﬃciency and feasibility of applications in practical 
scenarios. 
Dynamic graphs, where entities and their interconnections evolve over time, 
prevalent across various domains such as social networks, user-product networks, 
and transportation systems. Building upon the foundation of static graph repre-
sentation, the computational paradigm extends to dynamic graphs, which encap-
sulate temporal variations in structure and node attributes. The integration of 
temporal dynamics into node embeddings presents a complex problem, requir-
ing methodologies that can eﬀectively encode time-evolving patterns within the 
graph structure. Addressing these challenges is important for applications in 
anomaly detection, traﬃc optimization, and recommendation systems, where 
temporal insights can substantially augment analytical outcomes. 
Transition from static graph analysis to dynamic graph analysis, the latter 
incorporates temporal dynamics, introducing complexities and expanding the 
analytical framework. Dynamic graphs, akin to multi-channel image data, evolve 
over time, necessitating advanced methods for capturing these changes. Lever-
aging Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN) [ 5] as an inspiration, we introduce 
a convolutional aggregation module designed to aggregate neighbor informa-
tion across temporal snapshots. This module accounts for both immediate and 
adjacent temporal neighbors, enhancing the embedding process by considering 
multi-temporal contexts. 
Building on time series analysis advancements, we apply Bidirectional Long 
Short-Term Memory (BiLSTM) [ 6] networks to encapsulate temporal depen-
dencies across all graph snapshots, enriching the embedding with temporal con-
text. Furthermore, we integrate a module that aggregates the reconstruction loss 
across all snapshots, focusing on the overall topological structure to reﬁne the 
embedding vectors. The proposed method, a dynamic graph node embedding 
framework, aims to enhance the performance of existing models by incorporat-
ing temporal neighbor information, temporal context, and overall topological 
integrity. 
The contributions of this work are as follows: 
1. We design a ﬂexible dynamic graph node embedding framework CBR-FIF 
that exploits temporal neighbor information, temporal context, and recon-
struction loss to improve embedding quality. 
2. CBR-FIF is used to enhance the CGCN [ 7] graph node embedding method, 
producing an improved model. 
3. The proposed method is validated through extensive experiments on various 
datasets, demonstrating its eﬀectiveness and potential to advance dynamic 
graph analysis.

2
Related Work 
Dynamic graph embedding techniques have seen advancements, with many draw-
ing inspiration from static graph embedding methods [ 8]. Dynnode2vec [ 9] pro-
poses improved random walk and initialization methods to address the chal-
lenges of extending Node2Vec from static graphs to dynamic graphs. DynGraph-
GAN [ 10] integrates Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) into dynamic 
graph node embedding models. It employs a generator to create connections 
between nodes and a discriminator to distinguish between real and generated 
connections. In recent years, some researchers have focused more on exploring 
and analyzing the dynamic graph structure, such as [ 11]. Many studies have 
also applied dynamic graph representation learning to traﬃc ﬂow forecasting 
(TFF) [ 2,12,13] by capturing both the latent spatial patterns and the temporal 
dynamics of traﬃc ﬂow data. [ 14] utilizes graph embedding to discover correla-
tions among time series data and address traﬃc incident detection. [ 15] presents 
a dynamic graph learning approach to generate an ensemble of subject-speciﬁc 
dynamic graph embeddings, which uses brain networks to disentangle cognitive 
events more accurately. Dynamic graph embedding is also used in other ﬁelds, 
such as social networks [ 16], political networks [ 17] and animal behavior [ 1]. 
Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) [ 18] are classical computational oper-
ators in deep learning, and graph convolutional neural networks (GCN) [ 19]s are 
commonly used models in graph learning. Some researchers have introduced 
GCN into dynamic graph ﬁeld. For example, M-GCN [ 20] applies multi-scale 
graph convolutional neural networks to the knowledge graph domain to address 
the challenges posed by complex relationships. T-GCN [ 21] and GC-LSTM [ 22] 
employ GCN as a topology-based feature aggregation method independently 
for each snapshot. Inspired by CNNs, the dynamic graph can be viewed as an 
image, and the convolutional kernel becomes a three-dimensional operator that 
can integrate nodes and their neighbors from multiple snapshots. 
BiLSTM, which is commonly used in the processing of temporal and textual 
data, is introduced into the dynamic graph node embedding to enhance the eﬀec-
tiveness of dynamic graph node embedding models. For example, AGBN [ 13] and 
[ 12] incorporate BiLSTM into dynamic graph node embedding model to improve 
the accuracy of forcasting in traﬃc ﬂow time series. BiLSTM is also introduced 
in knowledge graph relationship discovery to enhance the representation vec-
tors of entities [ 23]. Researchers use graph attention networks (GAT) [ 24] and 
BiLSTM to improve the accuracy of predicting epileptic seizures [ 25]. Dynam-
icG2B [ 26] uses BiLSTM to avoid the vanishing gradient problem of RNN in 
dynamic graph embedding process and achieves good results in dynamic graph 
node classiﬁcation problems. 
Reconstruction loss of the topological structure is widely used in static graph 
node embedding techniques such as graph autoencoder(GAE) [ 27] and Graph-
SAGE [ 28]. In dynamic graph node embedding techniques, preserving the topo-
logical structure is also a commonly used method, such as DynamicTriad [ 29], 
which constructs a loss function by ensuring the Triadic Closure relationship 
between three points to make the embedding vectors of closely related points

closer. CTGCN [ 7], for example, establishes relationships between diﬀerent nodes 
through random walks and constructs a loss function to maintain this topological 
structure as much as possible. In paper [ 16] and [ 17], they add temporal smooth-
ness to the loss function to ensure that the diﬀerences in embedding between 
adjacent snapshots are not signiﬁcant. In contrast to these diﬀerent topological 
reconstruction methods, the framework proposed in this paper optimizes the 
overall reconstruction loss by summing the reconstruction losses of all snapshots 
to ensure the eﬀectiveness of the embedding for all time slices. 
3
Models
 
First we deﬁne the problem and then detail the Convolution Bidirectional RNN 
and Reconstruction Flexible Integration Framework (CBR-FIF). 
3.1
Deﬁnition 
A dynamic graph is deﬁned as G = {G_t, \ t=1,2,...,T}G = {Gt, t = 1, 2, ..., T}, where TT represents the 
number of time slices considered. Each G_tGt, also known as a snapshot, is deﬁned 
as G_t = (V_t, E_t, X_t)Gt = (Vt, Et, Xt), where V_tVt represents all the nodes in G_tGt, E_tEt represents all 
the edges, and X_tXt represents the feature data of the nodes. A dynamic graph GG
is characterized by the fact that V_tVt, E_tEt, and X_tXt can change for diﬀerent tt. In this 
paper, it is assumed that the nodes in the graph will not increase or decrease for 
diﬀerent tt, but the connectivity between nodes and node features, i.e., E_tEt and X_tXt
may change. Therefore, each snapshot can be deﬁned as G_t = (V, E_t, X_t)Gt = (V, Et, Xt). Let NN
be the number of nodes in GG, i.e. |V| = N|V | = N. A_tAt denotes adjacency matrix of G_tGt
and A_t(i, j)At(i, j) denotes the element of the ii-th row and jj-th column of matrix A_tAt. 
A_t(i,j) = 1At(i, j) = 1 if there is a connection between node ii and node jj, and A_t(i,j) = 0At(i, j) = 0
otherwise. DD represents the dimension of the original feature space.Therefore, 
X_tXt has a shape of N \times DN × D. 
3.2
Details 
Overview. The overall framework is illustrated in Fig. 1. Given a daynamic 
graph, the ﬁrst step is to apply graph convolution to each snapshot. Then, 
the features of each snapshot’s nodes are concatenated with the features of 
their adjacent snapshots, followed by a Multilayer Perceptron (MLP) model. 
This module is referred to as the Temporal Convolution module (TCM). Next, 
the model proceeds to the Backbone module (BM), where a static graph node 
embedding model is employed for each snapshot’s embedding computation. The 
output embeddings from the BM are fed into the Bidirectional Enhancement 
module (BEM) to incorporate more temporal context into the embedding vec-
tors. The embeddings from BEM are fed to the Topology Enhancement module 
(TEM) to reconstruct the topology of each snapshot using a decoder. This way, 
a reconstruction error can be calculated for each snapshot, and the reconstruc-
tion losses of all snapshots are summed and added to the loss function of the 
BM. This completes the forward process. The model parameters can be updated 
using backpropagation during training.

Fig. 1. The architecture of CBR-FIF. The input dynamic graph ﬁrst undergoes the 
Temporal Convolution module, where the feature of each node is enriched with infor-
mation from its neighbors at the current snapshot and neighbors of adjacent snapshots. 
It is then passed through the Backbone module, which primarily focuses on computing 
the embedding of each snapshot. The next module is the Bidirectional Enhancement 
module, which utilizes algorithms like BiLSTM to incorporate temporal context infor-
mation into the embedding. The ﬁnal module is the Topology Enhancement module, 
which constructs a reconstruction loss involving all the snapshots in the training pro-
cess. This loss is then combined with the loss from the Backbone module to form the 
overall loss of the entire model. 
Temporal Convolution Module (TCM). The main purpose of the TCM is 
to incorporate both the neighboring information of nodes in the current snapshot 
and the adjacent snapshot.

Backbone Module (BM). This module is primarily responsible for the embed-
ding computation of each snapshot. We have chosen CGCN [ 7] in this module 
for instance. CGCN is an eﬃcient model taking into account the graph structure 
and is the core part of dynamic graph node embedding method CTGCN. 
CGCN partitions the original topology based on node degrees, dividing the 
graph into a series of nested subgraphs with diﬀerent degrees, known as k-core 
subgraphs. Formally, a k-core subgraph of a graph G_tGt refers to the maximal 
subgraph in G_tGt where all the nodes have a degree greater than or equal to 
kk. k_{max}kmax represents the total number of distinct partitions. Within each k-core 
subgraph, a GCN is applied, as show in Formula 4.

4
Experiment 
This section begins by outlining the experimental setup, including the baseline 
models and their parameter conﬁgurations. We then focus on two key tasks: 
dynamic graph link prediction, which evaluates the graph embeddings’ ability 
to capture dynamic changes between nodes, and structural role classiﬁcation, 
which examines the accuracy of embeddings in identifying the functional roles of 
nodes in diﬀerent regions. Subsequently, we visualize the results of graph embed-
ding to demonstrate the model’s capability in capturing structural information. 
Additionally, through ablation study, we conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 
the proposed method, investigating the impact of various model components on 
performance, to showcase the eﬀectiveness and advantages of our approach in 
dynamic graph analysis. 
4.1
Experimental Setup 
Baselines are as follows: 
– Graph Convolutional Network (GCN) [ 19]: GCN extends convolutional neu-
ral networks to graph-structured data, eﬀectively capturing dependencies in 
graph topology.

[Vista original de la página 152](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-152.png)

![Página PDF 152, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-152.png)


<!-- PDF page 153 -->

## Página PDF 153

CBR-FIF
143
– Graph Attention Network (GAT) [ 24]: GAT introduces an attention mecha-
nism into graph neural networks, allowing nodes to weigh the importance of 
their neighbors’ features. 
– Graph Isomorphism Network (GIN) [ 30]: GIN aims to capture the capabil-
ity of the Weisfeiler-Lehman graph isomorphism test, enhancing the GNN’s 
ability to distinguish diﬀerent graph structures. 
– CGCN-C & CGCN-S [ 7]: These models are static adaptations of their respec-
tive CTGCN counterparts, omitting the graph evolution component. CGCN-
C derives its ﬁnal node embeddings from outputs of a linear feature trans-
formation layer, mirroring CTGCN-C. Conversely, CGCN-S uses outputs 
from a non-linear feature transformation layer for node embedding, similar 
to CTGCN-S. 
– DynGEM [ 31]: DynGEM adapts deep autoencoders for dynamic graph 
embedding, capturing the evolving characteristics of graphs over time. 
– Dyngraph2vec: The dyngraph2vec [ 32] method leverages past adjacency 
matrices to regenerate the present ones using an encoder-decoder frame-
work. This approach comes in three diﬀerent versions: dynAE, dynRNN, and 
dynAERNN. 
– Graph Convolutional Recurrent Network (GCRN): GCRN combines graph 
convolutional networks with recurrent neural networks, eﬀectively processing 
spatio-temporal graph data [ 33]. 
– EvolveGCN [ 34]: EvolveGCN adjusts GCN over time to learn the dynamics 
of evolving graphs, making it suitable for tasks like link prediction and node 
classiﬁcation. 
– CTGCN-C & CTGCN-S [ 7]: Both are k-core based temporal graph convo-
lutional networks, aiming to eﬃciently learn dynamic graph node represen-
tations. The main diﬀerence lies in their methods of ﬁnal node embedding 
feature transformation: CTGCN-C uses outputs from a linear feature trans-
formation layer, while CTGCN-S uses outputs from a non-linear feature trans-
formation layer [ 7]. 
Parameter Settings: In our study, we primarily follow the parameter settings 
of the CTGCN model, while introducing speciﬁc conﬁgurations of the CBR-FIF 
method. Speciﬁcally, for methods based on static graph convolutional networks 
(GCN), such as GCN, GAT, and GIN, we adopt a two-layer network structure. 
Similarly, for GCRN and EvolveGCN, we also set two layers. For DynGEM, we 
set \alphaα to 10^{-5}10−5, \betaβ to 10, and both \gamma_1γ1 and \gamma_2γ2 to 10^{-4}10−4. For dynAE, dynRNN, and 
dynAERNN, we set \betaβ to 5, look back to 3, and both \gamma_1γ1 and \gamma_2γ2 to 10^{-6}10−6. 
In the CBR-FIF method, we adopted a similar setup to CTGCN-C, specif-
ically including a linear layer and two CGCN layers. Additionally, we set the 
number of layers for the multi-kernel graph convolution layer to 1. The k-core 
subgraph number for the CGCN layer is still set as the maximum k-core number 
across all dynamic graphs. We chose the Adam optimizer, setting the learning 
rate to 0.001 and weight decay to 5 \times 10^{-4}5 × 10−4. In terms of node features, for the 
CBR-FIF method, we use one-hot encoding based on CGCN-C and CTGCN-C; 
while based on CGCN-S and CTGCN-S, we adopt degree-based node features.

4.2
Link Prediction 
In link prediction, we aim to forecast if a link between two nodes will emerge

with embeddings $ \mathbf{v}_i^t $vt
i and $ \mathbf{v}_j^t $vt

dict future links. Various embedding combinations, like their average, Hadamard 
product, and L1/L2 norms, serve as predictor inputs. The prediction process is 
captured by Formula 15, with $ \hat{y}_{ij}^{t+1} $ˆyt+1

Datasets. Our link prediction study employed ﬁve datasets as shown in Table 1. 
The UCI network dataset 1 consists of 1,899 nodes and 59,835 edges across 7 snap-
shots, reﬂecting social interactions in a university setting. The Bitcoin Alpha 
Trust Network (BA) 2, with 3,783 users and 24,186 edges over 63 snapshots, 
represents trust relationships in cryptocurrency trading. The Autonomous Sys-
tems Network (AS) 3, encompassing 6,828 nodes and 1,947,704 edges in 6 snap-
shots, illustrates Internet communication patterns. The Wikipedia Vote Network 
(WVN) 4, containing 7,115 nodes and 103,689 edges over 47 snapshots, is used 
for analyzing voting behavior in online communities. Lastly, the MathOverﬂow 
Network (Math) 5, with 24,740 nodes and 323,357 edges in 77 snapshots, aids 
in studying collaboration in professional communities. These diverse datasets 
provide a comprehensive environment for evaluating link prediction models.

Performance. As shown in Table 2, the best results are highlighted in bold, and 
the second-best results are underlined. The link prediction results for real-world 
graphs indicate that our proposed CBR-FIF method signiﬁcantly outperforms 
other methods across all datasets. CBR-FIF achieved the highest AUC perfor-
mance on the UCI, BA, WVN, and MATH datasets, and ranked second on the AS 
dataset, slightly below CTGCN-S. Speciﬁcally, CBR-FIF achieved AUC values 
of 0.9637, 0.9854, 0.9962, and 0.9845 on the four best-performing datasets, out-
performing the next-best CTGCN-C model by 2.03%, 3.17%, 2.87%, and 1.54% 
respectively. This indicates that CBR-FIF is capable of capturing the temporal 
information in dynamic graphs as well as the changes in nodes’ adjacency rela-
tions, aiding in its node representation for predicting the formation and deletion 
of links. On the AS dataset, although CBR-FIF still has a slight advantage over 
the CTGCN-C algorithm, it further illustrates that deep mining of structural 
and temporal information helps enhance the utility of node representation in 
link prediction. The fact that it is lower than CTGCN-S suggests that the struc-
ture of CBR-FIF needs to be ﬁne-tuned to accommodate computation on denser 
graphs. 
To further assess the stability of graph embedding methods over timestamps, 
we recorded the AUC scores of some representative methods at each timestamp, 
as shown in Fig. 2. We observe that, during the evolution of the graph, most 
methods exhibit signiﬁcant ﬂuctuations in their AUC values, while CTGCN-
C shows more stability across most timestamps compared to other methods. 
Therefore, these results further demonstrate that CTGCN can learn stable and 
expressive node representations for diﬀerent timestamps.

4.3
Structural Role Classiﬁcation 
The structural role classiﬁcation task is a critical method to assess the capability 
of diﬀerent graph embedding techniques in preserving structural similarity. In 
this task, we leverage the node representations learned by the methods to predict 
the structural role-related labels of nodes within each dynamic graph. The clas-
siﬁcation process is conducted using logistic regression, and the primary metric 
for evaluation is accuracy. This approach allows us to quantitatively measure the 
eﬀectiveness of each method in capturing and representing the structural roles 
of nodes in dynamic graphs. 
We conduct this evaluation on two datasets: a European air-traﬃc network 
and an American air-traﬃc network [ 35] as shown in Table 3. Since these datasets 
are inherently static, we employ a speciﬁc strategy to transform them into 
dynamic graphs for our analysis. Initially, we randomly select 10% of the edges 
from each network to serve as the starting point. We then incrementally add 
an additional 10% of the edges in each step, creating a series of 10 dynamic 
graph snapshots. This process simulates the evolving nature of dynamic graphs, 
providing a realistic scenario for testing the algorithms. 
The structural role classiﬁcation task can be symbolically represented as 
follows:

Performance. In the task of structural role classiﬁcation, the results summa-
rized in Table 4 oﬀer a direct comparison of the methods under evaluation. The 
CBR-FIF method distinguishes itself by securing the highest accuracy scores of 
0.6101 and 0.5590 on the America-Air and Europe-Air datasets, respectively. 
This indicates the method’s eﬃciency in capturing the subtle nuances of node 
roles within dynamic graphs. 
DynAE, with accuracy scores of 0.5978 and 0.5495, assumes the second 
position, underscoring the signiﬁcance of reconstruction loss in the domain of 
dynamic graph embedding. This performance highlights the essential role that 
accurate graph structure reconstruction plays in understanding the evolving roles 
of nodes. 
CTGCN-C, posting scores of 0.5840 and 0.5385, conﬁrms the advantage of 
incorporating temporal information into the embedding computations. It demon-
strates the necessity of temporal dynamics consideration for improving node 
representation in structural role classiﬁcation tasks. 
The exemplary performance of CBR-FIF is attributed to its amalgamation 
of the strengths inherent in dynAE and CTGCN-C, fusing reconstruction loss

4.4
Embedding Visualization 
In our visualization task, we opted for the BA dataset over the UCI dataset 
due to its richer node count but sparser edge distribution. This presents a chal-
lenge for algorithms to capture and preserve structural features with insuﬃcient 
connectivity information. To vividly demonstrate the embedding outcomes, we 
employed the t-distributed stochastic neighbor embedding (t-SNE), a nonlinear 
dimensionality reduction technique for projecting high-dimensional data into a 
two-dimensional space, aiming to evaluate the model’s ability to maintain global 
structural integrity. As illustrated in Fig. 4, despite the dataset’s connectivity 
traits posing challenges to structural information preservation, notable observa-
tions were made. Speciﬁcally, with the CTGCN-C and CTGCN-S approaches, 
Fig. 4(b) and (c) depict the aggregation of 0-core nodes into larger clusters, while 
higher-order nodes are localized in speciﬁc regions, demarcated by bold black

high-order structural characteristics. 
4.5
Ablation Study 
In the ablation study for link prediction tasks presented in Table 5 and Table 6, 
we systematically evaluate the contribution of each module to the performance 
of the CTGCN-C framework by considering AUC as the performance metric. 
Starting with the CTGCN-C baseline, which achieves a solid performance 
with AUC scores ranging from 0.9434 on the UCI dataset to 0.9691 on the Math 
dataset, we can see that the foundational model is already well-tuned for the 
task at hand. 
Upon adding the BEM, there is a noticeable increase in AUC scores for the 
UCI dataset (from 0.9434 to 0.9527) and a more signiﬁcant improvement for 
the BA dataset (from 0.9537 to 0.9695), demonstrating the BEM’s capability 
in enhancing node embeddings by considering the bidirectional nature of links, 
which is particularly evident in the context of trust and social interactions as 
represented by the BA dataset. 
When the TEM is introduced in conjunction with the BEM, we observe a 
mixed eﬀect on performance. Notably, there’s an improvement in the AUC for

the AS dataset, which sees an increase to 0.9628 from the CTGCN-C baseline. 
This suggests that the TEM’s focus on network topology is particularly eﬀec-
tive in capturing the more complex link patterns present within communication 
networks. 
The full model, CBR-FIF, which integrates BEM, TEM, and TCM, achieves 
the highest AUC on the UCI dataset (0.9637), BA dataset (0.9854), and WVN 
dataset (0.9962), indicating the combined eﬃcacy of all modules. Interestingly, 
the AUC slightly decreases on the AS dataset (to 0.9593 from 0.9628) when TCM 
is added, suggesting that while the temporal aspect is generally beneﬁcial, its 
integration needs careful tuning to avoid overshadowing the spatial topological 
patterns that are paramount in certain datasets. 
The ablation study reveals that while BEM and TEM provide substantial 
improvements to the model’s ability to predict links by enhancing structural 
and topological understanding, the TCM’s focus on temporal patterns is most 
eﬀective when the dynamic aspect of the graph is as crucial as its structure. 
5
Conclusion 
In this paper, we presented CBR-FIF, a novel framework for dynamic graph 
embedding computation. Our method integrates a convolutional aggregation 
module considering temporal context, BiLSTM for bidirectional temporal data 
aggregation, and a topological reconstruction loss function, providing a compre-
hensive approach to embedding dynamic graphs. Through extensive experiments

[Vista original de la página 160](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-160.png)

![Página PDF 160, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-160.png)

## Página PDF 161

CBR-FIF
151
on tasks like dynamic link prediction and structural role classiﬁcation, CBR-FIF 
demonstrated superior performance in capturing the evolving characteristics of 
graphs and the functional roles of nodes across various domains. 
The ablation study further highlighted the signiﬁcance of each component, 
showing that the combination of temporal and structural information signiﬁ-
cantly enhances the model’s predictive capabilities. Additionally, visualization 
of embeddings oﬀered insights into the model’s ability to preserve structural 
information, even in sparsely connected networks. 
Our ﬁndings suggest that eﬀectively integrating temporal dynamics with 
graph structural information is crucial for advancing dynamic graph analysis. 
Future work could explore more sophisticated temporal modeling techniques 
and extend the framework to a wider range of dynamic graph applications, such 
as anomaly detection or network evolution prediction. 
CBR-FIF sets a new benchmark in dynamic graph embedding, providing a 
robust tool for researchers and practitioners in ﬁelds ranging from social network 
analysis to bioinformatics. By oﬀering a deeper understanding of graph dynam-
ics, CBR-FIF paves the way for innovative applications and further advance-
ments in graph representation learning. 
6
Future Work 
Despite the advantages of the CBR-FIF framework in dynamic graph embedding, 
it still faces many challenges and areas for improvement when applied to large-
scale graphs and non-continuous time series data. Future research will focus on 
enhancing the eﬃciency and applicability of the CBR-FIF framework. 
Firstly, more eﬃcient algorithms need to be designed. For example, sparse 
matrix computation algorithms can be developed by leveraging the sparsity of 
graph data. Additionally, parallel and distributed computing strategies should 
be employed, where large-scale graph data is divided into several subgraphs 
and processed in parallel on multi-processor or multi-core architectures, thereby 
reducing the computational burden on a single node. 
Furthermore, the application scope of CBR-FIF needs to be expanded to 
include areas such as dynamic social network analysis and biological network 
research to thoroughly evaluate the framework.

(2017)

## Página PDF 164

KG-ASI: A Knowledge Graph Enhanced 
Model-Based Retriever for Document 
Retrieval 
Zhongqin Bi1, Haomin Shen1, Weina Zhang1(B), and Wei Zhong2 
1 School of Computer Science and Technology, Shanghai University of Electric 
Power, Shanghai, People’s Republic of China 
mszhangwn@shiep.edu.cn 
2 No. 34 Research Institute, CECT, Guilin, Guangxi, People’s Republic of China 
Abstract. Model-based retrieval is an emerging information retrieval 
paradigm, which directly returns ranked docids of relevant documents 
for a given query input, based on a pre-trained generative language 
model. For model-based retrievers, how to deﬁne and represent docid is 
a vital issue aﬀecting retrieval performance. Existing methods lean solely 
on mining information from the unstructured corpus itself to generate 
docids, suﬀering from a lack of structural information to characterize doc-
ument relations. To tackle this problem, we explore a novel model-based 
retrieval framework named KG-ASI, enhanced by an external knowl-
edge graph. Speciﬁcally, rich structural knowledge is injected into docids 
through graph representation learning on a corpus-related KG subgraph 
as external knowledge enhancement, facilitating the end-to-end training 
process. Besides, to mitigate the exposure bias in query generation, we 
extend beyond original supervised ﬁne-tuning by implementing a novel 
contrastive learning method to ﬁne-tune the pre-trained query generation 
model, augmenting the quality of pseudo-queries. Experiments on two 
public benchmarks demonstrate the superiority of KG-ASI over strong 
baselines across common metrics, verifying the eﬀectiveness of our pro-
posed method. 
Keywords: Document retrieval · Model-based IR · Knowledge graph · 
Graph neural network 
1
Introduction 
Document retrieval plays a key role in web search engines and a series of down-
stream Knowledge-Intensive Language Tasks (KILT) such as entity linking, fact 
veriﬁcation, and open-domain question answering. Given a user’s query, it ﬁrst 
retrieves multiple candidate documents from a large corpus and returns them in 
a ranked list. For traditional retrieval methods, term-based sparse retrieval meth-
ods [ 1– 3] are widely used across various search scenarios, but suﬀer from lexical 
mismatching [ 4]. Instead, semantic-based dense retrieval methods [ 5– 7] are cre-
ated to solve this issue. Despite their excellent performance, the dense retrievers

H. Gao and X. Wang (Eds.): CollaborateCom 2024, LNICST 626, pp. 154–176, 2026. 
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-93257-1_9

[Vista original de la página 164](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-164.png)

![Página PDF 164, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-164.png)


<!-- PDF page 165 -->

## Página PDF 165

KG-ASI: A KG-Enhanced Model-Based Retriever
155
generally lack ﬁne-grained interactions between queries and documents. Further-
more, all of these traditional methods follow a three-stage “index-retrieval-rank” 
pipeline, which has its inherent ﬂaws. First, the decoupled components of the 
three stages are trained separately, making it diﬃcult to optimize them end-to-
end towards a global objective. Second, regardless of which kind of index is used, 
a large pre-computed index incurs considerable memory costs and computational 
overhead. 
Recently, with the remarkable progress in Pre-trained Language Model 
(PLM), Tay et al. [ 8] have pioneered a fundamentally new end-to-end neural 
retrieval paradigm, known as the model-based retrieval method, which eﬀec-
tively addresses the aforementioned challenges. Concretely, for a given query 
input, it employs a generative language model to directly return ranked docu-
ment identiﬁers (docids) of potentially-relevant documents as retrieval results, 
integrating the three-stage pipeline into a single consolidated model. This inte-
gration enables the model to learn a retriever in an end-to-end way, simplifying 
the complex search process associated with traditional methods. Meanwhile, all 
documents in the corpus are stored into parameters, which is equivalent to build 
a diﬀerentiable index implicitly [ 9]. Compared to separate indexes, it signiﬁcantly 
reduces memory consumption. 
Recent studies [ 8, 10– 12] have already explored the utilities of the model-
based paradigm for document retrieval tasks. One of the primary distinctions 
among these studies is their deﬁnition of the docid. In fact, the method by 
which documents are tokenized into identiﬁers determines their distribution 
within the semantic space [ 8]. During training, the docid of each document is 
utilized as a supervisory signal to convey semantic information about the docu-
ment and structural relationships between documents to the model. Therefore, 
how docids are represented signiﬁcantly aﬀects retrieval performance. However, 
existing methods rely solely on mining information from the corpus itself to 
generate docids. While the text of the documents in unstructured corpora can 
elucidate their semantic meanings well, the unstructured nature of these cor-
pora inherently makes them lack suﬃcient structural information to character-
ize the relationships among documents. This kind of deﬁciency is prevalent in 
existing model-based methods and potentially hampers their capacities. Knowl-
edge Graph (KG) harbors rich structural information and relational knowledge 
among entities, many of which can be further mapped to corresponding doc-
uments [ 13]. Numerous studies [ 13– 16] have demonstrated that KG can eﬀec-
tively enhance the document retrieval process in various KILT tasks, especially 
for dense retrieval methods. Motivated by these insights, we attempt for the 
ﬁrst time to integrate KG with the model-based retrieval method, serving as an 
external knowledge enhancement to oﬀset this deﬁciency. 
In this work, we propose the Knowledge Graph-Autoregressive Search Indexer 
(KG-ASI), a novel model-based retrieval framework built on a transformer-based 
language model. It features a so-called KG-enhanced semantic docid, which 
is enriched with abundant structural information between documents from an 
external KG. Speciﬁcally, diverging from existing methods, we design three main

[Vista original de la página 165](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-165.png)

![Página PDF 165, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-165.png)


<!-- PDF page 166 -->

## Página PDF 166

156
Z. Bi et al.
modules to incorporate the additional knowledge from the KG into the docids. 
First, we aim to eliminate redundant parts of the entire KG and preserve only the 
essential corpus-related elements in a KG subgraph, where nodes are KG enti-
ties uniquely mapped from documents in the corpus, and edges correspond to 
the relationships represented by the KG triplets among these entities. Then, we 
employ an inductive Graph Neural Network (GNN), GraphSAGE [ 17], to learn 
the feature representations of entity nodes in an unsupervised minibatch setting, 
encoding graph-structured data on the subgraph. Importantly, the aggregation 
functions we consider for each layer are modiﬁed to account for edge weights, 
which are treated as crucial graph structural information. As a result, the infer-
ence of a well-trained GraphSAGE acquires updated embedding representations 
for all entity nodes, which contain both graph-structured knowledge and con-
textual semantic information. Eventually, the structured KG-enhanced semantic 
docids are produced by hierarchically clustering the updated entity node embed-
dings. Therefore, the semantic and structural information encapsulated in the 
embeddings is ultimately transferred to the docids. 
Furthermore, we identify a potential challenge within the query generation 
module of the paradigm. Despite the improvements made to the retriever [ 12], 
the module fundamentally remains a seq2seq task, akin to text summarization. 
Thus, the module also encounters exposure bias [ 18] due to the gap between 
training and inference. Drawing inspiration from techniques in abstractive sum-
marization [ 19], we introduce a novel contrastive learning strategy that employs 
ranking loss for ﬁne-tuning this module, moving beyond the original supervised 
ﬁne-tuning. Through contrastive learning, the decoder of retriever can assess 
the relative quality of candidate queries, allocating higher probabilities to high-
quality candidates within target distribution, not limited to the reference query 
alone. Consequently, the impact of exposure bias on inference performance is 
reduced eﬀectively. 
We conduct extensive experiments on NQ320k [ 20] and TriviaQA [ 21], two 
mainstream public benchmarks, to evaluate the performance of KG-ASI in doc-
ument retrieval tasks. The main results consistently demonstrate the advantages 
of our proposed method over existing baselines. Our main contributions can be 
summarized as follows: 
• Along with the model-based retrieval blueprint, we propose KG-ASI, a novel 
end-to-end retriever for document retrieval, enhanced by an external knowl-
edge graph. By designing a new type of KG-enhanced semantic docid, our 
method compensates for the deﬁciency of structural information among doc-
uments in the limited unstructured corpus. 
• To mitigate the exposure bias in query generation, we implement a novel 
contrastive learning method based on ranking loss to ﬁne-tune the pre-trained 
query generation model, augmenting the quality of pseudo-queries. 
• Empirical results indicate the superiority of our method over existing 
approaches, meanwhile quantitative analysis reveals that KG-enhanced 
semantic docids are more inclined to assign the identical preﬁx to similar

[Vista original de la página 166](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-166.png)

## Página PDF 167

KG-ASI: A KG-Enhanced Model-Based Retriever
157
documents compared to the standard semantic docids, facilitating the train-
ing process. 
The remainder of the work is arranged as follows: Sect. 2 introduces related 
works in the ﬁeld, Sect. 3 elaborates on our method in detail, Sect. 4 presents 
experimental settings, results and analysis, the conclusions are drawn in Sect. 5. 
2
Related Work 
2.1
Traditional IR Methods 
Traditional IR methods typically follow a pipeline paradigm comprising three 
steps: “index-retrieve-rank”. The indexing and retrieval stages are particularly 
crucial to models, depending on their text embedding representation and index-
ing modes, existing studies are mainly categorized into sparse vector retrieval 
and dense vector retrieval. 1) sparse vector retrieval: TF-IDF and BM25 [ 1] 
are the most widely used classic sparse retrieval methods. They ﬁrst build an 
inverted index on the entire corpus, which encodes a series of term-based features 
such as term frequency, term position, document length etc. Then they calcu-
late the relevance between query and documents, enabling retrieval of relevant 
documents via lexical matching. Furthermore, more works [ 2, 22] employ word 
embedding to improve semantic matching of models, allowing for a more accu-
rate measurement of the semantic similarity between query terms and document 
terms. Recently, pre-trained language models have been explored for enhanc-
ing sparse retrieval methods. For example, DeepCT [ 23] utilizes contextualized 
text representation from BERT [ 24] encoder to obtain term importance for the 
inverted index, instead of using traditional term features. Other studies [ 3, 25] in 
this ﬁeld explicitly generate possible word expansion for queries and documents, 
alleviating the mismatches between query and document terms [ 4], subsequently 
increasing recall rates. 2) dense vector retrieval: diﬀering from sparse meth-
ods that rely on lexical overlap matching, dense vector retrieval methods ﬁrst 
embed each document from a corpus into a low-dimensional dense vector and 
establish a separated index. Then, the issued query is mapped into the identical 
latent space with the same dimensions, and the dot product or cosine similarity 
between the pair is calculated as a metric of relevance [ 7, 26]. Relevant docu-
ments are retrieved by Product Quantization (PQ) [ 6] or eﬃcient Approximate 
Nearest Neighbor (ANN) search algorithms [ 5]. With the development of PLM 
and the iterations of negative sampling strategies for contrastive learning [ 5, 7], 
dual-tower dense models, whose eﬀective capture of semantic relevance signiﬁ-
cantly relieves the term mismatch challenge [ 4], generally obtain higher-quality 
representations and better training samples, possessing notable retrieval perfor-
mance. DPR [ 7] demonstrated that the dual-encoder composed of independent 
BERT models outperforms the mainstream sparse retrievers by sampling simple 
in-batch negatives. Besides, considerable work has been dedicated to optimizing 
dense retrieval methods from various aspects. Multi-representation bi-encoder 
[ 27] is designed to encode multiple document embeddings for each single docu-
ment passage from diﬀerent perspectives, modeling ﬁne-grained query-document

[Vista original de la página 167](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-167.png)

![Página PDF 167, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-167.png)


<!-- PDF page 168 -->

## Página PDF 168

158
Z. Bi et al.
connections. Cross-encoder [ 28] treats query-document pair as an entire sentence, 
applying cross-attention mechanism for token-level semantic interaction. Hybrid 
model [ 29] that combines both approaches above also has been developed. 
2.2
Model-Based IR Methods 
Traditional IR models encompass a complex, pipelined multi-component frame-
work that includes indexing, retrieval and ranking stage. To simplify the model 
structure and retrieval process, diverse parts are compressed into a single seq2seq 
model that can be optimized end-to-end, leading to the proposal of Model-based 
IR methods. It fundamentally encodes the knowledge of all documents in cor-
pus into model’s parameters throughout end-to-end training implicitly [ 9], and 
each document is assigned a unique identiﬁer (docid), the whole retrieval pro-
cess is characterized by directly generating the identiﬁers of relevant documents 
given a query in an autoregressive manner. Existing research reviews two main 
types of the document identiﬁer: unstructured text-based identiﬁers and struc-
tured semantic identiﬁers. The former typically include Wikipedia page title 
[ 30, 31], URL [ 10], random or sequential number [ 8], which are literally read-
able, interpretable, and easy to understand. However, text-based docids lack 
semantic relationships among themselves, limiting generalization capabilities for 
unseen new documents [ 8]. The latter are acquired by ﬁrst producing docu-
ments’ dense embeddings using PLM, followed by executing hierarchical cluster-
ing [ 8, 12], diverse clustering [ 32] or product quantization [ 10]. The model is able 
to learn relational information between semantically related documents with the 
structured identiﬁers set which also renders the document retrieval less inter-
pretable. A wealth of studies has indicated that Model-based IR methods are 
applicable to a range of specialized tasks, such as entity linking [ 30], fact check-
ing [ 31], recommendation systems [ 33] and document retrieval [ 8, 11, 12]. GENRE 
[ 30] employs an autoregressive language model to directly generate entity names 
as retrieval results, accomplishing entity linking and disambiguation. GERE [ 31] 
was designed to fulﬁll the task of fact veriﬁcation, retrieving relevant evidence by 
outputting sentence identiﬁers. As for document retrieval, DSI [ 8] innovatively 
presents end-to-end training of a diﬀerentiable search indexer via a multi-task 
learning framework, utilizing both document contents and queries as indexing 
and retrieval task samples, rather than focusing on mapping queries to docu-
ment identiﬁers as a single learning objective, achieving better retrieval precision. 
SEAL [ 11] suggests using all distinctive n-grams of the passage as docids, out-
putting relevant words for retrieval. Furthermore, existing autoregressive retriev-
ers are capable of being improved by query generation [ 12], continuous learning 
[ 34], pre-training that reduces reliance on annotated data [ 10, 35], and better 
identiﬁers [ 36]. 
2.3
KG-Enhanced Methods for Retrieval 
In many NLP downstream tasks that depend on neural retrieval methods, par-
ticularly challenging KILT such as open-domain question answering [ 37] and fact

[Vista original de la página 168](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-168.png)

## Página PDF 169

KG-ASI: A KG-Enhanced Model-Based Retriever
159
checking [ 38], recent endeavors have been directed towards leveraging structured 
knowledge graphs as additional knowledge enhancement to improve the perfor-
mance of retriever models and the quality of results. For instances, UniK-QA [ 16] 
transforms structured KG knowledge triples and semi-structured tables into a 
ﬂattened format before integrating them into a uniﬁed corpus along with unstruc-
tured text, which enables evidence to cover more world knowledge. KAQA [ 15] 
builds a KG-based relation graph among candidate passages to calculate retrieve 
scores and answer span scores, advancing retrieval module through re-ranking. 
Similarly, Graph-Retriever [ 14] iteratively expands the graph in terms of KG 
relations between passages, with the goal of enhancing passage selection during 
answer extraction. Furthermore, KG-FiD [ 13] implements GNN to model struc-
tural and semantic information of candidate entities in KG, eﬀectively reducing 
noise in passage ﬁltering, from where performance improvement comes. However, 
the existing methods mentioned above still focus on the integration of KG with 
traditional retrieval methods, especially dense retrieval framework, applying KG 
enhancement to emerging model-based IR methods remains largely unexplored. 
3
Methods 
In this paper, we propose KG-ASI, an end-to-end neural retriever based on a 
pre-trained sequence-to-sequence language model. We explore how to integrate 
a knowledge graph into model-based IR methods as a means of external knowl-
edge enhancement, improving retrieval performance. Additionally, we attempt 
to alleviate the exposure bias in the query generation module, which is involved 
in generating training samples for the model-based retriever, inﬂuencing the 
training process. In the following sections, we elaborate on the details of our 
proposed model (Sect. 3.1). We ﬁrst introduce how to apply KG to construct 
a document-aligned entity subgraph on the corpus, then demonstrate how the 
graph-structured knowledge is learned via a graph neural network, GraphSAGE, 
and fused into entity nodes’ representations. Next, we detail the generation of 
document identiﬁers based on the updated node features, which contain both 
the semantic information of documents and structured information among docu-
ments. Finally, we show how to mitigate exposure bias in query generation utiliz-
ing a contrastive learning ﬁne-tuning paradigm based on ranking loss (Sect. 3.2). 
The overview of model’s workﬂow is illustrated in Fig. 1. 
3.1
KG-Enhanced Structured Identiﬁer 
KG Subgraph Construction. Introducing KG as an external knowledge 
enhancement essentially involves obtaining docids that cover richer structural 
information from KG, teaching the model to better understand the map-
ping between document semantics and identiﬁers during indexing. Referenc-
ing [ 13, 14], our proposed model ﬁrst constructs subgraph that containing valid 
corpus-related knowledge from KG for subsequent graph representation learning 
and docid generation. Assuming the external KG meets the following condition:

from e_{i}ei to e_{j}ej. 
Diﬀering from traditional dense methods, model-based retrieval models store 
all document information about the corpus [ 8], implicitly building an index 
within the parameters. Consequently, the scope of the corpus is limited by the 
capacity of the model’s parameters and is incapable of incorporating millions 
of documents as retrieval evidence in the manner of dual-tower retrievers such 
as DPR [ 7], merely including relevant documents that have appeared across 
dataset samples. As a result, we attempt to directly establish KG subgraph 
across all documents within the comprehensive corpus, aiming to preserve all 
potentially relevant information without encountering ineﬃciency and memory-
consumption. Note that multiple documents may be mapped to an identical 
entity, but each document can only be aligned to one certain entity, regarding 
the mapping function.

[Vista original de la página 170](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-170.png)

## Página PDF 171

KG-ASI: A KG-Enhanced Model-Based Retriever
161
The retrieval texts involved in existed studies [ 8, 10– 12, 34, 36] are usually 
English Wikipedia pages. To meet the above condition, our research adopts the 
English version of Wikidata [ 39] as external KG, whose entities also come from 
English Wikipedia pages, achieving text-entity alignment. Speciﬁcally, construct-
ing subgraph mainly consists of two steps: entity matching and triplet matching. 
1) Entity Matching: The vast majority of documents can be precisely matched 
to their corresponding KG entities, mainly through the “sitelink” attribute of 
Wikidata entities. This attribute provides the most accurate and eﬃcient way 
for text-entity alignment, where the value of key “title” demonstrates the title 
of corresponding Wikipedia page. Few remaining documents cannot be mapped 
to entities due to iterated database or homonym. An eﬀective solution is to per-
form numerous rounds of document matching using more dimensions of entity 
attributes such as “label”, “aliases”, and “description”. 2) Triplet Matching: Once 
the mapped entity set EE has been obtained, it is necessary to fetch all the rela-
tionships among them. the KG is converted into a simpliﬁed triplet set TT ﬁrstly, 
and the matching process is simply realized by inspecting whether both entities 
in each triplet of TT belong to EE. Taking the Wikidata entity “universe” as an 
example, its “sitelink” attribute points to the Wikipedia page title “Universe” 
that it aligns with, while its “description” attribute, such as “totality of planets, 
stars, galaxies...”, can also be used for text similarity calculation to retrieve the 
correct page “Universe” mentioned above. 
Entity Node Representation Learning with GNN. Graph neural net-
works eﬀectively encode graph-structured data by passing and aggregating node 
information from local neighbors [ 40], which is known as graph representation 
learning. Due to the large scale of the subgraph, common GNN frameworks such 
as GCN [ 41] and GAT [ 42], which perform transductive learning on ﬁxed graphs, 
struggle to learn the embeddings of all nodes eﬃciently at a low computational 
cost. To model the structure information in the KG, we utilize GraphSAGE 
[ 17] as the GNN model. It trains a set of learnable aggregation functions in an 
inductive fashion on each minibatch composed of sampled positive and nega-
tive node pairs, learning to aggregate features from the node’s local neighbors. 
For a target node, each aggregation function gathers information at a speciﬁed 
number of hops or search depth to memorize the connection patterns of nodes. 
Accordingly, by applying these aggregators, the well-trained GNN infers embed-
dings that integrate both graph-structured information from KG and contextual 
semantic information from documents for all entity nodes in the subgraph. The 
details of the GraphSAGE we used are elaborated as follows: 
Forward Propagation. Algorithm 1 describes the forward propagation process 
of the GraphSAGE module. Note that as the subgraph is transformed to an 
undirected graph with edge weights (detailed later in Sect. 4.1), the aggregators 
deﬁned in our module are modiﬁed to account for edge weights, varying from 
the original GraphSAGE model. Speciﬁcally, Algorithm 1 consists of two main 
stages, sampling and aggregating: Given a subgraph \mathcal{G} = \left(\mathcal{V}, \mathcal{E}\right)G = (V, E), the dense doc-
ument embedding representations \mathbf{x}_{v}xv corresponding to all entity nodes, \forall v\in \mathcal{V}∀v ∈V,

Aggregation Functions. The core components of GraphSAGE are alternative 
aggregation functions, which determine the way to aggregate and transfer infor-
mation from local neighbors. In our research, four types of aggregation functions 
are selected: 
1) GCN-like aggregator: This aggregator emulates the convolutional propagation 
method utilized in the original GCN model [ 41]. The aggregator performs a 
GCN-like operation for the target node u\in \mathcal{B}^{k}u ∈Bk at the layer kk, calculating 
the weighted average of the node’s own input embedding and those of its 
neighbors. As illustrated in line 12 of Algorithm 1, the aggregator can be 
demonstrated as follows: 
\bALT{} \mathbf{h}_{u}^{k}\gets \mathcal{\sigma}\Big(\mathbf{W}^{k}\cdot \frac{1}{\sum_{u'\in \mathcal{N}\left(u\right)}\mathcal{\alpha}_{u,u'}+1}\big(\mathbf{h}_{u}^{k-1}+\sum_{u'\in \mathcal{N}\left(u\right)}\mathcal{\alpha}_{u,u'}\cdot \mathbf{h}_{u'}^{k-1}\big)\Big)\eALT{} hk

Signiﬁcantly, our method treats edge weights as part of the graph-structured 
information that reﬂects the importance of each neighboring node. Hence, 
during the aggregation, the weighted average of the node representations is 
used, rather than a simple arithmetic mean of these representations. 
2) mean aggregator: The distinction from the GCN-like aggregator lies in the 
manner of the mean aggregator towards handling representations. It processes 
the representation of the target node and the weighted average of the neigh-
boring nodes’ representations separately through two independent fully con-
nected layers. These outputs are then summed and subjected to a non-linear 
activation function \sigmaσ, to yield the node representation: 
\bALT{} \mathbf{h}_{u}^{k}\gets \mathcal{\sigma}\Big(\mathbf{W}_{u}^{k}\cdot \mathbf{h}_{u}^{k-1} + \mathbf{W}_{\mathcal{N}\left(u\right)}^{k}\cdot \frac{1}{\sum_{u'\in \mathcal{N}\left(u\right)}\mathcal{\alpha}_{u,u'}}\big(\sum_{u'\in \mathcal{N}\left(u\right)}\mathcal{\alpha}_{u,u'}\cdot \mathbf{h}_{u'}^{k-1}\big)\Big)\eALT{} hk

\bALT{} \mathbf{h}_{u}^{k}\gets \mathcal{\sigma}\Big(\mathbf{W}_{u}^{k}\cdot \mathbf{h}_{u}^{k-1}+\mathbf{W}_{\mathcal{N}\left(u\right)}^{k}\cdot \mathrm{max}\big(\left{\mathcal{\alpha}_{u,u'}\cdot \mathcal{\sigma}\big(\mathbf{W}_{\mathrm{pool}}^{k}\mathbf{h}_{u'}^{k-1}\big),\forall u'\in \mathcal{N}\left(u\right)\right}\big)\Big)\eALT{} hk

Training. The process of generating document identiﬁers based on entity node 
embeddings is typically without a task-speciﬁc objective. Thus, a graph-related 
loss function is adopted in a fully unsupervised setting, training all weight param-
eters of layers via stochastic gradient descent. For a node u\in \mathcal{V}u ∈V, the positive 
sampler and negative sampler respectively select its direct neighbors \mathcal{N}\left(u\right)N (u), and 
NN unrelated nodes \left{u_{n}|n = 1, \ldots, N\right}{un|n = 1, . . . , N} which are beyond a ﬁxed number of hops, 
as the positive and negative samples. The learning of GraphSAGE aims to min-
imize the contrastive loss \mathcal{L}_{c}Lc: 
\bALT{} \mathcal{L}_{c}\left(\mathbf{z}_{u}\right) = -\log\big(\sigma\left(\mathbf{z}_u\cdot \mathbf{z}_{u'}\right)\big)-Q\cdot \mathbb{E}_{u_{n}\sim P_{n}\left(u\right)}\log\big(\sigma\left(-\mathbf{z}_{u}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{u_{n}}\right)\big)\eALT{} Lc (zu) = −log σ (zu · zu )
−Q · Eun∼Pn(u) log σ (−zu · zun)
(5) 
where \mathbf{z}_{u'}zu is the representation of a positive sample node u'\in \mathcal{N}\left(u\right)u ∈N (u), P_{n}Pn refers to 
a negative sampling distribution, and QQ is a factor that adjusts the contribution 
of negative samples to the loss. By minimizing the similarity metrics of features 
between uu and unrelated nodes while maximizing the similarity metrics between 
uu and its neighboring nodes, the network learns how to inject graph-structured 
information into node representations on the subgraph. 
Clustering for Structured Identiﬁer. Encoding unique document identiﬁers 
for the corpus employing clustering algorithms has proven eﬀective in capturing 
the text’s semantics [ 8, 12, 32]. our proposed method leverages the hierarchical 
k-means algorithm over the entity node representations output by GNN to gen-
erate structural semantic identiﬁers. It aims to integrate both contextualized 
information and structural information into the decoding process, improving 
the learning process of the retriever model. Given a corpus to be indexed, all 
documents are ﬁrst clustered into kk clusters by k-means. For the cluster with 
more than cc documents, k-means is applied recursively. As for the cluster includ-
ing cc documents or fewer, each document inside is assigned an index number 
n_{e}\in \left[0,c\right)ne ∈[0, c). When a document is clustered at level ii, it receives an index number 
n_{i}\in \left[0,k\right)ni ∈[0, k) according to the cluster it belongs to as well. As a result, a document 
that has been hierarchically clustered mm times has its identiﬁer string composed 
by sequentially concatenating the index numbers: n_{1}, \ldots, n_{m}, n_{e}n1, . . . , nm, ne, which is equiv-
alent to the deterministic index path from the root node to the leaf node of this 
document in the clustering tree. The updated representations of neighboring doc-
uments in the KG become more similar compared to those of distant documents. 
Therefore, their identiﬁers are likely to share the same preﬁxes theoretically, indi-
cating that the topological information of the subgraph is transferred into the 
structure of the identiﬁers. We deﬁne the ﬁnal output structured strings as the 
KG-enhanced semantic docids.

KG-ASI: A KG-Enhanced Model-Based Retriever

3.2
Fine-Tuning Query-Generation with Contrastive Learning 
End to end model-based IR methods are unable to see the content of documents 
explicitly during inference. This characteristic means that autoregressive retriev-
ers need to implicitly store all document information in the weight parameters 
during the training phase. If the retriever struggles to associate the semantics 
of documents within its parameters with the corresponding identiﬁers, it will be 
challenging to predict the identiﬁer based on a single unseen query. To address 
this issue, the indexing task [ 8] is created to bind document semantics with their 
identiﬁers, which is uniﬁed under a multi-task learning framework. This indexing 
task typically requires the model to predict the corresponding identiﬁers given 
a sequence of document tokens, allowing the model to learn to align documents 
with their identiﬁers. 
In particular, alongside sampling various sequences or sets of terms from 
the document text as input for indexing, pseudo-queries [ 12] represent another 
concise type. Distinct from real queries, semantic-related pseudo-queries are 
commonly generated by a pre-trained query generation model using documents 
as input. The pairs from pseudo-queries to identiﬁers enhance the scale and 
diversity of indexing samples, allowing the retriever to understand the mapping 
between document semantics and identiﬁers from broader perspectives. Funda-
mentally, the query generation module is a seq2seq model that converts the long 
token sequences of input documents into shorter ones for the pseudo-queries, 
which are expected to encapsulate crucial semantic information. Consequently, 
query generation also encounters the same problem as abstractive summariza-
tion: due to the teacher forcing strategy during training, the previous tokens on 
which the decoder relies for decoding the current token come from the correct 
reference sequence. Nevertheless, with respect to inference, these previous tokens 
predicted by the model itself may deviate from the reference, causing a training-
inference gap. This risk of performance degradation is formally recognized as 
exposure bias [ 18]. 
Inspired by solutions in abstractive summarization tasks [ 19], we introduce 
a novel method to tune the pre-trained query generation model. This method 
employs the contrastive learning with ranking loss, moving beyond the exist-
ing supervised ﬁne-tuning paradigm, to alleviate the impact of exposure bias on 
inference performance. The intuition behind this method stems from the nature 
of exposure bias, where training with maximum likelihood estimation (MLE) 
often leads the seq2seq model to produce outputs closely mirroring the refer-
ence sequence, allocating the entire probability of the target distribution to the 
reference. Such a deterministic distribution may hinder the model’s ability to 
eﬀectively account for other plausible candidate outputs beyond the reference, 
especially when meeting erroneous previous tokens during inference. Therefore, 
by applying this method, we aim for the query generation model to be able to: 
1) estimate the relative quality of diﬀerent candidate queries, 2) select the best 
option in light of evaluation, 3) possess strong generalization capabilities when 
dealing with unseen documents.

[Vista original de la página 175](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-175.png)

![Página PDF 175, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-175.png)

To achieve these goals, the query generation model is trained not only with 
normal MLE training but also as a reference-free evaluation model, endowing it 
with the ability to score the quality of candidate queries. Given a tuning sample 
TT, similar to the process applied in other supervised seq2seq model settings, the 
model is ﬁrst trained on TT using the standard cross-entropy loss when serving 
as a generative model: 
\bALT{} \mathcal{L}_{CE} = -\sum_{i=1}^{l}\sum_{s}^{}p_{\mathrm{true}}\left(s\mid D,Q_{<i}^{*}\right)\log p_{\theta}\left(s\mid D,Q_{<i}^{*};\theta\right)\eALT{} LCE = −
l

query whose length is ll, Q^{*} = \left{q^{*}_{1}, \ldots, q^{*}_{i}, \ldots, q^{*}_{l}\right}Q∗= {q∗
1, . . . , q∗

probability distribution of the token, which can be replaced by label smoothing. 
After generating candidate queries for TT, the model itself is further trained 
as a reference-free evaluation model. We deﬁne the ROUGE score R\left(Q, Q^{*}\right)R (Q, Q∗)
of a candidate query QQ given the reference query Q^{*}Q∗as the quality evaluation 
metric, then rank all candidate queries in TT: for the two diﬀerent candidate 
queries Q_{i}Qi and Q_{j}Qj in TT with the sorting indexes ii and jj, \forall i, j, i < j∀i, j, i < j, it holds that 
R\left(Q_{i},Q^{*}\right) > R\left(Q_{j},Q^{*}\right)R (Qi, Q∗) > R (Qj, Q∗). Based on the ranking results, the following ranking 
loss [ 43] is introduced as the optimization objective for evaluation model: 
\bALT{} \mathcal{L}_{R} = \sum_{i}^{}\sum_{j>i}^{}\max\big(0,s\left(Q_{j}\right)-s\left(Q_{i}\right)+\lambda_{ij}\big)\eALT{} LR =
i

max 0, s (Qj) −s (Qi) + λij
(7) 
where s\left(Q\right)s (Q) represents the evaluation score for the candidate query QQ by model, 
\lambda_{ij} =\left(j-i\right)\cdot \lambdaλij = (j −i) · λ denotes a penalty margin controlled by hyperparameter \lambdaλ. 
Intuitively, this contrastive loss calculates the summing of the diﬀerence in the 
model’s evaluation scores between any two adjacent candidates in the ranking 
queue. When the model’s evaluation score for the lower-quality candidate Q_{j}Qj is 
higher than that for the higher-quality candidate Q_{i}Qi, a loss is added to the sum-
ming as a penalty. The evaluation score s\left(Q\right)s (Q) is the length-normalized estimated 
log probability for candidate query QQ: 
\bALT{} s\left(Q\right) = \frac{1}{\left|Q\right|^\alpha}\sum_{t=1}^{l}{\log p_{\theta}\left(q_{t}\vert|D,Q_{<t};\theta\right)}\eALT{} s (Q) =
1

(8) 
\alphaα is a hyperparameter controlling the length normalization. Thus, the rank-
ing loss function assumes a non-deterministic distribution, guiding the model to 
assign higher decoding probabilities to higher-quality candidate queries, mitigat-
ing exposure bias. The two types of loss functions mentioned above are combined 
into a multi-task learning framework, to preserve both the generation and eval-
uation abilities of the model simultaneously: 
\bALT{} \mathcal{L}_{mul} = \mathcal{L}_{CE} + \gamma\cdot \mathcal{L}_{R}\eALT{} Lmul = LCE + γ · LR
(9) 
where \gammaγ is the weight hyperparameter of the ranking loss. The tuning process of 
our query generation model is shown in Fig. 2.
