4.1
Datasets and Knowledge Source 
We assess the document retrieval performance of our proposed method on two 
diﬀerent scales of widely recognized public benchmarks: Natural Questions [ 20] 
and TriviaQA [ 21]. For Natural Questions, each piece of sample is a query-
document pair, featuring a real question from Google search and a Wikipedia 
article containing the answer span. We adopt the NQ320k subset, comprising 
320k sample pairs, and further deduplicate the corpus documents by title com-
parison. TriviaQA is a smaller dataset for document retrieval, comprising 78k 
query-document pairs scraped from trivia and quiz-league websites. We adhere to 
the data preprocessing setup in NCI [ 12] for both datasets, and Table 1 displays 
their statistics. 
We utilize the English Wikidata [ 39] as the external knowledge graph, which 
contains 2.7M aligned entities, 974 types of relationships, and 14M entity triplets, 
ensuring that Wikipedia documents in the corpus are fully matched and linked 
to their corresponding entities. The subgraph initially constructed through the 
matching process is a complex heterogeneous directed multi-edge graph. For 
the convenience of subsequent experiments, we do not consider the relational 
information and simplify this heterogeneous directed multi-edge graph into a 
weighted undirected graph, where the weight of each edge represents the num-
ber of triplets between two entities. Table 1 summarizes the statistics of the 
constructed entity subgraphs. Importantly, a very small portion of documents 
are not able to be aligned to KG entities due to the version iterations, thus their 
embedding representations are not updated by the GNN.

4.2
Evaluation Metrics 
We evaluate the performance of our proposed method, KG-ASI, and all base-
line models on three commonly-accepted metrics for document retrieval: Recall, 
Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR) and R-precision. Recall@kk quantiﬁes the fre-
quency with which the desired document is successfully hit by the top-kk candi-
date results. It is also known as Precision@kk in some studies, deﬁned as: 
\bALT{} \mathrm{Recall}@k = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}|}\sum_{q=1}^{|\mathcal{Q}|}\frac{r_{q,k}}{k}\eALT{} Recall@k =

\bALT{} \mathrm{MRR} = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}|}\sum_{q=1}^{|\mathcal{Q}|}\frac{1}{\mathrm{rank}_{q}}\eALT{} MRR =

4.3
Implementation Details 
We deﬁne the retriever in KG-ASI based on the open-source PLM, T5-base 1
[ 25], with a maximum input length of 64 tokens. Following NCI [ 12], the same 
PAWA decoder is applied to our model to help focus on the structural informa-
tion within the preﬁxes at each decoding step. For the GNN model, we deploy a 
single-layer GraphSAGE 2 with the best-performing LSTM aggregator, provided 
by the Deep Graph Library (DGL) framework. The dense document embeddings
1 https://huggingface.co/google-t5/t5-base. 
2 https://github.com/dmlc/dgl/tree/master/examples/pytorch/graphsage.

[Vista original de la página 178](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-178.png)

169
are encoded by a BERT 3 encoder, and serve as initial node representations for the 
subgraph. Regarding the query generation module, we initialize its weights with 
the DocT5Query 4 checkpoint. Beyond pseudo-queries, we also consider extract-
ing the ﬁrst 64 tokens from each document to serve as input for the training 
samples used in indexing, similar to what is done in DSI [ 8]. 
The experiments are conducted on an Nvidia RTX A6000 GPU. We employ 
the AdamW optimizer with linear decay to optimize the end-to-end T5 retriever, 
setting the initial learning rates for encoder and decoder to 2e-4 and 1e-4, respec-
tively. The retriever uses a training batch size of 64, and is trained for 50 epochs. 
R-drop and dropout ratio are set to 0.15 and 0.1 respectively during training. 
4.4
Baseline Methods 
To evaluate the eﬀectiveness of our proposed method, we collect two categories 
of retrieval methods as baselines for comparison, including classic traditional 
retrieval methods (1–6) and recent model-based retrieval methods (7–11). Con-
sidering KG-enhanced traditional methods as baselines for comparison will be 
reserved for future research. 
1) BM25 + DocT5Query [ 1]: BM25 is a highly eﬃcient sparse retrieval method 
that uses TF-IDF features to measure term weights. DocT5Query expands 
a document with possible queries predicted by a ﬁnetuned T5 model with 
this document as the input, enhancing the performance of BM25. 
2) SPLADE [ 44]: SPLADE employs a BERT-based encoder to encode text 
sequences into sparse lexical representations, facilitating sparse retrieval. 
3) DPR [ 7]: DPR, a dense retrieval method based on a dual-encoder archi-
tecture, is trained with in-batch negatives and hard negatives sampled by 
BM25, making it one of the most robust and eﬀective methods. 
4) ANCE [ 5]: ANCE mines dynamic global hard negatives to train a RoBERTa-
based dual-encoder model, which is diﬀerent from DPR. 
5) Sentence-T5 [ 26]: This approach is also a dual-encoder dense retriever which 
utilizes T5 model to encode continuous sentence embeddings. 
6) Individual Top-k & Joint Top-k [ 45]: Two variants of an end-to-end dense 
retrieval method that combines unsupervised pre-train with supervised ﬁne-
tuning, with the distinction lying in the downstream task’s approach to 
handling kk candidate documents. 
7) DSI [ 8]: DSI innovatively utilizes a seq2seq model to map queries to relevant 
document identiﬁers in an end-to-end manner. We consider two representa-
tive variants, DSI-Semantic and DSI-Atomic, which employ the root-to-leaf 
paths of a hierarchical clustering tree and arbitrary tokens as docids respec-
tively. 
8) NCI [ 12]: NCI employs a preﬁx-aware weight-adaptive decoder and pro-
poses the generation of multiple pseudo-queries for each document via 
DocT5Query to augment the training data.

9) SEAL [ 11]: SEAL treats distinctive ngrams appearing in the collection as 
docid. Unlike DSI or NCI, it calculates matching scores under a separate 
full-text substring FM-index, rather than an index built into parameters. 
10) GENRE [ 30]: GENRE distinguishes itself by producing unique titles of doc-
uments as docids. The original model is trained across a series of KILT tasks 
in a multi-task setting. 
11) Ultron [ 10]: The model adopts a three-stage training pipeline, dividing 
docids into two types: document URLs and PQ docids, which are denoted 
as Ultron-URL and Ultron-PQ respectively. 
4.5
Experimental Results and Analysis 
Comparison with Baselines. We conduct an extensive comparison between 
KG-ASI and several baselines on Natural Questions and TriviaQA benchmarks. 
The main results are reported in Table 2 and Table 3. 
For the two main categories of retrieval methods outlined in the baselines, 
the Model-based retrievers, such as SEAL, NCI, and Ultron-URL, match or 
even outperform the strong traditional index-based baselines in most situations. 
The formerly top-ranked Model-based retriever, NCI, improves the solid dense 
retriever Sentence-T5 by 12.3% for recall@1, 2.2% for recall@10 and 9% for 
MRR@100 on NQ320k. Similarly, a signiﬁcant performance diﬀerence between 
these two types of methods can also be observed in the results on TriviaQA. We 
argue that the superiority of Model-based retrieval method lies in their ability to

jointly optimize indexing and retrieval stages end-to-end within a uniﬁed multi-
task learning framework. In comparison with the complicated index-retrieve-rank 
pipelines of traditional methods, it is more closely tailored to the ﬁnal retrieval 
target. 
For the baselines associated with traditional methods, Sentence-T5 achieves 
competitive performance across all metrics on NQ320k, while Individual Top-k 
shows similar success on TriviaQA. However, their performances are relatively 
inferior to our KG-ASI model, which gains an improvement of 13.7% for recall@1, 
2.9% for recall@10, 10.3% for MRR@100 over Sentence-T5 on NQ320k, and 
exceeds Individual Top-k by 13.4% on recall@5, 10.9% on recall@20, 9.7% on 
recall@100 on TriviaQA. The results underscore the remarkable ability of KG-
ASI in document retrieval, showcasing its advantage over traditional retrieval 
methods. 
Moreover, our proposed model also leads all the baselines which belong to 
the model-based methods by a signiﬁcant margin on both datasets. This can 
be attributed to the advanced KG-enhanced semantic docids and to more pre-
cise pseudo-queries that capture key document semantics. Concretely, we equally 
compare KG-ASI with four model-based baselines that employing diﬀerent types 
of docid, where DSI-Atomic refers to the DSI model with atomic docid, SEAL 
uses full n-grams as docid, Ultron-PQ corresponds to a product quantization 
docid, and Ultron-URL utilizes URL as docid. On NQ320k, KG-ASI outper-
forms all of them across all evaluated metrics. For KG-ASI, we notice relative 
performance gains of 7.4%, 4.7%, 2.2%, and 6.7% over the best model SEAL of 
these four, on metrics recall@1, recall@10, recall@100, and MRR@100, reveal-
ing that structured semantic docid prevails over other types of docids in docu-
ment retrieval. When it comes to NCI which takes the same type of semantic 
docids, KG-ASI is relatively higher than it in terms of performance on both 
datasets as well. In our opinion, the main reasons of the advantage are 1) identi-

ﬁers obtain richer global structural knowledge among the entities from updated 
entity node representations, 2) pseudo-queries generated by ﬁne-tuned query 
generation module eﬀectively capture exact key semantic information, which 
adequately characterizes the documents. These two reasons assist KG-ASI in 
better understanding the mapping relations from documents to their docids in 
indexing stage, facilitating the end-to-end training process. 
In summary, these results highlight the eﬀectiveness of KG-enhanced seman-
tic docids and higher-quality pseudo-queries, which collaboratively augment the 
performance of the retriever. Their individual inﬂuences are further discussed in 
subsequent sections. 
Ablation Study. In this paper, we devise a novel method to enhance document 
identiﬁers using an external knowledge graph for document retrieval. Besides, we 
also propose ﬁne-tuning the query generation module with contrastive learning as 
a new attempt to alleviate exposure bias. To empirically verify the eﬀectiveness of 
these two improvements on the ﬁnal results, we conduct ablation studies on both 
datasets. By disabling each improvement individually, we acquire two ablation 
variants, denoted as “w/o KG-enhanced docids” and “w/o DocT5Query tuning”, 
respectively. Table 4 demonstrates the ablation results. 
Our initial focus is on evaluating performance with the KG-enhanced method 
for docids removed. To this end, the variant without KG-enhanced docids 
replaces them with standard semantic docids, derived from hierarchically clus-
tering the BERT-encoded dense embeddings of the documents. Table 4 demon-
strates that disabling KG-enhanced semantic docids results in the biggest drop 
in overall results, highlighting its critical contribution to the model performance. 
Secondly, we aim to measure the metrics of the variant without DocT5Query 
tuning by directly initializing the query generation module with pre-trained 
DocT5Query weights. Consequently, the omission of the ﬁne-tuning process also 
causes a decline across all metrics, indicating that our ﬁne-tuning method for 
query generation signiﬁcantly boosts the retrieval performance. In other words, 
the decline in the test set conﬁrms that our approach eﬀectively mitigates the 
impact of exposure bias on the model’s inference performance. 
In general, these ﬁndings prove that the additional graph structural infor-
mation among entities in docids, and the improved pseudo-queries, enable the

## Página PDF 183

KG-ASI: A KG-Enhanced Model-Based Retriever
173
Model-based retriever to more eﬀectively recognize the potential semantic mean-
ings of documents and the mapping relations between docids and documents. 
Furthermore, utilizing KG as external enhancement is validated as a reasonable 
and eﬀective strategy for developing a strong Model-based retrieval method. 
Quantitative Analysis. According to the comparison results, diﬀerent types 
of document identiﬁers have varying eﬀects on the performance of model-based 
retrieval methods. To intuitively demonstrate the advantages of our designed 
KG-enhanced semantic docids over other types of identiﬁers, we visualize the 
similarity distribution between documents with identical docid preﬁxes for KG-
ASI and the baseline NCI, conducting a straightforward quantitative analysis 
on both datasets. Speciﬁcally, we ﬁrst randomly select 100 documents from the 
set of docids with the identical two-level preﬁx. For instance, docids “1-3-25” 
and “1-3-8” share the same two-level preﬁx “1-3”. After that, we calculate the 
cosine similarity between the embeddings of these documents pairwise. For NCI, 
its document embeddings are encoded by pre-trained BERT, while for KG-ASI, 
they are the updated node representations output by the GNN. In the end, we 
count the number of document pairs whose similarities fall within each interval 
of length of 0.025 and plot the distribution histograms of two models separately 
on NQ320k and TriviaQA. 
As shown in Fig. 3 and Fig. 4, the distributions show characteristics of the 
standard normal distribution, with some deviations in KG-ASI on both datasets. 
Compared to NCI, the document embeddings from KG-ASI are generally more 
similar, suggesting that our KG-enhanced semantic docids tend to assign the 
identical preﬁx to similar documents. From our perspective, similar documents 
assigned to distinct preﬁxes could pose challenges for the end-to-end retriever 
in building an implicit index within parameters, leading to performance loss. In 
other words, this tendency in assignment of our method could likely be a reason 
for the advantages of KG-enhanced semantic docids.

documents on TriviaQA.

## Página PDF 184

174
Z. Bi et al.
5
Conclusion 
In this work, we present KG-ASI, a novel model-based retrieval method that 
enhances a pre-trained language model through an external knowledge graph for 
document retrieval. Through eﬃcient graph representation learning on a corpus-
related KG subgraph, our proposed KG-enhanced semantic docids capture richer 
global structural information among corpus documents, facilitating model train-
ing. Moreover, for the ﬁrst time, a ﬁne-tuning approach based on contrastive 
learning is applied to pseudo-query generation to mitigate exposure bias, ele-
vating sample quality in indexing. Empirical results on both mainstream public 
datasets show that our method is able to improve retrieval performance over 
existing baselines, demonstrating its eﬀectiveness. 
In our current work, additional beneﬁcial information in the knowledge graph, 
such as relational information from triplets, remains unutilized by the model. 
Exploring how to fully utilize external knowledge sources will be a direction for 
future work.

## Página PDF 188

Free-Rider Attack Based on Data-Free 
Knowledge Distillation in Federated 
Learning 
Qiang Fu1, Xiaodong Fu1,2(B) 
, Li Liu1, Jiaman Ding1, and Lianyin Jia1 
1 Faculty of Information Engineering and Automation, Kunming University 
of Science and Technology, Kunming 650504, China 
xiaodong_fu@hotmail.com 
2 Yunnan Key Laboratory of Computer Technology Application, Kunming University 
of Science and Technology, Kunming 650504, China 
Abstract. The federated learning, characterized by multi-party collab-
orative training and the submission of model updates rather than raw 
data, is vulnerable to free-rider attacks. Free-rider disguise themselves 
as benign clients by submitting fake updates to minimize their data 
contribution, while expecting to beneﬁt from the well-trained global 
model. Numerous free-rider attack methods have been proposed, but 
they just simply utilize the parameters of the global model to create 
low-quality fake model updates and do not consider the evolution fre-
quency of the attacker’s model weights. To address these challenges, we 
propose a novel free-rider attack method based on data-free knowledge 
distillation. Speciﬁcally, we use the global and local models as a joint 
discriminator, along with a lightweight generative network, to generate 
pseudo-data. This pseudo-data serves as input for knowledge distillation 
between the global and local model to create the model updates. Fur-
thermore, we design a MixUp-based local-adaptive data augmentation 
method to augment the generated pseudo-data. Our method eﬀectively 
transfers knowledge from the global model to the attacker’s fake updates, 
making them more deceptive. Moreover, the knowledge distillation pro-
cess ensures a reasonable evolution frequency of the attacker’s model 
weights. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method achieves 
superior attack performance under robust aggregation defense models. 
Keywords: Federated learning · Free-rider attack · Data-free 
knowledge distillation · Data generation · Data augmentation 
1
Introduction 
Federated learning [ 16, 21, 22, 27], a novel distributed machine learning paradigm, 
has been proposed to address data privacy and security issues in artiﬁcial intel-
ligence. It allows diﬀerent clients to keep their private data locally, uploading

https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-93257-1_10

<!-- PDF page 189 -->

## Página PDF 189

180
Q. Fu et al.
only parameter updates during the training process to protect the privacy and 
security of local data. Consequently, federated learning has gained increasing 
popularity and has been applied in various ﬁelds, such as healthcare [ 4, 11], 
smart city [ 1, 10] and ﬁnance [ 18, 19]. 
Despite its novel training approach compared to traditional machine learning, 
federated learning remains vulnerable to attacks [ 7] such as poisoning attacks 
[ 20] and inference attacks [ 15]. Additionally, some opportunists launch free-rider 
attacks [ 6, 25, 30] in this multi-party collaborative training environment. Free-
rider attacks aim to minimize the attacker’s contribution, especially in terms 
of private data, while expecting to obtain a model of the same quality as other 
benign clients. Free-rider attacks undermine fairness among participating clients, 
reducing their willingness to contribute high-quality data to the community 
of federated learning. Furthermore, free-rider attackers disguise themselves as 
benign clients, stealing global model updates, which facilitates other attacks 
and increases potential privacy and security threats. 
Existing free-rider attack methods simply utilize parameters of received 
global model to generate fake model updates, such as adding noise to the param-
eters of the global model to simulate parameter updates [ 6] and calculating devi-
ation of global model parameters between local model parameters and used it to 
predict model updates with the Adam optimizer [ 25]. These methods only con-
sider generating fake updates from the perspective of model parameters, ignor-
ing the global knowledge contained in the global model. Consequently, the fake 
updates generated by these methods are diﬃcult to match the quality of the 
model updates submitted by benign clients, and these low-quality fake updates 
interfere with the aggregation of the global model to some extent. Addition-
ally, From defense perspective, most detection models against malicious clients, 
including free-riders, focus on analyzing the parameters of clients’ model. Chen 
et al. [ 3] reconsider the local behavior of free-riders, noting that free-riders typ-
ically do not engage in model training. Consequently, their local model weight 
evolving frequency diﬀers from that of benign clients. Inspired by this, they pro-
posed a targeted defense model that detects free-riders by comparing the model 
weight evolving frequency. 
To improve the quality of the forged parameter updates and achieve a rea-
sonable model weight evolving frequency, we introduce knowledge distillation 
into free-rider attacks for the ﬁrst time. We utilize knowledge distillation to 
generate the fake updates required for the attack. Since free-riders are self-
ishly protective of their private data, we generate pseudo-data as the input for 
knowledge distillation, forming data-free knowledge distillation (DFKD) to avoid 
using the free-riders’ private dataset. Furthermore, to reduce the amount of gen-
erated data and consequently the computational load, we designed a MixUp-
based local-adaptive data augmentation method. We generate synthetic pseudo-
data amounting to only 10% of the dataset of benign clients and then augment 
this data to get an equivalent amount of augmented data through data aug-
mentation. Our method allows free-riders to provide high-quality and stability 
model updates. The knowledge distillation process ensures a reasonable evolution

## Página PDF 190

Free-Rider Attack with Data-Free Knowledge Distillation
181
frequency of the attacker’s model weights. Additionally, our method is not sensi-
tive to the approaches taken in the initial stage. The main contributions of this 
paper are summarized as follows. 
1. We ﬁrst explore and deﬁne the two stages of free-rider attacks in federated 
learning. Additionally, we propose a new free-rider attacks method. 
2. We introduce data-free knowledge distillation into free-rider attacks to gen-
erate fake updates to address the instability and low quality of the attacker’s 
model updates. To further reduce the amount of generated data in data-free 
knowledge distillation and minimize computational overhead, we design a 
MixUP-based local-adaptive data augmentation method. 
3. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed method achieves bet-
ter free-rider attack performance compared to other state-of-the-art methods 
under diﬀerent defense models. 
2
Related Work 
2.1
Date-Free Knowledge Distillation 
Data-free knowledge distillation is a method for transferring knowledge from 
a teacher model to a student model without access to the raw training data. 
Traditional knowledge distillation uses the raw training data, allowing the stu-
dent model to mimic the teacher model’s outputs on the same data to achieve 
knowledge transfer. Since there is no access to the raw data, data-free knowledge 
distillation methods generate pseudo data from teacher model instead of using 
raw data. Do et al. [ 5] utilized the sum of a noise vector and a class embedding 
vector as the input to the generator, combined with an exponential moving aver-
age copy of the generator, to generate data for jointly training the generator and 
the student model. Tran et al. [ 24] utilized the output of a label-text embed-
ding, processed through a learnable noise layer, as the input to the generator, 
adversarially generating data with the teacher model and the student model. 
In federated learning, Zhang et al. [ 29] utilized a noise vector combined with a 
one-hot vector as the input of the generator, generating data from various local 
models of client to ﬁne-tune the global model using knowledge distillation. 
2.2
Free-Rider Attack in Federated Learning 
Free-rider attack primarily target cooperative learning or distributed systems, 
where one or more participants attempt to beneﬁt from the system without 
making corresponding contributions. In the context of federated learning, this 
behavior manifests as attackers deliberately reducing their data contributions or 
even contributing nothing when providing model updates or gradient informa-
tion. The aim is to minimize the cost of participation while still beneﬁting from 
the model trained collectively by all participants. 
The process of traditional federated learning, as shown in Fig. 1. At ﬁrst, 
the server distributes the initialized global model to each client (Step 1). After

[Vista original de la página 190](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-190.png)

![Página PDF 190, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-190.png)


<!-- PDF page 191 -->

## Página PDF 191

182
Q. Fu et al.
receiving the global model from the server, each client trains the model using 
their local dataset (Step 2.a) and then uploads the required parameter updates 
to the server (Step 2.b). Upon receiving the model updates from the clients, the 
server aggregates these updates according to a speciﬁc aggregation algorithm to 
form a new global model (Step 3.a) and sent it to each client (Step 3.b). Then 
repeat Step 2 and Step 3 until the global model converges. However, for free-
rider, upon receiving the global model in Step 2, they utilize it to forge model 
updates to be uploaded instead of training the model with their local data. 
Fig. 1. The Schematic Diagram of Federated Learning with Benign Clients and Free-
riders 
In implementing free-rider attacks, free-riders disguise themselves as benign 
clients by uploading forged model updates to the server. They have access to 
commonly available information in the federated learning system, including the 
global model, loss function, learning rate, and FL aggregation rules. Free-riders 
use this information to design methods for generating local fake updates, and 
upload them to the server each round, pretending to be benign clients. How-
ever, we have found that for free-riders, the initial rounds of federated learning 
lack suﬃcient information to generate the necessary fake updates compared to 
subsequent rounds. In other words, free-riders only obtain the initial informa-
tion required for training in the initial rounds. This initialization information 
is insuﬃcient for free-riders to design fake updates. In subsequent rounds, the 
global model aggregates local knowledge from various clients, and this signiﬁcant 
change can allows free-riders to use the parameters of the global model to design 
and generate fake model updates. 
Therefore, we divide the free-rider attack into two stages: the initial stage 
and the fake update generation stage. The initial stage corresponds to the initial 
communication rounds of federated learning. In this stage, benign participants 
receive the initialized global model and train it on local. Although free-riders

[Vista original de la página 191](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-191.png)

![Página PDF 191, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-191.png)


<!-- PDF page 192 -->

## Página PDF 192

Free-Rider Attack with Data-Free Knowledge Distillation
183
also upload fake updates during this stage, these updates signiﬁcantly diﬀer from 
those submitted by benign clients. The fake update generation stage corresponds 
to the rounds following the initial stage. In this stage, free-riders acquire global 
knowledge from the received global model and use it to generate fake model 
updates for upload. These model updates incorporate some genuine knowledge 
from the global model, making them more deceptive compared to the updates 
from the initial stage. 
Free-riders can employ diﬀerent strategies in the two stages. In the initial 
stage, those who are not entirely selﬁsh with their private data can use a small 
portion of their private dataset to train the local model. Conversely, those who 
are entirely selﬁsh with their private data can use public datasets to train the 
local model [ 25] or design noise to simulate the model parameter updates [ 6]. 
In the fake update generation stage, existing studies have employed various 
methods. Fraboni et al. [ 6] proposed a Gaussian noise-based free-rider attack, 
where designed Gaussian noise is added to the received global model parameters 
to simulate parameter updates that are submitted to the server. Lin et al. [ 17] 
proposed a delta weight free-rider attack in a gradient aggregation-based feder-
ated learning framework, where calculated the diﬀerences between the models 
received in the last two rounds to fabricate and submit fake model updates. 
Wang et al. [ 25] calculated the deviation of global model parameters between 
local model parameters and then used the ADAM optimizer to generate pre-
dicted model parameters, forging the required parameter updates to be submit-
ted. Although this method reduces the distance between the accuracy and loss 
of the model submitted by the attacker and that of the benign clients, it fails to 
eﬀectively evade the most advanced defense models of the time. Therefore, the 
authors allowed attackers to train the initial global model on a public dataset in 
the initial rounds of federated learning, and continued to use the previous forged 
parameter update method in subsequent rounds. 
However, the aforementioned methods simply utilize parameters of received 
global model to generate fake model updates without eﬀectively considering the 
global knowledge contained within the global model. This enables some defense 
models, such as RFFL [ 26], to distinguish between attackers and benign clients 
by evaluating the quality of the model updates. 
3
Proposed Method 
3.1
Synthesizing Pseudo Data 
To meet the need for attackers to selﬁshly protect their private data in free-rider 
attacks, We generate pseudo-data to replace private data and use data aug-
mentation to augment the pseudo-data. Speciﬁcally, we construct a lightweight 
generator network GG, using the obtained global model as the teacher model TT
and the local model as the student model SS. The teacher and student models are 
combined into a joint discriminator to update the generator. We ﬁrst sample a 
batch of random noise zz from a Gaussian distribution and assign them random 
class labels \hat{y}ˆy. Then, zz is input to the generator to obtain the synthetic data

![Página PDF 192, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-192.png)

184
Q. Fu et al.
\hat{x} = G(z)ˆx = G(z). After that, we optimize the generator GG with the loss function \mathcal{L}_{G}LG as 
follows. 
\bALT{}\label{equ1} \mathcal{L}_{G} = \lambda_{cls}\mathcal{L}_{cls}(\mathcal{T}(\hat{x}), \hat{y}) - \lambda_{KL}\mathcal{L}_{KL}(\mathcal{T}(\hat{x}), \mathcal{S}(\hat{x})) + \lambda_{BN}\mathcal{L}_{BN}(\mathcal{T}(\hat{x}))\eALT{} LG = λclsLcls(T (ˆx), ˆy) −λKLLKL(T (ˆx), S(ˆx)) + λBNLBN(T (ˆx))
(1) 
where \mathcal{L}_{cls}(\mathcal{T}(\hat{x}), \hat{y})Lcls(T (ˆx), ˆy) is the classiﬁcation loss of the teacher model TT on the sample 
pair (\hat{x}, \hat{y})(ˆx, ˆy), and in this paper, we use cross-entropy loss. Through \mathcal{L}_{cls}(\mathcal{T}(\hat{x}), \hat{y})Lcls(T (ˆx), ˆy), 
the generated data is made to have high conﬁdence for the teacher model. 
\mathcal{L}_{KL}(\mathcal{T}(\hat{x}), \mathcal{S}(\hat{x}))LKL(T (ˆx), S(ˆx)) is the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence loss between the out-
put of the student model SS and the teacher model TT on \hat{x}ˆx. The negative value of 
\mathcal{L}_{KL}LKL forces the generator to produce data that have a larger discrepancy between 
the teacher and student models, which is conducive to exploring the data syn-
thesis distribution and improving the eﬀective knowledge transfer between the 
teacher and student models. \mathcal{L}_{BN}LBN is a common loss in DFKD, used to constrain 
the mean and variance of the features in the batch normalization layer. 
Furthermore, to accelerate the generation of pseudo-data and reduce the 
use of computational resources, we adopt the method proposed by Tran et al. 
[ 24], adding a learnable noise layer before the generator and using label-text 
embedding instead of noise-label pairs as input. This allows for the generation 
of the required pseudo-data in just a few steps. 
We then design a MixUp-based local-adaptive data augmentation to enhance 
the same amount of data for subsequent knowledge distillation. MixUp is a data 
augmentation method proposed by Zhang et al. [ 28], which generates synthetic 
instances by linearly mixing the features of given instance pairs and their corre-
sponding labels. Speciﬁcally, MixUp generates enhanced instances (\tilde{x}, \tilde{y})(˜x, ˜y) through 
linear interpolation of a randomly selected pair of instances (x_{i}, y_{i})(xi, yi) and (x_{j}, y_{j})(xj, yj)
\bALT{}\label{equ2} \tilde{x} = \lambda x_{i} + (1-\lambda) x_{j}, \quad \tilde{y} = \lambda y_{i} + (1-\lambda) y_{j}\eALT{} ˜x = λxi + (1 −λ)xj,
˜y = λyi + (1 −λ)yj
(2) 
where \lambda \sim Beta(\alpha, \alpha), \alpha \in (0, \infty)λ ∼Beta(α, α), α ∈(0, ∞). To better apply this to synthetic data, we 
ﬁrst utilize the GradCAM++ method in [ 2] to obtain the region of interest of the 
teacher model on the generated data. We then extract this region as an instance 
for Mixup, allowing the student model to focus on the areas of the synthetic 
data that highly activate the teacher model’s response. Next, we consider that 
diﬀerent synthetic data contribute diﬀerently to the model and adaptively assign 
the mixing proportion of the synthetic data, which is the \lambdaλ in Eq. 2. Speciﬁcally, 
we utilize the teacher model’s prediction conﬁdence score on the synthetic data to 
generate the mixing proportion \lambdaλ for that data. The \lambdaλ in Eq. 2 will be calculated 
as follows. 
\bALT{}\label{equ3} \lambda = \frac{max(T(\hat{x}_i))}{max(T(\hat{x}_i)) + max(T(\hat{x}_j))}\eALT{} λ =
max(T(ˆxi))
max(T(ˆxi)) + max(T(ˆxj))
(3) 
Based on the new \lambdaλ calculated through Eq. 3, we perform data augmentation 
on the synthetic data to generate an equal number of augmented data. These 
augmented data will be used together with the synthetic data for subsequent 
knowledge distillation.

## Página PDF 194

Free-Rider Attack with Data-Free Knowledge Distillation
185
3.2
Free-Rider Attack Based on Data-Free Knowledge Distillation 
The main limitation of existing free-rider attack methods is that the quality of 
the submitted model updates is much lower than that of the benign clients. By 
measuring the quality of the parameter updates submitted by each participant, 
it is possible to distinguish malicious clients from benign clients from. This is pri-
marily because current free-rider attack methods simply utilize the parameters 
of the global model when generating fake parameter updates, ignoring the knowl-
edge from the global model. Additionally, [ 3] proposed a novel defense method 
against free-rider attack, which provide another perspective by considering the 
behavior of free-riders. Previous free-rider attacks did not account for the reason-
able the evolution frequency of the attacker’s model weights during generating 
model updates, which has also become a distinguishing factor between attackers 
and benign clients. 
To address both issues, we apply data-free knowledge distillation to free-
riding attack, utilizing knowledge distillation to obtain the required parameter 
updates during the construction of fake updates. Through knowledge distillation, 
it is eﬀectively utilize the knowledge in the global model to improve the quality 
of fake model updates, and the process of knowledge distillation also makes 
the evolution frequency of attacker’s model parameter similar to that of benign 
clients. As shown in Algorithm 1, we utilize the global model received from the 
server as the teacher model TT and the local model as the student model SS. 
We train the generator using teacher model TT and student model SS to obtain 
synthetic data and perform data augmentation. Then, using the synthetic data, 
we carry out knowledge distillation on teacher model TT and student model SS by 
optimizing the distillation loss \mathcal{L}_{KD}LKD as follows. 
\bALT{} \begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{KD} = &\ \alpha_{KL}\mathcal{L}_{KL}(\mathcal{T}(\hat{x}), \mathcal{S}(\hat{x})) + \alpha_{cls}\mathcal{L}_{cls}(\mathcal{S}(\hat{x}), \hat{y}) \\ & + \alpha_{feat}\mathcal{L}_{feat}(\mathcal{T}_{feat}(\hat{x}), \mathcal{S}_{feat}(\hat{x}))\label{equ4} \end{aligned}\eALT{} LKD = αKLLKL(T (ˆx), S(ˆx)) + αclsLcls(S(ˆx), ˆy)
+ αfeatLfeat(Tfeat(ˆx), Sfeat(ˆx))
(4) 
where \mathcal{L}_{KL}(\mathcal{T}(\hat{x}), \mathcal{S}(\hat{x}))LKL(T (ˆx), S(ˆx)) is the KL divergence loss between the output of the 
student model SS and the teacher model TT on \hat{x}ˆx. \mathcal{L}_{cls}(\mathcal{S}(\hat{x}), \hat{y})Lcls(S(ˆx), ˆy) is the classiﬁcation 
loss of the student model on the generated data (\hat{x}, \hat{y})(ˆx, ˆy). \mathcal{L}_{feat}(\mathcal{T}_{feat}(\hat{x}), \mathcal{S}_{feat}(\hat{x})Lfeat(Tfeat(ˆx), Sfeat(ˆx) is 
the feature loss between the intermediate layers of the teacher model TT and the 
student model SS. Since the architecture of the teacher and student models is the 
same, we can perform knowledge distillation based on features without adding 
any additional modules. We output the feature maps from the convolutional 
layer before the ﬁrst linear layer of the model and use the cosine similarity loss 
to calculate the diﬀerence in intermediate layer features between the teacher and 
student model. 
However, we found in experiments that the teacher model in knowledge dis-
tillation is dynamic, with its accuracy constantly improving, meaning the global 
model serving as the teacher is also continually learning from the data behind 
the clients, which is diﬀerent from the traditional knowledge distillation where 
the teacher model is a ﬁxed, pre-trained model. Therefore, the data it generates, 
while highly responsive to the teacher model, also potentially contains some 
negative noise, making it diﬃcult for the student model to closely resemble the 
teacher model. Meanwhile, the teacher model’s precision is improving with each

communication round in federated learning, and these two factors together cause 
a persistent accumulation of the gap between the student and teacher models 
until the global model converges, at which point this gap starts to mitigate. 
However, this still reduces the quality of the fake parameter updates we submit 
during the early and middle stages of federated learning. 
To alleviate this issue, after the knowledge distillation step and submitting 
parameter updates, we save the global model as the local model for the next 
round. This eﬀectively makes the student model in knowledge distillation almost 
the same as the global model from the previous round, preventing further accu-
mulation of the gap between the student and teacher models after each round 
of knowledge distillation. Furthermore, saving the global model as a local model 
for the next round also ensures that the global knowledge stolen by the attacker 
is purer. 
4
Experiments 
4.1
Datasets 
We utilize the MNIST [ 14] and CIFAR-10 [ 13] datasets to evaluate our method. 
The MNIST dataset comprises 60,000 grayscale handwritten digit images of 
28\,\times\, × 28 pixels for training and 10,000 test images. These images cover 10 digit

categories from 0 to 9. We consider two data distribution scenarios: IID and 
Non-IID. For the IID scenario, we assign the same number of samples to clients 
per category. For Non-IID, we utilize the Dirichlet distribution [ 8, 9] to partition 
the dataset and simulate Non-IID data distribution across clients. CIFAR-10 is 
a classical image classiﬁcation dataset containing 60,000 color images of 32\,\times\, × 32 
pixels, including objects of 10 diﬀerent categories. The CIFAR-10 dataset adopts 
the same data partitioning method as MNIST. For the model architecture, we 
adopt a similar architecture to the one in [ 21], which consists of two layers of 
5\,\times\, × 5 convolutional layers with 2\,\times\, × 2 max pooling layers and two fully connected 
layers for the MNIST dataset. For the CIFAR-10 dataset, we use the one consists 
of three layers of 3\,\times\, × 3 convolutional layers with 2\,\times\, × 2 max pooling layers, and 
three fully connected layers. 
4.2
Baselines 
We implemented two methods including state-of-the-art (SOTA) method as the 
baseline to compare our method. These are the Disguised free-rider attack [ 6] 
(referred to as the “vanilla method” in this paper) and the Selﬁsh Free-rider 
Attack [ 25] (SFR, the SOTA method). Additionally, we implemented two defense 
models to test the eﬀectiveness of our method, including RFFL [ 26] and WEF [ 3]. 
RFFL uses cosine similarity between updates uploaded by clients and aggregated 
global updates to assess their contributions in maintaining client reputations, 
detecting non-contributing or malicious participants. WEF distinguishes free-
riders from benign clients based on the model weight evolving frequency. 
4.3
Evaluation Metrics 
We utilize Highest Mean Accuracy (HMA) to measure the eﬀectiveness of our 
method. HMA denotes the average highest accuracy of the model stolen by the 
attacker. 
\bALT{}\label{equ5} HMA = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{fr}}ACC(M_{\theta_{fr}})_{max}}{N_{fr}}\eALT{} HMA =
Nfr
i=1 ACC(Mθfr)max
Nfr
(5) 
where M_{\theta_{fr}}Mθfr is the model stolen by the free-riders. N_{fr}Nfr denotes the total number 
of free-riders. 
4.4
Experimental Setting 
Adversaries. 
We consider scenarios with various numbers of adversaries, 
namely 1, 5, 15, and 20 adversaries. The number of benign clients is uniformly 
set at 10. To better compare with other attacks, we adopt the setup from [ 25]. 
We preclude that benign clients have the CIFAR-10 dataset, and attackers have 
both the CIFAR-10 dataset and the MNIST dataset. For the MNIST dataset, 
we resize the images to be consistent with the size of the CIFAR-10 dataset.

Attack Results Discussion 
We consider three diﬀerent initial stage strategies mentioned in Sect. 2.2: adding 
noise, using public data, and using private datasets. We set the benign clients 
to train on the CIFAR-10 dataset, while the free-riders perform the public data 
training strategy using the MNIST dataset and the private data training strategy 
using the CIFAR-10 dataset. As shown in Tables 3 and 4, our method achieves 
the best attack performance in most cases. Our method provides higher quality 
model updates, which are closer to those of benign clients compared to other

system because benign clients do not constitute a majority. In such cases, even if 
free-riders are not detected, the early misidentiﬁcation and exclusion of benign 
clients result in free-riders only being able to steal a low-accuracy global model. 
Additionally, low-quality model updates provided by free-riders further disrupt 
the aggregation process of the entire federated learning system, reducing the 
accuracy of the stolen global model and interfering with the defense model, 
accelerating the misidentiﬁcation of benign clients.

The results in Tables 5 and 6 indicate that under multiple free-rider scenar-
ios, the primary reason for free-riders stealing low-accuracy global models is 
the early exclusion of benign clients due to the disrupted defense model. Our 
method enables free-riders to provide highly deceptive model updates, confus-
ing the defense model’s detection mechanisms. When the number of free-riders 
is 1, 5, or 10, their ability to disguise is strongest, preventing early detection 
or even avoiding detection altogether. Additionally, when the number of free-
riders greatly exceeds that of benign clients, the defense model may prematurely 
misidentify and exclude benign clients. This results in insuﬃcient data contribu-

tions within the federated system, negatively impacting the overall performance 
of the system. 
5
Conclusion 
In this paper, we explore two stages of free-rider attack in federated learning 
and propose a novel free-rider attack method. Existing free-rider attack meth-
ods simply utilize the parameters of the global model to create low-quality fake 
model updates and do not consider the evolution frequency of the attacker’s 
model weights. Our method utilize data-free knowledge distillation to eﬀectively 
transfer global knowledge in fake model updates, while also making the free-
rider’s model update frequency as similar as possible to that of benign clients. 
Moreover, to reduce the computational requirements of the attacker, we propose 
a MixUp-based local-adaptive data augmentation method, allowing the gener-
ator to produce only a small amount of data to meet the attack’s needs. We 
have proven the superior attacking performance of our method and its good 
compatibility with the initial strategies of free-riding attacks through exten-
sive experiments. In future research, we plan to explore new initial-stage attack 
strategies to further enhance the disguising ability of free-riders.

## Página PDF 204

Client-Oriented Energy Optimization 
in Clustered Federated Learning 
with Model Partition 
Sinan Pan1,2, Lei Shi1,2(B), Han Wu1,2, Yingying Chen1,2, Huaili Liu3, 
and Hao Xu3 
1 School of Computer Science and Information Engineering, Hefei University 
of Technology, Hefei 230009, China 
shilei@hfut.edu.cn 
2 Engineering Research Center of Safety Critical Industrial Measurement and 
Control Technology, Ministry of Education, Hefei 230009, China 
3 Anhui and Huaihe River Institute of Hydraulic Research (Anhui Provincial Water 
Conservancy Engineering Quality Testing Center Station), Hefei, China 
Abstract. Clustered Federated Learning (CFL) based on the edge com-
puting (EC) environment has great potential to promote the realization 
of artiﬁcial intelligence at the network edge. However, due to the large 
network area and the limited energy of edge devices, excessive energy 
consumption may occur if all devices participate in parameter transmis-
sion after training. This can result in insuﬃcient energy for some devices, 
making it diﬃcult to complete the training tasks. In this paper, we 
aim to reduce client energy consumption and total transmission energy 
consumption by the model partition technique and the adjustment of 
transmission modes among clients. Firstly, we establish the mathemat-
ical model, and show that the transmission energy consumption can be 
eﬀectively reduced by optimizing the transmission distance. Secondly, we 
design the Distance Best K-Means (DBKM) algorithm to obtain the opti-
mal transmission distance among clients. Finally, we propose a method to 
dynamically adjust the client training mode based on the client’s energy. 
Simulation results show that compared with the other three methods, our 
algorithm can reduce the transmission energy consumption by 27%–82%. 
Keywords: Clustered federated learning · Model partition · Limited 
energy 
1
Introduction 
Federated Learning (FL) is a novel distributed deep learning framework [ 1]. 
The main idea of FL is to use a server to aggregate local model parameters 
from participating clients to obtain a global model [ 2, 3]. This approach can not 
only make full use of the computing resources of clients, but also better protect 
c ICST Institute for Computer Sciences, Social Informatics and Telecommunications Engineering 2026 
Published by Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026. All Rights Reserved 
H. Gao and X. Wang (Eds.): CollaborateCom 2024, LNICST 626, pp. 195–213, 2026. 
https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-93257-1_11

[Vista original de la página 204](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-204.png)

![Página PDF 204, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-204.png)


<!-- PDF page 205 -->

## Página PDF 205

196
S. Pan et al.
the privacy of each client’s data during the training process [ 4, 5]. Nowadays 
researchers have done lots of work on FL. For example, in [ 6, 7], authors proposed 
a FL framework that can eﬀectively reduce communication energy consumption. 
In [ 8], authors developed a federated optimization method named Cooperative 
Edge-Based Federated Average, which can greatly reduce the training time once 
the target model accuracy is achieved. In [ 9,10], authors optimized the problem 
of decreasing training accuracy due to limited resources. 
However, in the actual training environment, since clients usually have the 
characteristics of heterogeneity, using the traditional FL training method directly 
may result in poor training outcomes. In order to solve this problem, the Clus-
tered Federated Learning (CFL) framework is proposed [ 11]. To be speciﬁc, the 
main idea of CFL is to gather clients into diﬀerent clusters by their hetero-
geneity characteristics, and train diﬀerent in-cluster models within each cluster. 
The CFL framework can improve the accuracy of each in-cluster model and the 
training eﬃciency, and solve the problem of data heterogeneity in FL. Appar-
ently the clustering method is the key point in CFL framework [ 12– 14], and many 
researchers have focused their work on this point. For example, in [ 15,16], authors 
proposed a CFL framework, which can improve the performance and eﬃciency 
compared to the conventional CFL. In [ 17], authors proposed a collaborative 
clustering algorithm for energy and distribution in CFL, which can achieve the 
expected accuracy with the lowest energy consumption. In [ 18], authors proposed 
an iterative solution that can enable accurate learning with low energy cost. 
In [ 19], authors proposed a more ﬂexible dynamic adaptive CFL scheme, which 
provided the most reasonable cluster partitioning results in all cases. In [ 20], 
authors proposed a new clustering method, FL with soft clustering, combin-
ing the advantages of soft clustering and iterative federated clustering. In [ 21], 
authors proposed a Variational AutoEncoder Gaussian Mixture Model Cluster-
ing Vertical Federated Learning Model, and its clustering results are better than 
those of other clustering methods in precision and other metrics. In [ 22], authors 
proposed an energy-eﬃciency clustering method using parallel Gibbs sampling 
to ﬁnd the optimal client base, achieving improved accuracy. 
CFL solves the problem of data heterogeneity in FL, but in the FL environ-
ment, clients are often small devices which are energy limited. To reduce energy 
consumption, some researchers propose to use the model partition technique [ 23]. 
By using the model partition technique, layers of the deep learning model are 
divided into two or more parts and deployed in diﬀerent clients or servers for 
training [ 24]. For example, in [ 25], authors formulated a joint optimization prob-
lem of device scheduling and resource allocation by using the model partition 
technique. 
Similarly, clients’ energy limitations are an important problem in CFL. So the 
trade-oﬀ between client clustering and energy consumption has become a new 
hot research topic. In [ 26], authors investigated the training of machine learning 
models on a resource-constrained cluster of devices via a swarm of unmanned 
aerial vehicles. Hierarchical nested personalized federated learning is utilized 
to achieve improvements in machine learning performance, network resource

[Vista original de la página 205](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-205.png)

## Página PDF 206

Client-Oriented Energy Optimization in Clustered Federated Learning
197
savings, and group trajectory eﬃciency. In [ 27], authors proposed a joint problem 
of resource allocation and device clustering. It can solve the problem of training 
performance degradation and optimize the system utility. In [ 28], authors intro-
duced node collaboration via AirComp to hierarchical federated learning to solve 
the problem of limited communication and storage costs. In [ 29], authors pro-
posed a dynamic federated optimization strategy to solve the problem of using 
federated deep reinforcement learning to adjust cache consumption in cloud-
edge. In [ 30], authors proposed a decentralized FL scheme: federated learning 
empowered overlapped clustering for decentralized aggregation. This scheme can 
enable direct model transmission between clusters and reduces the energy con-
sumption of the equipment. 
Previous researchers have done a great deal of work on CFL from energy con-
sumption and clustering. However, in CFL, few researchers have applied model 
partition technique to reduce client energy consumption and optimize transmis-
sion energy consumption by adjusting the clustering. Also, in many CFL sce-
narios, the non-independent and identically distributed (non-IID) data among 
clients aﬀects the training accuracy. Additionally, the range of clients is wide, 
clients’ energy is limited, and clients take on a lot of training tasks. To solve 
the above problems in the mentioned scenarios, we will apply model partition 
techniques in CFL to reduce the energy consumption of clients and decrease 
the total transmission energy consumption by adjusting the transmission path 
between clients. The main contributions of our work are summarized as follows. 
1) In this paper, we design an energy dynamic balance method that comprehen-
sively considers the clustering of non-IID data on the clients and the clustering 
based on physical distance. In each iteration, we will re-cluster and reselect 
client heads (CHs) based on physical distance to maintain the overall energy 
balance. 
2) We propose a dynamic model partition method to ensure the energy balance. 
For any client whose energy is lower than the energy threshold E'E , the CH 
of the cluster to which this client belongs will undertake all the backward 
propagation training processes. Otherwise, the backward propagation process 
is performed by both the CH and the client. 
3) We have simulated the proposed scheme and compared it with other schemes. 
Simulation results show that our scheme can achieve a reduction in transmis-
sion energy consumption compared to other schemes. 
The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Sec.2, we present the system 
model and deﬁne the problem. In Sec.3, we analyze the problem and give our 
algorithm. In Sec.4, we give the simulation results and analyze them. In Sec.5, 
we summarize this paper. 
2
System Model and Problem Formulation 
In this section, we ﬁrst introduce the system model, and then give the problem 
deﬁnition. Consider a CFL scenario with one parameter server and many clients. 
Suppose the data in diﬀerent clients is non-IID, and the clients’ distribution

area is physically large while each client is limited in energy. Based on these two 
assumptions, we will process the clustering in two steps. For the ﬁrst step, we 
group these clients into diﬀerent clusters based on their data characteristics. For 
the second step, in each cluster, we group clients into some sub-clusters based 
on their physical distances. After ﬁnishing clustering, the whole system will try 
to train learning models for all clusters. Notice that in this scenario, each cluster 
(not each sub-cluster) has one training model. Since clients have limited energy, 
we use a model partition technique to help balance the clients’ energy. We will 
identify some CHs to train part of the models. Considering that CHs are in sub-
clusters, and client energy is constantly changing throughout the entire training 
period, which means that how to set sub-clusters and how to select CHs are 
important and diﬃcult. Our goal is to complete the entire training process with 
minimal transmission energy consumption without depleting the energy of any 
client. 
2.1
System Model 
Consider a CFL scenario consisting of a server and nn clients in a two-dimensional 
region, as shown in Fig. 1. Assume the server is located at the center of this area. 
Deﬁne DD as the set of clients and d_{i} (d_{i} \in D,1 \leq i \leq n)di(di ∈D, 1 ≤i ≤n) as one client. Suppose 
that the number of clusters after the ﬁrst step of clustering is QQ. Deﬁne that 
each client has a certain energy E_i (1 \leq i \leq n)Ei(1 ≤i ≤n). Deﬁne the energy threshold as 
E'E , which means that by using the model partition technique, if the energy of 
the client exceeds E'E , the CH can transmit backward propagation parameters 
to the client during the training process, and the training parameters are ﬁnally 
stored on the client. Otherwise, the backward propagation is carried out on the 
CH, and the training parameters are ultimately stored on the CH. Apparently, 
in each sub-cluster, the client with the highest energy is selected as the CH. To 
simplify the problem, we suppose that in each training round, the total number 
of sub-clusters will not change (and denote it as mm), and for a cluster, the number 
of sub-clusters will not change either, but the grouped clients may be diﬀerent. 
Deﬁne CC as the set of CHs and c_j (c_j \in C,1 \leq j \leq m)cj(cj ∈C, 1 ≤j ≤m) as one CH. Suppose the 
trained DNN model has VV layers and denote l_r(r = 1 . . . V)lr(r = 1...V ) as one layer. By using 
the model partition technique, some layers will be trained on clients, while others 
will be trained on the CH. To simplify the problem, we assume that the model 
partition point is l_v (1 \leq v \leq V)lv(1 ≤v ≤V ) for all clients. This means that each task will 
ﬁrst train the initial l_{v}lv layers on the client. Then, the intermediate parameters 
are transmitted to the CH of the sub-cluster where the client belongs, and the 
training of the subsequent layers will be completed on the CH. 
Since all model partition points are on the same layer, we suppose the size of 
transferred parameters between clients and CHs is the same. Denote the trans-
ferred data between d_idi and c_jcj as \gammaγ. The data transfer size between each CH and 
the server is also same, and denote it as \alphaα. Denote the channel bandwidth as 
BB. Denote the channel transmission power as PP. Denote the reference distance 
as D_{0}D0. Denote the transmission distance between d_idi and c_jcj as D_{ij}Dij. Denote the

Fig. 1. Clustered federated learning with model partition. 
In the tt-th round of training, we assume the parameter in the CH is w_{j,t}wj,t, and 
the parameter in the client is w_{i,t}wi,t. The trained model parameters are aggregated 
in the CH, and the parameters of multiple aggregated sub-clusters are sent to 
the server for classiﬁcation aggregation. The aggregation parameter of the server 
is W_{q,t}(1\leq q\leq Q)Wq,t(1 ≤q ≤Q). In the global iteration of round tt, the speciﬁc steps for w_{j,t}wj,t, 
w_{i,t}wi,t and W_{q,t}Wq,t are as follows. 
1) The server broadcasts the global model parameter W_{q,t}Wq,t to each CH, and then 
CHs broadcast W_{q,t}Wq,t to clients in sub-clusters. 
2) After receiving W_{q,t}Wq,t, clients and CHs cooperate to conduct local training 
of ee rounds according to the model partition technique. By using the model 
partition technique, clients will decide whether to perform the backward prop-
agation process based on their energy. 
3) After completing the local model training, clients in each sub-cluster send 
parameter w_{i,t}wi,t to their CHs for updating w_{j,t}wj,t with the CH parameter aggre-
gation algorithm. Then, CHs send w_{j,t}wj,t to the server for classiﬁcation aggre-
gation and update W_{q,t}Wq,t. 
We hope to design a scheme to minimize the total transmission energy con-
sumption in one iteration, while ensuring the accuracy of the training.

obtain the minimum transmission energy consumption under various distribution 
scenarios. 
3
Algorithm 
In this section, we will introduce the proposed algorithm for solving the above 
optimization problem. Firstly, in order to solve the problem where the distance 
between clients in the clusters is too far after the initial clustering, we propose 
the Distance Best K-Means (DBKM) algorithm. The core idea of the DBKM 
algorithm is to partition each cluster into multiple sub-clusters, thereby reducing 
the transmission distance from the client to the CH. Secondly, within each sub-
cluster, we train the model using the model partition technique, while selecting 
the client with the highest energy in each sub-cluster as the CH. Finally, in 
order to reduce transmission energy consumption by decreasing the number of 
parameter transfers between the client and the CH, we propose an algorithm to 
dynamically adjust the client training mode according to the client’s energy.

3.1
Problem Analysis 
In this subsection, we will analyze the speciﬁc optimization algorithm. We found 
that data heterogeneity in CFL aﬀects the training accuracy. Data heterogeneity 
is usually manifested as a non-IID data distribution, which means that diﬀerent 
clients have data with signiﬁcant statistical diﬀerences. These diﬀerences often 
lead to the global model and local model objectives being inconsistent, causing 
the client model to deviate from the ideal global optimization point. This results 
in the model performing well on local data but having poor generalization ability 
on global data, leading to overﬁtting. At the same time, updates from diﬀerent 
client models may also conﬂict with each other, causing instability in the training 
process, which in turn aﬀects the training accuracy. So all clients are divided 
into several clusters by hierarchical clustering based on data similarity. This 
ensures the accuracy of training. In a large-area scenario, the physical distance 
between clients in a cluster is relatively large after the hierarchical clustering. 
This consumes a lot of energy during parameter transfer. To reduce transmission 
energy consumption, we propose the DBKM algorithm to divide each cluster 
into multiple sub-clusters. The purpose is to divide clients that are far apart 
into diﬀerent sub-clusters and reduce the transmission distance from the client 
to the CH, thus reducing the total transmission energy consumption. Suppose 
we have mm sub-clusters. The speciﬁc DBKM algorithm is shown in Algorithm 1. 
Algorithm 1. DBKM Algorithm 
Input: The distribution of Q clusters after hierarchical clustering; 
Output: The distribution of m sub-clusters; 
1: for cluster q from 1 to Q do 
2:
Use k-means for intra-cluster clustering to get m sub-clusters, m >Q; 
3:
Select the client with the highest energy in the sub-cluster as CH; 
4:
repeat 
5:
for cluster j from 1 to m do 
6:
Find the client di that is farthest away from CH cj in sub-cluster j and 
calculate its distance Di,j from cj; 
7:
The distance between di and CH(cj1,cj2,...) is calculated and expressed as 
Di,j1,Di,j2, Di,j3..., the condition of CHs is that the data in the CH and 
the client di are IID, assuming that the minimum distance is Di,j1; 
8:
if Distance Di,j1 <Di,j then 
9:
Remove client i from sub-cluster j and add client i to the sub-cluster j1; 
10:
else 
11:
Don’t make any changes; 
12:
end if 
13:
Reselect the CH in sub-cluster j; 
14:
end for 
15:
until the members of each sub-cluster no longer change; 
16: end for

of three key steps. Firstly, after using hierarchical clustering, there are Q clusters.

In each cluster, multiple sub-clusters are divided based on physical distance. 
The client with the highest energy in each sub-cluster is selected as the CH. 
Secondly, after the ﬁrst iteration of the DBKM algorithm, there will be mm initial 
distribution sub-clusters. For the sub-cluster jj, select the client ii farthest from 
CH c_{j}cj, and calculate its distance D_{i,j}Di,j to CH c_{j}cj and its distances D_{i,j1}Di,j1, D_{i,j2}Di,j2, etc., 
to CHs c_{j1}cj1, c_{j2}cj2, etc., which have the same data similarity as CH c_{j}cj. Compare 
D_{i,j}Di,j, D_{i,j1}Di,j1, D_{i,j2}Di,j2, etc., and add client ii to the sub-cluster with the smallest 
distance. The client member within each sub-cluster is updated in each DBKM 
iteration with the goal of minimizing D_{t}^{Total}DT otal
t
, until the members of each sub-
cluster no longer change. Finally, after each subsequent DBKM iteration, the 
CH for each sub-cluster is updated once. Clients whose data distribution is IID 
may be distributed across multiple sub-clusters, but the data distribution of all 
clients within a sub-cluster must be IID. 
There are mm sub-clusters with identiﬁed members. In each sub-cluster, the 
training is based on model partition technique and the model is partitioned into 
two parts, the client undertakes the training of the bottom part of the model and 
the CH undertakes the training of the top part of the model. Simultaneously, 
based on the client’s energy, the training mode is determined and the energy 
thresholdE'E is set. In order to more evenly utilize each client’s energy to complete 
the training, we set the value of E'E to the average energy of all clients. If the 
client’s energy exceeds E'E , backward propagation occurs on the client during 
model partition training, and the ﬁnal parameters are updated on the client. 
If the client’s energy does not exceed E'E , backward propagation is carried out 
entirely at the CH during model partition training, and the ﬁnal parameters are 
updated on the CH. Meanwhile, considering the varying energy of clients and the 
possibility that some clients may not be able to complete all training rounds due 
to energy issues, we employ dynamic model partitioning technology to achieve 
energy balance among clients. The speciﬁc situation is as follows. Clients with 
energy below E'E perform backward propagation training at the CH, which does 
not consume the client’s energy, clients with energy above E'E perform backward 
propagation training on the client, which consumes the client’s energy. During 
the training process, clients with high energy will consume more energy than 
those with low energy. Therefore, the gap in energy between clients will gradually 
narrow during the training process, allowing clients with low energy to complete 
more training rounds, achieving a kind of energy balance. 
After the clients in the sub-clusters complete their training, the parameters 
are transmitted to the CH for aggregation. Then, the CH transmits the aggre-
gated parameters to the server. The relationship between energy consumption, 
transmission distance, and the amount of data transmitted can be expressed as 
follows. 
\bALT{} E_{t,i,j}^{comm} = \frac{P\gamma}{B\log_{2}\left(1+\frac{Ph_{0}\rho\left(\frac{D_{0}}{D_{i,j}}\right)^{v}}{BN_{0}} \right)} ,\eALT{} Ecomm

and D_{i,j}Di,j is the transmission distance.

## Página PDF 214

Client-Oriented Energy Optimization in Clustered Federated Learning
205
Based on this, we design the Energy Consumption Optimization(ECO) algo-
rithm to dynamically adjust the transmission path between clients to reduce 
the total transmission distance and minimize the total transmission energy con-
sumption. 
3.2
ECO Algorithm 
According to the analysis in the previous section, the ECO algorithm can be 
summarized as follows. 
1) Firstly, nn clients are divided into multiple clusters using hierarchical cluster-
ing. In each cluster, the clients are distributed across diﬀerent regions. 
2) In each cluster partitioned in Step 1, the DBKM algorithm is used again to 
further divide each cluster into multiple sub-clusters. 
3) Select the client with the highest energy in each sub-cluster as the CH. 
4) The model partition technique is used to train the client, and the client’s 
energy determines whether it conducts backward propagation training. 
The algorithm is shown in Algorithm 2. 
Time Complexity Analysis: Let the number of iterations of kk-meansmeans and the 
repeated adjustment of each sub-cluster member processes in the DBKM algo-
rithm be T_{1}T1 and T_{2}T2, respectively. Therefore, the time complexity of the DBKM 
algorithm can be expressed as O(T_{1} + T_{2})O(T1 + T2). Let the total number of iterations be 
TT, the number of local iterations be ee, and the number of iterations for clustering 
nn clients according to data similarity be T_{3}T3. The total time complexity of the 
ECO algorithm can be expressed as O(T(e+T_{1} + T_{2}+T_{3}))O(T(e + T1 + T2 + T3)). 
4
Simulation and Experiment 
In this section, we will introduce experiments and simulations. The DNN model 
used in our experiments is VGG16. The model is trained using the EMNIST 
dataset, which consists of 814255 28\times×28 pixel grayscale images in 62 classes, 
and the framework used is pytorch. We use GPU to simulate the computing 
power of server and use CPUs of diﬀerent computers as clients. We set the scene 
area size to 10 km \times× 10 km. To get the constants in Sect. 2, such as E_{i}Ei, D_{i,j}Di,j, 
\gammaγ, \alphaα and so on, we ﬁrst assign each client a random number between 50 and 
100 as the client’s energy consumption E_{i}Ei. Then, we obtain D_{i,j}Di,j by calculating 
the Euclidean distance between clients and CHs. Finally, perform forward and 
backward propagation of the model 50 times, then calculate the average output 
data amount of the partition layers, i.e. the value of \gammaγ. The values of \alphaα are 
obtained by the same way. We set the other parameters in the experiment as 
shown in Table 2.

In Sect. 4.1, based on the DBKM algorithm, we provide the transmission 
distribution for diﬀerent numbers of clients, which achieves the minimum trans-
mission distance. In Sect. 4.2, we simulate diﬀerent numbers of clients to verify 
the proposed system model and algorithm. We use the following algorithms in 
terms of accuracy and transmission energy consumption for comparison with 
our proposed algorithms: 1) Hierarchical Clustering (HC), in which clients 
are clustered based on their data similarity; 2) Federated Learning (FL), in 
which clustering is not performed, and each client transmits parameters directly 
to the server after training; 3) K-Means (KM), in which clients are clustered 
based on their distance.

data similarity. Figure 2(a) represents the initial distribution of clients when the 
number of clients nn is 20 and the number of sub-clusters mm is 8. The dots of 
the same color indicate that the data is IID for the clients. Then according to 
the DBKM algorithm, the clients in each cluster are divided into several sub-
clusters. After clustering, the client with the highest energy in each sub-cluster is 
selected as the CH. Figure 2(b) shows the transmission distribution of the client 
after the CH is selected.

4.2
Simulation Results for More Experiments 
To demonstrate the eﬀectiveness of our algorithm, we conducted additional 
experiments. Firstly, in Fig. 3, we increase the number of clients from 20 to 60 in 
steps of 10. For each type, we conducted 50 sets of experiments. As we can see, 
in Fig. 3(a), among all algorithms, the total transmission energy consumption 
increases with the number of clients. Compared to other algorithms, our algo-
rithm achieves the minimum total transmission energy consumption for every 
number of clients. The experimental results show that compared with the HC 
algorithm, FL algorithm, and KM algorithm, our algorithm reduces the trans-
mission energy consumption by 27%, 61%, and 82% respectively. Figure 3(b), 
Fig. 3(c), and Fig. 3(d) respectively show the changes in total transmission energy 
consumption of each algorithm as the number of clients increases when the num-
ber of sub-clusters is 10, 12, and 14. Secondly, in Fig. 3, we can see that as the 
number of sub-clusters increases, our scheme achieves more signiﬁcant results 
compared to other schemes. Because as the number of sub-clusters increases, 
more clients can transmit parameters to closer CHs during training. At the 
same time, the total transmission energy consumption of the FL algorithm does 
not vary signiﬁcantly with diﬀerent numbers of sub-clusters. Because the FL 
algorithm does not use clustering, clients directly transmit parameters to the 
server after training, so it is not aﬀected by the number of sub-clusters. This 
experiment validates the eﬀectiveness of our algorithm.

## Página PDF 220

Client-Oriented Energy Optimization in Clustered Federated Learning
211
5
Conclusion and Future Work 
In this paper, we study the optimization problem of transmission energy con-
sumption in CFL. Firstly, we establish a mathematical model for transmission 
energy consumption in a CFL scenario and ﬁnd that the problem of transmis-
sion energy consumption can be transformed into the problem of transmission 
distance. Then, we propose the DBKM algorithm to divide multiple sub-clusters 
within each cluster. Finally, by dynamically adjusting the training mode of the 
clients to reduce the number of transmissions and by partitioning the model to 
lower the client’s energy usage, we ensure that the client has enough energy to 
complete the training. The experimental and simulation results show that the 
transmission energy consumption can be reduced eﬀectively by using model par-
tition, the DBKM algorithm, and dynamically adjusting the training mode of 
the clients. 
This article attempts to reduce transmission energy consumption through 
techniques such as intra-cluster sub-clustering and model partition. In the future, 
we will consider dynamic changes in the partition points of the client model and 
the number of sub-clusters.

## Página PDF 223

FedUDA: Towards a Novel Unfairness 
Distribution Attack Against Federated 
Learning Models 
Zihao Wang1, Xiaodong Fu1,2(B) 
, Li Liu1, Fei Dai3, and Jiaman Ding1 
1 Faculty of Information Engineering and Automation, Kunming University 
of Science and Technology, Kunming 650504, China 
wzh kust@stu.kust.edu.cn, xdfu@kust.edu.cn 
2 Yunnan Provincial Key Laboratory of Computer Technology Application, 
Kunming University of Science and Technology, Kunming 650504, China 
3 College of Big Data and Intelligent Engineering, Southwest Forestry University, 
Kunming 650228, China 
Abstract. Despite the remarkable success and widespread application 
of deep neural networks (DNNs), the aspect of fairness in DNNs often 
goes overlooked. In particular, the unfairness threats is more practical 
and urgent in federated learning (FL), which allows multiple clients to 
collaboratively train models and greatly facilitates attackers to manipu-
late models. Surprisingly, this palpable risk has yet to be fully acknowl-
edged and explored in the research community. To bridge this gap, 
this paper introduces the Federated Unfairness Distribution Attack 
(FedUDA), a pioneering strategy designed to deliberately induce bias 
covertly and realistically into FL systems, which poses serious and sig-
niﬁcant threats in real worlds. FedUDA is achieved by the strategic 
manipulation of data distribution within a single malicious client, with-
out sacriﬁcing the accuracy of the model’s outputs by leveraging gradient 
decomposition to isolate and inject bias-inducing updates orthogonal to 
the main learning objective. It improves durability by focusing on stable 
neural connections. Our experimental results demonstrate the eﬀective-
ness and real threat of the proposed method, and reveal for the ﬁrst time 
the vulnerability of FL to unfairness attacks. Our research sheds light 
on the often-neglected unfairness risks in FL, emphasizing the critical 
need for the development of stronger safeguards to promote fairness in 
federated learning. 
Keywords: Federated Learning (FL) · Fairness in Machine Learning · 
Unfairness Attack · Data Distribution Manipulation 
1
Introduction 
Recent years have witnessed the rapid advancement of Artiﬁcial Intelligence, sig-
niﬁcantly impacting society but also bringing to light fairness issues related to 
c ICST Institute for Computer Sciences, Social Informatics and Telecommunications Engineering 2026 
Published by Springer Nature Switzerland AG 2026. All Rights Reserved
