https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-93257-1_12

[Vista original de la página 223](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-223.png)

![Página PDF 223, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-223.png)


<!-- PDF page 224 -->

## Página PDF 224

FedUDA: A Novel Unfairness Attack in Federated Learning
215
protected attributes like race, gender, and nationality [ 5,28]. Several high-proﬁle 
cases have underscored these concerns. Amazon’s automated recruitment system 
showed bias favoring male candidates over females [ 9], while the COMPAS sys-
tem for assessing recidivism risk displayed racial biases, assigning higher risk 
assessments to speciﬁc racial groups [ 2]. In the ﬁnancial sector, AI-driven credit 
scoring systems often lack transparency, operating as ‘black boxes’ and poten-
tially perpetuating discrimination against certain socio-economic groups [ 8,31]. 
More recently, Google’s ‘Gemini’ AI image generator caused controversy by pro-
ducing historically inaccurate portrayals of racial identities, such as depicting the 
Founding Fathers and Nazi soldiers as African Americans, leading to signiﬁcant 
public backlash. These instances highlight the growing concern over AI fairness, 
especially in complex and distributed systems [ 18]. 
Federated Learning (FL) [ 16,27] oﬀers privacy preservation in collaborative 
model training, but introduces new fairness challenges [ 21,35]. The heterogeneity 
of client data [ 40] and privacy constraints make detecting and mitigating biases 
in FL particularly diﬃcult [ 29]. For instance, in cross-hospital disease prediction, 
demographic variations could lead to unfair model outcomes. 
Recent research on fairness in FL has addressed several key areas: 1) Col-
laborative fairness and resource allocation [ 22,37]; 2) Client selection and model 
aggregation strategies [ 20,42]; 3) Privacy-fairness trade-oﬀs [ 1,10]; 4) Bias miti-
gation and personalization [ 23]; 5) Security, robustness, and theoretical fairness 
guarantees [ 25,41]. 
Despite signiﬁcant advances in promoting fairness in FL, current research 
largely overlooks potential security threats and fairness attacks. This over-
sight could lead to inadequate defenses against malicious attacks in practi-
cal FL deployments. To address this gap, we introduce ‘Federated Unfairness 
Distribution Attack (FedUDA)’, a novel method designed to inject and amplify 
unfairness in the global model by enhancing spurious correlations between target 
labels and protected attributes. 
FedUDA employs a sophisticated dual optimization strategy that simultane-
ously addresses the normal loss function and a malicious loss function. The key 
innovations of FedUDA are: 
1) Data Distribution Manipulation: Malicious clients adjust their local data dis-
tribution to introduce bias, using the Wasserstein distance [ 33] to measure 
and optimize the disparity between the manipulated and original data distri-
butions. 
2) Gradient Decomposition: To preserve the model’s performance on the primary 
task while injecting unfairness, we employ gradient decomposition technique. 
This method projects the malicious gradient onto a space orthogonal to the 
benign gradient, eﬀectively isolating the bias-inducing updates from those 
critical to the main learning objective. 
3) Selective Neuron Targeting: FedUDA further strengthens its durability by 
focusing on stable neural connections, ensuring that the injected biases persist 
over time without signiﬁcantly impacting the model’s overall accuracy.

Fig. 1. Illustration of FedUDA in a smile detection task, demonstrating induced gender 
bias. After the attack, the model shows a biased tendency to classify women as smiling 
and men as not smiling, regardless of their actual expressions, without altering input 
data labels or model architecture. 
As shown in Fig. 1, FedUDA demonstrates how a single malicious client can 
compromise model fairness while maintaining overall accuracy. This exposes a 
critical vulnerability in FL systems where traditional metrics may not capture 
induced biases. FedUDA also shows resilience against existing FL defense mecha-
nisms, highlighting the need for more sophisticated fairness-aware security mea-
sures. 
To our knowledge, this work represents the ﬁrst comprehensive investigation 
into unfairness attacks in FL, unveiling a signiﬁcant vulnerability and providing 
crucial insights for developing more robust and fair FL frameworks. These ﬁnd-
ings underscore the importance of incorporating fairness considerations into the 
core design of FL systems. 
2
Related Work 
2.1
Fairness Challenges in Federated Learning 
Federated Learning (FL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for collaborative 
model training while preserving data privacy. However, the decentralized nature 
of FL introduces unique challenges to ensuring fairness in the resulting models. 
Li et al. [ 21] highlight that the heterogeneity of data across clients can lead to 
biased global models, as each client’s local data may not be representative of 
the overall population. This data heterogeneity problem is exacerbated in FL

[Vista original de la página 225](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-225.png)

![Página PDF 225, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-225.png)


<!-- PDF page 226 -->

## Página PDF 226

FedUDA: A Novel Unfairness Attack in Federated Learning
217
settings where direct access to client data is prohibited, making it diﬃcult to 
detect and mitigate biases [ 13]. 
Xu et al. [ 38] demonstrate that in cross-silo FL applications, such as health-
care, variations in patient demographics across diﬀerent hospitals can signiﬁ-
cantly skew model fairness. For instance, a model trained on data from multi-
ple hospitals might exhibit biased performance across diﬀerent racial or gender 
groups due to the uneven distribution of these attributes among the participating 
institutions. 
Furthermore, Kairouz et al. [ 18] argue that the privacy-preserving nature of 
FL, while beneﬁcial for data protection, creates a tension with fairness objectives. 
The inability to directly examine individual datasets due to privacy constraints 
makes it challenging to implement traditional fairness-enhancing techniques that 
rely on comprehensive data analysis. 
2.2
Security Threats in Federated Learning 
The security landscape of FL is complex and multifaceted. Lyu et al. [ 26] pro-
vide a comprehensive survey of threats to FL, categorizing them into privacy 
attacks (e.g., membership inference), poisoning attacks (e.g., model poisoning), 
and inference-time attacks (e.g., evasion attacks). However, their survey, like 
much of the existing literature, primarily focuses on attacks that aim to com-
promise model performance or privacy, rather than explicitly targeting fairness. 
This gap in the literature regarding fairness-speciﬁc attacks in FL is partic-
ularly noteworthy, the potential for malicious actors to deliberately manipulate 
or compromise the fairness of FL models has been largely overlooked. This over-
sight presents a critical vulnerability in FL systems, as attackers could poten-
tially exploit fairness-enhancing mechanisms to introduce subtle biases that are 
diﬃcult to detect using conventional performance metrics. 
2.3
Wasserstein Distance and Gradient Manipulation in Machine 
Learning 
The Wasserstein distance has gained prominence in machine learning for mea-
suring distribution distances. Arjovsky et al. [ 3] demonstrated its eﬀectiveness 
in training GANs, while Jiang et al. [ 17] applied it to quantify and mitigate 
demographic disparities in models. 
Gradient manipulation techniques, introduced by Goodfellow et al. [ 12] 
for creating adversarial examples, have signiﬁcant implications in FL due to 
its decentralized nature. Bagdasaryan et al. [ 4] showed how gradient updates 
could be exploited to inject backdoors in FL settings. Gradient decomposition, 
explored by Santurkar et al. [ 34] for analyzing neural network loss landscapes, 
presents potential applications in fairness manipulation within FL.

3
Preliminary 
3.1
Federated Learning 
Federated Learning (FL) is a distributed machine learning paradigm that enables 
multiple parties to collaboratively train a model without sharing their raw data. 
In a typical FL setting, there is a central server and $ N $N participating clients 
$\mathcal{C} = {C_1, C_2, ..., C_N}$C = {C1, C2, ..., CN}. The objective is to train a global model $\theta_g$θg that performs 
well on a test dataset $\mathcal{D}_{\text{test}}$Dtest by aggregating updates from local models. This can 
be formulated as an optimization problem: 
\bALT{} \min_{\theta_g \in \mathbb{R}^d} \left[F(\theta_g) := \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^N f_i(\theta_g)\right]\eALT{} min
θg∈Rd F(θg) := 1

\bALT{} f_i(\theta_i) = \mathcal{L}({x_j^i, y_j^i}_{j \in \mathcal{D}_i}, \theta_i)\eALT{} fi(θi) = L({xi

where $\gamma$γ is the global learning rate. This process continues until convergence or 
a predeﬁned number of rounds. 
3.2
Attacker Abilities 
In our FedUDA scenario, we consider a single malicious client $ C_m \in \mathcal{C} $Cm ∈C with the 
following capabilities: 
1. Full control over local training process: The attacker can manipulate his local 
data distribution and modify his local model updates. 
2. Scaling of malicious updates: The attacker can scale his model updates by a 
factor $\alpha$α before sending them to the server: $\alpha\Delta\theta_m^r$αΔθr
m. 
3. No control over server or other clients: The attacker cannot inﬂuence the 
server’s aggregation method, the global training process, or other clients’ 
updates.

FedUDA: A Novel Unfairness Attack in Federated Learning

3.3
FedUDA Attack 
Unlike traditional backdoor attacks, FedUDA does not introduce triggers or 
modify data labels. Instead, it aims to induce unfairness by manipulating the 
distribution of sensitive attributes while maintaining performance on the main 
task. Let $ s $s denote the sensitive attribute (e.g., gender) and $ y $y the target variable 
(e.g., smile detection). The objective of FedUDA is to maximize the disparity 
between diﬀerent groups of the sensitive attribute while minimizing the impact 
on the main task performance. 
The attacker’s objective can be formulated as: 
\bALT{} \min_{\theta_m} \left[ \mathcal{L}_{\text{main}}(\theta_m) - \lambda \mathcal{L}_{\text{unfair}}(\theta_m) \right]\eALT{} min
θm [Lmain(θm) −λLunfair(θm)]
(4) 
where $\mathcal{L}_{\text{main}}$Lmain is the loss for the main task, $\mathcal{L}_{\text{unfair}}$Lunfair is a measure of unfairness, 
and $\lambda$λ is a balancing parameter. To quantify unfairness, we use the Wasserstein 
distance between the distributions of diﬀerent groups: 
\bALT{} \mathcal{L}_{\text{unfair}}(\theta_m) = W(\mathbb{P}_{s=0}, \mathbb{P}_{s=1})\eALT{} Lunfair(θm) = W(Ps=0, Ps=1)
(5) 
where $ W $W denotes the Wasserstein distance, and $\mathbb{P}_{s=i}$Ps=i is the distribution of model 
outputs for the group with sensitive attribute$ s=i $s = i. By maximizing this distance, 
the attacker aims to create a larger disparity between groups, thus inducing 
unfairness. 
To ensure that the main task performance is not signiﬁcantly aﬀected, the 
attacker employs a gradient decomposition technique. Let $ g_{\text{main}} $gmain and $ g_{\text{unfair}} $gunfair be 
the gradients of $\mathcal{L}_{\text{main}}$Lmain and $\mathcal{L}_{\text{unfair}}$Lunfair respectively. The attacker projects $ g_{\text{unfair}} $gunfair onto 
the space orthogonal to $ g_{\text{main}} $gmain: 
\bALT{} g_{\text{proj}} = g_{\text{unfair}} - \frac{g_{\text{unfair}} \cdot g_{\text{main}}}{||g_{\text{main}}||^2} g_{\text{main}}\eALT{} gproj = gunfair −gunfair · gmain
||gmain||2
gmain
(6) 
The ﬁnal update direction is then a combination of $ g_{\text{main}} $gmain and $ g_{\text{proj}} $gproj, allowing the 
attacker to induce unfairness while maintaining performance on the main task. 
This formulation of FedUDA allows the attacker to subtly manipulate the 
model’s behavior with respect to sensitive attributes, potentially creating biased 
predictions that are diﬃcult to detect through standard performance metrics. 
3.4
Fairness Metrics 
Model fairness is crucial in machine learning, especially as algorithmic decisions 
increasingly impact society [ 5,28,30]. To address this, researchers have developed 
various fairness metrics [ 36]. We focus on two key metrics: Demographic Parity 
and Equalized Odds, which assess outcome equality and predictive performance 
equality across protected groups. 
Deﬁnition 1. (Demographic Parity): Demographic Parity, also known as Sta-
tistical Parity or Independence, is a group fairness notion that requires the

[Vista original de la página 228](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-228.png)

![Página PDF 228, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-228.png)


<!-- PDF page 229 -->

## Página PDF 229

220
Z. Wang et al.
model’s predictions to be independent of the protected attribute [ 11]. Formally, 
for a binary classiﬁer, Demographic Parity is satisﬁed if: 
\bALT{} P(\hat{y} = 1\,s = i) = P(\hat{y} = 1), \quad \forall i \in {0, 1}\eALT{} P(ˆy = 1 s = i) = P(ˆy = 1),
∀i ∈{0, 1}
(7) 
where \hat{y}ˆy is the predicted outcome, and ss is the sensitive attribute. In practice, 
we often measure the violation of Demographic Parity, deﬁned as the diﬀerence 
in positive prediction rates across groups: 
\bALT{} DP = |P(\hat{y} = 1\,s = 0) - P(\hat{y} = 1\,s = 1)|\eALT{} DP = |P(ˆy = 1 s = 0) −P(ˆy = 1 s = 1)|
(8) 
A lower DP value indicates better fairness in terms of outcome parity across 
groups. However, it’s important to note that strictly enforcing Demographic 
Parity can sometimes lead to suboptimal decisions for individuals, as it doesn’t 
account for potential diﬀerences in base rates across groups [ 24]. 
Deﬁnition 2. (Equalized Odds): Equalized Odds, introduced by Hardt et al. 
[ 14], addresses some limitations of Demographic Parity by conditioning on the 
true outcome. This metric requires equal true positive rates and false positive 
rates across all protected groups. Formally, a predictor \hat{y}ˆy satisﬁes Equalized Odds 
with respect to sensitive attribute ss and true outcome yy if: 
\bALT{} P(\hat{y} = 1 | y = k, s = i) = P(\hat{y} = 1 | y = k), \quad \forall k, i \in {0, 1}\eALT{} P(ˆy = 1|y = k, s = i) = P(ˆy = 1|y = k),
∀k, i ∈{0, 1}
(9) 
In practice, we often measure the violation of Equalized Odds as: 
\bALT{} EO = \max_{k \in {0,1}} |P(\hat{y} = 1 | y = k, s = 0) - P(\hat{y} = 1 | y = k, s = 1)|\eALT{} EO = max
k∈{0,1} |P(ˆy = 1|y = k, s = 0) −P(ˆy = 1|y = k, s = 1)|
(10) 
Equalized Odds ensures that the model’s performance, in terms of both true 
positive and false positive rates, is consistent across all protected groups, given 
the true outcome. This metric is particularly useful in scenarios where base rates 
may legitimately diﬀer across groups, e.g., in medical diagnoses where disease 
prevalence may vary [ 32]. 
Notably, achieving perfect fairness may conﬂict with maximizing overall 
prediction accuracy [ 19], emphasizing the need for context-speciﬁc considera-
tions in model deployment. In our study, we use Demographic Parity (DP) 
and Equalized Odds (EO) to evaluate the fairness of federated learning models 
under our proposed Federated Unfairness Distribution Attack. This approach 
allows us to comprehensively assess model fairness and demonstrate the potential 
impacts of fairness-targeted attacks in federated learning environments. 
4
Proposed Method 
Our proposed Federated Unfairness Distribution Attack (FedUDA), as illus-
trated in Fig. 2, consists of three key components: (1) Wasserstein distance-based 
unfairness optimization, (2) Gradient decomposition for main task performance 
preservation, and (3) Selective perturbation of stable connections for enhanced 
attack persistence. The ﬁrst two components are utilized during the attack opti-
mization phase, while the third is employed to improve attack longevity.

4.1
Wasserstein Distance-Based Unfairness Optimization 
The core objective of FedUDA is to maximize the disparity between diﬀerent sen-
sitive attribute groups while maintaining the main task performance. We utilize 
the Wasserstein distance to quantify and optimize unfairness. The optimization 
objective for the attacker client C_mCm is deﬁned as: 
\bALT{} \min_{\theta_m} \left[\mathcal{L}_{\text{main}}(\theta_m, \mathcal{D}_m) - \lambda(t) \mathcal{L}_{\text{unfair}}(\theta_m, \mathcal{D}_m)\right]\eALT{} min
θm [Lmain(θm, Dm) −λ(t)Lunfair(θm, Dm)]

\lambda(t)λ(t) is a time-dependent balancing parameter, and \mathcal{D}_mDm is the attacker’s local 
dataset. The unfairness measure \mathcal{L}_{\text{unfair}}Lunfair is deﬁned using the Wasserstein distance 
between output distributions of diﬀerent sensitive attribute groups: 
\bALT{} \mathcal{L}_{\text{unfair}}(\theta_m, \mathcal{D}_m) = W(\mathbb{P}_{s=0}, \mathbb{P}_{s=1})\eALT{} Lunfair(θm, Dm) = W(Ps=0, Ps=1)
(12) 
Here, WW denotes the Wasserstein distance, and \mathbb{P}_{s=i}Ps=i represents the model 
output distribution for the group with sensitive attribute s=is = i. 
To dynamically optimize \lambda(t)λ(t), we employ a proportional-integral (PI) con-
troller: 
\bALT{} \lambda(t) = K_p \epsilon(t) + K_i \sum_{j=0}^t \epsilon(j) + \lambda_{\text{min}}\eALT{} λ(t) = Kp (t) + Ki
t
j=0

5
Theoretical Analysis 
In this section, we present a detailed theoretical analysis of the Federated Unfair-
ness Distribution Attack (FedUDA) in federated learning. Our goal is to prove 
that FedUDA can induce unfairness in the global model while preserving main 
task performance in a distributed setting. 
5.1
Preliminaries and Notations 
We consider a federated learning system with N clients \mathcal{C} = {C_1, C_2, ..., C_N}C = {C1, C2, ..., CN}, 
where client C_mCm is the malicious attacker. Let us deﬁne: 
– \theta_g^r \in \mathbb{R}^dθr
g ∈Rd: Global model parameters at round rr
– \theta_i^r \in \mathbb{R}^dθr
i ∈Rd: Local model parameters of client C_iCi at round rr
– \mathcal{D}_iDi: Local dataset of client C_iCi
– \mathcal{L}_{\text{main}}(\theta, \mathcal{D})Lmain(θ, D): Main task loss function 
– \mathcal{L}_{\text{unfair}}(\theta, \mathcal{D})Lunfair(θ, D): Unfairness measure based on Wasserstein distance 
– \lambda(t)λ(t): Time-dependent balancing parameter 
– \etaη: Local learning rate

5.2
Convergence of FedUDA 
We begin our theoretical analysis by proving the convergence of FedUDA in a 
federated learning setting. 
Theorem 1 (Convergence of FedUDA). Under the following assumptions: 
1. \mathcal{L}_{\text{main}}Lmain and \mathcal{L}_{\text{unfair}}Lunfair are both LL-smooth and \muμ-strongly convex. 
2. The gradients of \mathcal{L}_{\text{main}}Lmain and \mathcal{L}_{\text{unfair}}Lunfair are bounded: \|\nabla \mathcal{L}_{\text{main}}(\theta, \mathcal{D}_i)\| \leq G_1
main(θ, Di)
G1 and 
\|\nabla \mathcal{L}_{\text{unfair}}(\theta, \mathcal{D}_i)\| \leq G_2
unfair(θ, Di)
G2 for all \thetaθ and ii. 
3. The local learning rate \etaη satisﬁes 0 < \eta < \frac{2}{L}0 < η < 2

6
Experimental Evaluation 
In this section, we ﬁrst outline the “Experimental Setup” (Sect. 6.1), establishing 
the testing ground for the FedUDA attack. We then assess its “Attack Perfor-
mance” (Sect. 6.2) in the FL environment, followed by an “Ablation Study” 
(Sect. 6.3) to examine Gradient Decomposition’s impact on key metrics. Our 
“Defense Analysis” (Sect. 6.4) evaluates FedUDA’s resistance to FL defenses. 
6.1
Experimental Setup 
Datasets: We employ two datasets suitable for federated learning environ-
ments: CelebA (202,599 images, 40 attributes) and LFW (13,244 images, 73 
attributes). For CelebA, we focus on predicting Smiling, Attractive, and 
Young attributes, while for LFW, we predict Smiling, Wavy Hair, and 
Young. All images are resized to 178\,\times\, × 178 pixels. To simulate a realistic non-
IID scenario, we distribute the training data among 100 clients using a Dirichlet 
distribution. The attack perturbations are applied only to the training set, pre-
serving the integrity of validation and test sets.

[Vista original de la página 235](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-235.png)

![Página PDF 235, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-235.png)

## Página PDF 236

FedUDA: A Novel Unfairness Attack in Federated Learning
227
Model and Training Details: We use ResNet-18 [ 15] as the base model. The 
SGD [ 6] optimizer is employed with a learning rate of 1e-2 for benign training, 
increased to 5e-2 during the attack phase. Each client performs one epoch of 
training per round with a batch size of 64. The maximum perturbation magni-
tude \epsilon is set to 0.05 to maintain image quality. 
Evaluation Metrics: We assess model fairness using Demographic Parity dif-
ference (DP) [ 11] and Equal Opportunity diﬀerence (EO) [ 14], alongside the 
model’s accuracy (ACC) in predicting target labels. Higher DP and EO values 
indicate greater unfairness.

6.2
Attack Performance 
We evaluated FedUDA in a FL scenario with 100 clients over 130 rounds, strate-
gically initiating the attack at round 121. This timing simulates real-world sce-
narios where attackers might exploit established systems. Table 1 and Fig. 4 illus-
trate FedUDA’s impact on model fairness and accuracy. 
Our results reveal a consistent pattern across both CelebA and LFW 
datasets: post-attack, fairness metrics (DP and EO) deteriorate signiﬁcantly 
while accuracy (ACC) remains relatively stable. In the CelebA dataset, the Smil-
ing attribute exempliﬁes this trend. At round 121, ACC decreases marginally 
from 90.58% to 87.75%, while DP increases from 0.1526 to 0.2975 and EO from 
0.0417 to 0.2340. This pattern of minimal ACC change coupled with substantial 
DP and EO increases is consistent across attributes and datasets. 
Figure 4 visually conﬁrms these trends. The green shaded area at round 121 
marks a clear inﬂection point, with DP and EO values sharply increasing across 
all attributes while ACC remains relatively stable. Notably, the fairness metrics 
continue to diverge in subsequent rounds, indicating FedUDA’s lasting impact. 
Fig. 4. FedUDA Attack Impact on Fairness During Late FL System Training. The 
graph illustrates the eﬀects of a single FedUDA attack at epoch 121 (highlighted in 
green) on fairness metrics (DP and EO) for the CelebA and LFW datasets, contrasting 
‘good’ and ‘malicious’ scenarios as the training progresses. (Color ﬁgure online) 
To further illustrate FedUDA’s subtle yet signiﬁcant impact, we present 
GradCAM heatmaps in Fig. 5. Pre-attack (a), the model correctly focuses on the

[Vista original de la página 237](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-237.png)

Before and After FedUDA Attack for the CelebA Dataset. 
mouth region for smile detection. Post-attack (b), we observe a notable shift in 
attention to other facial features, suggesting altered decision-making processes 
that rely more on spurious correlations. These results demonstrate FedUDA’s 
ability to signiﬁcantly alter fairness metrics while maintaining model accuracy, 
highlighting a subtle yet critical vulnerability in FL systems. 
6.3
Ablation Study 
To evaluate the eﬃcacy of our gradient decomposition technique, we conducted 
an ablation study on the CelebA and LFW datasets, focusing on the Smiling 
attribute. Figure 6 illustrates the impact of this technique on model accuracy 
and fairness metrics (DP and EO) over training epochs, particularly around the 
FedUDA attack point. 
The upper plots in Fig. 6 demonstrate that FedUDA with gradient decompo-
sition (‘FedUDA ACC’) maintains accuracy levels close to the baseline (‘Good 
ACC’). Conversely, FedUDA without gradient decomposition shows a marked 
decline in accuracy post-attack, highlighting the importance of our technique in 
preserving model performance while pursuing adversarial objectives. 
The lower plots reveal the technique’s impact on fairness metrics. Without 
gradient decomposition, DP and EO values diverge signiﬁcantly from the baseline 
after the attack. In contrast, with our technique applied, the deviation in fairness 
metrics is less severe, indicating a more subtle introduction of bias. 
Notably, for the CelebA dataset, the DP and EO metrics without gradient 
decomposition spike dramatically post-attack, whereas with our technique, the 
increase is more gradual and controlled. The LFW dataset shows a similar trend, 
albeit with less pronounced diﬀerences. 
This ablation study conﬁrms that our gradient decomposition technique 
enables FedUDA to introduce unfairness subtly while maintaining model accu-

racy. It achieves a balance between compromising fairness and preserving overall 
model performance, making the attack more challenging to detect through con-
ventional evaluation metrics. 
6.4
Defense Analysis 
To evaluate the eﬀectiveness of existing defenses against FedUDA, we examined 
three classic federated learning aggregation algorithms: Median [ 39], Trimmed-
Mean [ 39], and GeoMed [ 7]. Table 2 presents their performance on the CelebA 
and LFW datasets for the Smiling attribute at round 121, immediately following 
the FedUDA attack. 
Comparing these results with the undefended FedUDA attack scenario in 
Table 1, we observe several key points:

## Página PDF 240

FedUDA: A Novel Unfairness Attack in Federated Learning
231
1. Accuracy Preservation: All three defensive algorithms maintain higher accu-
racy levels compared to the undefended FedUDA scenario. For CelebA, the 
defensive methods achieve accuracies between 88.97% and 89.30%, compared 
to 87.75% under FedUDA attack without defense. Similarly, for LFW, accu-
racies range from 86.99% to 87.35%, surpassing the 87.63% of the undefended 
attack. 
2. Partial Mitigation of Fairness Impact: While the defensive algorithms show 
some improvement in fairness metrics, they do not fully mitigate FedUDA’s 
impact. For CelebA, the DP values (around 0.25) and EO values (about 
0.20) with defenses are lower than the undefended scenario (DP: 0.2975, EO: 
0.2340), but still higher than the benign case (DP: 0.1526, EO: 0.0417). 
3. Varied Eﬀectiveness Across Datasets: The defensive algorithms appear more 
eﬀective on the CelebA dataset than on LFW. For LFW, the DP and EO 
values remain signiﬁcantly elevated (DP \approx≈0.40, EO >> 0.22) compared to 
the benign scenario (DP: 0.3053, EO: 0.1272), though still showing some 
improvement over the undefended attack (DP: 0.4472, EO: 0.2487). 
4. Trade-oﬀs in Defense Mechanisms: While TrimmedMean slightly outperforms 
in accuracy preservation, it doesn’t consistently excel in fairness metrics. This 
suggests that no single method decisively outperforms the others across all 
metrics, highlighting the complexity of defending against fairness attacks. 
These ﬁndings reveal that while existing aggregation algorithms provide some 
resilience against FedUDA, they fail to fully neutralize its impact on model 
fairness. This partial eﬀectiveness highlights FedUDA’s sophistication and the 
challenges it poses to FL systems. Our analysis underscores the urgent need for 
advanced defense mechanisms tailored to preserve both accuracy and fairness in 
adversarial FL environments. 
7
Conclusion 
This paper exposes a signiﬁcant oversight in federated learning (FL): its vulner-
ability to fairness attacks. By introducing the Federated Unfairness Distribution 
Attack (FedUDA), we demonstrate that a single malicious client can covertly 
bias the global model without impacting performance. Our ﬁndings emphasize 
the need for defenses that ensure both accuracy and fairness in FL systems. 
Future research should focus on developing robust mechanisms to detect and 
counteract unfairness, involving a multidisciplinary approach to secure and eth-
ically align FL technologies. This work not only broadens the understanding 
of security challenges in FL but also encourages proactive steps toward more 
trustworthy federated systems.

## Página PDF 244

Mal-GAT: A Method to Enhance 
Malware Traﬃc Detection with Graph 
Attention Networks 
Jingrun Ma1,2, Qi Wang3, Xiaolin Xu4, Tianning Zang1,2(B), and Yuwei Zeng5 
1 Institute of Information Engineering, Chinese Academy of Sciences, Beijing, China 
{majingrun,zangtianning}@iie.ac.cn 
2 School of Cyber Security, University of Chinese Academy of Sciences, 
Beijing, China 
3 National Computer Network Emergency Response Technical Team/Coordination 
Center of China, Beijing, China 
4 Zhongguancun Laboratory, Beijing, China 
5 Information Engineering University, Henan, China 
zengyuwei16@mails.ucas.ac.cn 
Abstract. The rapid growth of malicious activities on the Internet is 
severely impacting the security of the Internet infrastructure. As a pre-
vention strategy, many malware traﬃc detection methods are proposed 
to classify malicious behaviors through the analysis of traﬃc charac-
teristics. However, the rising encryption techniques render some traﬃc 
characteristics unavailable, posing signiﬁcant obstacles to the classiﬁ-
cation of malware traﬃc. This signiﬁcantly diminishes the eﬀectiveness 
of existing malware traﬃc detection methods that depend on a single 
type of attribute. In this paper, we propose Mal-GAT, a malware traf-
ﬁc classiﬁcation method based on Graph Attention Networks (GAT). In 
addition to the conventional ﬂow attributes, Mal-GAT explores the local 
and global features of network behaviors reﬂected in the graph connectiv-
ity structure present in traﬃc and integrates all available information to 
enhance the detection of malware traﬃc. Initially, Mal-GAT constructs 
malware traﬃc behavior graphs through packet-level and ﬂow-level anal-
ysis of raw network traﬃc data. Subsequently, it integrates graph neural 
networks and attention mechanisms to automatically extract local node 
features and global graph structure features from the traﬃc behavior 
graph. Extensive experimental results on multiple datasets demonstrate 
that Mal-GAT achieves high accuracy in malware traﬃc classiﬁcation. 
Keywords: Malware Traﬃc Classiﬁcation · Graph Neural Network 
1
Introduction 
The widespread deployment of Internet infrastructure has driven a surge in both 
the volume and technical complexity of cyber threats. Malware refers to intrusive

## Página PDF 245

236
J. Ma et al.
software programs developed by cybercriminals with malicious intentions, such 
as data theft, server disruptions, and network breaches, which serve as a pri-
mary catalyst for malicious cyber operations [ 4, 9]. Adversaries in cyber attacks 
systematically leverage malware to execute malicious intent, thereby inﬂicting 
severe ﬁnancial and operational losses on individuals, enterprises, and state enti-
ties. According to Statista’s cybersecurity analysis report, the global volume of 
malicious intrusion attempts has surged to 5.5 billion incidents in 2022, posing 
signiﬁcant pressure on network security [ 2]. Due to the highly concealed and 
high-risk nature, the detection of malware has attracted signiﬁcant attention 
from both academia and industry [ 16,26]. Accurately identifying malware within 
network infrastructures presents a critical yet technically challenging task for 
cybersecurity professionals. Employing an automated detection system enables 
more eﬀective malware identiﬁcation, which potentially reduces the cyber attack 
risks faced by enterprise networks to some extent. 
Many previous studies have focused on using machine learning methods to 
identify malicious traﬃc generated by known malware samples [ 7,17,25]. Addi-
tionally, researchers utilize deep learning methods to minimize dependence on 
feature selection techniques and domain expertise [ 3,12,15]. These methods 
typically handle network ﬂows. Malware traﬃc classiﬁcation approaches typi-
cally deﬁne network ﬂows using ﬁve-tuple identiﬁers (source/destination IPs, 
source/destination ports, and protocol), process traﬃc at the individual packet 
or aggregated ﬂow level, and leverage machine learning models to autonomously 
analyze extracted traﬃc characteristics, demonstrating high detection accuracy. 
However, detection methods relying on features of individual ﬂows cannot 
be generalized to unknown types of malware and ignore the graph connectivity 
behavioral features reﬂected by the traﬃc graph structure. To address this lim-
itation, another trend of studies [ 6,16,24] aim to reveal the essential attributes 
of malware behavior by examining the relationships between hosts and servers. 
Previous research has primarily focused on utilizing single type of character-
istics, like ﬂow features or contextual information, which lacks robust feature 
representation for malware traﬃc. 
Therefore, this paper introduces Mal-GAT, a graph neural network-based 
method that leverages both local and global graph behavior features to charac-
terize malware traﬃc behavior. Firstly, we construct a traﬃc behavior graph to 
represent the end-to-end communications among hosts and servers. Speciﬁcally, 
we use IP addresses as nodes and the communication traﬃc ﬂows between hosts 
and servers as edges to build the graph. The local features represent the traﬃc 
attributes extracted from traﬃc ﬂows for nodes and edges, which are embedded 
in the ﬂow representations. On the other hand, the global features are depicted 
by the graph structure. The generated graph at this point is a multigraph. In 
order to reduce the number of edges for computational eﬃciency and further 
feature aggregation, we perform edge clustering and aggregate multiple clusters 
to create new edges that replace the original multiple edges between nodes. Sub-
sequently, we employ graph attention networks to autonomously capture the 
local and global features. Compared with existing methods, Mal-GAT enhances

## Página PDF 246

Mal-GAT
237
traﬃc characterization by incorporating graph-structured behavioral correla-
tions. With graph representation learning, Mal-GAT extends the spectrum of 
information usable for identifying malware traﬃc, which enhances detection 
accuracy and robustness. We conduct comprehensive comparative evaluations 
between our method and others on multiple datasets to validate the eﬀective-
ness of Mal-GAT. 
The contributions are as follows: 
– We propose Mal-GAT, a malware traﬃc detection method based on graph 
attention networks. By exploring local and global features, Mal-GAT eﬀec-
tively reveals malicious behaviors within a traﬃc behavior graph, which 
achieves the classiﬁcation of malware traﬃc. 
– We propose a method for constructing graph-based traﬃc behavior from mal-
ware traﬃc. This approach recasts network traﬃc categorization as a graph 
classiﬁcation task by modeling traﬃc interactions as graph-structured behav-
ioral patterns. Empirical evaluations conﬁrm the method’s capability to eﬀec-
tively characterize malware traﬃc behavior, enhancing the accuracy of mal-
ware traﬃc detection. 
– Rigorous experimental validation conﬁrms Mal-GAT’s superior classiﬁcation 
eﬃcacy over existing approaches, with cross-dataset performance averaging 
96.27% accuracy and 94.74% F1-score. 
The rest of this paper is structured as follows: Sect. 2 reviews relevant works. 
We introduce the methodological framework in Sect. 3. Section 4 details experi-
mental validation. Finally, Sect. 5 concludes this paper. 
2
Related Work 
Malicious network traﬃc classiﬁcation methodologies have been extensively 
investigated across diverse application domains. With the development of tech-
nology, these methods can be primarily categorized into three groups: rule-based, 
machine learning-based, and deep learning-based methods. 
Rule-Based Methods. Rules-based malicious traﬃc identiﬁcation methods 
often execute network analytics by leveraging deep packet inspection (DPI). 
This approach serves as a widely adopted technique in network traﬃc analysis 
for application ﬁngerprinting and malicious activity detection. In particular, the 
Intrusion Detection System (IDS) extensively relies on DPI techniques to accu-
rately monitor traﬃc and detect malicious activities. TorWard [ 18] integrates an 
IDS at the Tor exit router for the classiﬁcation of malicious Tor traﬃc. The IDS 
identiﬁes malicious traﬃc through signature-based rule matching. Nevertheless, 
the growing prevalence of network encryption protocols has rendered payload 
inspection-based detection methods increasingly ineﬀective.

[Vista original de la página 246](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-246.png)

![Página PDF 246, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-246.png)


<!-- PDF page 247 -->

## Página PDF 247

238
J. Ma et al.
Machine Learning-Based Methods. With encrypted traﬃc having become 
mainstream in the cyber world, rule-based methods have become ineﬀective. 
Therefore, machine learning-based approaches have become an important direc-
tion for detecting malware traﬃc. These methods typically extract payload-
agnostic features from either the packet level or ﬂow level, and then employ 
suitable machine learning techniques for training [ 7,13,19,25]. Shaﬁq et al. [ 20] 
propose a feature selection metric called CorrAUC and subsequently develop a 
new feature selection algorithm, which uses the Area Under the Curve (AUC) 
metric to accurately ﬁlter features and select eﬀective features to achieve accu-
rate malware traﬃc detection. Li et al. [ 17] developed a machine learning frame-
work for IoT malware attribution analysis in APT campaigns, enabling pre-
cise identiﬁcation of adversarial infrastructure. Bovenzi et al. [ 5] systematically 
benchmarked classical machine learning models (1-D CNN, Decision Tree, and 
Random Forest) against deep learning counterparts for Android malware traf-
ﬁc classiﬁcation, demonstrating that conventional machine learning approaches 
are still eﬀective in some scenarios. They establish robust malware detection 
frameworks that exploit network traﬃc behavior to identify malicious activities. 
Deep Learning-Based Methods. Machine learning based methods can over-
come the limitations of rules-based methods. However, traditional machine learn-
ing approaches in malware traﬃc analysis depend on domain expertise for man-
ual feature engineering and dataset-speciﬁc feature adaptation. Deep learning, 
with its end-to-end feature extraction capabilities, has become a popular research 
focus among scholars. Many researchers employ deep learning for feature distil-
lation to address feature selection issues. Current research predominantly relies 
on building deep learning models to classify malware traﬃc [ 15,21]. Han et 
al. [ 14] propose a method called ContraMTD based on contrastive learning, 
which extracts local behavior features and global interaction features from nor-
mal network traﬃc. Subsequently, it utilizes contrastive learning to understand 
the relationship between these features and detects malware traﬃc through a 
multi-round scoring approach. Fu et al. [ 10] propose a real-time unsupervised 
machine learning system for malware traﬃc detection. It can detect unknown 
patterns of encrypted malware traﬃc by capturing ﬂow interaction patterns 
through graph-based structural characteristics. 
3
Methodology 
In this section, we develop a model based on Graph Attention Network to classify 
malware traﬃc. Subsequently, we systematically delineate the model’s overall 
architecture and elucidate its algorithmic workﬂows. 
3.1
Overview 
In this section, we develop Mal-GAT which is a graph neural network based 
method to detect suspicious communication traﬃc generated by internal hosts

Fig. 1. Workﬂow of Mal-GAT. 
accessing external servers. Traﬃc patterns within the same malware family typ-
ically exhibit a clustering of behavioral traits, indicating their similarity. This 
similarity, a result of code framework reuse, is reﬂected in the target servers that 
the malware attempts to connect to and in traﬃc interaction behaviors [ 11]. After 
an infection, victims establish connections with the adversary’s control server to 
receive and execute commands. Malware commonly establishes connections to 
distinct destination servers at various stages, constructing a connection graph. 
The malicious behaviors observed in the interactions between attackers and vic-
tims vary across diﬀerent malware types. Therefore, Mal-GAT constructs graphs 
to maintain interaction patterns among various ﬂows and performs malware traf-
ﬁc detection by analyzing the interaction patterns features of the graph structure 
regardless of whether the traﬃc is encrypted or non-encrypted. Figure 1 shows 
three key parts of Mal-GAT, i.e., graph construction, graph pre-processing, and 
GAT model construction. 
3.2
Graph Construction 
Mal-GAT utilizes network ﬂows for graph construction, beneﬁting from the 
inherent graph structure of network connectivity patterns. Initially, we apply 
ﬂow reassembly to reconstruct the entire communication. In traﬃc classiﬁca-
tion, a ﬂow is commonly deﬁned as a sequence of packets sharing the same 
quintuples (source/destination IPs, source/destination ports, and protocol). We

[Vista original de la página 248](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-248.png)

![Página PDF 248, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-248.png)


<!-- PDF page 249 -->

## Página PDF 249

240
J. Ma et al.
extract the ﬁve-tuple from each packet and integrate the packets with identical 
ﬁve-tuple into a single ﬂow. 
In Mal-GAT, IP addresses serve as nodes, while ﬂows represent the edges. 
Therefore, the graph is formally deﬁned as G = (V, E)G = (V, E), where VV represents 
the set of vertices (IP addresses) and E = \left\lbrace e|e=(u,v), u \in V, v \in V \right\rbraceE = {e|e = (u, v), u ∈V, v ∈V } denotes 
the set of edges representing the connections between these vertices. To extract 
topological graph characteristics comprehensively, we employ a sliding window 
mechanism to partition packets into time windows. Each time window encom-
passes a mixture of diﬀerent traﬃc types, including both malicious and benign, 
reﬂecting real-world scenarios. Within each sliding window, we extract node and 
edge features at both the packet and ﬂow levels. It is worth noting that we 
employ unidirectional ﬂows that fully comply with the deﬁnition of ﬂows. Obvi-
ously, since two IP addresses typically generate numerous ﬂows between them, 
the resulting graph is a multigraph.

Node Features Extraction. For the nodes, we extract a 58-dimensional fea-
ture vector, stratiﬁed into three taxonomically distinct groups. 
– Aggregate features: They represent comprehensive traﬃc metrics derived 
from network ﬂows, encompassing the total count of packets, total length of

[Vista original de la página 249](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-249.png)

<!-- PDF page 250 -->

## Página PDF 250

Mal-GAT
241
packets, TCP packet count, the number of HTTP packets, and the number 
of TLS packets, etc. 
– Statistical features: They are computed from packet size and TCP window 
size data, including maximum, minimum, mean, and standard deviation of 
both packet size and TCP window size. 
– Protocol features: These features pertain to protocol-speciﬁc ﬁelds, primarily 
including certain ﬁelds from protocols such as TCP, HTTP, TLS, and DNS. 
They include metrics such as the count of FIN packets, SYN packets, ACK 
packets, and the number of unique HTTP user agents, etc. 
The extracted node features are detailed in Table 1, with the column Index 
serving to identify each feature. These features reﬂect various aspects of node 
behavior. Some capture the volume of inbound and outbound traﬃc, others 
indicate the connection state at the transport layer, and some represent the 
number of diﬀerent application layer protocols used by the node. Together, they 
characterize the node’s behavior patterns from diﬀerent perspectives. 
We perform Z-score normalization to vertex attribute vectors for optimizing 
gradient dynamics during graph neural network training. Z-score normalization 
standardizes data into a standard normal distribution via mean subtraction and 
standard deviation scaling. It eliminates the scale diﬀerences in data, resulting 
in a mean of 0 and a standard deviation of 1. This normalization method makes 
data easier to compare and analyze during processing while being less aﬀected 
by outliers.

Edge Features Extraction. Compared to node features, edge features are 
relatively simpler. Table 2 shows the details of edge features. Each edge represents 
a unidirectional ﬂow, from which we extract features such as the maximum, 
minimum, mean, standard deviation, and sum of both packet length and ﬂow 
duration. After edge preprocessing, we also incorporate the number of ﬂows in 
each cluster, which will be discussed in the next section. 
Graph Building. After extracting node and edge features, the graph construc-
tion process is outlined in Algorithm 1. Following the generation of sliding win-
dows, the temporal windowing mechanism generates sequential traﬃc graphs, 
with each window corresponding to the representation of one traﬃc graph sam-
ple. The latter part of the algorithm involves edge clustering and weighted edge 
aggregation, which will be detailed in the following sections.

3.3
Graph Pre-processing 
We pre-process the constructed interaction graph to reduce processing overhead 
through edge clustering and weighted aggregation. Communication between two 
IP addresses typically involves a large number of ﬂows. To manage this, we 
cluster the edges connecting two nodes into multiple clusters and represent the 
ﬂows within each cluster using cluster centers. This clustering method eﬀectively 
detects and aggregates edges with similar ﬂow statistical features, thereby reduc-
ing the scale of the graph. Additionally, we apply weighted edge aggregation to 
merge multiple cluster centers, converting the multigraph into a simple graph. 
Following this aggregation process, each pair of nodes in the same direction 
has at most one edge between them, signiﬁcantly reducing the graph’s size and 
complexity.

Fig. 2. Edge clustering and edge feature aggregation. 
Edge Clustering. We now proceed to further preprocess the edges to enhance 
detection eﬃciency. As shown in Fig. 2, the graph is a multigraph with multi-
ple edges connecting vertices. We observe that the distribution of the edges is 
sparse, meaning that most edges are adjacent to numerous similar edges in the 
feature space. To exploit this sparsity, we perform the DBSCAN algorithm [ 8] 
for clustering, which groups edges based on their attributes. During edge clus-
tering, we compute the center of each cluster and select the cluster centers of 
the identiﬁed clusters to represent all edges within each cluster, thereby reducing 
the processing overhead of the graph. 
The primary purpose of edge clustering is to aggregate edges with similar 
attributes to better understand network structures, uncover hidden patterns, 
and reduce the complexity of graphs. This provides a more eﬀective foundation 
for subsequent analysis and processing. 
Edge Feature Aggregation. Through clustering, we have divided multiple 
edges between nodes into diﬀerent clusters and represented each cluster with

To precisely capture the dependencies between nodes and make the model more 
focused on the graph structure, we employ edge feature aggregation. Through 
this mechanism, we calculate weight scores for the parallel edges between each 
pair of nodes and aggregate them using a weighted sum. Consequently, we merge 
these multiple parallel edges into a new edge, transforming the multigraph into 
a simple graph. The new edge representation is calculated as:

In addition, the weight of a cluster is the quantity of ﬂows in it, which is then 
processed through a softmax calculation. 
By performing weighted edge aggregation, at most one edge exists between 
any pair of nodes in the same direction after merging clusters. This signiﬁcantly 
decreases the graph’s scale while preserving crucial structural features. 
3.4
GAT Model Construction 
Graph neural networks (GNNs) are an advanced category of deep learning tech-
niques designed for analyzing graph data. Their impressive ability to capture 
intricate relationships within graph structures has led to their increasing promi-
nence in recent research. Among the various GNN architectures, the Graph Con-
volutional Network (GCN) stands out as a fundamental model, which generates 
node embeddings by combining the attributes of adjacent nodes. Building on 
this foundation, the Graph Attention Network (GAT) introduces enhancements 
through an attention mechanism, which reﬁnes node features by dynamically 
weighting the importance of neighboring nodes. 
In this paper, we address the traﬃc classiﬁcation problem by framing it as 
a graph classiﬁcation task. This is achieved through the construction of traﬃc 
behavior graphs. Speciﬁcally, we deﬁne the graph classiﬁcation problem as fol-
lows: let H = (G, L)H = (G, L) represent a labeled graph dataset, where GG is a collection 
of graphs and LL is a corresponding list of labels for these graphs. The goal of 
the graph classiﬁcation task is to learn a mapping function \phiφ from the dataset 
HH to predict the labels of unlabeled graph samples. This approach leverages the 
strengths of graph neural networks to eﬀectively analyze and classify malware 
traﬃc data. 
Graph Attention Mechanism. GAT extends GCN by incorporating an atten-
tion mechanism. While GCN aggregates features from neighboring nodes with 
equal weight, GAT introduces a weighted aggregation process that allows for

[Vista original de la página 253](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-253.png)

245
more reﬁned information integration. In a GCN, every neighbor node con-
tributes equally to the updated node representation. This approach, however, 
may not adequately capture the varying importance of diﬀerent neighbors. GAT 
addresses this limitation by applying an attention mechanism, which assigns dif-
ferent weights to neighboring nodes based on their relevance. This mechanism 
enables the model to focus more on important neighbors and less on less rel-
evant ones, thereby improving the quality of the learned node representations. 
The global interaction feature is jointly determined by node attributes, edge 
attributes, and graph typology. To capture these features and leverage edge 
attributes, we enhance the GAT layer by incorporating edge features. Speciﬁ-
cally, while GAT traditionally focuses on the features of neighboring nodes, we 
also consider edge features during the aggregation process. This improves the 
representation of node interaction patterns by accounting for edge attributes. 
Formally, the graph attention mechanism can be described as follows: 
\bALT{} o_{ij} = \mathrm{LeakyReLU} \big( \mathbf{a}^T [ \mathbf{W}_{n} \cdot \mathbf{h}_i || \mathbf{W}_{n} \cdot \mathbf{h}_j || \mathbf{W}_{e} \cdot \mathbf{e}_{i,j} ] \big)\eALT{} oij = LeakyReLU aT [Wn · hi||Wn · hj||We · ei,j]
(3)

where \mathbf{h}_ihi, \mathbf{e}_{i,j}ei,j represent features of node ii and features of edge i,ji, j, \mathbf{a}a corre-
sponding to a learnable vector. \mathbf{W}W denotes the learnable weight matrices that 
transform features for the target node (n = node), neighboring nodes (n = neigh-
bor), and edge attributes (e = edge). \mathrm{softmax_i}softmaxi refers to the normalization across 
all incoming edges of node ii, and \alpha_{ij}αij represents the attention weight bewteen 
node ii and node jj. The attention score calculation process is shown in Fig. 3.

GAT enhances malware traﬃc classiﬁcation by adaptively weighting the 
importance of neighboring nodes and edges, allowing for more precise feature

Architecture of Mal-GAT Model. Next, we introduce the model structure. 
Figure 4 shows the architecture of Mal-GAT. First is the embedding layer. Node 
features and edge features can have better feature representation after embed-
ding. Then, there are NN layers of GAT (Graph Attention Network) layers, where 
we consider edge features when computing attention. Each GAT layer consists of 
EdgeGATConv, residual connections, and layer normalization. Finally, there is 
the MLPReadout layer, which aggregates and reads out features from the graph. 
We choose the mean aggregation method to output features.

4
Evaluation 
In the previous sections, we discussed the construction and preprocessing of mal-
ware traﬃc graphs, introduced the graph attention mechanism, and outlined the 
model architecture. This section methodically delineates three principal compo-
nents: experimental datasets, evaluation criteria, and systematic benchmarking 
of Mal-GAT against existing approaches.

4.1
Datasets 
We perform comprehensive experiments across three benchmark malware traﬃc 
datasets to evaluate the eﬀectiveness of Mal-GAT, with Table 3 summarizing 
their composition.

MTA. Malware-traﬃc-analysis.net [ 1] serves as an open-access website hosting 
packet capture (PCAP) and correlated malware samples. In our experiment, 
we preprocessed the raw data by excluding PCAP ﬁles with multiple labels and 
malware samples with only a small number of packets, as these can not construct 
a suﬃciently clear graph structure. Ultimately, we retained ﬁfteen diﬀerent types 
of malware samples for further experimentation. 
Stratosphere. Stratosphere ISP [ 22] operates the Malware Capture Facility 
Project, which is dedicated to long-term malware capture and the ongoing col-
lection of both malicious and benign traﬃc data. For our evaluation, we ﬁltered 
out malware samples that lacked explicit labels and used the remaining data for 
our evaluation. Consequently, we incorporate 24 distinct malware variants in our 
experiment. 
USTC-TFC2016. Wang et al. [ 23] curate a network traﬃc dataset comprising 
malicious and benign traﬃc instances. After excluding the malware with only a 
small number of packets, we used four types of malware traﬃc for evaluation. 
4.2
Metrics 
Here, we use common evaluation metrics, such as precision (P), recall (R), F1-
score (F1), and accuracy (ACC), to quantify the performance of Mal-GAT. For 
each malware, operationaldeﬁnitions are established. True Positive (TP): Graphs 
were accurately classiﬁed to their corresponding malware. False Positive (FP): 
Benign traﬃc graphs erroneously mapped to malicious categories. False Negative 
(FN): Malicious behavioral graphs misclassiﬁed into non-malware clusters. The 
above metrics are deﬁned for each malware as follows: 
\bALT{} Precision = \frac{TP}{TP+FP}\eALT{} Precision =
TP
TP + FP

Micro average and macro average are two methods for evaluating performance 
in multi-class classiﬁcation tasks. Micro average focuses on the overall classiﬁ-
cation performance of all samples and is suitable for scenarios where balancing 
the classiﬁcation of numerous samples is crucial. Macro average emphasizes the 
performance of each individual class and is appropriate for cases with signiﬁ-
cant class imbalance, providing a fair assessment of each class’s eﬀectiveness. 
Therefore, we adopt the macro average across these three evaluation metrics. In 
addition, we also use accuracy as an evaluation metric: 
\bALT{} Accuracy = \frac{#\ of\ correct\ identification}{#\ of\ total\ identification}\eALT{} Accuracy = # of correct identification
# of total identification
(9) 
4.3
Experimental Setting 
We take the graph representations of network traﬃc ﬂows as the model input. 
The time window is set to 300 s. The GAT architecture adopts a 4-layer hierarchi-
cal structure with 256-dimensional hidden states, optimized via AdamAdam (learning 
rate = 0.001) and regularization (dropout = 0.3). We implement our approach 
with Pytorch 2.3.1 and DGL 2.3, deployed on a computational server equipped 
with a 28-core CPU, 128GB RAM, and NVIDIA 3090 GPU acceleration.

4.4
Experimental Results 
We utilize three benchmark malware traﬃc datasets to evaluate the performance 
of Mal-GAT based on precision, recall, F1-score, and accuracy. After constructing 
graphs from network traﬃc, we randomly split these graphs into three groups, 
80% for training, 10% for validation, and 10% for testing.

As shown in Table 4, Mal-GAT achieves detection accuracies of 88.98% 
(MTA), 99.83% (Stratosphere), and 100.0% (USTC-TFC2016), with precision, 
recall, and F1 all exceeding 98.95% on the latter two datasets. Figure 5 shows the 
confusion heatmap for Stratosphere, where rows correspond to the actual labels, 
while columns correspond to the predictions. It reveals row-column alignment 
between ground-truth labels and predicted classes, with diagonal dominance 
indicating robust classiﬁcation consistency. Our method demonstrates recall 
rates 100% for 20 malware variants, with marginal misclassiﬁcations limited 
to morphologically similar families. These empirical results validate Mal-GAT’s 
capability for precise malware traﬃc characterization through graph-structured 
behavioral analysis. 
Fig. 5. Confusion matrix on Stratosphere dataset. 
Comparison with Traditional Machine Learning Methods. To validate 
the methodological superiority, we perform a comparison with six baseline tradi-
tional machine learning methods: Logistic Regression (LR), Decision Tree (DT), 
Random Forest (RF), Support Vector Machine (SVM), Naive Bayes (NB), K-
Nearest Neighbors (KNN). Although the machine learning models used were 
introduced relatively early, they have been thoroughly validated for traﬃc clas-
siﬁcation and are widely adopted as baseline models in this domain. To ensure 
experimental parity, we merge all ﬂows related to a single graph sample into one

Our approach shows a signiﬁcant improvement over the baselines on the MTA 
and USTC-TFC2016 datasets. For the Stratosphere dataset, while the improve-
ment is less pronounced, our method still delivers a clear advantage. Among the 
baseline models, random forest consistently achieves the best results across all 
three datasets. Nevertheless, our method outperforms RF across all evaluation 
metrics on each dataset. In particular, it achieves 9.75% higher accuracy and 
10.72% higher F1-score than RF on the MTA dataset. Additionally, it surpasses 
RF in every metric on the remaining two datasets as well. These results against 
traditional machine learning approaches conﬁrm the eﬀectiveness of Mal-GAT.

Comparison with Deep Learning Methods. We also conduct a comparative 
analysis using two deep learning methods: Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) 
and Deep Neural Network (DNN). DNN and CNN are basic neural network 
models. Table 5 shows the experimental results across three datasets. Compared 
to the two methods, ours achieves better performance on two of the datasets, 
and matches the performance of DNN on the USTC-TFC2016 dataset. We reach 
11.5%, 10.54%, 11.22%, and 11.38% more than theirs across all metrics on the 
MTA dataset. Our GAT-based method, along with edge features, demonstrates 
signiﬁcant eﬃcacy against comparative benchmarks. 
Comparison with NF-GNN. Busch et al. [ 6] propose NF-GNN, which uti-
lizes GNN for malware traﬃc classiﬁcation. Firstly, it constructs communication 
graphs using all the network ﬂows that have been captured between any pair 
of endpoints within a time window to capture comprehensive network commu-
nication patterns. Then, NF-GNN builds an edge-attentive GNN architecture 
that learns appropriate representations from the ﬂow-based graph structures. 
Additionally, it develops three model variants, which are capable of performing 
supervised malware detection as well as unsupervised malware detection. We 
conduct comparative experiments with the graph classiﬁer. As shown in Fig. 6 
We achieve a signiﬁcantly higher accuracy than NF-GNN, with improvements 
of 10.75%, 2.66%, and 1.14%, respectively. Similarly, our F1-score also surpasses

Ablation Study. Table 6 reveals substantial performance diﬀerentials between 
Mal-GAT and w/o E-F, where w/o E-F denotes training without the edge fea-
tures, while Mal-GAT refers to training with all node and edge features. Table 6 
indicates that edge attribute integration elevates MTA dataset performance in 
accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score by 10.55%, 6.01%, 12.14%, and 10.91%, 
respectively. Similarly, on USTC-TFC2016, edge-enriched modeling achieves an 
increase in accuracy, precision, recall, and F1-score by 4.76%, 4.17%, 2.63%, and 
3.66%, respectively, validating the eﬀectiveness of edge features. Regarding the 
Stratosphere dataset, Mal-GAT exhibits marginal yet consistent gains. Experi-
mental results demonstrate that integrating edge features during the aggregation 
of neighboring node features leads to signiﬁcant performance improvement. 
Discussion. Mal-GAT eﬀectively utilizes the structural information embedded 
in malware traﬃc graphs. By incorporating edge features into the GAT model, 
we can extract valuable insights from the edge ﬂows, signiﬁcantly improving 
the experimental outcomes. Our extensive evaluations on various datasets have 
consistently demonstrated the high accuracy of Mal-GAT. However, our analysis 
reveals several limitations in our method. For instance, while there is a slight

combining the strengths of both traditional and deep learning methods to further

5
Conclusion 
In this paper, we propose Mal-GAT, a graph neural network-based method for 
the purpose of malware traﬃc classiﬁcation. Mal-GAT processes traﬃc ﬂow rep-
resented as graphs and leverages these representations to automatically deter-
mine the malware class. We speciﬁcally utilize a Graph Attention Network 
(GAT) integrated with edge features to capture and learn distinctive charac-
teristics of various malware types from the network traﬃc graph. 
Additionally, we employ edge cluster and edge feature weighted aggrega-
tion to eﬀectively extract features from the edges of graphs, thereby enhancing 
its classiﬁcation capability and reducing the size and computational complexity 
of the graph. Experimental results demonstrate the eﬀectiveness of Mal-GAT, 
highlighting its superior performance in accurately classifying malware traﬃc. 
Mal-GAT oﬀers a robust solution for malware detection by eﬀectively leverag-
ing graph structures and edge features to enhance malware traﬃc classiﬁcation 
capabilities.

## Página PDF 264

A Federated Learning Framework 
with Blockchain and Cache Pools for Unreliable 
Devices in a Cloud-Edge-End Environment 
Junfeng Tian1,2, Tengfei Xu1,2envelope symbol, and Qi Zhu1,2 
1 School of Cyber Security and Computer, Hebei University, Baoding 071002, China 
tjf@hbu.edu.cn, {xutengfei,zq}@stumail.hbu.edu.cn 
2 Hebei Key Laboratory of High Conﬁdence Information Systems, Hebei University, 
Baoding 071000, China 
Abstract. The rapid popularization of the Internet of Things has enabled the edge 
environment to obtain massive data resources. Usually, these data resources need 
to be further utilized with the help of cloud servers. But this will bring huge com-
munication overhead and privacy security issues. In order to meet this challenge, 
we propose a safe and efﬁcient data processing scheme based on cloud-edge-
end collaboration for federated learning, which can use edge data resources for 
model training while ensuring low communication overhead and a reliable pri-
vacy and security environment. This scheme builds blockchains in sub-regions 
in the edge environment to ensure the safety and reliability of federated learn-
ing end devices. In addition, the solution adopts a layered computing strategy to 
parallelize the cloud-edge network and the edge-end network. At the same time, 
the cache pool mechanism is used to alleviate the communication congestion of 
asynchronous federated learning and the problem of outdated parameter versions, 
which improves the overall efﬁciency of the system. Finally, simulation experi-
ments show that our scheme is very suitable for the cloud-edge-end environment, 
tolerates the heterogeneity and unreliability of terminal devices, and improves the 
efﬁciency of global model construction. 
Keywords: Federated Learning cdotEdge Networks cdotMobile Edge Computing cdot
Distributed Machine Learning 
1 
Introduction 
With the rapid development of social networking applications, the data generated by end 
devices (e.g., cell phones, computers, cars, etc.) is growing exponentially. Meanwhile, 
the rise of the Internet of Things (IoT) and Edge Intelligence (EI) has shifted the frontier 
of Artiﬁcial Intelligence (AI) from the cloud to the network edge, enabling computing 
to be closer to end users and unlocking the vast potential of AI services [1]. These 
research trends have led to a growing focus on leveraging the massive data generated 
by end devices to empower AI applications. Edge computing (EC) [2] is a distributed

https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-93257-1_14

![Página PDF 264, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-264.png)


<!-- PDF page 265 -->

## Página PDF 265

256
J. Tian et al.
computing paradigm similar to cloud computing, but with the distinction of being closer 
to end users. It complements cloud computing and enables efﬁcient data processing 
in the era of big data through cloud-edge-end collaboration. Mobile Edge Computing 
(MEC) [3, 4], which encompasses cloud, edge nodes, and end devices, is an emerging 
technology that facilitates cloud-edge-end collaboration. It serves as an infrastructure 
for providing edge AI services in collaborative scenarios. However, in real-world AI 
scenarios, the training of machine learning models often involves a large number of 
intelligent end devices, which brings challenges despite the availability of massive data 
resources. Firstly, the data generated by terminal devices is often isolated (known as data 
silos). Due to privacy protection concerns, this data cannot freely leave the local area, 
making centralization and utilization extremely difﬁcult. Additionally, while traditional 
distributed machine learning techniques can partially utilize decentralized data, their 
high communication overhead becomes burdensome for end devices in cloud-edge-end 
collaboration scenarios. 
Initially proposed as a new distributed machine learning framework, Federated 
Learning (FL) [5, 6] has the ability to alleviate the aforementioned data privacy and 
communication efﬁciency issues. FL enables the cross-silo exploitation of end-device 
generated data to build a global machine learning model without compromising the 
data privacy of each participant. Additionally, FL only requires the exchange of locally 
updated model parameters, greatly improving communication efﬁciency. As data privacy 
and security protection receive increasing attention, FL has become a suitable choice 
for building global machine learning models under classical two-layer architectures like 
cloud computing. The development of cutting-edge communication technologies such as 
5G and the popularity of edge devices provide a solid foundation for the application and 
development of FL. However, with the advancement of collaborative cloud-edge-end 
integration, there is still great potential to explore FL applications in a three-layer cloud-
edge-end architecture. The abundant resources (computation, storage, etc.) at the edge 
can greatly alleviate the slow and unreliable communication between cloud parameter 
servers and end devices, as well as the network load problem caused by the large number 
of devices involved in training. Existing edge-based studies [7, 8] bring FL to the edge 
environment to provide more stable connectivity and sufﬁcient computational resources 
for end devices. Nevertheless, there are still many limitations for the application of FL 
in the collaborative cloud-edge-end environment. 
Firstly, ensuring the reliability of end-device communication is challenging. Whether 
in a cloud-based or edge-based setting, end devices have a high degree of freedom and 
may join or drop out of training at any time, or communication may be interrupted due 
to issues like power outages. To address this, a research [9] proposed a FL protocol 
that employs a client selection algorithm to mitigate the impact of device dropouts and 
withdrawals. However, this approach may result in some clients with high-quality data 
but slow training speed never being selected. 
Secondly, the reliability of local training on end devices is unknown. Due to the 
privacy-preserving nature of FL, the local training process of each end device is not 
visible to the global system. This opens up the possibility of malicious clients training 
falsely and launching forged parameter attacks to disrupt the optimization of global 
model aggregation. Swarm Learning [10] introduces a decentralized FL framework

## Página PDF 266

A Federated Learning Framework with Blockchain and Cache Pools
257
based on blockchain networks to ensure client-side conﬁdentiality and facilitate the 
utilization of distributed data for disease classiﬁer development. However, traditional 
blockchains impose signiﬁcant resource overhead, especially for FL on end devices in 
edge environments, which is a challenge that cannot be ignored. 
Lastly, the speed of global model building is slow. In the context of cloud-edge-end 
collaboration, adopting a synchronous FL framework often leads to unbearable waiting 
times during the synchronization process. This creates mutual constraints among the 
cloud, edge, and end components, and the overall training speed depends on the slowest 
component. Asynchronous FL frameworks can eliminate the waiting time, but they often 
introduce the issue of parameter update version delay. 
In this paper, we propose a new framework for semi-asynchronous FL called 
BCA-FL, which aims to address the limitations mentioned above and achieve privacy-
preserving, reliable, and efﬁcient FL in a cloud-edge-end collaborative environment. 
BCA-FL leverages edge computing resources while considering the unreliability and 
resource constraints of end devices. BCA-FL introduces a Directed Acyclic Graph 
(DAG)-based blockchain into the sub-regions of the edge environment, ensuring relia-
bility without excessively increasing resource overhead. By leveraging the DAG-based 
blockchain, BCA-FL mitigates the impact of malicious clients in the edge environment. 
This mechanism helps maintain the integrity of the global model while minimizing 
the inﬂuence of adversaries. Furthermore, BCA-FL adopts a semi-asynchronous FL 
framework, utilizing an aggregation cache pool mechanism and parameter compensa-
tion algorithm. These techniques aim to mitigate client dropout and parameter version 
control delays, improving the overall efﬁciency and reliability of the FL process. By 
combining compensatory caching, DAG-based blockchain, and a semi-asynchronous FL 
framework, BCA-FL addresses the challenges of privacy, reliability, and efﬁciency in a 
cloud-edge-end collaborative environment. It offers a promising solution for implement-
ing FL while considering the unique characteristics and constraints of edge computing 
and end devices. 
The main contributions of this paper are as follows: 
• We propose a federated learning framework BCA-FL that adapts to the new sce-
nario of cloud-edge-end collaboration, combining blockchain and edge computing to 
empower federated learning. It can efﬁciently build a global machine learning model 
through collaboration between cloud-edge-end and three parties while satisfying 
privacy protection conditions; 
• We divide the edge environment into independent sub-region, and build a DAG-based 
blockchain in each sub-region to ensure the honesty and reliability of end devices. 
It prevents model tampering attacks and false training by malicious clients from 
destroying the construction of the global machine learning model; 
• We combine the aggregation cache pooling mechanism and parameter compensation 
algorithm, thus alleviating the communication pressure and parameter version delay 
phenomenon at the aggregation end caused by asynchronous FL, and reducing the 
negative impact of stale versions on the model aggregation effect. 
The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Sect. 2, the work related to FL is 
introduced, mainly the basic concepts of FL and the work related to the combination of 
FL with edge computing and blockchain. Section 3 details the mechanisms related to the

2 
Literature Review 
2.1 
Federated Learning 
FL aims to build a global learning model based on distributed datasets [11]. It was 
ﬁrst proposed by Google to solve the problem of data silos while satisfying privacy 
protection, and to use data distributed across different devices for efﬁcient learning 
[5]. They proposed the classical FedAvg algorithm, as shown in Fig. 1 (FL FedAvg 
schematic), where a parameter server performs global coordination and sends down the 
global model to the selected clients. After waiting for all selected clients to upload their 
local training result models, the parameter server performs the weighted aggregation 
update of the global model. After that, the above process is repeated for another batch 
of selected clients until a global model that meets the conditions is obtained before 
terminating the iteration. 
The main features of FL [11] are as follows. (a) Multiparty participation: multiple 
participants collaborate to build a global machine learning model based on each party’s 
local model training. (b) Data privacy: each participant’s local data does not leave the 
local area during the FL training process, and each participant’s local training process 
is not visible to the global area. (c) Parameter communication: during the FL training 
process, only the communication of training model-related parameters exists, and each

[Vista original de la página 267](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-267.png)

![Página PDF 267, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-267.png)

## Página PDF 268

A Federated Learning Framework with Blockchain and Cache Pools
259
Fig. 2. The asynchronous aggregation federal learning process is outlined as follows: (1) the 
device obtains the initial model from the parameter server; (2) the device is trained locally; (3) 
the update results can be uploaded when the local training is completed; (4) the parameter server 
receives the update for model aggregation. 
participant’s local data does not participate in the communication process. (d) Model 
sharing: all participants work together to build the same shared global model. 
The FedAvg algorithm belongs to the synchronous FL algorithm, and the study [12] 
also uses the synchronous aggregation of the FL framework, which reduces the frequency 
of communication between the device and the parameter server and avoids frequent 
updates of the global model. However, the synchronous nature of the synchronous FL 
framework makes the algorithm less tolerant to unreliable devices. The efﬁciency of 
global aggregation will be greatly affected in case of communication unreliability such 
as communication failure or abnormal exit of the selected device [13]. 
In addition to the synchronous FL algorithm, the study [14] proposed the asyn-
chronous FL algorithm. As shown in Fig. 2, unlike the synchronous FL algorithm, the 
asynchronous federation does not require the end devices participating in the training 
to be selected in advance, and all end devices can download the global model for local 
training when they are idle. The global model can be updated when the parameter server 
receives the training result model parameter uploaded by the end devices. However, asyn-
chronous FL may also cause the parameter server to update the global model parameters 
frequently, causing communication congestion problems. Moreover, asynchronous FL 
is often accompanied by the problem of model obsolescence. When the end-device 
uploads the resultant model parameters after completing local training, the parameter 
server often has already performed too many rounds of global model parameter updates. 
If the resultant parameters of end-device training are updated with the current global 
model in an aggregated manner directly, it often brings certain negative effects [15].
