## Página PDF 269

260
J. Tian et al.
For the model staleness problem, FedAsync [14] introduces a hybrid hyperparameter 
and performs adaptive model optimization based on model staleness. Overlap-FedAvg 
[16] uses Taylor series expansions as a compensation mechanism for staleness model 
parameters as a way to alleviate the gradient staleness problem and ensure the conver-
gence speed of FL. Research [13] proposed a semi-asynchronous FL protocol SAFA to 
solve the problems of inefﬁcient rounds and slow convergence in extreme cases (e.g., 
frequent client disconnections) in FL. 
2.2 
Edge Computing Empowered FL 
As the Internet of everything continues to advance, a large number of devices connected 
to the Internet continue to generate massive amounts of data. The traditional cloud 
computing model is no longer sufﬁcient to support the demand for data processing in 
today’s intelligent era, and mobile edge computing (MEC), as a new computing model, 
can provide data processing services at the edge of mobile networks to better meet the 
demand for data processing in the era of big data. 
The MEC-based FL framework allows building smarter machine learning models 
while keeping the privacy of the client’s local data secure. FL can train global machine 
learning models without uploading edge user data to cloud servers. The study [9] pro-
posed a new protocol (HybridFL) to drive the FL process in the MEC three-layer archi-
tecture. HybridFL facilitates efﬁcient model exchange through a combination of quo-
tatriggered regional aggregation (edge aggregation) and instant cloud aggregation. A 
regional slack factor for each edge node (region) is introduced into the client selection 
step via a probabilistic estimation method to mitigate the impact of client exit. The study 
[7] addresses the problem of how to efﬁciently utilize the limited computational and 
communication resources at the edge to obtain the best machine learning model per-
formance. Its focus on gradient descent-based federal learning algorithms analyzes the 
convergence bound of distributed gradient descent from a theoretical perspective and 
proposes a control algorithm to determine the optimal trade-off between local updates 
and global parameter aggregation to minimize the loss function for a given resource 
budget. The study [8] proposes a cloud-edge-end hierarchical federation learning sys-
tem that supports the HierFAVG algorithm and allows multiple edge servers to perform 
local model aggregation. HierFAVG enables faster training of models and better trade-
offs between communication and computation. Research [17] combines digital twins 
with edge networks and proposes Digital Twin Edge Network (DITEN) to ﬁll the gap 
between physical edge networks and digital systems, and builds a FL scheme based on 
blockchain, which signiﬁcantly improves the communication efﬁciency and data secu-
rity of IoT applications. Inspired by edge computing, the study [18] proposed Edge 
FL (EdgeFed), which divides the local update process of model parameters into two 
parts, on mobile devices and edge servers, respectively. The mobile device can focus 
on the low-level training, and more computational tasks are assigned to the edge server, 
which is more abundant in computational resources. The output of the mobile device is 
aggregated in the edge server to improve learning efﬁciency and reduce the frequency 
of global communication.

## Página PDF 270

A Federated Learning Framework with Blockchain and Cache Pools
261
2.3 
Blockchain Empowered FL 
Blockchain is a decentralized technology that utilizes a peer-to-peer (P2P) distributed 
ledger to securely store and maintain information [19]. The traditional blockchain tech-
nology employs the Proof of Work (PoW) consensus algorithm, which requires signiﬁ-
cant resources to solve the hash problem and validate new blocks entering the chain [20]. 
To enhance the efﬁciency and reduce resource consumption, a DAG-based blockchain 
was proposed [21]. This approach involves attaching new transactions to the fork topol-
ogy without the need to maintain a main chain, allowing immediate recording of any 
newly arrived transaction in the blockchain. Hashgraph is a widely recognized DAG-
based blockchain that employs the gossip protocol for nodes to communicate with each 
other [22]. Through this protocol, each honest node eventually obtains the same Hash-
graph. Another example of a DAG-based consensus algorithm is Tangle [23, 24], which 
is utilized by IOTA. In Tangle, when a node creates a transaction, it must approve two 
previous transactions represented by directed edges in the DAG. Teegraph [25] is an 
innovative blockchain consensus algorithm model designed for IoT scenarios, combin-
ing Trusted Execution Environment (TEE) and DAG techniques. It introduces a unique 
self-parenting mechanism utilizing TEE to prevent forking attacks, ensuring that a node 
cannot create two different events with the same parent. 
FL guarantees the privacy of local data for each participant and enables them to 
contribute to the creation of a global model without having to upload their local user data. 
However, FL also poses challenges in identifying malicious participants and protecting 
against backdoor attacks [26]. Malicious participants may attempt to undermine the 
effectiveness of the global model by injecting fake or even malicious training samples. 
To address this issue, a recent study [27] proposes the use of blockchain’s tamperproof 
and traceability features to ensure participant honesty and integrity. 
Swarm Learning is a FL framework that combines edge computing and blockchain-
based peer-to-peer networking, ensuring conﬁdentiality without the need for a central 
coordinator [10]. In a recent study [28] based on a blockchain-based FL framework, a 
veriﬁcation committee is introduced to assess participant trustworthiness metrics. How-
ever, traditional blockchain networks often employ proof-of-work (PoW) consensus 
mechanisms, which tend to incur signiﬁcant computational resource overhead that is 
unsuitable for edge environments. To address this challenge, a study [29] proposes an 
asynchronous DAG-authorized FL approach, which establishes an efﬁcient on-device 
FL system without introducing excessive additional resource consumption. This app-
roach utilizes a voting consensus mechanism in a directed acyclic graph (DAG) based 
ledger technology to transition the blockchain from synchronous to asynchronous book-
keeping, eliminating the need for traditional mining. Furthermore, another study [30] 
integrates blockchain and FL into Telematics and develops a hybrid blockchain called 
PermiDAG, based on DAG. In this hybrid blockchain, the permission blockchain oper-
ates on the RSU (roadside unit) node as the main blockchain, while the local DAG runs 
on the vehicle node.

3 
Methodology 
This section ﬁrst outlines the BCA-FL framework, then introduces the aggregated 
cache pool mechanism with parameter compensation and region-independent edge 
blockchains, and ﬁnally describes the federated learning update process (Table 1). 
3.1 
System Design 
Table 1. List of Symbols

Traditional federated learning typically relies on a synchronous learning scheme, 
where the global model is updated between the server and client. However, in a cloud-
edge-end environment, using the synchronous learning scheme to train the global model 
efﬁciently with data distributed across numerous terminal devices presents several chal-
lenges: 1) Instability of terminal devices: In the edge environment, a large number of 
terminal devices may only perform learning tasks when they are idle. Additionally, 
the local resources of terminal devices can vary, resulting in the system runtime being 
dependent on the slowest participant to complete the task. 2) Dynamics of commu-
nication links: The edge environment is complex, and the stability of communication 
links between terminal devices and edge nodes can be dynamic. When communication 
stability deteriorates, it signiﬁcantly extends the system’s waiting time. 3) Lack of end 
device honesty guarantee: Due to privacy protection rules, end devices may carry out 
model forgery attacks to disrupt the system’s training process. This poses a challenge in 
ensuring the honesty and integrity of the participating end devices. 
To address challenges 1) and 2), we propose the use of asynchronous federated learn-
ing to construct the global model, reducing waiting time caused by synchronization. Our 
framework implements asynchronous federated learning through cached aggregation

[Vista original de la página 271](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-271.png)

## Página PDF 272

A Federated Learning Framework with Blockchain and Cache Pools
263
pools and a hierarchical sub-network structure. For challenge 3), we leverage blockchain 
technology to store and verify model parameters, enhancing the reliability and security 
of the scheme. Furthermore, we employ a parameter compensation algorithm based on 
an approximate Hessian matrix to mitigate the issue of stale model parameter versions 
introduced by asynchronous federated learning [31]. 
Our proposed federated learning framework is designed to facilitate collaborative 
learning in cloud-edge-end environments. The framework comprises three key compo-
nents: edge nodes, cloud servers, and terminal devices. Figure 3 illustrates the architec-
ture of the framework. The edge network is divided into multiple regions based on geo-
graphical relationships. Each region establishes a blockchain network using the directed 
acyclic graph (DAG) consensus mechanism. These blockchain networks ensure secure 
and efﬁcient storage of transactions and facilitate consensus among the end devices 
within each region. The cloud server acts as the central point for coordination and syn-
chronization of the entire framework. It facilitates communication and data exchange 
between the edge nodes and terminal devices. Terminal devices are responsible for 
performing local training tasks and contributing their results to the collective training 
process. They have limited computing and communication resources. The edge nodes 
serve as intermediaries, connecting the cloud server and the terminal devices. They per-
form regional aggregation within the edge network. The framework adopts a two-layer 
sub-network structure: the cloud-edge network and the edge-end network. Communica-
tion between these two subnets is only possible through the edge nodes. The edge nodes 
connect to the cloud server through uplink communication and connect to the end devices 
through downlink communication. There is no direct communication between the cloud 
server and the end devices. The cloud parameter server governs multiple independent 
edge block networks, and each edge block network oversees multiple end devices. This 
hierarchical structure allows for efﬁcient management and coordination of the federated 
learning process.

Restricted by privacy protection rules, the local data and operations of the end device 
are invisible to the system, which means that the end device may become malicious in 
order to obtain other people’s model parameters for free or hinder the global learning 
process. Malicious end devices may provide false local model parameters to the system, 
thereby destroying the construction of the global federated model. The hierarchical 
structure of the framework and the blockchain network built in different regions make 
the framework scalable, and can effectively handle a large number of equipment and 
training tasks distributed in multiple regions, while meeting privacy requirements. 
3.2 
Parameter Compensation Empowers Cache Aggregation 
The aggregation cache pool mechanism operates by caching the failed upload results 
that were unable to participate in the aggregation process immediately. These results are

[Vista original de la página 273](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-273.png)

![Página PDF 273, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-273.png)


<!-- PDF page 274 -->

## Página PDF 274

A Federated Learning Framework with Blockchain and Cache Pools
265
cached and later utilized when an opportunity arises to participate in the aggregation. In 
a federated learning framework employing asynchronous aggregation, the aggregation 
endpoint needs to frequently receive the locally trained result parameters uploaded by 
the training endpoint. The aggregation endpoint performs model aggregation operations 
and subsequently sends the aggregated updated model back to the training endpoint. This 
continuous communication process can lead to congestion issues. To address this prob-
lem, the aggregation buffer pool mechanism is introduced. It enables parallel processing 
of receiving underlying result parameters, model parameter aggregation, and delivery 
of model updates. Whenever the aggregation process is idle, the status of the cache pool 
is checked. If the cache pool is not empty, all parameters stored in the cache pool are 
directly aggregated, and the cache pool is cleared simultaneously. Once the aggregation 
is completed, the latest model parameters are updated. This mechanism helps allevi-
ate communication congestion and ensures efﬁcient aggregation and delivery of model 
updates in asynchronous federated learning. 
The aggregation cache pool mechanism is implemented on both the edge nodes and 
cloud servers. The purpose of the cache pool is to store the model parameters that have 
been published on the blockchain but have not yet undergone edge aggregation (on edge 
nodes) or global aggregation (on the cloud server). 
While the aggregation cache pool mechanism improves the efﬁciency of model 
parameter aggregation by reducing the global waiting time, it also introduces a chal-
lenge related to inconsistent model parameter versions. Aggregating parameters with 
different versions directly during the aggregation operation can negatively impact the 
global model. To address this challenge, BCA-FL (the proposed framework) incorpo-
rates a parameter compensation mechanism in the aggregation pool. This mechanism 
compensates for the various versions of parameters before performing the aggregation 
operation, thereby enhancing the quality of the aggregated model. The paper [31] sug-
gests that asynchronous updates in federated learning approximate the gradient using the 
zero-order term of the Taylor expansion of the parameters in their own rounds, neglecting 
higher-order terms. While using the full Taylor expansion for delayed gradient compen-
sation would be ideal, it is impractical in practice. To overcome this limitation, BCA-FL 
employs a Hessian matrix approximator to estimate the Hessian matrix of the original 
loss function. By approximating the Hessian matrix, the framework can perform gradient 
delay compensation, thereby improving the aggregation model’s quality. 
In our framework, the parameter compensation pipeline for edge aggregation is 
similar to that for global aggregation. In the edge aggregation stage, the version parameter 
in the compensation algorithm is the version upper T in the edge area, and in the global 
aggregation stage, the version parameter in the compensation algorithm is the version 
tauof the global model. In addition, the complete intra-domain blockchain stored at the 
edge node contains the historical version parameters required by the algorithm, while a 
separate space is required to store the historical version parameters in the cloud server. 
We believe that such storage overhead is completely affordable for cloud servers. In 
the following, the algorithm will be described by taking parameter compensation in the 
global aggregation stage as an example.

The aggregation process of the two subnetworks of BCA-FL is similar. In the edge-
end network, the role of the parameter server is provided by the edge nodes with relatively 
high arithmetic power, and the training end is the respective end device. In the cloud-edge 
network, the parameter server is provided by the cloud, and each edge block network is 
equivalent to the training-side, because for the cloud to send models to the edge block 
network and receive training result models from the edge block network, macroscopically 
the edge block network functions as equivalent to the end devices within the edge-end 
network.

3.3 
Regional Independent Edge Blockchain 
Swarm Learning [10] is a decentralized machine learning framework based on a 
blockchain network, which also belongs to the category of federated learning and is 
equivalent to an end-to-end federated learning framework. Its participants interact based 
on a private blockchain network with a speciﬁc participation protocol that guarantees 
each participant the freedom to opt in or out of training. However, the huge resource over-
head of the blockchain network used by Swarm Learning constrains its application in the 
edge environment, so a blockchain network that can meet the resource constraints of the 
edge environment is needed. Drawing on the idea of Swarm Learning using blockchain 
for federation learning, this framework builds a blockchain network in the edge com-
puting environment and architects a blockchain network-based interaction environment 
between edge nodes and end devices as a way to secure data. Meanwhile, in order to 
reduce the resource overhead, the edge blockchain with DAG-based consensus for parti-
tioning is used, and the edge is partitioned based on geographic zoning, and each partition 
forms an independent edge blockchain. Its ability to attach new transactions to the fork 
topology without maintaining the main chain allows any newly arrived transactions to 
be recorded in the blockchain immediately, reducing resource overhead and increasing 
system throughput. 
Fig. 4. DAG generation process 
The overall process of enabling asynchronous federated learning with independent 
edge blockchains in different regions is as follows: the cloud server ﬁrst distributes the 
global model to each edge node, and the edge node publishes it as its own transaction 
on the blockchain network in the region. Afterwards, the end device participants in 
the region download the global model from the blockchain network in the region and 
perform training based on local data to obtain a local model. Model validation is then 
performed on the end-end devices and published on the in-domain blockchain. The

269
aggregator of the edge node performs edge aggregation based on the buffer pool state. 
It should be noted that in our blockchain solution, the terminal device does not store 
a complete blockchain, because the storage of a complete blockchain requires a large 
amount of storage resources, and the terminal device is often unable to meet it. The 
complete blockchain network in the region will be stored in the edge nodes, making full 
use of edge storage resources. 
Fig. 5. (a) Local DAG of end device A; (b) Local DAG of end device B; (c) Local DAG of end 
device A after transaction veriﬁcation. 
(1). Lightweight blockchain Scheme. 
In our solution, the edge environment is divided into multiple independent areas 
according to geographical distribution, and the edge nodes and end devices in each 
area jointly build an intra-domain blockchain. According to the resource character-
istics of end devices and edge nodes, edge nodes maintain a complete blockchain 
in the domain, and end devices only maintain a local lightweight blockchain. 
The lightweight blockchain solution is based on literature [25]. In Teegraph, each 
node is responsible for packaging its own transactions. Nodes send transactions to 
the network by choosing random neighbors as destinations. Nodes will also receive 
transactions from neighboring nodes. Based on the received transactions, nodes can 
generate DAG locally. In our scheme, each end device in the area is equivalent to 
a node in Tee-graph, and the model parameters are equivalent to transactions. The 
transactions issued by each node jointly generate a global DAG, as shown in Fig. 4.

[Vista original de la página 278](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-278.png)

![Página PDF 278, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-278.png)


<!-- PDF page 279 -->

## Página PDF 279

270
J. Tian et al.
The end device only saves part of the most recently veriﬁed transactions locally, 
and the full version of the blockchain in the domain is saved at the edge node. 
When the edge node updates the DAG in the domain, the end device will perform 
lightweight processing on the local DAG according to the DAG version of the edge 
node. Speciﬁcally, when end device A completes a training task, the local DAG 
saves the full version of the latest transaction issued by A and the lightweight 
versions of the remaining transactions, as shown in Fig. 5(a). Then A veriﬁes the 
events received from neighboring node B, and then publishes its own transaction, 
end device A updates the local DAG, and the edge node updates the complete DAG 
in the domain. As shown in Fig. 5(c). 
(2). Conﬁrmation and Consensus. 
In a DAG-based blockchain, the transactions published by each node are model 
parameters obtained from local training. Before publishing a transaction, the node 
needs to select a received transaction published by its neighbors, and calculate the 
average value with the model parameters recently released locally to obtain the 
average model. The node needs to calculate the accuracy rate of the average model 
on the local test set and compare it with the accuracy rate obtained locally. Validation 
passes if the average model’s accuracy on the local test set is lower than the local 
model’s accuracy. Otherwise, the node needs to choose the next neighbor to verify. 
If multiple veriﬁcations fail, the result is discarded and the training is performed 
again. After the transaction is veriﬁed, the node needs to add the transaction to the 
DAG and broadcast the transaction to other nodes. Other nodes also validate the 
transaction and add it to their own DAG. In DAG, each node represents a transaction, 
and each node can point to multiple other nodes, representing the dependencies of 
the transaction. 
The average model accuracy standard is used for transaction veriﬁcation, which 
can ﬁlter poor quality or purely malicious model parameters, effectively guarantee 
the honesty and reliability of end devices, and reduce model tampering attacks and 
malicious client wrong training forged models negative impact on federated learning 
systems. The speciﬁc implementation is shown in Algorithm 2.

A Federated Learning Framework with Blockchain and Cache Pools

4 
Experiments 
4.1 
Experimental Setup 
In this paper, we evaluate and analyze BCA-FL by applying it to traditional models and 
datasets. The experiments are set up with 100 simulated end devices, 6 edge parameter 
servers, and 1 cloud parameter server. The global learning rate r is set to 0.01, and 
the compensation algorithm weight parameter r is set to a ﬁxed value of 0.04. There 
exists an adaptive adjustment algorithm for the value of the weight parameter r [31], and 
this experiment mainly veriﬁes the adaptability of BCA-FL to the collaborative cloud-
edge-end environment, so a relatively simpliﬁed ﬁxed r value is chosen. All end devices 
are randomly divided into attributions, which are governed by each edge parameter 
server. Each edge parameter server and its subordinate end devices form an edge block 
network, and each edge block network is independent of each other to simulate the edge 
distribution in real situations. All end devices drop out with a certain chance of dr, so as 
to simulate the unreliability of end devices in the real situation. The experiments in this 
paper set different dropout rates for comparison, so as to verify the tolerance capability 
of BCA-FL for the highly free unreliability of end devices in the edge environment.

All experiments treat the datasets with Non-IID partitioning, and the sizes of the 
datasets owned by all end devices conform to normal distribution, so as to simulate the 
local data heterogeneity of the end devices in real situations. Under the same experimental 
base setup, FedAvg (adapting to cloud-edge-end environment), ALL-Data (one place in 
the data set) and Edge-Data (each edge block network trained independently) are selected 
as the baseline for comparison experiments to verify the adaptability of BCAFL in the 
cloud-edge-end collaboration environment. 
4.2 
Models and Datasets 
In this paper, we select some classical datasets in some deep learning applications to 
validate the performance of BCA-FL. Benchmark datasets in image classiﬁcation: Mnist, 
FashionMnist, EMnist and Cifar10. 
The MNIST dataset is a handwritten numeric image dataset with 60,000 images and 
labels in the training set and 10,000 images and labels in the test set, initiated by the 
National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST). FashionMNIST is an image 
dataset that replaces MNIST, covering 70,000 frontal images of different products from 
10 categories, with the same size format and training set test set division as MNIST. The 
CIFAR-10 dataset contains 60,000 32x32 color images, divided into 10 categories with 
6000 images in each category. 
In order to make the simulation experiments closer to the actual application environ-
ment, the data set is subjected to the Non-IID partitioning process in this paper, so that 
there are differences in the number and categories among the partitioned data sets, and 
the size of the data set held by each end device conforms to the normal distribution. 
The following models are trained on these datasets: Multilayer Perceptron (MLP), 
MnistNet and CNNCifar. 
4.3 
Experimental Results
(1) Tolerance of unreliability of end devices. 
In this paper, we ﬁrst train the same model using BCA-FL and FedAvg on the 
same dataset with different dropout probability dr, so as to compare and measure the 
tolerance of this paper’s method for unreliability (free join exit) of end devices in edge 
environment. Based on CNN convolutional neural network for MNIST handwritten 
dataset recognition, the experiments were conducted using FedAvg and BCA-FL 
with global epochs set to 20 and local epochs set to 5. The dropout probability d r
were 0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, and 0.9, respectively, and the data were all No-IID distributed. 
The results are shown in Fig. 6(a) and Fig. 6(b). 
From the experimental results, we can see that traditional FedAvg is more sen-
sitive to the reliability of end devices in the edge environment, and when a large 
proportion of end devices drop out, the construction of the overall model will be 
greatly affected. When the dropout rate is greater than 0.5, the accuracy of the global 
model decreases signiﬁcantly, and decreases sharply with the increase of the dropout 
rate, and when the dropout rate reaches 0.9, the accuracy of the global model drops 
to about 50%, while the accuracy of the global model of BCA-FL ﬁnally remains

In addition, this paper compares the results of training in a data set and training 
each edge network individually. The global model accuracy results are shown in 
Table 2. 
For edge-independent training, it is assumed that all end devices are normal, so 
the accuracy of FedAvg is lower than edge-independent training when the chance 
of end device dropout is 0.9, which is due to the fact that too frequent end device 
dropout has a large negative impact on the aggregation effect of FedAvg global 
model. However, when the chances of end-device dropouts are less extreme, the 
global models of both FedAvg and BCAFL outperform edge-independent training. It 
is easy to see that BCAFL has a more obvious advantage for end-device unreliability

tolerance, and the global model quality is closer to the global model trained in the

(2) Compensation of delayed version parameters. 
Multiple sets of experiments are conducted on end devices with the same dropout 
chance, and the utility of the cache aggregation pool and parameter compensation 
mechanism in the BCA-FL framework is veriﬁed by comparing the model accuracy 
of BCA-FL and FedAvg by training different models on different datasets. The 
dropout chance is set to 0.7, and the maximum edge delay version is restricted to 5, 
i.e., the global model must be synchronized after a maximum of 5 edge updates. The 
comparison between FedAvg and BCA-FL is validated in the following experiments, 
respectively: training the CNNCifar model on the CIFAR10 dataset (Fig. 7a); training 
the MLP model on the Mnist dataset (Fig. 7b); and training the MnistNet model on 
the EMnist (Fig. 7c) and FashionMnist (Fig. 7d) datasets. The experimental results 
are measured in terms of accuracy for comparison, and higher accuracy indicates 
less inﬂuence by the delayed version parameters. 
The experimental results show that BCA-FL is much less negatively affected 
by delayed versions than FedAvg. The combination of the aggregation cache pool-
ing mechanism and parameter compensation better mitigates the delayed version 
problem, making BCA-FL asynchronous and efﬁcient while not being affected by 
excessive delayed version parameters in the global model aggregation. 
(3) Efﬁciency improvement. 
To highlight the efﬁciency improvement of BCA-FL, the experiment was con-
ducted with a ﬁxed client dropout rate, identical client grouping, and data distribu-
tion. The simulation accounted for the performance heterogeneity of each client in 
a real environment to measure the communication overhead between clients and the 
server. The total communication overhead (T) was set to 5000 virtual time units, 
with the cloud-edge communication overhead being three times that of the edge-end 
communication overhead. Using the Mnist and Cifar10 datasets, CNN models were 
trained using BCA-FL, HierFAVG, FedAvg, and FedCS federated learning frame-
works. The experimental results, presented in Fig. 8 and summarized in Table 3, 
demonstrate the performance of these frameworks under the same communication 
overhead. 
According to Table 3, BCA-FL achieves better global model accuracy than the other 
frameworks, even with the same communication overhead. Additionally, BCA-FL can 
undergo more rounds of training and model aggregation, enhancing the overall model 
learning efﬁciency.
(4) Malicious Client Filtering in Real Environment. 
To demonstrate the robustness of BCA-FL against malicious clients in real-world 
scenarios, we deployed BCA-FL on a small-scale cloud-edge-end collaborative net-
work platform. The platform consisted of one host server node, ﬁve edge server 
nodes, and 20 real terminals under each edge server. In this setup, the host server 
performed the tasks of the cloud parameter server, while the edge servers also pos-
sessed computational and storage resources. Direct communication was established 
between the host server and the edge servers, as well as between the edge servers 
and the end devices. By deploying BCA-FL in this network conﬁguration, we aimed

assess how well BCA-FL could handle malicious clients compared to Fed-Avg. From the

of adversaries.
5 
Conclusion 
In this paper, we propose a hierarchical asynchronous federated learning framework 
BCA-FL based on the characteristics of cloud-edge-end collaboration scenario, which 
enables each end device to participate in the construction of global models without shar-
ing local data. In the era of big data, it is essential to leverage data across silos in a 
collaborative cloud-edge environment. BCA-FL is closer to the centralized data training 
results in terms of accuracy, and consumes less resources than traditional FedAvg, and is 
more secure due to the intro-duction of the DAG consensus-based edge blockchain net-
work. BCA-FL uses the aggregation cache pool mechanism to parallelize the receiving 
model parameters, aggregation operation, and downlinking to update the model, which 
improves the efﬁciency of the aggregation side and alleviates the communication conges-
tion problem caused by asynchronous nature. At the same time, it introduces parameter 
compensation mechanism to alleviate the aggregation model version difference prob-
lem brought by the parallelism of edge-end network and cloud-edge network, and has 
a certain tolerance effect on the unreliability of end devices, which is more suitable for 
the new era application scenario of cloud-edge end collaboration. 
There are also some shortcomings in this paper. The global learning rate is ﬁxed 
during the training process, which may cause the problem of low learning efﬁciency 
in some end devices. The follow-up work should be adjusted according to the local 
conditions of each end device in order to improve the local learning efﬁciency.

## Página PDF 289

Model Similarity Based Clustering 
Federated Learning in Edge Computing 
Xiaoyan Liu1, Jiwei Huang1(B), and Ying Chen2 
1 Beijing Key Laboratory of Petroleum Data Mining, China University of 
Petroleum-Beijing, Beijing 102249, China 
huangjw@cup.edu.cn 
2 Beijing Information Science and Technology University, Beijing 100101, China 
chenying@bistu.edu.cn 
Abstract. Federated learning (FL) has emerged as a novel framework 
of distributed machine learning in edge computing for ensuring data pri-
vacy meanwhile reducing communication traﬃc. Recently, an enhanced 
FL technology namely Clustered Federated Learning (CFL) has been 
developed to address the challenges posed by non-independent and iden-
tically distributed (non-IID) data across clients in FL. Nevertheless, 
existing CFL strategies face signiﬁcant challenges in model parameter 
aggregation, because the aggregation weights are calculated according 
to the size of the dataset at each client instead of its contribution to 
the global model. In this paper, we propose a Model Similarity based 
Clustering Federated Learning (MS-CFL) framework in edge comput-
ing architecture. Speciﬁcally, we design an aggregation strategy based 
on client clustering, and we propose a model-similarity client cluster-
ing algorithm tailored to select optimal base stations for clients located 
in areas with overlapping resources. To precisely evaluate each cluster’s 
impact on the global model, we apply the Deep Reinforcement Learn-
ing (DRL) techniques to ensure a balanced consideration of dataset size 
and testing accuracy on global model during the aggregation process. 
Finally, we evaluate our approach based on open-source datasets, and 
the experimental results show the superiority of MS-CFL over several 
state-of-the-art methodologies in terms of communication and computa-
tion eﬃciency. 
Keywords: Clustered Federated Learning · Non-IID · cluster 
contribution · Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) 
1
Introduction 
Edge Computing (EC) [ 1] relocates cloud services to the network edge, signif-
icantly reducing latency and facilitating real-time processing through content 
caching and computation oﬄoading. As edge computing evolves, deploying arti-
ﬁcial intelligence (AI) at the edge has emerged as a burgeoning ﬁeld of research.

## Página PDF 290

Model Similarity Based Clustering Federated Learning in Edge Computing
281
However, the limited computing power and data available on edge devices pre-
vent fully leveraging the potential of edge computing systems. 
Federated Learning (FL) signiﬁcantly enhances AI deployment at the net-
work edge by enabling the creation of high-quality models without centralizing 
raw data on cloud servers. In the context of edge computing, FL is particu-
larly valuable because it facilitates localized data processing, which reduces the 
need for data transfer over the network and minimizes latency. For example, in 
smart city applications like traﬃc management systems, FL can process data in 
real-time at the network edge, enabling timely decision-making. Moreover, FL 
is designed to improve privacy by operating on various edge devices (clients) 
[ 2], making it especially suitable for protecting user privacy and data security in 
healthcare scenarios. Unlike traditional distributed learning, which requires data 
transmission to centralized servers, FL allows edge devices to process machine 
learning tasks locally [ 3, 4]. This approach not only enhances privacy by conduct-
ing analytics directly on the devices but also decreases dependence on central 
data storage and processing. 
Although federated learning brings privacy advantages, various challenges 
still exist, especially data heterogeneity and communication costs. In this work, 
we address the challenges posed by these issues. Data heterogeneity, delineated 
as scenarios wherein speciﬁc clients accumulate a signiﬁcant volume of samples 
for a particular category, while samples for other categories are relatively scarce-
commonly referred to as non-independent and identically distributed (non-IID) 
in federated learning—gives rise to a scenario where diverse local models opti-
mize for distinct local objectives, resulting in varied optimization directions. 
Zhao et al. [ 5] illustrated that in situations involving non-IID datasets, the per-
formance of federated learning may reduce by as much as 55%, especially in cases 
of extremely skewed datasets. Consequently, amalgamating these divergent local 
models to derive a robust global model becomes a formidable undertaking, and 
this challenge has also been extensively and thoroughly documented in the exist-
ing body of research [ 6]. 
Current methodologies typically involve client grouping, often utilizing 
dataset size as the primary criterion for determining aggregation weights [ 7]. 
However, a notable limitation of these strategies lies in their tendency to overlook 
the distinctive contributions of each category to the global model. This omis-
sion becomes particularly crucial during the optimization of the global model, as 
categories with more substantial global inﬂuence may not receive commensurate 
attention. Within this framework, the clustering process predominantly empha-
sizes diﬀerences in client data distribution or weights, neglecting to consider the 
geographical coverage of base stations. This oversight concerning such a critical 
aspect may hinder the practical applicability of the study ﬁndings. 
In order to overcome the diﬃculty of heterogeneous data across statistical 
distributions, this study considers resource overlapping areas and resource non-
overlapping areas, as shown in Fig 1. It proposes a client clustering method based 
on weight similarity. Diverging from traditional approaches that rely on dataset 
size as aggregation weights, we consider both dataset size and contributions to

[Vista original de la página 290](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-290.png)

![Página PDF 290, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-290.png)

## Página PDF 291

282
X. Liu et al.
Fig. 1. Clustered Federated Learning architecture. 
the global model when constructing the global model. Our main contributions 
are as follows: 
(1) We introduce the concept of parameter similarity, which enables an edge 
server to discern groups of clients with akin data distributions without 
accessing their private data. This method facilitates collaborative enhance-
ment of training among clients in the same group, while preventing interfer-
ence from those with divergent data distributions. 
(2) We design an aggregation method, wherein the aggregation weights are deter-
mined by the dataset size and the testing accuracy of each cluster on the 
global model. To dynamically adjust the weights of the dataset size and the 
cluster’s contribution, we employ a method that utilizes Deep Reinforce-
ment Learning (DRL). This approach allows the system to adaptively opti-
mize weight allocations in response to changing conditions and performance 
metrics, thereby enhancing the overall eﬃcacy of MS-CFL. 
(3) We present a novel hierarchical aggregation approach that integrates an edge 
layer. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MS-CFL eﬀectively dimin-
ishes communication overhead. 
The remainder of this paper is structured as follows: Sect. 2 provides a review 
of the literature that is most relevant to our investigation. Section 3 provides the 
preliminary concepts and groundwork necessary for understanding our approach. 
In Sect. 4, we detail the algorithm developed in this study, including its princi-
ples, framework, and operational ﬂow. Section 5 presents extensive experimental 
evaluations of the proposed algorithm and oﬀers an objective analysis of its 
performance. Finally, Sect. 6 concludes the paper with a summary.

Model Similarity Based Clustering Federated Learning in Edge Computing
283
2
Related Work 
Federated learning (FL) has risen as a notable area of inquiry, marked by its 
method of jointly training machine learning models across distributed devices. 
This technique provides viable solutions to various challenges [ 8]. However, the 
eﬃcacy of FL is intricately linked to the underlying nature of data distribution 
among these decentralized devices. The existence of Non-IID data distribution, 
manifesting as substantial variations in local datasets across devices, introduces 
a noteworthy challenge to the eﬀectiveness of FL systems. Furthermore, studies 
have shown that non-IID data settings can signiﬁcantly impair the performance 
of machine learning services [ 9– 11]. 
In non-IID scenarios, several studies have attempted to address the feature-
shift problem by employing synthetic data augmentation techniques [ 12,13]. 
However, the practice of sharing additional synthetic data among clients raises 
concerns regarding data privacy leakage. Meanwhile, other researchers have 
explored localized client ﬁne-tuning as an alternative approach. Inspired by 
the FedAvg methodology, several studies in federated learning aim to develop 
modiﬁed iterations of the FedAvg algorithm speciﬁcally tailored for non-IID 
data, including FedProx, Per-FedAvg, Fed-Dyn, and FedDC. FedProx [ 14] builds 
on FedAvg by introducing a regularization term that constrains the diﬀerence 
between local models and the global model to mitigate the eﬀects of non-IID 
data. Per-FedAvg [ 15] personalizes the global model by allowing each client to 
adjust it based on their local data, resulting in a model better suited to each 
client’s data distribution. Fed-Dyn [ 16] adjusts the weights of each client’s model 
dynamically to adapt to diﬀerent data distributions, improving performance on 
non-IID data. FedDC [ 17] introduces a decentralized training mechanism using 
multi-center clustering to reduce the impact of non-IID data on global model 
training. Nevertheless, a singular global model tends to be impersonal and can 
yield suboptimal results on test datasets for individual clients. Consequently, 
training a sole global model with signiﬁcantly skewed non-IID data for all clients 
might not be the optimal approach in numerous practical scenarios. 
The Cluster FL model posits that clients can be segmented into various 
clusters, where each client is classiﬁed based on their gradient or loss met-
rics [ 18]. Ghosh et al. introduced a cluster-oriented framework [ 19], where user 
groups characterized by distinct learning goals are restricted to aggregating only 
with nodes within the same cluster. FedEM [ 20] enhanced the methodology of 
client grouping in cluster-based systems by introducing a soft client clustering 
algorithm. Felix et al. [ 21] introduce a novel algorithm designed to tackle non-
iid scenarios by clustering clients based on jointly trainable data distributions. 
Chamath et al. introduce a strategy for model aggregation in federated learning 
guided by pairwise client similarity, where client gradients are employed to deter-
mine similarity [ 22]. Morafah et al. [ 23] proposed a federated learning approach 
that clusters based on inference similarity to address non-IID data distributions. 
However, all these approaches merely allocate aggregation weights based on the 
size of the local dataset, which proves inadequate in discerning the individual 
contributions of each client.

[Vista original de la página 292](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-292.png)

![Página PDF 292, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-292.png)

## Página PDF 293

284
X. Liu et al.
Several weight-adjustment methods have been explored in the literature. 
Pang et al. [ 24] developed a reinforcement learning (RL)-based methodology, 
wherein the server dynamically modiﬁes the weights of clients during the aggre-
gation process. Addressing non-iid challenges, Liu et al. [ 25] introduced Fed-
DWA (Federated Learning with Dynamic Weight Adjustment), which employs 
a parameter server (PS) to calculate customized aggregation weights derived 
from models gathered from clients. Ye et al. [ 26] introduced Federated Learning 
with Discrepancy-aware Collaboration (FedDisco), which determines aggrega-
tion weights based on both dataset size and discrepancy value. While these 
techniques provide valuable insights, they have certain limitations. Speciﬁcally, 
communication between clients and cloud servers incurs higher communication 
overhead compared to cluster-based federated learning. 
3
Preliminaries 
FedAvg: Each local model \hat{y}(W)ˆy(W) is trained by Federated Learning (FL), wherein 
the matrix WW contains model parameters, including weights w_NwN and biases b_NbN
of Neural Networks. Within FL, a global loss function L(W)L(W) is minimized in 
order to optimize the parameters WW: 
\bALT{} \min _w L(W)=\min _w \sum_{k=1}^K \frac{E_k}{E} \times L_k(w)\eALT{} min

function used by client kk. Commonly, clients initiate their tasks at t=0t = 0. At each 
subsequent iterationt > 0t > 0, we selectnn clients from a total ofNN to engage in every 
round of federation. The client then updates the local model by downloading

\bALT{} W_{t+1}=W_t-\mu_s \times \nabla L\left(W_t\right)\eALT{} Wt+1 = Wt −μs × ∇L (Wt)

where \nabla∇signiﬁes the gradient derived via backpropagation, and \mu_sμs represents 
the learning rate utilized in stochastic gradient descent (SGD). The training 
process continues until each model either converges or reaches the desired train-
ing accuracy, considering the phases of local model optimization and subsequent 
aggregation as described earlier. 
Cluster FL: Clustered Federated Learning (CFL), introduced in the work of 
Sattler et al. [ 21], oﬀers a novel framework within the broader scope of Federated 
Learning (FL) aimed at addressing the signiﬁcant challenge of non-independent 
and identically distributed (non-IID) data across client devices. Non-IID data 
can severely hamper the performance of a globally trained model because it 
leads to skewed local updates, which may not generalize well across the entire 
network. CFL addresses this issue by organizing clients into clusters based on the 
similarity of their local data distributions, thereby fostering a more homogeneous 
training environment within each cluster.

aims to partition \mathcal{D}D into KK clusters {C_1, C_2, \ldots, C_K}C1, C2, . . . , CK such that the data within 
each cluster is more IID relative to the complete dataset. The objective function

Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL): DRL extends the foundational struc-
ture of Reinforcement Learning (RL), which consists of ﬁve essential components: 
the agent, the environment, states, actions, and rewards. At each time step tt, 
the agent observes its current state s_tst from the environment, selects an action a_{t}at
based on its policy, and executes this action to inﬂuence the environment’s state. 
In response, the environment provides feedback in the form of reward signals r_trt. 
Subsequently, the environment issues feedback through reward signals r_trt. These 
rewards serve as indicators of the action’s immediate eﬀectiveness, guiding the 
agent in learning processes and decision-making strategies. Through this cycle 
of actions and feedback, RL agents continually reﬁne their policies and optimize 
their behaviors over time. The signiﬁcant advancements in RL, as highlighted 
by studies [ 27,28], are attributed to this continuous learning mechanism from 
dynamic interactions. The goal of RL is to maximize the anticipated total dis-
counted return, expressed as R=\sum_{t=1}^T \gamma^{t-1} r_tR =
T
t=1 γt−1rt. In this formula, \gammaγ, which ranges 
between 0 and 1 (inclusive), serves as the discount factor, assigning weight to 
future rewards. 
The emergence of DRL has been propelled by developments in deep learn-
ing, which enables the approximation of value functions or policies through 
Deep Neural Networks (DNNs). This integration allows RL to tackle previously 
intractable high-dimensional state spaces and complex decision-making scenar-
ios. A landmark in DRL, the Deep Q-Network (DQN), integrates the Q-learning 
algorithm and deep neural networks. It updates network weights by minimiz-
ing the discrepancy between the predicted Q-values and the target Q-values. 
Speciﬁcally, the DQN’s objective function is formulated as: 
\bALT{} L(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{\left(s, a, r, s^{\prime}\right) \sim U(D)}\left[\left(r+\gamma \max _{a^{\prime}} Q\left(s^{\prime}, a^{\prime} ; \theta^{-}\right)- Q(s, a ; \theta)\right)^2\right]\eALT{} L(θ) = E(s,a,r,s )∼U(D)

of the target network, U(D)U(D) refers to the process of randomly sampling from the 
experience replay buﬀer. The current state, the action taken, the reward received, 
and the next state are represented, respectively, by the variables s, a, r, and s^{\prime}s, a, r, ands .

4
Method
 
In this section, we present a thorough description of our algorithm, named Model 
Similarity based on Clustering Federated Learning (MS-CFL). By categorizing 
clients into clusters according to data distribution similarity, MS-CFL facilitates 
a more organized and eﬃcient federated learning process. By leveraging DRL, 
the algorithm dynamically adjusts cluster sizes and their contributions to overall 
test accuracy. MS-CFL encompasses two primary components: the formation of 
client clusters and the server’s aggregation of parameters. 
4.1
Clustering Clients by Model Similarity 
In this section, we describe details on the clustering based on distribution sim-
ilarity. Algorithm 1 presents the pseudo-code. Each client also collects D_iDi and 
D_i(t)Di(t), representing the distribution of all its data and the data utilized in round 
tt, respectively. It is important to note that D_i(t)Di(t) might diﬀer from D_iDi since not 
all of the data is used in this particular round. When client ii is selected at the 
ﬁrst time, it must undergo one epoch of training before uploading the model 
parameters to the base station. For all subsequent selections, the client uploads 
the model parameters after completing speciﬁed rounds of training.

associated with it. For the second type, considering the prevalence of users within

is necessary to select a suitable base station for group learning. Hence, grouping

from the clients, the base station proceeds to compute the Cosine Similarity. 
The expression for calculating the similarity between clients can be optimized

The equation establishes the similarity measure \alpha_{ij}αij between the model 
parameters of clients ii and jj by calculating the cosine similarity. This is achieved 
through the dot product of their parameter vectors, normalized by the product 
of their respective magnitudes. This measure ranges from -1 (exactly opposite), 
through 0 (orthogonal), to 1 (exactly the same), indicating the degree of simi-
larity in the parameter space of the two clients’ models. 
4.2
Clustered Federated Learning Framework 
Our proposed federated learning framework includes three layers, edge server, 
base stations, and clients (refers to the edge computing devices), as shown in 
Algorithm 2. The particular sequence of operations executed by each terminal is 
detailed as follows: 
Edge Server Processing. In the MS-CFL framework, the learning process is 
initiated by the edge server, which establishes the initial global model param-
eters, designated as \theta_g^0θ0
g. Moreover, the additional responsibilities of the edge 
server also include grouping clients. For clients in non-overlapping areas, they 
are directly assigned to the nearest group to streamline the process. In contrast, 
clients within the overlapping area are grouped based on their data similarity 
to optimize the collaborative learning process, as detailed in Algorithm 1. Sub-
sequently, during each learning iteration tt, the edge server randomly selects a 
subset of clusters S_t \in [C]St ∈[C] (where CC represents the total number of clusters), and 
broadcasts the latest model parameters \theta_g^tθt
g to the all base stations (lines 2–6). 
Among them, the aggregation formula is as follows:

To optimally balance the contributions of local model performance and data 
volume, we leverage the capabilities of the Double Deep Q-Network (DDQN) 
algorithm for dynamic adjustment of the hyper parameter \alphaα. 
In our federated learning framework, the state (s)(s) represents the aggregated 
model’s performance metrics, speciﬁcally including testing accuracy and testing 
loss. The action (a)(a) taken by the DDQN agent corresponds to adjusting the 
hyper parameter \alphaα, which balances the weight between local model performance 
and the volume of data in the aggregation process. The action space is deﬁned 
by three actions: increasing \alphaα, decreasing \alphaα, leaving \alphaα unchanged and limit 
\alphaα to the range [0,1]. Our objective is to identify a model that best ﬁts the 
global dataset, which involves maximizing the testing accuracy of the global 
model while minimizing its loss. Hence, we design the state ss to include testing 
accuracy and testing loss. Accordingly, we have designed the reward function (r)(r)
as follows: 
\bALT{} r=\beta*\left(\operatorname{acc}_{\text {current }}-\operatorname{acc}_{\text {last }}\right)+(1-\beta)*\left(\operatorname{loss}_{\text {last }}-\operatorname{loss}_{\text {current }}\right)\eALT{} r = β ∗(acccurrent −acclast ) + (1 −β) ∗(losslast −losscurrent )
(9) 
The Q-value (Q(s,a)Q(s, a)) represents the expected cumulative reward of taking 
a discrete action aa in state ss, under a policy \PiΠ. The objective of DDQN is to 
ﬁnd the optimal policy that maximizes the expected reward. The update of the 
Q-value follows the Temporal Diﬀerence (TD) learning approach: 
\bALT{} Q(s, a) \leftarrow Q(s,a)+\alpha_{\mathrm{lr}}\left[r+ \gamma Q\left(s^{\prime}, \arg \max _{a^{\prime}} Q(s^{\prime}, a^{\prime}; \theta); \theta^-\right) -Q(s, a)\right]\eALT{} Q(s, a)

Base Station Processing. In the processing of the base station, for each 
learning round tt, the base station acts as an intermediary node. It is responsi-
ble for aggregating the client models within its group and for transmitting the 
aggregated parameters from the clients to the upstream (lines 9–14). Follow-
ing this, the base station distributes the models updated \theta_g^tθt
g by the edge server

non-overlapping areas, have the latest model iteration for subsequent rounds of 
learning.

the initial setting of model parameters, clients conduct a comparative evaluation 
on their local dataset using three sets of model: the newly received global model, 
the model from within their cluster, and their own model parameters from the 
previous round tt. The model demonstrating the highest performance on the local 
dataset is selected as the starting point for further updates. Following this, the 
clients execute TT iterations of stochastic gradient descent (SGD) to update their 
local models. The model, w_{t+1}^{k}wk
t+1, is updated by:

5
Experiments 
5.1
Experimental Setting 
Our experiments utilized an Ubuntu 16.04 platform with Tesla P100 GPUs and 
32 GB of RAM for hardware support. We implemented our model and methods 
using the PyTorch framework. In federated learning simulation, we deployed 20 
clients, selecting a random subset for each learning iteration across 100 rounds, 
each containing 10 local iterations. The training process utilized the SGD opti-
mizer, set with a learning rate of 0.01. 
Datasets and Models. We explore four heterogeneous data distribution sce-
narios, designated as NIID-1, NIID-2, NIID-3, and NIID-4. For NIID-1 and 
NIID-2, we follows Dirichlet distribution Dir (\betaβ) [ 29], where \betaβ is an argument 
correlated with heterogeneity level. Set \betaβ to 0.1 and 0.5 respectively, indicating

a diﬀerent degree of heterogeneity. For the NIID-3 and NIID-4 scenarios, we ini-
tially distributed 20% and 30% of the total classes to each client through random 
allocation. Subsequently, each class was divided randomly and equally among the 
clients possessing that speciﬁc class. For the CIFAR-10 [ 30] and SVHN datasets 
[ 31], we employed the Simple-CNN architecture, whereas for the CIFAR-100 
dataset [ 30], we applied ResNet. 
Baselines. We compare MS-CFL with four representative baselines. FedAvg 
[ 2], recognized as the foundational FL approach, serves as the initial benchmark. 
Additionally, we evaluate against FedProx [ 14], a method enhancing robustness 
through proximal terms; Per-FedAvg [ 15] employs a meta-learning approach to 
develop a uniﬁed model capable of adapting to individual local datasets through 
a limited number of SGD iterations. Clustered-FL [ 21], which cluster by client 
similarity; and FLIS [ 23], which groups clients based on the inference similarity 
across client models.

5.2
Performance Comparison 
Tables 1 and 2 display the global test set accuracy and the average test accuracy 
across all participating clients for the FedAvg, FedProx, CFL, and MS-CFL 
algorithms under the NIID-1 and NIID-2 settings. 
Focusing on global test set accuracy as delineated in Table 1, a comprehensive 
analysis of various FL strategies reveals that the MS-CFL notably outperforms 
others. It achieves the highest accuracy rates across the board, surpassing the 
FedAvg benchmark by an impressive margin of up to 10% on average. Compared 
to the FLIS algorithm, which previously led in baseline tests, MS-CFL achieves 
a signiﬁcant improvement in accuracy of around 7% on average. 
Turning to average test accuracy as depicted in Table 2, the superiority of MS-
CFL remains consistent. It continues to deliver the highest accuracy rates across 
all evaluated tasks, exceeding the FedAvg model by up to 30%. Furthermore, 
when pitted against the FLIS algorithm, which had the best performance in 
initial experiments, MS-CFL boasts an accuracy enhancement of approximately 
12% on cifar-10 in NIID-1.

Our proposed method converges within 20–30 communication rounds on 
datasets, indicating that the algorithm requires less communication overhead 
to achieve model convergence. Among all benchmarks, CFL exhibits the poor-
est performance across all datasets and requires the highest communication 
overhead. Per-FedAvg beneﬁts from higher communication rounds, showing 
improved performance as the number of rounds increases. FLIS demonstrates 
the best convergence eﬀects among the compared solutions. Moreover, MS-CFL 
consistently outperforms all other compared solutions across various communi-
cation rounds, showcasing its robustness and eﬃciency. 
6
Conclusions 
In this article, we explored the Federated Learning (FL) challenge among clients 
holding Non-IID data. We introduced a novel approach based on model similar-
ity, enabling the server to form clusters of clients with similar data distributions 
without accessing their sensitive data. This technique leverages the similarity 
in models updated locally by clients, evaluated by the server in each training 
round. 
Meanwhile, we consider that conventional dataset-size-based aggregation 
manner is not optimal in non-IID scenarios. To tackle this issue, we proposed 
a aggregation method which determined not only by dataset size but also by 
each cluster’s contribution to the global situation. The size of the data set and 
the proportion of the cluster’s global contribution are determined by the DDQN 
algorithm. Our experimental results demonstrate that this method markedly 
surpasses baseline performances, notably decreasing the required communica-
tion rounds. 
In future work, we plan to focus on the security issues of federated learn-
ing while expanding the scope of our experiments by integrating larger and 
more diverse datasets, as well as a range of non-IID data distributions. This 
expansion is expected to yield more comprehensive insights into the scalability 
and robustness of the proposed aggregation method. To this end, we will con-
duct evaluations using real-world datasets from diverse domains to assess the 
method’s performance consistency and generalizability.

of Key Laboratory of Agricultural Blockchain Application, Ministry of Agriculture and

## Página PDF 306

A Privacy-Preserving Edge Caching 
Algorithm Based on Permissioned 
Blockchain and Federated Reinforcement 
Learning 
Zicheng Luo1, Zhenchun Wei1,2,3, Zengwei Lyu1,2,3(B), Xiaohui Yuan4, 
Lin Feng1, and Zhen Wei1,3 
1 School of Computer Science and Information Engineering, Hefei University of 
Technology, Hefei 230009, China 
lzw@hfut.edu.cn 
2 Anhui Province Key Laboratory of Industry Safety and Emergency Technology, 
Hefei 230009, China 
3 Engineering Research Center of Safety Critical Industrial Measurement and 
Control Technology, Ministry of Education, Hefei 230009, China 
4 Department of Computer Science and Engineering, University of North Texas, 
Denton, TX 76203, USA 
Abstract. With a massive inﬂux of terminal devices connecting to wire-
less network, the rapid growth in wireless network traﬃc poses sig-
niﬁcant challenges to existing network architectures. Device-to-Device 
(D2D) communication facilitates direct data exchanges between devices, 
reducing latency and network congestion. However, implementing D2D 
communication technology in edge caching poses privacy and security 
concerns. This paper proposes a privacy-preserving edge caching algo-
rithm based on permissioned blockchain and federated reinforcement 
learning (PBFRL) to ensure data privacy and security. The PBFRL algo-
rithm incorporates a permissioned blockchain deployed on both base sta-
tions and user devices to ensure data privacy and security. Considering 
the varied hardware resources of user devices, an asynchronous training 
method is introduced, separating the sample collection algorithm and 
the updating network algorithm to run on diﬀerent devices. Simulation 
results demonstrate that the proposed algorithm surpasses other bench-
mark algorithms in terms of content transmission delay and data privacy 
and security. 
Keywords: Edge caching · Federated learning · Deep reinforcement 
learning · Permissioned Blockchain 
1
Introduction 
With the emergence of advanced wireless applications such as localization, 
autonomous driving, virtual reality, and augmented reality, a massive amount

## Página PDF 307

298
Z. Luo et al.
of terminal devices accessing wireless network generate a tremendous volume of 
data requests [ 1]. According to Ericsson’s forecast, it is projected that by the 
end of 2024, the total global mobile network traﬃc will reach approximately 
211EB per month, rising to 563EB per month by the end of 2029 [ 2]. The sub-
stantial growth in mobile traﬃc exacerbates the burden on the backhaul links, 
posing signiﬁcant challenges to the architecture and system of existing commu-
nication networks [ 3]. As one of the core technologies of mobile edge computing, 
edge caching maximizes the cache capacity available in edge networks, optimiz-
ing data caching eﬃciency for edge nodes and user devices, thereby eﬀectively 
reducing response delay [ 4]. 
Research on edge caching algorithms has undergone considerable exploration. 
The authors in [ 5] introduce the concept of regional user preferences and pro-
poses a cache policy based on user preferences to maximize cache hit ratio. The 
authors in [ 6] propose a hybrid caching management scheme for edge caching 
scenarios. This scheme fully considers that the popularity distribution of cached 
content often follows Zipf’s law and speciﬁcally designs a matching cache replace-
ment algorithm, achieving higher energy utilization eﬃciency. However, due to 
the increase in data volume and the enhancement of dynamic environments, 
rule-based edge caching strategies struggle to adapt to complex caching environ-
ments. With the rapid development of reinforcement learning, leveraging learning 
algorithms to address complex edge caching problems has become a trend. 
In recent years, researchers have extended and applied reinforcement learning 
techniques to the ﬁeld of edge caching. The authors in [ 7] discuss the applica-
tion of deep reinforcement learning in cache optimization for vehicular networks, 
emphasizing the challenges posed by high mobility constraints and heterogeneous 
content demands, and proposes a deep reinforcement learning-based inter-vehicle 
caching framework to optimize cache resource allocation strategies. The authors 
in [ 8] propose a heuristic caching strategy to address the mobile edge caching 
problem in single timeslot scenarios. This strategy takes into account user mobil-
ity and ﬁle relevance and further proposes a multi-timeslot scenario caching 
strategy based on multi-agent reinforcement learning. By using the heuristic 
caching strategy in single timeslot scenarios and using the prediction results as 
observation results, the training of the MARL cold start phase is optimized. 
The authors in [ 9] consider dynamic, complex, and unobservable content pop-
ularity and formulate the maximization problem of cache hit ratio on devices 
as a distributed problem under privacy constraints. A distributed reinforcement 
learning-based P2D3PG algorithm is proposed to solve the distributed problem. 
However, traditional reinforcement learning algorithms aggregate all user data 
for centralized training, raising concerns about privacy and misuse of personal 
data. 
The federated learning framework is a privacy-preserving framework, and 
training edge caching decision models based on reinforcement learning within the 
federated learning architecture has gradually become a hot research direction. 
The authors in [ 10] introduce a recommendation system into the federated rein-
forcement learning edge caching strategy, not only pursuing direct content hits

[Vista original de la página 307](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-307.png)

![Página PDF 307, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-307.png)


<!-- PDF page 308 -->

## Página PDF 308

PBFRL
299
but also considering soft hits from recommended content by the recommendation 
system. The authors in [ 11] propose a training-time adaptive federated reinforce-
ment learning algorithm, alleviating the performance degradation caused by the 
non-independent and identically distributed characteristics of content popular-
ity among edge nodes. However, existing caching algorithms mostly concentrate 
cached content in edge servers, overlooking the abundant cache resources of ter-
minal devices. 
Caching content on network edge devices can reduce the transmission dis-
tance of data in the network, thereby reducing delay. When content is cached 
close to end users, D2D communication enables devices to exchange data directly 
with each other, avoiding going through edge servers, further reducing delay and 
network congestion. However, applying D2D communication technology in the 
ﬁeld of edge caching still faces many challenges. In D2D communication, direct 
data exchange between user devices may increase the risks of data leakage, mali-
cious attacks, and privacy infringement [ 12]. 
Therefore, we investigate edge caching algorithms assisted by D2D commu-
nication in wireless network, focusing on the privacy security of data during the 
training process, and propose a privacy-preserving edge caching algorithm based 
on permissioned blockchain and federated reinforcement learning (PBFRL). The 
main contributions of this paper is as follows: 
1) We ﬁrst model the edge caching scheduling problem in unreliable environ-
ments assisted by D2D communication in wireless network as a Markov decision 
process (MDP) and adopt a privacy-preserving edge caching algorithm to solve 
the MDP problem to minimize the content transmission delay. 
2) We propose the PBFRL algorithm. By introducing permissioned blockchain 
and deploying blockchain on base stations and user devices, we propose a per-
missioned blockchain and federated reinforcement learning algorithm, to ensure 
data privacy security. 
3) We propose an asynchronous local base station caching algorithm, running 
the sample collection algorithm and updating network algorithm separately on 
user devices and small base stations. 
4) We compare the proposed algorithm with other benchmark algorithms. Sim-
ulation results show that the proposed algorithm outperforms other algorithms 
in terms of both content transmission delay and data privacy security.

2.2
Communication Model 
When a user device requires a certain content, it ﬁrst checks if the requested 
content is already cached in its local cache. If the local cache hit occurs, the 
request is immediately satisﬁed. If the requested content is not cached, the user 
device who requesting the content will make content access mode decisions and 
content caching decisions based on its observed environment. SBSs make content 
caching decisions based on received content requests. Due to the limited cache 
capacities of SBSs and user devices, when the cached content exceeds capacity, 
the decision is made to evict the least popular cached content. The content access 
model consists of three main types: 
1) D2D caching: User devices requesting content send requests to nearby user 
devices to obtain the content. 
2) SBS transmission: User devices requesting content send content requests to 
SBSs to obtain the content. 
3) MBS transmission: User devices requesting content send content requests to 
MBS to obtain the content.

2.4
Problem Model 
In this system model, when a user device receives a content request, it must 
ﬁrst check if the required content is already stored in its local cache. If the 
content is cached, the user device can directly retrieve and satisfy the request 
from its local cache. However, if the required content is not present in the local 
cache, the user device must decide whether to request the content from nearby 
devices or from a SBS based on the current state of the system environment. 
According to the modeling analysis, the delay costs incrementally increase when 
obtaining content from local cache, SBSs, and MBS. User experience is inﬂuenced 
by various factors, among which the average delay of content acquisition is a 
key metric. Therefore, this paper focuses on optimizing the average delay of 
content acquisition. This discussion only considers the content transmission delay 
during the transmission process and does not account for delays that might 
occur in other segments. According to Eq. 8, the ﬁnal delay value is closely 
related to the cache hit ratio and the chosen content access mode. Therefore, 
to achieve system delay minimization, it is crucial that user devices make the 
correct choice of content access mode. After acquiring content, the system needs 
to make appropriate decisions on whether to cache this content and which cached 
contents to replace, aiming to maximize the overall cache hit ratio. By optimizing 
the caching strategy and the choice of content access mode, the delay experienced 
by users in accessing content can be eﬀectively reduced, thus enhancing the 
quality of user service experience. 
Our objective is to minimize the average content acquisition delay across the 
system; hence, the optimization objective in this paper is: 
\bALT{}\label{sps9} \begin{matrix} {P1} & {\mathop {\min }\limits_\pi {\mkern 1mu} {{\rm{T}}^f}(\pi )} & {} \cr {s.t.} & {\sum\nolimits_{f \in F} {h_z^f*{x_f} \le {M_{u_n^z}}{\rm{ }},z \le Z,n \le N} } & {(9a)} \cr {} & {\sum\nolimits_{f \in F} {h_{{B_n}}^f*{x_f} \le {M_n}{\rm{ }},n \le N} } & {(9b)} \cr \end{matrix}\eALT{}

## Página PDF 313

304
Z. Luo et al.
In the edge caching network, there are numerous uncertainties, such as 
changes in content popularity at base stations, diﬀerences in initial cache states, 
and user mobility patterns, leading the entire network to operate in a stochas-
tic and dynamic environment. As decision-makers, user devices need to make 
real-time cache update decisions, executing corresponding cache update opera-
tions, thereby changing their cache states. Evidently, there is a clear recursive 
relationship between cache states and caching behaviors. That is, the current 
cache state will inﬂuence the subsequent caching decisions, and the execution 
of caching operations will alter future cache states. Thus, the next section will 
model the described optimization problem as a Markov Decision Process to cap-
ture the dynamic changes of the network environment in real time, and by solv-
ing the established MDP, obtain the optimal caching strategy under the current 
conditions. The MDP is adept at describing the state transitions and behavior 
choices in the decision-making process. It seeks to solve for the optimal deci-
sion sequence based on the goal of maximizing cumulative returns. The next 
section will transform the edge caching issue into an MDP model, fully con-
sidering various uncertainties within the network, and aiming to maximize the 
expected cumulative returns to solve for the optimal caching update strategy 
in response to dynamic network environment changes, thereby enhancing the 
wireless network’s cache hit ratio and minimizing content transmission delays. 
3
MDP Model 
Reinforcement learning problems are typically modeled as Markov Decision 
Process . In this framework, an agent engages in continuous interaction with 
the external environment within a sequence of discrete time slots, denoted as 
\mathcal{T}={ 1,2,\cdots ,t,\cdots ,T}T = {1, 2, · · · , t, · · · , T}, where 1 \le t \le T1 ≤t ≤T and t \in \mathbb{N}^{+}t ∈N+. These discrete time slots 
constitute a complete training cycle. At each time slot tt, the agent ﬁrst acquires 
the current observation from the environment, thus perceiving the environmen-
tal state S_tSt. Based on the current state, the agent selects an action A_tAt. The 
environment then responds to this action by transitioning to a new state S_{t+1}St+1, 
and provides an immediate reward R_tRt for the agent’s action. The interaction 
between the agent and the environment at time slot tt is represented by the tuple 
M_t=\langle S_t, A_t, R_t, S_{t+1} \rangleMt = St, At, Rt, St+1 . 
In reinforcement learning, the agent decides on the action to take in each state 
according to a policy \piπ. This learning paradigm is built upon a reward hypoth-
esis: the agent continuously interacts with the external environment, learns con-
tinually from experience data, and constantly optimizes its strategy with the 
goal of maximizing the long-term cumulative reward. The system state space is 
composed of the state spaces of all user devices, which are segmented into the 
states of various time slots. The action space is similarly structured. Therefore, 
this section will directly describe the states, actions, and rewards at time slot tt.

3.3
Reward 
The optimization objective of this paper is to minimize the average transmis-
sion delay, which can be calculated for content ff using Eq. 8. During the initial 
stages of training in reinforcement learning, the agent often lacks suﬃcient under-
standing of the environment’s state and policies, necessitating extensive random 
exploration. If the reward increases slowly during this phase, the agent may 
struggle to ﬁnd the correct direction and potentially get stuck in local optima, 
impeding further progress. Conversely, a quicker rise in rewards can provide
