4
Algorithm Design 
This section is divided into two subsections. The ﬁrst subsection introduces an 
edge caching algorithm based on permissioned blockchain and federated rein-
forcement learning. It begins by outlining the problems existing in traditional 
federated reinforcement learning-based edge caching algorithms, then it proceeds 
to detail the newly proposed algorithm. The second subsection proposes the 
Local Base Station Caching Algorithm, starting with a user device local caching 
algorithm based on D3QN. Considering the limited hardware resources of users, 
we propose an asynchronous local base station caching algorithm, running the 
sample collection algorithm and updating network algorithm separately on user 
devices and small base stations 
4.1
Edge Caching Algorithm Based on Permissioned Blockchain 
and Federated Reinforcement Learning 
Federated learning allows user devices to jointly train a caching model without 
the need to upload data. However, various issues can arise in these distributed

[Vista original de la página 315](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-315.png)

![Página PDF 315, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-315.png)


<!-- PDF page 316 -->

## Página PDF 316

PBFRL
307
systems, which can generally be categorized into three types: 
1) Single Point of Failure Risk: In federated learning, the central node is sus-
ceptible to malicious model updates, which could compromise the global model 
and subsequently reduce the accuracy of all local model updates. Additionally, 
when numerous devices simultaneously send model data to the central node, it 
can lead to network congestion and possibly render the central node temporarily 
unavailable. 
2) Privacy Leakage Risk: Although training data is stored locally, the federated 
learning framework may still pose risks of privacy leakage. 
3) Model Poisoning Issue: Without a veriﬁcation mechanism, malicious users 
may upload carefully designed malicious models that aﬀect global model 
training. 
These issues can signiﬁcantly disrupt the learning process of the model. 
Therefore, this paper introduces permissioned blockchain technology to assist 
in the normal training of federated learning. The proposed PBFRL algorithm, 
as shown in Fig. 2, involves the following training steps: 
1) Identity Registration, System Initialization: Select appropriate SBSs to join 
the main chain. SBSs participating in training must ﬁrst complete identity reg-
istration to obtain a unique cryptographic account. An elliptical curve digital 
signature algorithm and asymmetric encryption technology are used to establish 
a trusted identity for nodes. Speciﬁcally, user devices and SBSs register through 
a central authority to obtain legal identities. These identities consist of pub-
lic/private keys and a digital certiﬁcate. The public key serves as the source 
address for caching, used to verify authenticity and integrity; the private key is 
used for digital signing to ensure non-repudiation; the digital certiﬁcate binds 
the registration information of the SBS, providing a unique identiﬁer and trust 
credential for the station’s identity. 
2) Sub-chain Creation: Each SBS selects appropriate user devices to participate 
in the federated learning training process. User devices also need to complete 
identity registration to obtain unique cryptographic accounts. Subsequently, the 
SBS downloads the global model from the server to individual user device nodes 
via cellular networks. Within the coverage area of the same SBS, all participat-
ing user devices form a blockchain sub-chain. 
3) User Device Interaction to Generate Experience Samples: User devices inter-
act with the environment using the downloaded latest global model to generate 
experience samples. All experience samples need to be shared among user devices 
in their respective sub-chains via D2D and cellular networks. All veriﬁed expe-
rience samples form blocks that are stored on the sub-chain. 
4) Cross-chain Communication: Each SBS participating in the main chain trans-
fers the ledger of its sub-chain to the main chain. Cross-chain communication 
technology supports this process. 
5)SBS Model Update: SBSs form aggregated updates based on the blocks 
received from their sub-chains. They upload signed local updates and cache hit 
ratios to smart contracts.

[Vista original de la página 316](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-316.png)

![Página PDF 316, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-316.png)


<!-- PDF page 317 -->

## Página PDF 317

308
Z. Luo et al.
6) Global Model Update: Smart contracts verify and accept local updates from 
SBSs. After collecting a suﬃcient number of models, the model with the highest 
cache hit ratio is used as a reference to calculate the similarity of every other 
model to it. Each SBS receives a queue of models from this round of federated 
learning along with model similarities from the blockchain, sorted in ascending 
order of similarity. The top models are selected to form a new subset. An aggre-
gation algorithm is used to obtain the next round’s global model, which is then 
stored in the blockchain and smart contracts. Smart contracts publish the global 
model to start the next round of federated learning. 
The deﬁnition of model similarity is detailed as follows: If a device’s private 
data all have the properties of independent and identical distribution (i.i.d.), the 
parameters of the deep learning models trained should exhibit some similarity. 
Conversely, if a device’s model is trained on data that is not i.i.d., its similarity to 
the standard model will be less than that of models trained on i.i.d. data. This 
subsection sums up the diﬀerences in parameters of each layer of the model. 
Model similarity is deﬁned as: 
\bALT{} {{S}_{ab}}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}{\frac{\left\| {{W}_{{{a}_{k}}}}-{{W}_{{{b}_{k}}}} \right\|}{\left\| {{W}_{{{b}_{k}}}} \right\|}}\eALT{} Sab =
K
k=1
Wak −Wbk
Wbk
(15) 
where KK is the total number of layers in the model, {{W}_{{{a}_{k}}}}Wak is the weight parameters 
of layer kk of model aa, and {{W}_{{{b}_{k}}}}Wbk is the weight parameters of layer kk of model bb. 
4.2
Local Base Station Caching Algorithm 
In this subsection, a caching algorithm based on D3QN is proposed to address 
problem 14. D3QN is a reinforcement learning algorithm that combines elements 
Fig. 2. PBFRL algorithm

[Vista original de la página 317](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-317.png)

of Dueling DQN and Double DQN, and like DQN, it is derived from the Q-
learning reinforcement learning algorithm. D3QN builds on Double DQN by 
incorporating a dueling structure, which decomposes the value function into a 
state value function and an advantage function. Speciﬁcally, for a state-action 
pair, the QQ value is decomposed as: 
\bALT{} Q({{S}_{t}},{{A}_{t}})=V({{S}_{t}})+Adv({{S}_{t}},{{A}_{t}})-{\sum\nolimits_{a\in A}{\left[ Adv({{S}_{t}},a) \right]}}/{|A|}\eALT{} Q(St, At) = V (St) + Adv(St, At) −
a∈A [Adv(St, a)]/|A|
(16) 
where V({{S}_{t}})V (St) is the state value function representing the expected return under 
state {{S}_{t}}St; Adv({{S}_{t}},{{A}_{t}})Adv(St, At) is the advantage function, indicating the advantage of 
choosing action {{A}_{t}}At over other actions under state {{S}_{t}}St; and \frac{\sum_{a\in A}{\left[ Adv({{S}_{t}},a) \right]}}{|A|}
a∈A [Adv(St,a)]
|A|
is the 
average advantage of actions at state {{S}_{t}}St. D3QN uses the Temporal Diﬀerence 
(TD) algorithm to update the QQ values: 
\bALT{} Q({{S}_{t}},{{A}_{t}};{{\theta }_{t}})=Q({{S}_{t}},{{A}_{t}};{{\theta }_{t}})+\alpha \left( {{y}_{t}}-Q({{S}_{t}},{{A}_{t}};{{\theta }_{t}}) \right)\eALT{} Q(St, At; θt) = Q(St, At; θt) + α (yt −Q(St, At; θt))
(17) 
where \alpha \in (0,1)α ∈(0, 1) represents the learning rate, and the TD target value {{y}_{t}}yt can be 
calculated by Eq. 18. 
\bALT{}\label{sps18} {{y}_{t}}={{R}_{t+1}}+\gamma Q\left( {{S}_{t+1}},{{\operatorname{argmax}}_{a\in A}}Q\left( {{S}_{t+1}},a;{{\theta }_{t}} \right);\theta _{t}^{-} \right)\eALT{} yt = Rt+1 + γQ St+1, argmaxa∈AQ (St+1, a; θt) ; θ−
t
(18) 
where {{\theta }_{t}}θt is the parameters of the online network, and \theta _{t}^{-}θ−
t is the parameters of 
the target network. The loss function for D3QN is expressed as: 
\bALT{} Loss(\theta )={{({{y}_{t}}-Q({{S}_{t}},{{A}_{t}};{{\theta }_{t}}))}^{2}}\eALT{} Loss(θ) = (yt −Q(St, At; θt))2
(19) 
The parameters \thetaθ can then be updated by the following formula: 
\bALT{} {{\theta }_{t+1}}={{\theta }_{t}}-\alpha \nabla (Loss(\theta ))\eALT{} θt+1 = θt −α∇(Loss(θ))
(20) 
Given the varying hardware resources (CPU, GPU, etc.) of user devices 
and their limited computational capabilities, this subsection proposes an asyn-
chronous training algorithm that separates the sample collection algorithm from 
the updating network algorithm, running them on diﬀerent devices. The sam-
ple collection algorithm involves the agent interacting with the environment and 
storing the generated samples in an experience pool; the updating network algo-
rithm refers to the agent retrieving samples from the experience pool to update 
the network parameters. The speciﬁc algorithm is as shown in Algorithm 1.

5
Simulation Experiments 
This section analyzes the security of the proposed PBFRL algorithm and veri-
ﬁes its performance. The ﬁrst subsection analyzes the algorithm’s security; the 
second subsection ﬁrst introduces the experimental environment and settings, 
then discusses the benchmark algorithms, and ﬁnally veriﬁes the convergence 
of the algorithm and shows the performance comparison with other diﬀerent 
benchmark algorithms. 
5.1
Security Analysis 
The introduction of permissioned blockchain provides trust in unreliable envi-
ronments for edge caching scenarios.

[Vista original de la página 319](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-319.png)

![Página PDF 319, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-319.png)


<!-- PDF page 320 -->

## Página PDF 320

PBFRL
311
1) Independence from Third Parties: The permissioned blockchain reduces the 
reliance on third parties in D2D caching. If third-party involvement is required 
during the D2D caching process, the entire system’s security will be subject 
to the security of the third party. If the security of the third party cannot be 
ensured, the privacy within the system could be at risk of exposure. In the 
PBFRL algorithm proposed in this paper, cache providers and requesters estab-
lish D2D communications by verifying legal identities without the need for third-
party intervention. 
2) Privacy Security: All user devices and SBSs use their public keys as source 
addresses, and transactions are signed and can only be accessed by devices with 
the correct private keys. Malicious devices can only forge signatures if they 
obtain the private keys, but devices can only obtain other devices’ public key 
information. Since it is not possible to derive private keys from public keys, 
malicious devices cannot forge the signatures of legitimate devices. The use of 
public/private keys and signatures ensures the privacy of user devices. 
3) Traceable Transactions: All transactions broadcasted on the blockchain are 
permanently recorded with timestamps and cannot be modiﬁed by any sin-
gle entity. Because the blockchain is a distributed ledger and transactions are 
updated synchronously, they can be easily obtained from any SBS. In the event 
of malicious activity, any user device can easily verify and trace previous records 
by accessing the SBS. 
4) Veriﬁcation Mechanism: The legitimacy of models uploaded by SBSs is ver-
iﬁed through the similarity of models, eﬀectively preventing malicious devices 
from poisoning the system. 
5.2
Performance Analysis

Table 1 provides a detailed listing of the hyperparameters used during the train-
ing process, including the maximum number of training rounds in federated 
learning, the number of base stations selected per round, and the maximum 
number of iterations per round. The simulated environment considers scenar-
ios involving one macro base station, ﬁve small base stations, and 200 user

[Vista original de la página 320](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-320.png)

![Página PDF 320, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-320.png)


<!-- PDF page 321 -->

## Página PDF 321

312
Z. Luo et al.
devices. The content library contains 200 items, randomly set between 1–10M. 
The caching space at small base stations is 100M, and the default caching space 
on user devices is 20M. The transmission power and bandwidth of user devices 
are 0.25 W and 10 MHz, respectively, and the D2D communication range for 
users is 30 m. The coverage area of a small base station is a circle with a radius 
of 200 m, and the bandwidth of small base stations is 10 MHz. The transmission 
power of small base stations is 40 W. The Gaussian white noise level is -174 dBm, 
the path loss constant is $10^{-2}$10−2, and the path loss exponent is 4. The assumed 
transmission rate between small base stations is 50 M/s, and between macro 
base stations and user devices, it is 5M/s. Each iteration of federated training 
consists of 10 cycles, with a maximum of 500 steps per cycle, a discount factor 
of 0.999, a learning rate of 1e-5, and a batch size of 32. Moreover, the network 
comprises an input layer, an output layer, and three hidden layers, with neuron 
counts of 32, 64, and 32, respectively. 
To evaluate the performance of the proposed PBFRL algorithm, this study 
compares it with three baseline caching algorithms: 
1) Edge Caching Algorithm based on Hierarchical Federated Learning and Rein-
forcement Learning (ECHFRL): Each user device updates its model based on 
local data, with ﬁrst-level aggregation occurring at small base stations. The mod-
els from each small base station are further aggregated at the macro base station 
to form a global model. 
2) First In First Out (FIFO): This method involves a random content access 
approach. When the cache is full, the content with the least recent access is 
replaced. 
3) Least Recently Used (LRU): This approach also involves random content 
access. When the cache is full, the content that has not been accessed for the 
longest period is replaced. 
4) Random Cache Algorithm (RANDOM): This method involves a random con-
tent access approach. When the cache is full, content is replaced at random. 
We initially verify the convergence of the proposed PBFRL algorithm, as 
illustrated in Fig. 3, where the x-axis represents the number of rounds and the 
y-axis represents the average reward per round. The data in the curve has been 
smoothed, and the shaded area around it represents the distribution range of 
results from ten independent experiments. As seen from Fig. 3, in the proposed 
PBFRL algorithm, the system’s average reward exhibits a generally increasing 
trend with the number of rounds. The initial system reward value is approxi-
mately 0.53, and after about 400 rounds, the system’s reward value converges and 
stabilizes around 0.85. This indicates the feasibility of the proposed algorithm. 
Furthermore, the minimal ﬂuctuation within the shaded area indicates a cer-
tain level of stability in the algorithm. Overall, the PBFRL algorithm eﬀectively 
addresses the edge caching scheduling issues in wireless network as proposed in 
this paper. 
As illustrated in Fig. 4, box plots display the average delay for ﬁve diﬀerent 
algorithms under varying Zipf skewness factors. The x-axis of the three subplots

Fig. 4. Comparison of average delay of diﬀerent algorithms 
represents the abbreviations of the names of ﬁve algorithms, while the y-axis indi-
cates the average delay in seconds. When skewness factors \sigma = 0.7σ = 0.7, the average 
delay of the PBFRL algorithm is approximately 3.2 s, compared to about 4.1 s 
for the RANDOM algorithm, indicating a 21.95% improvement in performance 
of the PBFRL over the RANDOM. Across all three subplots, the average delays 
of the proposed PBFRL algorithm consistently remain lower than those of the 
other benchmark algorithms, and the range of observations for PBFRL always 
remains within a smaller scope. Furthermore, the ECHFRL algorithm’s average 
delay is closest to that of the PBFRL, though ECHFRL has a broader range of 
observations than PBFRL. The observation ranges for FIFO, LRU, and RAN-
DOM algorithms are narrower than that of PBFRL, but their average delays

are signiﬁcantly higher than that of PBFRL. Outliers (represented by black dots 
in the ﬁgures) are observed in the average delays of the other four algorithms. 
Overall, the proposed PBFRL algorithm shows the lowest average delay and a 
more concentrated distribution of delay times, conﬁrming its excellent stability. 
This supports the superior performance of the PBFRL algorithm in managing 
average delay eﬀectively in comparison to other baseline algorithms. 
Fig. 5. Trend of average delay with the number of user devices 
Figure 5 shows the trend of average delay as a function of the number of user 
devices under diﬀerent Zipf distribution parameters. The x-axis of each subplot 
indicates the number of user devices covered by each small base station, while 
the y-axis represents the average delay in seconds. From Fig. 5, it is evident that 
as the number of users increases, the average delay decreases. This reduction in 
delay is attributed to the increase in user devices capable of D2D communication, 
leading to a higher D2D cache hit rate and consequently, a reduction in average 
delay. Additionally, as the Zipf skewness factor increases, the average delay for 
all algorithms decreases. This is because the content distribution becomes more 
concentrated, making content requests more regular and thus increasing the 
cache hit rate of the algorithms, which in turn lowers the average delay. Despite 
the increased cache hit rates due to larger cache spaces and skewness factors, 
the proposed PBFRL algorithm consistently maintains the lowest average delay. 
When the Zipf skewness factor is $ \sigma = 0.9 $σ = 0.9 and the number of user devices covered 
by a small base station is 70, the average delay of the PBFRL algorithm is 
approximately 2.10 s, representing improvements of 38.6%, 35.2%, and 38.2% 
over the FIFO, LRU, and RANDOM algorithms, respectively. This is because 
the PBFRL algorithm is a federated reinforcement learning-based edge caching 
algorithm that dynamically selects better content access methods and makes 
more accurate content replacement decisions. 
6
Conclusion 
This paper primarily investigates edge caching problems in wireless NetWork, 
with a focus on data privacy and security. Initially, the edge caching problem in

[Vista original de la página 323](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-323.png)

![Página PDF 323, representación visual original](../../../../../images/v5/estructura_colecciones/Same%20Solution/secondary/Repository-Level%20Code%20Generation%20Method%20Enhanced/page-323.png)


<!-- PDF page 324 -->

## Página PDF 324

PBFRL
315
wireless network assisted by D2D communication is modeled as a Markov Deci-
sion Process. Subsequently, a privacy-preserving edge caching algorithm based 
on permissioned blockchain and federated reinforcement learning is proposed 
to address the MDP problem, aimed at minimizing the content transmission 
delay at small base stations. The PBFRL algorithm incorporates a permissioned 
blockchain deployed on both base stations and user devices to ensure data pri-
vacy and security. Considering the varied hardware resources of user devices, 
an asynchronous training method is introduced, separating the sample collec-
tion algorithm and the updating network algorithm to run on diﬀerent devices. 
Ultimately, simulation results demonstrate that the proposed PBFRL algorithm 
surpasses other benchmark algorithms in terms of content transmission delay 
and data privacy and security.
